The eighth volume of Locke's Correspondence had been substantially completed and proof-read by Esmond de Beer before failing eyesight compelled him to cease work. Oxford University Press is grateful to the following for assistance in locating and preparing for publication the Additional Letters which appear in an Addendum to the volume, and for completing the Index to Correspondents:
Dr. M. R. Ayers, Mr. R. Baxter, Mr. I. C. Cunningham, Mrs. Elfrida Dubois, Dr. L. A. Holford-Strevens, Dr. J. R. Milton, Dr. G. A. J. Rogers, Dr. H. A. S. Schankula, Dr. M. A. Stewart, Mrs. Jean Yolton, Professor J. W. Yolton.
Thanks are also due to Mr. J. S. G. Simmons for reading the proofs of this volume, and of all its predecessors.
Dr. de Beer originally intended the edition to be completed by an Index to the Correspondence, which was to have appeared in this volume. It is now planned to publish the Index as a Supplementary Volume.

Locke's Life: Principal Dates for this Volume
This table is planned to provide dates that should be serviceable to readers of the correspondence. It is not a chronological framework for Locke's life.
The principal difficulty in its compilation is that for many incidents in his life precise dates do not exist. Thus, if the mention is accurate, Locke was acquainted with Robert Boyle on 20 May 1660; there is nothing to show how much earlier the acquaintance was made. Unsatisfactory as many of them are, the dates are the best available.
In the dates a. (for ante) is to be taken to imply shortly before. In the dates for books when a precise date accompanies the word 'published' it is to be taken as the date of the earliest notice that the book had been, was, or was about to be, published, and not necessarily as the day of publication.
   
1704 	17 August. The first proofs of Locke's Paraphrase and Notes on the Epistle to the Galatians. 
16 September. Peter King married. 
28 October. Locke dies.

Letters Nos. 3287-3648
1703
May
3287. Isaac (later Sir Isaac) Newton to Locke, 15 May 1703 (1664, 2488)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 16, ff. 155-6. Printed in King, pp. 225-7; Newton, Corr. iv. 405-6.
London 15 May 1703.
Sir
Upon my first receiving your papers†1 I read over those concerning the first Epistle to the Corinthians, but by so many intermissions that I resolved to go over them again so soon as I could get leasure to do it with more attention. I have now read it over a second time and gone over also your papers on the second Epistle. Some faults which seemed to be faults of the Scribe I mended with my pen as I read the papers. Some others I have noted in the inclosed papers.†a †2
In your Paraphrase on 1 Cor.VII. 14, you say, The unbeleiving husband is sanctified or made a Christian in his wife. I doubt this interpretation because the unbeleiving husband is not capable of baptism as all Christians are. The Jews looked upon themselves as clean holy or separate to God and other nations as unclean unholy or common and accordingly†b it was unlawfull for a man that was a Jew to keep company with or come unto one of another nation. Act.X.28. But when the propagation of the Gospel made it necessary for the Jews who preached the Gospel to go unto and keep company with the Gentiles, God shewed Peter by a vision in the case of Cornelius that he had cleansed those of other nations so that Peter should not any longer call any man common or unclean, and on that account forbear their company: and thereupon Peter went in unto Cornelius and his companions who were uncircumcised and did eat with them. Act.X.27, 28 and XI.3. Sanctifying therefore and cleansing signify here, not the making a man a Jew or Christian but the dispensing with the law whereby the people of God were to avoyd the company of the rest of the world as unholy or unclean. And if this sense be applied to St Pauls words they will signify that altho Beleivers are a people holy to God and ought to avoyd the company of unbeleivers as unholy or unclean, yet this law is
― 2 ―
dispensed with in some cases and particularly in the case of marriage. The beleiving wife must not separate from the†a unbeleiving husband as unholy or unclean nor the beleiving husband from the unbeleiving†b wife. for†c the unbeleiver is sanctified or cleansed by marriage with the beleiver, the Law of avoyding the company of unbeleivers being in this case dispensed with. I should therefore interpret St Pauls words after the following manner.
'For the unbeleiving husband is sanctified or cleansed by the beleiving wife so that it is lawful to keep him company and the unbeleiving wife is sanctified by the husband, else were the children of such parents to be separated from†d- you-†d and avoyded as unclean, but now by nursing and†e educating them in your families you allow that they are holy.'
This interpretation I propose as easy and suiting well to the words and designe of St Paul, but submit it wholy to your judgment.†1
I had thoughts of going to Cambridge this summer and calling at Oates in my way, but am now uncertain of this journey. Present I pray my humble service to Sir Francis Masham and his Lady. I think your Paraphrase and Commentary on these two Epistles is done with very great care and judgement. I am
Your most humble and obedient servant
IS. NEWTON.
Address: For John Locke Esqr at Oates in Essex.
Endorsed by Locke: J: Newton 15 May 03
3288. Dr. Pieter Guenellon to Locke, 21 May/1 June 1703 (3274, 3313)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 11, ff. 110-11.
Monsieur,
Ma femme survit encore a l'etat extréme ou nous la vismes le 8 et 9 du mois passé, elle etoit presque sans pou et sans parole.
― 3 ―
Monsieur veen et d'autres Medecins, aussi bien que moy, la crumes mourante et aux abois. sa vie est encore triste et languissante; mais avec quelque amandement, elle mange un peu davantage, et avec assez d'appetit, elle a moins de fievre. cela luy a fait renaitre un peu de force. elle sort du lict, peut marcher quelque pas, et elle a disné aujourdhuy avec moy a table. son courage la soutient. plust a Dieu quelle put vaincre son mal et se retablir! ses phlegmes sont moins purulents, et surnagent, et sont moins copieux. ainsy je flotte entre la crainte et l'esperance. Si j'avois quelqu'exemple d'un semblable retour, je me flatterois davantage; il est vray quelle a essuyé deux orages approchants de celuycy, a l'age de 8 et de 17 ans, outre le mauvais etat ou elle fut a sa premiere couche, quand veritablement toute la famille la crut agonisante. Si Dieu luy prolonge encore un peu sa vie, et que ses forces augmentent, elle pretend vous écrire, et de vous remercier pour vos tendres expressions envers elle et nótre famille. je n'ay point recu celle que vous m'avez fait l'honneur d'ecrire en Avril, apparemment quelle s'est egarée avec celles qui furent pris ce tems la avec la pacquetboot;†1 Monsieur Limburgh y perdit aussi les siennes. votre état nous afflige beaucoup, et de ce que votre Asthme ne relache pas au beau tems quil fait. cela nous fait craindre pour vous. Dieu veuille vous donner du soulagement et prolonger vos jours, qui ne scauroient finir sans étre regrettés de tous ceux qui vous connoissent, et sans une perte considerable pour ceux qui aiment la droitture, la paix et la verité.
toute la famille vous presente ses tres humbles respects. je suis de tout mon cœur,
Monsieur,
Votre tres humble et tres obeissant serviteur
P. GUENELLON.
d'Amsterdam ce 1 juin 1703
Address: A Monsieur Monsieur Joh. Locke A Oates
Endorsed by Locke: Dr Guenellon 1 Jun 03 Answered 24
― 4 ―
3289. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 24 May 1703 (3285, 3291)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, f. 282.
Oates 24° May 03
Dear Cosin
Sir Francis goeing to town I suppose will see you and therefore I cannot forbear by him to enquire how you got to town you and your fellow traveller,†1 and how you doe. I have a desire for a little while to see the Flying post. I therefore desire you will put that also into your packet of printed news, when I have enough of him, which I beleive will be in a little while I will say hold: My service to Mr Collins and the rest of my friends as they come in your way. I thank you for your company and am
Dear Cosin
Your most affectionate Cosin and humble servant
J LOCKE
All here are your servants
Address: For Peter King Esqr To be left at Mr Potmans in the Middle Temple London
Endorsed by King: Mr Locke. May. 24. 1703.
3290. Samuel Clarke to Locke, 27 May 1703 (3309)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 6, f. 187. The writer was Locke's tailor.: nos. 1347, 1755.
Honnoured Sir
Some Time since I have Received your Letter and since I have Received my bill by the hand of Mr <Ansham>†a Churchell for which I humbly†b thanke you. and with outt that I should Gladlie searved you and my bussnes or Annything Elles and Ame glad of your hellth which pray God Conntinew is the whole Desir of Sir your
Humble searvatt†c To Commande
SAMUEL CLARKE
London the 27th May 1703

3291. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 1 June 1703 (3289, 3292)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 283-4. This letter and nos. 3292, 3294, 3298, and 3300 relate to a projected marriage for King, probably with Elizabeth Clarke. Allusions in these letters show that there had been an earlier negotiation; it is not traceable elsewhere in the extant correspondence.
Oates 1° Jun 03
Dear Cosin
If my friend, who you tell me in yours of the 27th of the last month is so uncertain what to doe, that he knows not how to resolve himself, had, as I expected from him, declared his inclination one way or other, I might perhaps have put a stop to any farther motion, or else have enterd a little into the matter accordingly. But since it is his part and noebodys else to determin himself, the great concerne being his and the whole matter before him, I can onely tell him that it is fallen out as I guessed it would. I have been pressed by the old Lady†1 to propose the matter again to him, and to let him understand, that if he be at liberty and has an inclination that way he shall be favourably received. This I could not refuse to promise to communicate to him, and I doe it by the first oportunity that he may have the more time to consider. He knows the persons and circumstances of things, I can give him noe new view in the matter which he has not had already. And as I have noe inclination, so I can say no thing to perswade him either way: His own likeing or dislikeing upon consideration of the whole matter must determin him. If he has noe inclination to it he has noe more to doe but to deliver a No (mollified <by>†a such reasons or excuses as he shall think proper) to Mr Freke,†2 who has been named to me as the person by whom they have a desire to receive the answer. If he has an inclination, by the same Mr Freke he may make what new propositions
― 6 ―
he thinks fit now circumstances are changed on both-sides. I say this of new propositions to him from my self not from others. Which way ever he inclines I wish heartily it may be for his advantage. They express a willingness to have an answer as soon as conveniently may be. I as his friend wish he may be happy, but as to the matter it self I have neither concerne or inclination one way or tother. Let him take care and please himself, and I shall be perfectly pleased, which way ever he takes. Pray let him be assured of this, and have noe thoughts or phansys concerning me in the case, but doe just as if I were out of the world. Pardon me Dear Cosin that I trouble you so much concerning the affair of my friend. I love him truly, and would have him happy, but he must be judg of what will make him so by his own reason and relish. I thank you for the news you sent me though I wish some of it better. I am
Dear Cosin
Your most affectionate Cosin and humble servant
J LOCKE
This family salutes you
The strangers that are here have taken a coach for Friday next†1
Address: For Peter King Esqr member of Parliament. To be left at Mr Potmans in the Middle Temple London Frank.
Endorsed by King: Mr Locke June. 1. 1703.
3292. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 3 June 1703 (3291, 3294)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 285-6. See no. 3291, head-note. Mrs. and Mr. Clarke; the Bachelor.
Oates 3 Jun 03
Dear Cosin
By the last post I acquainted you that Mrs C — had desired me to let you understand that if you were at liberty and had any inclination you should be very kindly received. Since that Mr C— begining the discourse with me ended in expressions of a very high esteem of you addeing that if you should think fit to renew that treaty again there was noe thing in his power that you can desire which he would not doe. I asked him whether he said this to me
― 7 ―
with a desire and intention that I should communicate it to you. he answerd yes, and that he authorised me to doe it, addeing again that he would doe any thing in his power. I thought it fit to acquaint you with this also by the first oportunity, That you might see in what disposition these people are. Judg for your self, please your self, and as your own free choise and inclination leads you give them your answer by Mr Freke when you have maturely considerd of it. If any thing farther be said to me before they goe from hence you shall receive it by the next post, they intend to be in town tomorrow. I wish you all happyness and am
Dear Cosin
Your most affectionate Cosin and humble servant
J LOCKE
Address: For Peter King Esqr member of Parliament. To be left at Mr Potmans in the Middle Temple London Frank.
Postmark: IV 4
Endorsed by King: Mr Locke June 3. 1703.
3293. Locke to Anthony Collins, 3 June 1703 (3278, 3301)
The Carl H. Pforzheimer Library, New York. Transcribed from photostat. Printed in A Collection of Several Pieces, pp. 254-5.
Oates 3 Jun: 03
Sir
It is not enough to have heard from my Cosin King that you got safe to town, or from others that you were since that well there. I am too much concerned in it not to enquire of your self how you doe. Besides that, I owe you my thanks, for the greatest favour I can receive, the confirmation of your freindship by the visit I lately received from you. if you knew what satisfaction I feele spread over my mind by it you will take this acknowledgment as comeing from something beyond civility, my heart goes with it and that you may be sure of, and soe useless a thing as I am have noe thing else to offer you. As a mark that I think we are past ceremony I hear with send you a new book in quires, with a desire you will get it bound by your Binder. In the parts of good binding besides folding beating and sowing well I count strong pastboards and as large margents
― 8 ―
as the paper will possibly afford, and for lettering I desire it should be upon the same leather blackd, and barely the name of the author as in this case Vossius.†1 Pardon this liberty and beleive me with perfect sincerity and respect
Sir
Your most humble and most obedient servant
J LOCKE
Sir Francis and all this family give you their most humble service
Address: For Mr Anthony Collins at his house in Holburn row in Lincolns Inne feilds London
3294. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 4 June 1703 (3292, 3296)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 287-8. See no. 3291, head note.
Oates 4 Jun 03
Dear Cosin
If you received my two letters one of the 1st the other of the 3d instant sent you by the two last posts you will have understood how matters wherein you are concerned have passed here. I know not which way your inclinations will lead you, be carefull not to constrain your self, but to pursue your own content and happyness. But if you have any inclination to the thing I think if I were in your case I should interpret what the Gentle—— that went from hence to day†2 said on purpose that it might be told you, I should <interpret>†a it I say as if he expected that I should enlarge my demands and that he intended to comply with them, and therefore I if I (after what had passed) should enter upon that matter again I should make my proposals accordingly. I doe not doubt but they who have been here are mightily for it and I doe not doubt but Mr F ,†3 by whom your answer is desired, is of the same mind with them. This I tell you as my opinion of the state of the business, that you may make your advantage of it and take your measures of proceeding by it, if you have any mind to proceed. How persons
― 9 ―
please, you alone can judg: how they will prove, time onely can shew. But this is past doubt, that silver being always a valueable commodity, 4 ounces will always be better than three by one fourth part. When he began his discourse with me he would have laid the former breakeing off upon the not comeing up to his termes. I told him I had demonstration to the contrary of that. Whereupon he alledged that no farther. I am Dear Cosin
Your most affectionate Cosin and humble servant
J LOCKE
Address: For Peter King Esqr member of Parliament. To be left at Mr Potmans in the Middle Temple London Frank
Postmark: IV 7
Endorsed by King: Mr Locke June. 4. 1703.

3296. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 8 June 1703 (3294, 3300)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, f. 289.
Oates 8° Jun 03
Dear Cosin
I writ unto you on Friday last†2 which I hope you received yesterday
According to your desire in yours of June 5th I here with send you the book you sent for to dispose of as you think fit. The translator after it was printed corrected several places in his translation, and if you look into it you will find many places writ in which is according to the translators correction. This is the onely book I have but if you think it will be of use you may send it. I am
Dear Cosin
Your most affectionate Cosin
J LOCKE
Address: For Peter King Esqr To be left at Mr Potmans in the Middle Temple London
Endorsed by King: Mr Locke. June. 8. 1703.

3297. John Bonville to Locke, 8 June 1703 (3265, 3302)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 4, f. 130.
London June 8th 1703
Honoured Cousin
Sir Just now I Received A Letter from Mrs Lockhart desireing me to get A scribner To Copie the Leases of her Estate, which I have by me, and she desirs me to send her word, when she may have Thes Copies drawne, Sir It Is your Aprobation In this matter before I shall Give An Answar, I find she Is very desirous to have It don, pray Cousin, If Esquire Clerk be still with you be pleased To Give him and Madam Clerk my very Humble Servis, I shall be glad to see them, for by Them I shall Know how you Are, Sir your speedy Answar Is desired, My wife Gives her humble servis To you,
I Am Deare Cousin
Your Faithfull Kinsman And Humble Servant
JNO: BONVILLE
I hope you have received The packett which I sent you by Lenham, from Mr Locke
Address: For Mr Locke [at Oates, by Joslyn, Bishops Stortford]
Postmark: IV 8
Endorsed by Locke: J: Bonville 8 Jun 03 Answered 11
3298. Edward Clarke to Locke, 8 June 1703 (3257, 3396)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 6, ff. 145-6. Printed in Rand, p. 589. See no. 3291, headnote.
London June the 8th. 1703.
Deare Sir,
Your many and greate Favours to mee and mine, I doe with a true sence of them gratefully acknowlidge, But most particularly That wherein you gave my Wife and mee hopes of your Assistance for Reviveing the Affayre in relation to Mr. King, And I doe assure you there shall bee nothing wanting on our parts to bring it to a happy conclusion, Which is, and ever was, heartily desiered by
your most Affectionate and obleiged humble servant,
EDW: CLARKE
I hope your Wifes Letters went safe to you by the last Post;
― 12 ―
Pray Accept our repeated Thankes for your greate Kindnesse to us All at Oates, And lett Sir Francis and my Lady Know how sencible wee All are of the obligations they layd on us there etc:
Doctor Cole this day sent the Inclosed to mee to bee conveyed to you:
I have not a Word of News to send you:
Address: For John Locke Esqr [at Oates, by Joslyn, Bishops Stortford] Frank: Edw: Clarke:
Postmark: IV 8.
Endorsed by Locke: E: Clarke 8 Jun 03 Answered 18
3299. Dr. William Cole to Locke, 8 June 1703 (1414, 3580)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 6, ff. 209-10. Enclosed in no. 3298.
Honoured Sir,
Madam Clarke has done me the favour to deliver your very obliging lettre; in which I am sorry to find your Asthmaticall symptoms are still so troublesome. Though I make no doubt You, who understand Physick so well, and cannot but have much conversed with the most eminent Physitians that have beene these 30 yeeres (For, as I remember, you have heretofore told me, Sir, that you have beene subject to this distemper for so long a time, that it must now amount to that number of yeeres) in relation to your Case, must know as effectually what is proper in it as any man; and your Judgment is indisputable, since you have preserved your life so long under it, which I question whether any man else could: Yet, Sir, to adde my mite, at your command; I have observed that the use of Garlick has in some persons given great reliefe.
I knew one Mr Cooper, an Upholsterer in Bloomsbury, to whom 7 or 8 yeeres agoe I was called in a violent Fitt of an habituall Asthma (and had, besides, a great Jaundice, and Ascites, which appeared deplorable). That I then took off by bleeding, and other evacuations; But being no farther consulted by him (who was altogither addicted to try receits given him by any body, but Physitians, except in that extremity) I met him some moneths after, altogither freed from his three distempers; and asking him how he came to be so well, he told me that going to (I thinke) my Lord Yarmouths,†1 who was his
― 13 ―
Customer, his Lordship enjoyned him to eat Garlick, raw, in great quantities (to 5 or 6 heads, not cloves, of it in a day) and directed him to make some kind of Diet drink, which he being shy of discovering, I could not know: Applying himselfe to this direction closely, he told me he in a little time recovered, and continued still to take his garlick, which I could easily find by the smell before I came very neare him. I heare he is lately dead, but of what distemper, I know not. Others have told me that they have used it Preserved, as also the Syrup of it, with good effect.
I have long since, and frequently, used to direct a pouder of white Sugar Candy (or even loafe sugar) and Bals. Sulphuris terebinthinatum, by adding gutt. 20 or 30 of the balsame to ℥j of the Sugar; to be taken in the quantity of as much as will lye on the point of a broad knife severall times in the day, and to be permitted to dissolve in the saliva before tis swallowed; that the halitus of it may be drawne inter inspirandum into the Lungs as well as the body of it passe into the Stomack, and so perhaps it may assist Nature as a Topic, aswell as in the course of the lustration of the liquors with which tis to be carryed inwards: and you know, Sir, what esteeme Authors have of [sulphur] (especially when the body of it opened, and no way, I conceive, better than by oyle of Turpentine) in affects of the Lungs. It has, in my observation, given many reliefe; and the tast after a little tryall, uses to be gratefull ynough to most palates.
I have severall times directed the Linctus benedictus Pharmacopœiæ Batianæ, which I presume you have by you, with good effect to promote expectoration; and Mr Shipton has heretofore told me Dr Bates, who contrived it, frequently, and successfully, prescrib'd it.†1
I have also found a good effect of these pills. ℞ gummi Ammon. ʒij flor. Benzoini, [sulphuris] an. ʒj croci ℈ij, Cum tereb. Chiâ. F. pil. magn. pisor. Sum. iiij vel v bis in die.
I propose, Sir, that for a while you familiarly take Infusions (not decoctions) of Pectoralls, as Enula camp. Ir. Florent. hedera terrest. or the like, in white wine and water, in such proportions as may not make them ungratefull. The Inf. may if you please be edulcorated with Syr. bals. Tolutani, or some other pectorall or diuretick syrup. I use to order the Apoth. to cover the vessell, inter infundendum, with a wetted bladder to confine the Energetticall
― 14 ―
particles of the Ingredients. I have somtimes found Gas [sulphuris], added to pectorall drinks, to agree very well.
If the Asthma be convulsive, you know, Sir, Dr Willis uses antispasmodicks, as sp. sal. armon. cum gum. Ammoniaco prep. castor, etc. with good effect. Of this kind there is a considerable history at the end of his chapter de Asthmate.†1
But I must needs say I looke on Insensible perspiration, duely promoted after the last sleepe in the mornings, to be the best prophylactic, aswell in this as most other chronicall distempers, if not in almost all. For the effects of it I rely upon Sanctorius,†2 whom nobody that I know of has, in neare a hundred yeers, contradicted. But if I have beene mistaken in tracing the causes and source of it, I humbly request, Sir, you will please to shew me my false reasonings, since tis my firme persuasion no man is more able.
I am,
Sir,
Your most humble servant,
WM COLE.
Jun. 8. 703.
Address: For the very much honoured, John Lock Esqre, at
Endorsed by Locke: Dr Cole 8 Jun 03
3300. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 11 June 1703 (3296, 3305)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 290-1. See no. 3291, head-note. The quoted passage is from no. 3298.
Oates 11 June 03
Dear Cosin
In a letter I received this post I find these word
Your etc —— I doe with a true sense of them gratefully acknowledg but most particularly that wherein you gave my wife and me hopes of your assistance for reviveing the affair in relation to Mr —— and I doe assure you there shall be no thing wanting on our parts to bring it to a happy conclusion which is and ever was heartily desired by ——
― 15 ―
All the assistance I gave hopes of was onely to report what was said to me. Indeed when she broke it to me at the end of our discourse she insinuated as if I should say it as from my self But to this I in words at length told her I could not nor would not doe. what she had a mind I should say from her I would which then she desired me to doe and was what I told you. I doe not find my self in any likely hood to come to town, If you can come to Oates you are sure to be welcome. I am
Yours
J L
Address: For Peter King Esqr member of Parliament To be left at Mr Potmans in the Middle Temple London Frank.
Postmark: IV 14
Endorsed by King: June. 11. 1703. Mr Locke.
3301. Locke to Anthony Collins, 18 June 1703 (3293, 3306)
The Carl H. Pforzheimer Library, New York. Transcribed from photostat. Printed in A Collection of Several Pieces, pp. 255-7. Collins did not receive the letter until 29 or 30 June: no. 3311, which answers it.
Oates 18 Jun 03
Sir
It would be strange if after all those marks of friendship and esteem I have received from you in the little time I have had the honour of your acquaintance I should quarrel with you, and should repay the continuance of your good offices imploid even in things beneath you, with grumbleing at you, and yet this I can hardly forbear to doe. Doe not I beseech you take this to be altogether ill nature but a due estimate of what I enjoy in you. And since upon just measures I count it the great treasure of my life, I cannot with patience hear you talke of condescention in me when I stick not to wast your time in lookeing after <the binding of my books>.†a If you please let us live upon fairer termes, and when you obleige me give me leave to be sensible of it. And pray remember that there is one Mr Colins with whom if I desire to live upon equal termes, it is not that I forget how much he is superior to me in many things wherein he will always have the precedencie, But I assume it upon the
― 16 ―
account of that freindship that is between us. Freindship levelling all inequalitys between those whom it joyns, that it may leave noething that may keep them at any distance or hinder a perfect union and enjoyment. This is what I would be at with you and were I not in earnest in it out of a sincere love of you, I would not be soe foolish to rob my self of the onely way wherein I might pretend to enter the lists with you. I am old and useless and out of the way, all the real services are then like to be on your side: In words, expressions and acknowledgments there might have been perhaps some room to have made some offers of holding up to you. But I desire that noe thing of the court guise may mix in our conversation. Put not I beseech you any thing into your letters to make me forget how much I am obleiged to you by the liberty you allow me to tell you that I am
Sir
your most affectionate freind as well as most obedient servant
J LOCKE
The book was excellently well bound as well as the dispatch quick. for both which I thank you
Sir Francis and my Lady and the rest of the family are your humble servants
Address: For Mr Anthony Collins at his house in Holborne row in Lincolns Inne feilds London†a
Postmark: IV 21†b

3303. Humfry Smith to Locke, [19 June 1703?] (3321)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 18, ff. 130-1. Apparently enclosed by the Revd. Richard King in his lost letter of 8 July: p. 42. The date 19 June is from Locke's endorsement; King's letter may have provided authority for it. Answered by no. 3321.
Humfry Smith, B.A., Oxford, 1675; M.A. 1682; vicar of Dartmouth 1685-1709: Foster. He was now collecting materials for a life of Dr. Edward Pococke; they were eventually used by Dr. Leonard Twells in the life of Pococke prefixed to Pococke's Theological Works, 1740.
Worthy Sir
Being lately informd that you were, for several years, very intimately acquainted with Dr Pocock; I must desire you to impart to me what Observations you made of his Piety and Learning, and any other Memoirs you may have relating to that Great and Good man. I cannot doubt but you will pardon the confidence of this request, since my only end in it is the doing all the honour I can to his memory, in the Account I am drawing up of his Life, his Sons having put his Papers into my hands for that purpose. I hope in a little time to finish this design, which I have been long engaged in: Wherefore your Answer, at your first Leisure, will be a great obligation on,
Sir,
Your most humble servant
HUMFRY SMITH
Dartmouth in Devon
Address: For the much esteemed Mr John Locke
Endorsed by Locke: H: Smith 19 Jun 03 Answered Jul 23

3306. Locke to Anthony Collins, 24 June 1703 (3301, 3311)
The Carl H. Pforzheimer Library, New York. Transcribed from photostat. Printed in A Collection of Several Pieces, pp. 257-9. Answered by no. 3311.
There are conflicting statements in nos. 3307 and 3308, and they bring into question the date of the present letter. No. 3308, dated by King in his endorsement '24 June', was written later on the day on which Locke sent a letter to King by Bold. The latter is identifiable as no. 3307, dated 25 June: King answered it on 1 July (no. 3312), and in his letter of 3 July (no. 3314) wrote that in no. 3312 he answered 'the particulars contained in Mr. Bold's letter'. The contents of no. 3307 also indicate that it is earlier than no. 3308; it is Locke's first letter to King after the latter's visit to Oates that began on 19 June. That no. 3308 should be dated 25 June is further shown by the endorsement of no. 3305, which it answers; by a mention of it in Locke's Journal; and by the postmark: if it had been dispatched on Thursday 24 June it should have borne the London postmark 25 June; dispatched on the Friday, it bears the postmark Monday 28 June.
The most likely resolution of the problem is that Bold took leave on the 24th and left Oates early the following morning (Locke made him a farewell present on the 24th: Journal); that no. 3306 was written on the 24th and is correctly dated. It is probable that no. 3307 was written also on the 24th but postdated the 25th (the date is altered), the day of Bold's departure.
Oates 24 Jun 03
Sir
Mr Bold who leaves us to day intends to see you, and I cannot forbear goeing as far as I can to make the third in the company.
― 20 ―
Would my health second my desires not onely my name and a few words of freindship should goe with him to you, but I my self would get to horse, and had I noething else to doe in towne I should think it worth a longer journey than it is thither to see and enjoy you. But I must submitt to the restraints of old age, and expect that happyness from your Charity. Tis but six days since that I writ to you and see here an other letter. you are like to be troubled with me. If it be soe why doe you make your self beloved? why doe you make your self soe necessary to me? I thought my self pretty loose from the world but I feele you begin to fasten me to it again For you make my life since I have had your freindship much more valuable to me than it was before. you thankd me in your last for the imployment I gave you, I wish I doe not make you repent it. for you are like to have my custome. I desire you would doe me the favour to get me Dr Barrows English Workes bound as Vossius's Etymologicum was.†1 I am in noe manner of hast for them and therefore you may get them from your bookseller in quires when you goe to his shop upon any other occasion and put them to your binder at leisure. I have them for my owne use already, these are to give away to a yonge Lady here in the country.†2 When they are bound I desire your binder would pack them up carefully and cover them with paper enough to keep the corners and edges from being hurt in the carriage. For carriers are a sort of brutes and declared enemies to bookes. I am
Sir
Your most humble and most obedient servant
J LOCKE
Sir Francis and my Lady give you their humble service
Address: For Mr Anthony Collins these
― 21 ―
3307. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 25 June 1703 (i) (3305, 3308)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 294-5. For date and transmission see no. 3306, headnote. Answered by no. 3312.
Oates 25†a Jun 03
Dear Cosin
I hope you and your fellow traveller†1 got well to town. I should have been glad to have enjoyd your company longer. But you men of Business suppose the name of that must stop all desires of that kind. Yours being such business as you like and I can not disapprove I the more easily submit to it. But pray remember though your friends bear with the incroachment of business, yet your health must be preferd to it
When you were here the time before you left behind you in town the paper where in you had set down the dates of Mr Gatwards and Mr Jolliffes Morgages. I desire you to set them down in your next letter to me and also the time when the low-wine Tally was sold and for how much it was Sold. And also the date of Mr Fordes bond
Before you goe your Journey I shall send to you a letter to Mr Lyde and what else may concerne my country affairs, In the mean time I here again mention to you the peice of plate that I designe for him. I would have it to be between £5 and 6. I must leave it to you to order it soe as may be most acceptable
I had forgot to aske you when you were here whether you had deliverd to my Lord Shaftesbury his Julians works which I put into your hands two or three year agoe to that purpose.†2 If you have not I would be glad you would take care to give it to him before you goe your journey. I am sorry he had it not sooner. I shall deserve his censure for it. I am
Dear Cosin
your most affectionate Cosin and humble servant
J LOCKE
This family send you their service
I doubt not but you rememberd to put into the Penny Post our letter to Mr Limborch

3308. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, [25? June 1703] (ii) (3307, 3312)

Dear Cosin
Though I writ to you to day by Mr Bold yet to dispatch away to you the above written receit according to your desire I trouble you again. Pray return my humble thanks and service to my Lord and with the receit let him also have his Julians works if he has it not already. Tell him I have a particular reason to be sorry for this bad weather since to the other harme it does it has hitherto hinderd the enjoyment of the honour which his Lordship has made my Lady Masham and me hope. We pray heartily for fair weather for the countrys sake and for our own private interest, that his Lordship may have noe inconvenience in his way when he looks favourably hither ward. Sir Francis is at Eppeing and so I have noe answer to return you from him. I shall acquaint him with what you say as soon as he comes home. My Lady and all at home give you their service. I am
Dear Cosin
your most affectionate Cosin and humble servant

Pray send me word how I may direct a letter to your Cosin John Freke if there should be occasion

3310. Locke to Cornelius Lyde, 28 June 1703 (3034, 3330)
The Pierpont Morgan Library, New York. Transcribed from Xerox print. Answered by no. 3330.
Oates 28 Jun 03
Sir
I here with send you by my Cosin King an account of arears of rent that were due to me at our Lady day last was twelve month according to the account you sent me the 15th of August last. I have also sent you a warrant for distraining on Mary Cooke if she has not paid you before it comes to your hands. I beleive Pensford is not without a Baylif in it, he perhaps will be fitest to execute it. You must tell my Cosin King whom you think fitest to direct it to, that it may be donne accordingly. Eight year is too long to let a rent run in arear, upon promise from time to time of payment which is never donne. I am resolvd to stay noe longer
I desire also that the arear may be got in from William Gullock and that he may be sufferd noe longer to be behindhand. if he will not pay by fair means I shall take a shorter way than to aske and solicit from time to time to noe purpose which tends to noe thing but with a great deale of trouble to loose the money at last
― 24 ―
Hanny also has a long time promised to pay some, if he gives me fair words and does noe thing I can looke on it as noe other but an intention to deceive me and to laugh at me. He were therefore best shew an honest mind and pay some least it cost him dearer.
I have desired my Cosin King to present Mrs Lyde your†a wife†a with a piece of plate from me which I desire her to accept
I not writeing to you last winter near the time I know not whether the poor of Pensford had the bread that I usualy give them about Christmas. That it may not hereafter be forgotten I desire that you would as long as you doe me the favour to receive my rent there, every year about Christmas lay out twenty shillings in bread to be distributed to the poor of Pensford espetialy to those that are old and cannot worke and those who have a numerous familye of small children whom they cannot well maintain. still prefering the honest and industrious to those that are or have been lazy and vitious: and this I desire you to doe every year whether I mention it again to you hereafter or noe. Pray give my humble service to Mr Strachy and to his mother and sister, and to my cosin Lyde. I am
Sir
Your most humble servant
J LOCKE
Address: For Cornelius Lyde Esquire at his house at Stanton Weeke
Note by Lyde: August the 9th 1703
Joseph Hanny doe promis to pay 10s this weeke and 20s by Christmas and soe 10s a quarter untill it is paide
Geor Horwood doe promise to pay all at Pensford faire next
3311. Anthony Collins to Locke, 30 June 1703 (3306, 3318)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 7, ff. 1-2. Answers nos. 3301 and 3306; answered by no. 3318.
Lincolns Inne numb. 6 in Searl's court.
June. 30/03
Sir
I had the honour of a letter from you by Mr Bold on Munday last,†1 and by means of that had yours of the 18th: which being
― 25 ―
dated since my removal to Lincolns Inne I was deprivd of till I sent for it. This must be my excuse for the slow return of my thanks. Your freindship is so highly valuable to me, that I shall rejoyce at it upon any terms that you can propose, and am heartily sorry, my own reflection did not prevent the conviction I have receiv'd from you of the right way of application to you. I beg of you to attribute my very pardonable mistake, of not putting my self on the level with you, to the prevalency of my affection, and the awe that I had upon me, on account of those sentiments I have of you and my self which I can never depart from, that I can hardly yet perswade my self that I stand in such freindship, as you honour me with, any longer than your letters lye before me. I grant all disguise to be inconsistent with Freindship, and I can truly say that I have made use of no way to shew my esteem of you but still my heart went with it, and if there was any court-guise I am sure there was no court-intention. I wish I may not make you repent of your own terms: for I can now think of coming to see you at Oats without blushing at my own forwardness and begin to believe that I may write any thing to you without the fear of your judgment through the hold I have of your affection. I therefore acquaint you with a new book that is just publish'd by one Mr Broughton, Chaplain to the Duke of Marlborough, in the first part of which you are particularly concern'd by having your Name and the words of your third letter to the Bishop†a of Worcester frequently quoted; intituled Psychologia: or a Discourse of the nature of the Rational soul, but the remaining part of the title seems to me to discharge you from the trouble of reading what he says against you, since he pretends to have prov'd the Immateriality and consequently the Immortality of the soul upon sufficient grounds only.†1 To give you my opinion of the book it is to me a Discourse upon nothing, or which is all one on something that no body knows anything of, or if any one did, would know less from reading his book. I have given it to Mr Bold from whose better judgment you may have farther satisfaction about it.†2
― 26 ―
Mr Bold tells me that you have not as yet seen the New Testament of Mr Le Clerc, which by an accident I have had above this fortnight and can therefore very well spare it for your use.†1 It is not likely this good while as I am inform'd to fall into the Booksellers hands, from the great price it is sold at, since the loss of the 200 that were taken by the French,†2 as well as the want of a Convoy. Pray let me know your mind, and you shall have it with Dr Barrow's Eng: works which I will endeavour to get bound and sent according to your directions. I fear by this time you are too sensible of the effects of the liberty you allow me who am
Your most Affectionate friend
as well as most Obedient Servant
ANTH. COLLINS
My most humble service to Sir Francis Masham, My Lady and the rest of the family.
Endorsed by Locke: A: Collins 30 June 03

3313. Dr. Pieter Guenellon to Locke, 2/13 July 1703 (3288, 3325)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 11, ff. 112-13.
Monsieur
J'ay eu l'honneur de vous ecrire il y a quelque sepmaines sous couvert de Monsieur Limburgh, je vous mandois le recouvrement de ma femme, qui a tousjours augmenté depuis. je l'excitois deslors d'aller a la Campagne, et que le bon air pouroit contribuer beaucoup a son restablissement. elle l'a fait a ma persuasion, et partit il y a un mois, et depuis elle a profité de jour en jour, elle reprend ses forces, se promene pour le moins deux heures le jour, a diverses reprises, au grand air, mais a l'ombre du Jardin, mange d'appetit, dort d'un bon somme, passe des jours entiers sans fievre; mais tous les matins a son reveil elle essuye une toux, quelquefois assez facheuse, et qui luy produit des phlegmes gris et recuits et qui paroissent encore menacants. elle n'oseroit encore quitter son syrope de diacode, dont elle prend tous les jours environ une once, plus ou moins suivant la toux, qui sans ce secours la fatigue davantage, et empesche son dormir. quand elle a commencé a marcher les jambes luy enfloient beaucoup. cela a diminué a mesure que ses forces ont augmenté. en verité Monsieur, cest une chose tout a fait extraordinaire, qu'une personne aussi delicate et si foible de son naturel, dont les poulmons ont souffert depuis sa naissance, emaciée comme un scelet, puisse revenir de si loin! apres un exemple si rare, je n'oserois plus rien prononcer. Monsieur Furly m'a envoyé il y a trois sepmaines une piece de flanelle tres fine de la part de Monsieur Limburgh de Londres, sen s'expliquer autrement, j'en ay fait ecrire a Monsieur Limburgh par Monsieur son pere, qui a mon absence a fait scavoir au logis, que cette Piece vient de votre part, sans en dire davantage, apparemment, Monsieur, que la lettre que vous m'avez fait l'honneur d'ecrire en Avril dernier et qui ne m'a
― 28 ―
pas eté rendue, comme je vous l'ay desja marqué, en faisoit mention. je vous prie Monsieur de m'escrire, ce que vous souhaittez, que j'en fasse, et pour qui vous la destinez. oserois je vous demander l'etat de votre santé, j'espere que ce beau tems vous remet un pe<u>†a et vous rend la vie plus supportable. M<on>sieur†a Veen se porte presentement assez bien. il aime fort la Campagne et s'i divertit, c'est un bonheur pour ma femme, elle ne scauroit avoir meilleure compagnie. j'avois esperé qu'elle vous auroit escrit par cet ordinaire; mais elle vous demande encore un peu de dilay: elle m'ordonne de vous presenter ses tres humbles respects. et ses souhaits pour votre santé. Monsieur veen, mes enfants, et la reste de la famille vous font leur compliments, et je suis sans reserve,
Monsieur,
Votre tres humble et tres obeissant serviteur
P. GUENELLON.
d'amsterd. ce 13 juillet 1703
Address: For Mr.John Locke at Mr Powlings over against the plough in Little Lincolns inne feilds London
Postmark: IV 5
Endorsed by Locke: Dr Guenellon 13 Jul: 03 Answered 12

3316. Francis Limborch to Locke, 6 July 1703 (3304, 3327)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 13, ff. 215-16.
London 6 July 1703
Honoured Sir
When I considder the innumerable benefitts I received from your hands, j really finde myself att a stand and know nott how to excuse my self for the neglect I have had in nott acquainting you sooner the alteration off my condition,†1 and for which I confesse my father severily censured me, and cannott butt expect the same from you, who I know art so much a friend and wellwisher to me; but this j really can assicure you that itts nott proceeded from any disrespect but a meere scruple and for which I most sincerily begg pardonn; I really was in hopes to have been the messenger my self but the Continuall businesse which I have had on my hand and then the badnesse off my legg have hither to putt a stop to itt; and haveing now againe this week a fleett to goe for Holland j thank god I have my hands full againe so that I know nott how to stirr; this evening Mr Freake desired <me.>†a to tell you that Mr King with his humble service had desired him to lett you know that in case you might have occasion to draw any moneys on Mr King you would draw itt during his absence on Mr Freake who will respect your bills and Mr Freak desires youll direct for him att Mr Benjamin Tudmans goldsmith att the signe off the Crowne in Lumbard Streett or to me which be most convenient to you; haveing many letters to write I must Conclude these Once more begging you'll forgive the error Committed by
Honoured Sir
Your most oblidged humble servant
FRANCIS LIMBORCH
Address: For John Locke Esquire These
Endorsed by Locke: F: Limborch 6 Jul 03

3317. Elizabeth Clarke to Locke, 8 July 1703 (3258, 3347)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 6, ff. 163-4. Printed in Rand, pp. 589-90.
London July the 8th 1703:
Sir
The present of Bookes you have Lately made me,†1 will not Suffer me to be silent. but I find My Selfe so wholly incapable of Saying anything answerable to the greatness of the favour, that could I find out any other expedient that might convince you that I want no Sense of the reality of your friendship, I Should certainly chuse it; and So ease my Mind of the confusion it is under at sending you Nonsense, and giving you the trouble of reading it. But this Last civility has made it impossible for me not to trouble you with My humble thankes, which I beseech you to accept though in a Most unpolish't Manner, and do me the right to believe that I am
Dear Sir
Your most affectionate humble Servant
E: CLARKE
My Father and Mother are Servants to you, My Lady, Sir Francis, Mrs Masham and the young Gentleman. and we all joyne with 'em in the Same.
I remember when I was at Oates you asked me who it was that had Lately translated Don Quixote into english? I have Since inquired, and I hear 'tis done by Mr Motteux, and that it is as well or better than in French. The Woman that makes up Mr Frekes Chocolate (who you also inquired after) is One Mrs Emanson a relation of Mrs Popple's, who through her own industry Maintains her Selfe and Mother, and Lives at the blew-flower-pott in Eagle Streett near Red Lyon Square.
I hope you have received the Neckclothes.
Address: To John Locke Esqr at Oates
Endorsed by Locke: El: Clarke 8 July 03

3318. Locke to Anthony Collins, 9 July 1703 (3311, 3332)
The Carl H. Pforzheimer Library, New York. Transcribed from photostat. Printed in A Collection of Several Pieces, pp. 259-61. Answers no. 3311.
Oates 9 Jul 03
Sir
Yours of the 30th June I received just now and cannot forbear a moment to tell you that if there were any thing in my last letter that gave you an occasion after haveing mentioned disguise to say you have made use of no way to shew your esteem of me but still your heart went with it I am very sorry for it. For however I might think the expressions of your letter above what I could deserve, yet my blameing your excess of civility to me, tended not to any doubt of the sincerity of your affection. Had I not been secure of that I could not have talked to you with the freedome I did. nor have endeavourd to perswade you that you were lodgd soe near my heart, as you are. Though my freindship be of very litle value or use, yet being the best thing I have to give, I shall not forwardly bestow it where I doe not think there is worth and sincerity both which I am satisfied are in you, and therefore pray pardon me the forwardness where with I throw my armes about your neck, and holding you soe, tell you you must not hope by any thing that lookes like complement to keep me at a civiller and more fashionable distance. You complye with me I see by the rest of your letter, and you beare with my treating you with the familiarity of an establishd freindship. you pretend you have got the advantage by it. I wish it may be so, for I should be very glad there were any thing where in I could be usefull to you, find it out I beseech you, and tell me of it, with as litle ceremony and scruple as you see I use with you. The Greek Testament you mention†1 I shall be glad to see since Mr Bold has told you how desirous I was to see it. I have expected one of them from Holland eversince they have been out, and so I hope to restore it you again in a few days. The other book you mentioned I have seen, and am soe well satisfied by his fifth Sect what a douty squire he is like to prove in the rest, that I think not to trouble my self to looke any farther into him. He has there argued very weakly against his adversary but very effectualy against himself. But this
― 34 ―
will be better entertainment for you when I see you, than matter for a letter wherein I make it my business to assure you that I am
Sir
Your most humble and most faithfull servant
J LOCKE
All this family return you their most humble service
Address: For Mr Anthony Collins at his Chamber Numb: 6 in Searls Court in Lincolns Inne London
Postmark: IY 12
3319. Jean Le Clerc to Locke, 9/20 July 1703 (3279, 3468)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 13, ff. 146-7. Printed in Bonno, Le Clerc, pp. 126-7.
A Amsterdam le 20 de Juillet 1703.
Je vous aurois d'abord répondu, Monsieur, si je n'avois crû le faire par un de mes amis, qui doit bientôt partir pour Londres, et j'aurois remercié en même tems Madame Masham et Mr. son Fils. Mais cet ami tardant ici plus long tems que je n'avois crû, j'ai jugé qu'il valloit mieux que je vous écrevisses par la poste.
Pour commencer par vôtre commission, je suis fâché de ne pouvoir vous trouver tous les livres, que vous demandez, aussi promtement que je le voudrois. On ne trouve les Concordances de Kircher,†1 que dans les auctions, et je ne manquerai pas d'acheter le premier exemplaire, que j'en trouverai. Il y a un Ministre à Groningue nommé Trommius,†2 qui a entrepris il y a plus de six-ans d'en donner de nouvelles, mais je croi qu'il faudra trop long-tems attendre son travail, et je ne sai s'il executera bien son projet. Pour les Theses de Kemter,†a †3 je n'en ai pû apprendre aucunes nouvelles, mais je m'en informerai†b- de Mr.-†b de Volder, qui doit bientôt venir ici. Comme on n'imprime ces sortes de choses, que pour l'Auteur, elles
― 35 ―
se perdent très-souvent. Mais s'il est possible d'avoir ces Theses, je les aurai. Le dernier Tome de la Bibliotheque de du Pin, qui contient l'histoire du XVI siecle n'est pas encore sous la presse, mais Mr. Huguetan m'a dit qu'il l'y mettroit incessamment. Je m'imagine que c'est de son Edition, que vous avez les autres volumes, puis qu'ils sont in 4°. car l'édition de Paris est in 8°. Mais ils ont mis au titre à Paris, pour mieux vendre le livre.†1 Je n'accepterai, Monsieur, le reste de l'argent, que pour l'employer à vôtre ordre, car ce que je vous envoye ne mérite pas que vous l'achetiez. J'ai déja la Bible de Smidt,†2 que je vous envoyerai, par la premiere occasion, avec ce que je pourrai recouvrer, et ce que vous m'ordonnerez d'acheter, si l'on peut trouver ici quelque autre chose qui vous manque. J'ai fait adresser au Sieur Cailloüé Libraire François deux exemplaires du 2. Tome de la Bibliotheque choisie, l'un pour vous et l'autre pour Madame vôtre Hôtesse. Le Sieur de Varennes, autre libraire François, a aussi reçu de ma part deux exemplaires de mon Nouveu Testament, qui vous sont adressez, l'un pour vous et l'autre pour la même Dame. Vous aurez la bonté de les faire retirer, s'il ne vous les a pas encore envoyez. Il y en a aussi deux autres, dont l'un est adressé à Mylord Comte de Schaftsbury, et l'autre à Mylord Pawlet. Ces Seigneurs ayant été ici et m'ayant fait l'honneur de me venir voir, outre mille civilitez, j'ai crû devoir leur en témoigner ma reconnoissance, par ce petit présent. Mais comme je ne sai comment le leur faire tenir, je prends la liberté de vous supplier de les faire retirer et de les leur envoyer. Faites moi aussi la grace de me marquer leur adresse, afin que je me puisse donner l'honneur de leur écrire. Il n'y a qu'à me mander en Anglois les titres, qu'on leur met au dessus d'une lettre, et l'endroit où ils sont. Je vous en aurai une obligation très-sensible. Je suis bien-aise que le dessein de ma Bib. Chois. ne vous déplaise pas. Je tâcherai de faire en sorte qu'elle puisse divertir les honêtes gens, et ne me détourne pas trop de mon Ouvrage sur la Bible. Comme il ne paroît que très-peu de chose, qui mérite la peine d'être lû, les Ouvrages de Mr. Cudworth me seront d'un grand secours. Tout le monde en a lû ici les Extraits avec plaisir; excepté quelques entêtez Cartesiens, ou Spinosistes, qui sont ennemis de tout ce qui est different de leurs systemes. Vous verrez dans le 2. Tome un petit Abregé de la Reasonnableness of
― 36 ―
Christianity, qui est comme un Apologie oblique de ce livre.†1 S'il y manquoit quelque chose, on pourroit bien l'ajoûter une autre fois. Je suis, Monsieur, Vôtre très-humble et très-obeïssant serviteur
J LE CLERC.
Address: A Monsieur Monsieur Locke at Mr. Pawlings, in little Lincolns inne fields, over against the plough London.
Postmark: IY 16
Endorsed by Locke: J: le Clerc 20 Jul 03 Answered Aug. 20
3320. Rabsy Smithsby to Locke, 10 July 1703 (3277, 3517)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 18, ff. 140-1.
10 July 03
Sir
As you desired, I sent you six pounds of chocolate, which I hope you have received.
I have made you six paire of new sleves, and put wrest bands and ruffles to the two paire you sent.
I have allso bought you some wash Leather gloves, which I hope will Fitt, and you may Like, if you doe not, they may be returned and Changed for others.
I intreat a word or two from you that I may be assured of your health, and receive your direction for sending these things to you. I am
Sir
your most humble servant
RABSY SMITHSBY
I have marked the new sleves with stiches of Crimson silke, that your servant may give them to you in paires.

3321. Locke to [Humfry Smith], 23 July 1703 (3303, 3494)
The Remains of John Locke, 1714, pp. 1-6. That the letter was addressed to Smith is shown by its relationship to nos. 3303 and 3494. The subject is Dr. Edward Pococke. The corrections follow those in the reprint in A Collection of Several Pieces, pp. 332-43. The original letter was in the hands of Dr. Leonard Twells (D.N.B.); he printed extracts from it in his life of Pococke prefixed to Pococke, Theological Works, 1740, i. 83-4. I have checked Twells's text in The Lives of Dr Edward Pocock, etc., 1816, i. 349-53. The text there appears to be unreliable and I have not registered variations.
Oates, 23 July 1703.
Sir
I have so great a Veneration for the Memory of that Excellent Man, whose Life you tell me you are writing, that when I set my self to recollect what Memoirs I can (in answer to your Desire) furnish you with; I am asham'd I have so little, in particular, to say on a Subject that afforded so much. For I conclude you so well acquainted with his Learning and Virtue, that I suppose it would be superfluous to trouble you on those Heads. However, give me leave not to be wholly silent upon this Occasion: So extraordinary an Example, in so degenerate an Age, deserves for the Rarity, and I was going to say, for the Incredibility of it, the Attestation of all that knew him, and consider'd his Worth. The Christian World is a Witness of his great Learning; that, the Works he publish'd would not suffer to be conceal'd: nor could his Devotion and Piety lie hid, and be unobserv'd in a College where his constant and regular assisting at the Cathedral Service, never interrupted by sharpness of Weather, and scarce restrain'd by down-right want of Health; shew'd the Temper and Disposition of his Mind. But his other Virtues and excellent Qualities, had so strong and close a covering of Modesty and unaffected Humility, that tho they shone the brighter to those who had the Opportunities to be more intimately acquainted with him, and Eyes to discern and distinguish Solidity from Shew, and esteem Virtue that sought not Reputation; yet they were the less taken notice and talk'd of by the Generality of those to whom he was not wholly unknown. Not†a- that he was at all close-†a and reserv'd, but on the contrary, the readiest to communicate to any one that consulted him. Indeed he was not forward to talk, nor ever would be the leading Man in the Discourse, tho it were on a Subject that
― 38 ―
he understood better than any of the Company; and would often content himself to sit still and hear others debate, in matters which he himself was more a Master of. He had often the Silence of a Learner, where he had the Knowledg of a Master: And that not with a Design, as is often, that the Ignorance any one betray'd, might give him the Opportunity to display his own Knowledg with the more Lustre and Advantage, to their Shame; or censure them when they were gone. For these Arts of Triumph and Ostentation, frequently practis'd by Men of Skill and Ability, were utterly unknown to him; 'twas very seldom that he contradicted any one: or if it were necessary at any time to inform any one better, who was in a Mistake, it was in so soft and gentle a manner, that it had nothing of the Air of Dispute or Correction, and seem'd to have little of Opposition in it. I never heard him say any thing that put any one that was present the least out of Countenance; nor ever censure, or so much as speak diminishingly of any one that was absent. He was a Man of no irregular Appetites; if he indulg'd any one too much, it was that of Study, which his Wife would often complain of (and, I think, not without Reason) that a due Consideration of his Age and Health could not make him abate. Tho he was a Man of the greatest Temperance in himself, and the farthest from Ostentation and Vanity in his way of Living; yet he was of a liberal Mind, and given to Hospitality: which, considering the Smallness of his Preferments, and the numerous Family of Children he had to provide for, might be thought to have out-done those who made more Noise and Shew. His Name, which was in great Esteem beyond Sea, and that deservedly, drew on him Visits from all Foreigners of Learning, who came to Oxford to see that University. They never fail'd to be highly satisfy'd with his great Knowledg and Civility, which was not always without Expence. Tho at the Restoration of King Charles, when Preferment rain'd down upon some Mens Heads, his Merits were so over-look'd, or forgotten, that he was barely restor'd to what was his before, without receiving any new Preferment then, or at any time after; yet I never heard him take any the least Notice of it, or make the least Complaint in a Case that would have grated sorely on some Mens Patience, and have fill'd their Mouths with Murmuring, and their Lives with Discontent. But he was always unaffectedly chearful; no Marks of any thing that lay heavy at his Heart for his being neglected, ever broke from him. He was so far from having any Displeasure lie conceal'd
― 39 ―
there, that whenever any Expressions of Dissatisfaction for what they thought hard Usage broke from others in his Presence, he always diverted the Discourse: And if it were any body with whom he thought he might take that liberty, he silenc'd it with visible Marks of Dislike.
Tho he was not, as I said, a forward, much less an assuming Talker; yet he was the farthest in the World from sullen or morose. He would talk very freely, and very well of all parts of Learning, besides that wherein he was known to excel. But this was not all; he could discourse very well of other things. He was not unacquainted with the World, tho he made no shew of it. His backwardness to meddle in other People's matters, or to enter into Debates, where Names and Persons were brought upon the Stage, and Judgments and Censures were hardly avoided; conceal'd his Abilities in matters of Business and Conduct from most People. But yet I can truly say, that I knew not any one in that University, whom I would more willingly consult in any Affair that requir'd Consideration, nor whose Opinion I thought better worth the hearing than his, if he could be drawn to enter into it, and give his Advice.
Tho in Company he never us'd himself, nor willingly heard from others, any personal Reflections on other Men, tho set off with a Sharpness that usually tickles, and by most Men is mistaken for the best, if not the only seasoning of pleasant Conversation; yet he would often bear his part in innocent Mirth, and by some apposite and diverting Story, continue and heighten the Good-Humour. I shall give you an instance of it in a Story of his, which on this Occasion comes to my mind; and I tell it you not as belonging to his Life, but that it may give you some part of his Character: which, possibly, the very serious Temper of this good Man may be apt to make Men over-see. The Story was this. There was at Corpus-Christi College, when he was a young Man there, a proper Fellow, with a long grey Beard, that was Porter of the College. A waggish Fellow†a Commoner†a of the House, would be often handling and stroaking this grey Beard, and jestingly tell the Porter, he would, one of these days, fetch it off. The Porter, who took his Beard for the great Ornament that added Grace and Authority to his Person, could scarce hear the mention, in Jest, of his Beard being cut off, with any Patience. However, he could not 'scape the mortal Agony that such a Loss would cause him. The fatal Hour came; and see
― 40 ―
what happen'd. The young Gentleman, as the Porter was standing at the College-Gate with other People about him, took hold of his Beard with his Left-hand, and with a pair of Scissars which he had ready in his Right, did that Execution, that the Porter and Bystanders heard the cutting of Scissars, and saw a handful of Grey-Hairs fall to the ground. The Porter, on that sight, in the utmost Rage, ran immediately away to the President of the College; and there, with a loud and lamentable Out-cry, desir'd Justice to be done on the Gentleman-Commoner, for the great Indignity and Injury he had receiv'd from him. The President demanding what Harm the other had done him; the Porter reply'd, An Affront never to be forgiven; he had cut off his Beard. The President, not without Laughing, told him, That his Barber was a Bungler, and that therefore he would do him that Justice, that he should have nothing for his pains, having done his Work so negligently: for he had left him, for ought he could see, after all his cutting, the largest and most reverend Beard in the Town. The Porter, scarce able to believe what he said, put up his Hand to his Chin, on which he found as full a grown Beard as ever. Out of Countenance for†a his Complaint, for want of a Beard, he sneak'd away, and would not show his Face for some time after.
The Contrivance of the young Gentleman was innocent and ingenious. He had provided an handful of white Horse-Hair, which he cut, under the Covert of the other's Beard, and so let it drop: which the testy Fellow, without any farther Examination, concluded to be of his own Growth; and so, with open Mouth, drew on himself every one's Laughter: which could not be refus'd to such sad Complaints, and so reverend a Beard.
Speaking of the expedite way of Justice in Turkey, he told this pleasant Story; whereof he was an Eye-witness at Aleppo. A Fellow, who was carrying about Bread to sell, at the Turn of a Street spying the Cadee coming towards him, set down his Basket of Bread, and betook himself to his Heels. The Cadee coming on, and finding the Basket of Bread in his way, bid some of his Under-Officers to weigh it; (for he always goes attended, for present Execution of any Fault he shall meet with) who finding it as it should be, left it, and went on. The Fellow watching at the corner of the Street, what would become of his Bread; when he found all was safe, return'd to his Basket. The by-standers ask'd him, why he ran
― 41 ―
away, his Bread being Weight? That was more than I knew, says he; for tho it be not mine, but I sell it for another, yet if it had been less than Weight, and taken upon me, I should have been drub'd. Many things of this nature, worth notice, would often drop from him in Conversation; which would inform the World of several Particularities, concerning that Country and People, among whom he spent several Years. You will pardon me, if on the sudden my bad Memory cannot, after such a distance of Time, recollect more of them. Neither, perhaps, had this now occur'd, had I not, on an occasion that reviv'd it in my Memory sometime since, by telling it to others, refresh'd it in my own Thoughts.
I know not whether you find amongst the Papers of his, that are, as you say, put into your Hands, any Arabick Proverbs, translated by him. He has told me that he had a Collection of 3000, as I remember; and that they were, for the most part, very good. He had, as he intimated, some thoughts of Translating them, and adding some more, where they were necessary to clear any Obscurities; but whether he ever did any thing in it before he died, I have not heard. But to return to what I can call to mind, and recover of him.
I do not remember, that in all my Conversation with him, I ever saw him once angry, or to be so far provok'd, as to change Colour or Countenance, or Tone of Voice. Displeasing Accidents and Actions would sometimes occur; there is no help for that: But nothing of that kind moved him, that I saw, to any passionate Words; much less to Chiding or Clamour. His Life appear'd to me, one constant Calm. How great his Patience was in his long and dangerous Lameness, (wherein there were very terrible and painful Operations) you have, no doubt, learnt from others. I happen'd to be absent from Oxford most of that time; but I have heard, and believe it, that it was suitable to the other parts of his Life. To conclude, I can say of him, what few Men can say of any Friend of theirs, nor I of any other of my Acquaintance; that I do not remember I ever saw in him any one Action that I did, or could in my own Mind blame, or thought amiss in him.
Sir, If I had been put upon this Task soon after his Death, I might possibly have sent you a Paper better furnish'd than this is, and with Particularities fitter for your purpose, to fill up the Character of so good and extraordinary a Man, and so exemplary a Life. The Esteem and Honour I have still for him, would not suffer me to say nothing; tho my decaying bad Memory did ill second my Desire, to
― 42 ―
obey your Commands. Pray accept this, as a Mark of my willingness, and believe that I am,
Your most Humble Servant,
JOHN LOCKE.
3322. Locke to [Richard King], 23 July 1703 (2846, 3328)
The Remains of John Locke, p. 7. For the identification of the person addressed as King see no. 2846, head-note.
Oates, 23 July 1703.
Sir,
I cannot but think my self beholden to any Occasion that procures me the Honour of a Letter from you. I return my Acknowledgments for those great Expressions of Civility and Marks of Friendship I receiv'd in yours of the eighth Instant; and wish I had the Opportunity to shew the Esteem I have of your Merit, and the Sense of your Kindness to me, in any real Service. The Desire of your Friend in the inclos'd Letter you sent me,†1 is what of my self I am inclin'd to satisfy: and am only sorry, that so copious a Subject has lost, in my bad Memory, so much of what heretofore I could have said, concerning that Great and Good Man, of whom he enquires. Time, I daily find, blots out apace the little Stock of my Mind, and has disabled me from furnishing all that I would willingly contribute to the Memory of that Learned Man. But give me leave to assure you, that I have not known a fitter Person than he, to be preserv'd as an Example, and propos'd to the Imitation of Men of Letters. I therefore wish well to your Friend's Design, tho my Mite be all I have been able to contribute to it. I wish you all Happiness, and am, with a very particular Respect,
Sir, Your most Humble Servant,
JOHN LOCKE.
3323. Anonymous to Locke, 24 July 1703 (3271)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 23, ff. 194-5.
Sir
On the twentyth of April last I adventur'd to write to you, about a difficulty which occur'd to me in reading the 10 Chap: of the 4
― 43 ―
Book of your Essay concern. Hum. Und. I did not this till I had consider'd it very often my self, and taken all the other ways I could think of to get satisfaction; but seeing it was still my unhappyness to miss it, I thought the best course I could take was to consult your self about it. it was neither the vanity of writing to a man of your reputation, nor a humor to cavil and find fault, which made me trouble you; but a sincere desire of finding out the truth, and being satisfy'd in a mater of so much importance. I did not doubt but you would be ready to assist one in a difficulty which arises from your own writings, expecially when it concerns a point of such moment; and therefor I was perswaded you would give me an answer, and I pleas'd my self extreamly with the thoughts, that I should at length meet with that satisfaction which I have hitherto in vain sought after. but I have never yet heard from you, which makes me fear that my Letter has miscarry'd; though it was directed as this is to your Bookseller Mr Churchil, which is the only way I know to make it find you. I doubt not but you have frequently got ignorant or impertinent Letters about what you have publish'd in your Writings, nor do I wonder tho' you should sometimes neglect to answer such. and since I neither have nor pretend to any Learning, 'tis very likely that my difficulty may arise only from my ignorance: yet seeing it is insuperable to me, I must earnestly entreat you to condescend to solve it to me. if I could have got satisfaction elsewhere, or if the mater it self were not of so great consequence, I would be very loath to trouble you with any trifle which I can write.
My difficulty is about your way of proving the Existence of a God, and I shall repeat what I wrote to you about it formerly.
[The writer now repeats the passage in no. 3271 extending from 'You tell us Sir' (p. 764) to 'with all the clearness I could' (p. 766); there are slight variations, and a short passage 'This mightily inclines me to belive ... full information about maters of this kind' (p. 765) is omitted.]
when I consider how necessarly it behooves me to be well inform'd upon this Subject, and that I should be so dull as not to perceive the force of ane Argument which you tell us is a clear obvious Demonstration, I must again with all possible earnestness entreat you to remove my doubts, which will be ane infinite favour to
Sir
Your most humble tho' unknown Servant.
Edinburgh. july 24 1703.
― 44 ―
When you are pleas'd to write to me, direct your Letter <thus>,†a To Mr James jermyn. to be left with Mr David Erskine Advocat, at his Chamber in the Writer's Court, Edinburgh.
Endorsed by Locke: Anonymus 24 Jul. 03
3324. James Tyrrell to Locke, 25 July [1703] (3283, 3358)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 22, ff. 159-60. Year from Locke's endorsement.
July 25th
Dear Sir
I hope you will pardon me, if I have not given you so speedy an Account as I intended of the small Affair you trusted me with in seeing the Books you gave to the Library superscribed ex Dono Authoris, which I can assure I caused to be done: I hope you have had rather an Increase of health, since the Alteration of the weather from the wetest I ever saw in any summer to warme and drie, which I pray God continue, and I suppose you have now taken this oppertunity of coming to Towne and visiting the Lord P.†1 and your other Freinds for a day or two: I <have>†b little more to trouble you with, onely that I have read the Gentlewomans Defence of your Essay,†2 etc against the cavilling Animadverter and am so well pleas'd with it, that I think none but your self could have performed it better: which since you might think below you to undertake, yet I am very glad it is done, since I have heard some of our Academicks, speak of those†c letters†3 as things too hard for you to answere, which false surmise is now sufficiently confuted. I pray give my humble service to the worthy Couple with whom you are, and the rest of that good Family: I pray returne my Lady thanks on my behalf, for her obliging letter, which was too great an acknowledgment for so small a present: and had I any thing worth the trouble of Postage I would write to her a Reply, however, since I know her Ladyship has lived in an University I will to satisfy her Curiosity give you
― 45 ―
a short account of the late Act, at Oxford;†1 where there has bin a great deal of Company, to see and hear the Exercises of the memory rather then wit of the Performers, few things there repeated being the proper Goods of those that vented them; there was no musick speech, but to make amends for that losse the same Terræ Filius came up two days together and with greater confidence then wit, abused several who did not deserve it and especially some young Ladies of Quality, nor did he spare their owne Doctors, not even the Bishop of Bristol,†2 and therefore it was no wonder if your humble servant had allso a rub for the drienesse of his History, and it is well you have removed your quarters far enough from the Waspes nest, otherwise I would not warrant, that you should have escaped stinging; but these are among the Academical Priviledges, to lash those they hate, and flatter those they admire, and commonly they meet with labia talibus Lactucis digna.†3 this is all at present from
your most humble servant,
J TYRRELL
my Daughter is well and much your Servant. James is gone for Ireland to his Troop.
I could not tell you in the letter, that there are still at Oxon the Lincolns Inne Feilds Players,†4 and the Italian songstress has bin allso there tho she did not stay long:†5 the Place not affording her a Constant Audience.
Address: For John Lock Esqr [at Oates, by Joslyn, Bishops Stortford]
Postmark: IY 26
Endorsed by Locke: J: Tyrrell 25 Jul 03 Answered Oct. 4
― 46 ―
3325. Mevr. C. M. Guenellon (born Veen) and Dr. Pieter Guenellon to Locke, 27 July/7 August 1703 (2519; 3313, 3341)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 10, ff. 208-9.
[Mevr. Guenellon:]
Monsieur,
je ne croiois pas qu'aÿant perdu mes forces petit a petit, et qu'etant dans une si grande extenuation, il ÿ put avoir quelque retour pour moÿ. cepandant Diêu ma fait comme renaistre. je commence tout doucement a reprendre mes forces. jaÿ languis de parvenir a l'etat de vous pouvoir ecrire, et vous tesmoigner la joye que je sens que vous survivez aussi a tant de maux dont vous avez eté accablé. je me flatte tousjours dans un evenement si extraordinaire, que nous vous reverrons encore dans cette vie. je le souhaite passionement. votre absence Monsieur na rien diminué de la haute estime que nous avons tousjours eu pour vous. les eminentes vertus que vous possedez, cette grandeur d ame, et cette sincerité si pure, dont votre amitié est tousjours accompagnée, nous charment; il nÿ a que vos presens, qui nous embarassent, et qui nous mettent dans la confusion. si encore en eschange on pouvoit vous regaler de quelque chose; mais vos lois sont si austéres a notre egard, quelles ne permettent aucune entrée a nos denrées et quon ne scait quoÿ vous envoÿer. si j'aurois quelque reproche a vous faire, M<o>nsieur,†a c'est que vous nagissez pas assez librement ave<c>†a nous. mon mari voudroit de tout son coeur vous pouvoir rendre quelque service. ne le scauriez vous employer a quelque chose, soit pour vous ou pour vos amis? je vous prie Monsieur, d'y songer, et de croire que nous ne souhaittons rien davantage. je finis Monsieur, en vous remerciant bien humblement de votre beau present, dont j'espere de faire un bon usage. je prie Dieu de tout mon coeur, quil soulage votre estat, et prolonge vos jours. je suis avec toute la reconnoissance que je vous doibs,
Monsieur,
Votre tres humble et tres obligee servante
CORNELIA MARIA VEEN
D'Amsterd. ce 7 Aoust 1703.
― 47 ―
[Dr. Guenellon:]
Monsieur
Je vous remercie de tous vos bien faits. je vous ecrits avec une espece de honte et confusion. vous nous honoréz et nous aimez avec tant de distinction, que je voudrois meriter cette bienveuillance. et je ne scaurois. chacune de vos lettres porte des marques si singulieres de votre interessement pour nous, que je ne scaÿ comment ÿ respondre. il me fache quil me reste quelque doubte sur votre bellis major. ma femme auroit fort besoin d'un tel remede. sa toux diminue peu, et elle jette beaucoup de phlegmes.
dans le theatrum botanicum de Parkinson l'ox eÿe s'appelle buphthalmum et le bellis major the great white wilde daisie.†1 dans le hortus eysstettensis il y a une figure du bellis major. sive consolida media foliis primulæ veris.†2 nous avons icy une sorte de buphthalmum aussi appellée Cotula alba. non fætida. Oculus bovis. ital. occhio di bove. hisp. ojo de bueÿ cette mesme herbe s'appelle aussi chamemelum sylvestre. elle a la fleur que vous marquez. croit icy tres abondamment, j'en feray ceuillir et secher, et ne m'en serviraÿ pas jusquace que vous m'escriviez, si c'est la mesme. j'ay appris que notre Cousin Limburgh†3 s'est marié a Londres. son bon homme de pere en paroit assez contant. je souhaitte que cela luy ammene bonheur. toute la famille vous salue et je suis avec un tres profond respect,
Monsieur,
Votre tres humble et tres obeissant serviteur
P. GUENELLON
