August
3326. Samuel Bold to Locke, 23 August 1703 (3273, 3520)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 4, ff. 43-4. Perhaps the letter forwarded by John Churchill on 15 September: p. 65.
Steeple 23d Aug: 1703
Honored Sir
I staid so much longer in London (failing twice of a place in the Coach for my wife) than I intended when I parted from Oates, I could hardly refrain wishing my self again with the good company I so lately left; And had not the smalness of my Mare discouraged me, I should have given a real proof that I am so exceedingly pleased with the Conversation at Oates, Nothing can hinder me from making very frequent visits there, but my being seated at such a distance, I cannot reach thither in a day.†1 I think I may modestly say I have a Heart ful of Gratitude for the Honour you do me in esteeming me a Friend, and for the Mighty and Numerous Kindnesses you have lately, and ever since I had the Happiness to know you, heaped on me. It is my trouble I can no other way signify my Grateful sentiments, but by saying I thank you, and entreating you to believe that I am not to the last degree stupid and Illnatur'd; In which every one can equal me, whilst the Obligations I am under are very peculiar. I am sensible that I am very much obliged to sir Francis Masham, my Lady, and every one of that excellent and most justly esteemed Family, I hope you wil do me the favour to intimate the same to them, and to present my most humble service to every one of them. Sir, I had writ to you sooner, but Mr Collins having given me Mr Broughton's Book,†2 in the former part of which He pretends to prove that Matter is not capable of having a power of thinking superadded to It, and to answer what you have writ on that subject, I was willing to send you an account of what I should, upon reading Him, apprehend to be the substance of what He delivers about It; And that Book not coming to me so soon as I expected It, and some little countrey affairs intervening to prevent my reading It presently after I received
― 49 ―
It, have occasioned my deferring to write to you til now. This Essay (as the Author sometimes cal's It) hath not at all enlightened me. The principal effect It hath had on me, is, that It confirm's me in my opinion, that there are a sort of men who affect to write about what they do not understand. I think the whole He saith doth amount but to these two things, first That Matter considered as matter cannot think. secondly. That God cannot superadd a power of thinking to matter, because we can conceive that He cannot do It. And we can conceive that He cannot doe It, Because Extension (which He wil have to be the first property of matter) and thought are perfectly distinct Attributes, and there is no third thing by which they can be connected to the same substance. But that you may perceive the whole strength of his Discourse, I wil give you a particular account of what He saith. I venture (saith He) to lay down this as a self evident proposition, That where ever there are Accidents, there is of necessity a Substance. And this as next to self evident, That from the difference we find in the nature of Accidents, we must conclude a like difference in the nature of those substances to which they belong. For where ever a thing hath Its dependance as to being, there It must have it's dependance as to all the modes of existence. He grants some pages after, that all kind of difference in properties do not argue different substances; But when (saith He) we finde any two properties, which after a thorough examination are neither immediately connected themselves, nor mediately by any third thing, we must conclude by the same rule (and 'tis the only one we have in such cases) that they do appertain unto two different natures or essences. Of this latter kind is the difference between our Idea of cogitation, and that of extension. He hath these words also in another place, viz. The difference between extension and Thought is so wide, that there is no third Idea to unite them in one Substance; And this must render our endeavours to conceive them as so united for ever fruitless, and whatsoever we cannot conceive in created nature, to be so much as possible, is a repugnancy in the nature of the thing. It is easie for any one to experience in his minde the repugnancy of conceiving extension and thought as the properties of one and the same substance. Let Him as method requires frame as clear and distinct an Idea as he can of each of them seperately, and then let Him endeavour what He can to conjoyn them. let him try to make out an Inch of Reason, or an Ell of Contemplation, which would be as easie to do, were they
― 50 ―
two properties consistent, as to conceive a Reasoning Inch, or contemplating Ell of matter; For where ever two properties are united in one substance, they may be reciprocally predicated. Thus I can conceive an extended whiteness, as wel as a white extension.
By comparing our Faculty of Cogitation (saith He) with immaterial substance, we may perceive thought belongs properly to Immaterial substance. Here, He insist's on sensation, Which is a sort of thought (saith He) belonging to Man. It cannot be either in the object, or Organ, and therefore must precisely consist in that Idea which is raised in us; which Idea tho it is not to be described in words, (so far remote is It from any material Impression) yet it is something real within us; for we can as perfectly distinguish the several kinds of It, as those of any other nature. Now this Idea of sensation does of It self discover something Immaterial in us; this He proves thus, viz. Because different degrees of Heat give us the sense of pleasure and of pain, which are two contrary Ideas. (If I apprehend him aright, He argues thus, the difference in the Heat, is only gradual, and therefore our Idea's would differ only in degree, and not be contrary, but being contrary, there being so must proceed from something Immaterial in us. Now I think if there were something immaterial in us, and no power of thinking, we should neither have contrary Idea's, nor any Idea's at all. But I forget my self, for I did not intend to make any reflection at all)
After this He takes notice that Matter is purely passive, and Divisible, on both which accounts It is (He saith) utterly uncapable of thought. But we both move and reason from our selves, which necessarily infers the power of motion within our selves, and this is a power absolutely inconsistant with the passive nature of matter; Therefore so long as our first Idea of matter includes in It a passive nature, It includes also an utter incapacity of thought. There is no Corporal property, but what is divisible with the Body It belongs to, and therefore til the Instrument is invented so fine as in reality to split a thought, I thinke we must be forced to take up with this conclusion, That physically extended substance is in It's own nature utterly uncapable of Humane thought. We do not absolutely assert that Omnipotence cannot create cogitative matter, but upon supposition of our Idea in contemplating, and the truth of our faculties in collecting from that Idea of matter, that is, we assert that God cannot do It, because It appears to us no less than a contradiction that It should be done. So long as our Idea of
― 51 ―
physical extension, and of Cogitation continues the same, they must remain properties inconsistant in the same subject for the reasons before alledged. The great variety of qualities found in matter, and the excellency of some above others, as of vegetation above bare motion, are (saith He) all reconcilable to It's first property, and so may have a dependance upon It, tho we know not the manner how they depend. But it is not only above, but contrary to our conception, we not only cannot conceive how matter should think, but we do conceive that It cannot think. And tho we cannot conceive how an Immaterial being thinks, yet we can conceive that It can think. We are conscious of our thinking, and have all the reason in the world to believe that many other beings are endued with the same faculty, and hence, that is, from what we do perceive and know of thought, we collect that it belongs to a subject uniform and self active in It's nature; which is sufficient to satisfy us that such a being can think, or rather is capable of thought. To this, That God might create both a solid and unsolid substance, and both in a state of perfect inactivity etc He answers, It must be considered that God hath not created all natures or substancies so, as to be susceptible of all manner of properties. If he has all our reasoning from the nature of the thing is at an end. But as sure as our faculties are true, and as sure as our Creator has imparted variously differing natures to his creatures, so sure it is that He has tied himself to that Rule in conferring properties upon them, that is, has conferred such and only such as are suitable to their respective natures. But how do we know that such and such natures are capable of such and only such properties? Answer, Inconsistency with some known property is the chief rule whereby we can exclude any other from being grafted and nourished on the same stock. A self moving, and a sensless being are contradictory Epithets. When we find every solid substance, physically extended, and Divisible, and Passive, then we clearly conceive that such a solid substance is incapable of thought. He concludes thus, To whatsoever substance omnipotence hath given the faculty of thought, He either has never given to that substance, or actually taken away from It physical Extension. What I have said (He adds) I take to be a foundation for a ful answer to all that Mr L. has objected upon this Head to the late great Bishop of Worcester.
Lest I should have omitted any thing that would contribute to set his arguing in a ful light, I wil transcribe what He saith in his
― 52 ―
preface concerning his performance in this part of his Book. Where I can see little else than an elaborate vainglorious owning that He begs or supposes what is in dispute, and what He should prove. His words follow thus. It is indeed but one Reason that runs thro' the greatest part of the Essay, but I need not apologize for that, if I have made It conclusive. However I may without vanity say I have carry'd it farther than where I found it, and I cannot upon the most deliberate reveiw, but believe it wil conclude at last. The Reason, in short, is the αὐτοκίνετον, or self moveing power of the Soul, exerting it self in all the operations of thought and spontaneous motion. This, it's true, is an Argument of old Date, and I like it the better for that. It appear'd to me an Advantage to the Notion, that all the best esteem'd Philosophers of old, Thales, Pythagoras, Socrates, Plato, Aristotle, Cicero, and many more, differing among themselves in all other notions, and most of them Whimsical, and without ground, upon the subject, should yet unanimously center in this. I cannot but suppose they were led to it, and dwelt upon it by the same Reason, which wil I presume incline any man that believes God has created Spirit as wel as Body. for in consequence of this, he wil finde it highly reasonable to suppose that Spirit is the principle of life and sensation, and Body of Mechanism and Local motion. As therefore Spirit is, in it's nature, utterly incapable of Local motion, either from Trusion,†1 or Suction, and of any mechanical Affection, or Modification, so it is highly reasonable to conclude that Body is as incapable, on the other hand of Life and Sensation. For by what other Essential Character, can we distinguish these two, if they are two distinct species of beings. I grant, what mr Lock contends for, that 'tis possible to conceive a spirit devested of the operation of thought, and barely in his own Term as an unsolid substance;†2 but it is sufficient that we place the Distinction upon the Capacity, and Incapacity of Thought; for the other [of solid and unsolid]†a is no way serviceable to us; because we did not come by the knowledge of spirit, as really existing, by our Idea of something Unsolid, but only by our Idea of something that thinks; so that our Idea of an Unsolid substance, is not formed from the Contemplation of any thing without us, as that of solidity is,
― 53 ―
but is a bare Hypothesis to solve the Phænomenon of Thought. But now if a solid substance be possibly capable of this faculty of Thought, then there is no necessity to recur to the Hypothesis of an unsolid substance to solve that Phænomenon; and since Entia non sunt multiplicanda sine necessitate,†1 what is this but to introduce universal Corporealism?†2 Which is neither better nor worse than Atheism. Which consideration may serve to pull off the Mask this Notion, I do not say the Author of It wears; and to shew that He who makes both Body and Spirit think and live, does but comply with a vulgar mode of speech, but really destroys the distinction, and in effect ascribes all to Body. Here Sir, you see what value your Adversary hath for Truth, how strongly He argues, How consistent his discourse is, and what Idea He hath found out, for the word Atheism, to signify. But I hope you do not think I have spent all my time since I came home in reading such sort of Books as this I have given you so large an account of. Indeed there are such numbers of silly, worthless, Ostentatious Books published, it is no easie thing to light on such Books as a rational man can take much pleasure in. Yet about a fortnight back I accidentally mett with an extraordinary Book, the best I think that ever was, or ever wil be writ upon the subject. I think the Author hath absolutely exhausted It. It is admirably writ, And is the most entire and close piece of Reasoning that has bin published. A great variety of things are treated of, and in such a way as I apprehend no other man ever thought of. The original of very many things are most clearly accounted for, of which mankinde was all along in perfect ignorance, And there we have the best, the most rational account of the whole government of this Land from the very first, to the present Reig<n.>†a It is a Treatise that must surprize and astonish the Reader. One would not have thought that so much Light could have bin given to the subject, or that one should have mett with such a continued train of close Argumentation in a discourse of that nature. The Book is entituled Civil Polity.†3 It is printed for R. Wilkin. was published this present year. The Author hath Not thought fit to set his Name to
― 54 ―
It. What ever his Name is, He is undoubtedly a Man of prodigious parts, and I am perswaded He is a very good man. He has the truest notions that can be of Religion, both Natural, and Revealed. I am so exceedingly pleased with the Book, It is so very instructive and entertaining, I hardly know how to cease speaking of It, when once I begin. God Almighty bless the Author, abundantly reward Him, and exceedingly prosper his pains; Let him be had in everlasting remembrance. I am afraid the Book is hardly yet known. I speak of it wherever†a I come, and have not yet mett with any one, (but He who lent It me) who hath seen It. He from whom I had It, bought it purely for the title, sake, He never heard of It, but mett with It by chance, nor has He convers'd with any one who hath read It, or knows any thing of It. I wonder I should not meet with It in any Booksellers shop (for I was in several) when in London, nor hear any one, (no not mr Collins, whom I thought no book could escape) speak one word concerning It there. I wish the book may be every where known, read, and attended to. If mr Coste be at leisure, when He hath read It, He wil perhaps think It worth his pains to put It into French. Mr Clavel,†1 for whom at my desire, you were pleas'd to give Directions for Reading, return's you his hearty thanks for them, and presents his most Humble service to you. He acknowledges Himself exceedingly obliged to you, and He appears mightily pleased with your Instructions.†2 You see Sir I keep on my old way of doing what I can to tire you. If I do not write often to you, you perceive it is not easie to keep me in common bounds when I begin to write to you. I wrote some Notes, as I read Mr Broughton's Book, but I did <not>†b think it proper to trouble you with them, I may perhaps write to Mr Collins (God continuing
― 55 ―
health) and acquaint him with what thoughts came into my minde whilst I was reading that Book, and some which were the result of reflecting a little, after I had read It. I beg your pardon for my giving you so much trouble at a time. I repeat my heartiest thanks for all your kindnesses, and in all sincerity subscribe myself
Your most humble and obliged servant
SA: BOLDE.
Endorsed by Locke: S: Bold 23 Aug. 03 Answered Apr. 10
3327. Francis Limborch to Locke, 24 August 1703 (3316, 3331)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 13, ff. 217-18. The date is badly written; hence the error in Locke's endorsement.
London 24 Agust 1703
Honoured Sir
In conformity to your desire I shall send to morrow your flannen to Mr Pawlings and acquaint you the length thereoff per next;†a I am very sorry that my nott sending up to you sir has occasioned some disappointment to you and thereby deprived you off an opportunity off favouring me with some off your Commissions, which j shall alwayes esteem an exceeding honour iff j may be favoured with and iff you please Come a purpose next Saturday†1 downe for to receive, unlesse you think itt fitt to favour me with them by writeing; butt iff that should be inconvenient to you sir j desire to know itt by friday that so j may come downe next saturday being that shall be the onely opportunity off Comeing downe for me this good while because the weeks after I shall be taken up with our EJ sales,†2 and therefore I most sincerily begg off you to tell me freely whether my comeing downe will be off any service to you, I daring assicure you from my side that I shall reckon itt an exceeding greatt favour iff my mean service may prove off any Uze to you, and alwayes reckon itt my greatest happynesse to show by any reall service a small token off a due gratitude to your so very greatt kindnesses and friendship to me; that j may have your answhere by first j sent this by way of harlow to Mr Harrison; Cocao nutts good Caracao £12 the hundred; pray Sir Lett Sir Francis My Lady Mrs and
― 56 ―
Mr Masham finde my most sincere respects in these and believe me most sincerily
Honoured Sir
Your most oblidged humble servant
FRAN: LIMBORCH
Address: For John Locke Esquire att Oates jn Essex
Endorsed by Locke: F. Limborch 4 Aug. 03
3328. Locke to [Richard King], 25 August 1703 (3322, 3333)
The Remains of John Locke, pp. 9-12. For the identification of King as the person addressed see no. 2846, head-note. This letter was probably sent through Peter King: p. 60. Answered by no. 3333.
Oates, 25 Aug. 1703.
Sir,
Yours of the Fourth instant I receiv'd; and tho I am conscious I do not deserve those advantageous things which your Civility says of me in it, yet give me leave to assure you, that the Offers of my Service to you, which you are pleas'd to take notice of, is that part which I shall not fail to make good on all Occasions. You ask me, What is the shortest and surest way for a young Gentleman to attain a true Knowledg of the Christian Religion, in the full and just Extent of it? for so I understand your Question: if I have mistaken it, you must set me right. And to this I have a short and plain Answer: Let him study the Holy Scripture, especially the New Testament. Therein are contain'd the Words of Eternal Life. It has God for its Author; Salvation for its End; and Truth, without any mixture of Error, for its Matter. So that it is a wonder to me, how any one professing Christianity, that would seriously set himself to know his Religion, should be in doubt where to imploy his Search, and lay out his Pains for his Information; when he knows a Book, wherein it is all contain'd, pure and entire; and whither, at last, every one must have recourse, to verify that of it, which he finds any where else.
Your other Question, which I think I may call two or three, will require a larger Answer. As to Morality, which, I take it, is the first in those things you enquire after; that is best to be found in the Book that I have already commended to <you>.†a But because you
― 57 ―
may perhaps think, that the better to observe those Rules, a little warning may not be inconvenient, and some Method of ranging them be useful for the Memory; I recommend to you the Whole Duty of Man,†1 as a methodical System: and if you desire a larger View of the Parts of Morality, I know not where you will find them so well and distinctly explain'd, and so strongly inforc'd, as in the Practical Divines of the Church of England. The Sermons of Dr. Barrow, Archbishop Tillotson, and Dr. Whitchcot,†2 are Masterpieces in this kind; not to name abundance of others, who excel on that Subject. If you have a mind to see how far Human Reason advanc'd in the Discovery of Morality, you will have a good Specimen of it in Tully's Offices: unless you have a mind to look farther back into the Source, from whence He drew his Rules; and then you must consult Aristotle, and the other Greek Philosophers.
Tho Prudence be reckon'd among the Cardinal Virtues, yet I do not remember any profess'd Treatise of Morality; where it is treated in its full Extent, and with that Accuracy that it ought. For which possibly this may be a Reason, That every imprudent Action does not make a Man culpable in foro Conscientiæ. The Business of Morality I look upon to be the avoiding of Crimes; of Prudence, Inconveniences; the foundation whereof lies in knowing Men and Manners. History teaches this best, next to Experience; which is the only effectual way to get a Knowledg of the World. As to the Rules of Prudence, in the Conduct of Common Life, tho there be several that have imploy'd their Pens therein; yet those Writers have their Eyes so fix'd on Convenience, that they sometimes lose the Sight of Virtue, and do not take care to keep themselves always clear from the Borders of Dishonesty, while they are tracing out what they take to be, sometimes, the securest way to Success: most of those that I have seen on this Subject having, as it seem'd to me, something of this Defect. So that I know none that I can confidently recommend to your young Gentleman, but the Son of Syrac.†3
To compleat a Man in the Practice of Human Offices,†4 (for to that tend your Enquiries) there is one thing more requir'd; which tho it be ordinarily consider'd, as distinct from both Virtue and Prudence, yet I think it so nearly ally'd to them, that he will scarce keep
― 58 ―
himself from Slips in both, who is without it. That which I mean, is Good-breeding. The School for a young Gentleman to learn it in, is, the Conversation of those who are well-bred.
As to the last part of your Enquiry, which is after Books that will give an insight into the Constitution of the Government, and real Interest of his Country:†1 To proceed orderly in this, I think the Foundation should be laid, in inquiring into the Ground and Nature of Civil Society; and how it is form'd into different Models of Government; and what are the several Species of it. Aristotle is allow'd a Master in this Science, and few enter upon the Consideration of Government, without reading his Politicks. Hereunto should be added, true Notions of Laws in general, and Propriety,†2 the subject Matter about which Laws are made. He that would acquaint himself with the former of these, should thorowly study the judicious Hooker's first Book of Ecclesiastical Polity. And Propriety, I have no where found more clearly explain'd than in a Book intituled, Two Treatises of Government. But not to load your young Gentleman with too many Books on this Subject, which requires more Meditation than Reading; give me leave to recommend to him Puffendorf's little Treatise, De Officio Hominis et Civis.
To get an insight into the particular Constitution of the Government of his own Country, will require a little more Reading; unless he will content himself with such a superficial Knowledg of it, as is contain'd in Chamberlain's State of England, or Smith De Republicâ Anglicanâ.†3 Your Enquiry manifestly looks farther than that; and to attain such a Knowledg of it, as becomes a Gentleman of England to have, to the Purposes that you mention, I think he should read our antient Lawyers; such as Bracton, Fleta, the Mirror of Justice,†4 etc. which our Cousin King can better direct you to, than I; joining with them, the History of England under the Normans, and so continuing it down quite to our Times; reading it always in those Authors, who liv'd nearest those Times: their Names you will find,
― 59 ―
and Characters often, in Mr. Tyrrel's History of England. To which, if there be added, a serious Consideration of the Laws made in each Reign, and how far any of them influenc'd the Constitution; all these together, will give him a full Insight into what you desire.
As to the Interest of any Country, that, 'tis manifest, lies in its Prosperity and Security. Plenty of well-imploy'd People, and Riches within, and good Alliances abroad, make its Strength. But the ways of attaining these, comprehend all the Arts of Peace and War; the Management of Trade; the Imployment of the Poor; and all those other things that belong to the Administration of the Publick: which are so many, so various, and so changeable, according to the mutable State of Men, and Things in this World; that 'tis not strange, if a very small part of this consists in Book-Learning. He that would know it, must have his Eyes open upon the present State of Affairs; and from thence take his Measures, of what is Good, or Prejudicial, to the Interest of his Country.
You see how ready I am to obey your Commands, tho in Matters wherein I am sensible of my own Ignorance. I am so little acquainted with Books, especially on these Subjects relating to Politicks, that you must forgive me, if perhaps I have not nam'd to you the best in every kind. And you must take it as a Mark of my Readiness to serve you, that I have ventur'd so far out of what lay in my way of Reading, in the Days that I had leisure to converse with Books. The Knowledg of the Bible, and the Business of his Calling, is enough for an ordinary Man; a Gentleman ought to go farther.
Those of this Place return their Service and Thanks, for the Honour of your Remembrance.
I am, Sir,
Your most Humble and most Obedient Servant,
JOHN LOCKE
3329. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 27 August 1703 (3314, 3337)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 296-7.
Oates 27 Aug 03
Dear Cosin
I was glad to find by yours of the 10th that you were got to the end of your fatigue. Guesseing that you will be at Exeter by that time this gets thither I think I need not delay any longer to tell
― 60 ―
you that I hope to see you here speedily since you say the time hastens apace. you must contrive it so as to allow your self some time with me here, for I have a great deale to talk with you as you will see when you come. In the mean time I think fit to tell you that I have a mind you should sell the actions in the Bank whilst they are so high, for they are now £140 or very nigh and any cross accident tis like will make them tumble as I doubt not but you think as well as I.†1
You will see by my answer†2 what the Gentleman writ to me. His enquiry is for a yonge Gentleman. I take it he is the yonge Gentleman himself. You know him much better than I and can judg whether my answer will suit his principles temper and Genius. If you think it may be of use to him seal and deliver it to him with my service. If not return it with your opinion to me on the case. I hope your father is well recovered, my service to him. All this family are your servants. I am
Dear Cosin
Your most affectionate Cosin and humble servant
J LOCKE
Address: For Peter King Esqr member of Parliament at his fathers house in Exeter Frank.

Your most acceptable off the 26 by the post j received last saturday butt have perceived nothing off the other which you mention therein j received by Mr Daranda a letter from my Cousin Guenillon dated 27/16 Aug who with his humble service desires me to tell you that he hoped to receive the Medicines you sent him by me the next day and to acquaint you the receipt thereof with the next post, butt we want three; he tells me therein, my wife†1 is by the Grace off God from a desperate Condition so farre restored that she walkes aboutt againe, hath for a greatt while vomited blood and bloody fleames and been Continualy feaverish, butt is now restored againe to pretty good strength, however wants yett much, to which j hope these medicines which Mr Locke sent will contribute much; he sent me also a book for you written in Dutch by a minister off the Calvinist Church Called a description off heaven on Earth,†2

3332. Locke to Anthony Collins, 10 September 1703 (3318, 3342), with order, Locke to Robert Pawling
The Carl H. Pforzheimer Library, New York. The letter, the order, and the address are all on one sheet of paper. Transcribed from photostat. Part printed in A Collection of Several Pieces, pp. 262-3.
Oates 10 Sept 03
Sir
Yours of the 7th which I just now received, is the onely letter I have a long time wished for, and the welcomest that could come, for I longd to hear that you were well, that you were returned, and that I might have the oportunity to return you my thanks for the books you sent me which came safe, and to acknowledg my great obligation to you for one of the most vilanous books that I think was ever printed.†1 It is a present that I highly value. I had heard some thing of it when a yonge man in the university, but possibly should never have seen this quintessence of rayling but for your kindness. It ought to be kept as the pattern and standard of that sort of writeing as the man he spends it upon for that of good temper, and clear and strong argueing
You obleiged me with Mr Clarkes N Testament.†2 I have two of them now at Mr Pawlings who is Controuler of the Stamp Office, and has a house over against the plough Inn your neighbour in both places.†3 I desire you to take which of those copies (for they are both in quires) you please in exchange for yours unless you have some particular reason to desire your own again, which upon the first notice I shall return you. I writ some time since to Mr Pawling
― 63 ―
to send me word whether you were come to town for I intended to attaque you as soon as you were in distance. Mr Pawling writ me word he had some other books for me which were lately sent thither. Pray when you choose your French Testament look upon them too, that I may know what they are. Mr Pawling understands not French and so cannot tell me for I suppose they are French. All this family are the better for hearing that you are well and bid me tell you they Long to see you here. You know my wishes for I am Sir your most humble and most obedient servant
J LOCKE
[The order:]
Sir
Pray deliver to this bearer Mr Anthony Collins your neighbour in Searl's court one of the French New Testaments which you have received for me. The other pray keep till farther order. I desire you also to shew him the other books that you writ me were lately brought to you for me and to let him have them if he desires it. I am
Sir
Your very humble servant
J LOCKE
To Mr Robert Pawling
over against the plough in Little Lincolns Inne feilds
Address: For Anthony Collins Esq. at his chamber in Searl's court Numb. 6 in Lincolns Inne London
Postmark: SE 15
3333. Richard King to Locke, 13 September [1703] (3328, 3339)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 13, f. 3. Year from Locke's endorsement. Forwarded by Peter King: p. 71. Answers no. 3328; answered by no. 3339.
Exon Sept: 13
Sir
You have done me the honour to be very particular, to my great satisfaction, in your answer to every Question I have made you, except the first concerning the Study of Religion. which you are pleased to resolve into the Study of the Scriptures alone. and indeed I must acknowledge them to be the first and last in Divinity and I pay them the utmost Homage that the reason of man owes to the
― 64 ―
wisdom of God. But since there are Gain-sayers and Wresters of those Holy Writings, who are apt to deceive and delude the unskilful in Divine Truths, 'tis fitt to know how to make use of the succours that humane reason offers assisted by the knowledge of Theological Learning to attain the true sense and meaning of the Inspired Writers. 'tis upon this account I have applied my self to you, because I know those holy Books to be your daily Study, and that they draw the whole bent of your mind with an earnest desire to understand the infallible truths therein contained. I remember it was my Tutors advice though a Clergy-man Always to read what Laymen wrote concerning Divinity, as men more impartial in what they said.†1 I therefor in compliance with his good advice am desirous of learning from you as one most able to judge and most willing to communicate without partiality, what you know to be the best way for interpreting the Scriptures and to be the best method in the Study of Religion.
Sir
your most obliged and most humble servant
RIC: KING
I desire my thanks may be returned and my services made acceptable to the worthy Family where you are.
Endorsed by Locke: R: King 13 Sept 03 Answered 27

3336. Robert Pawling to Locke, 16 September 1703 (3247, 3588)

Lincolns Inn Sept 16 1703
Sir
Mr Bonville came with a message from you about my speaking to mr Collins about some pouder but he knew not whether it was white or black. I suspected you only desired to know whether Mr Collins was in Town or not, you having not then received my Letter, which I hope is come safe to your hands. And as for the pouder I thought you might want some and intended he should have desired me to buy some; of which I expect to hear farther.
Mr Collins was with me this day and shewed me what concernd me in your Letter, he went with me to your Chamber where I shewed him the Two parcells I received and wrot to you of. The one I brought from the French Booksellers tother came in my absence from I know not whom, but when we opened them it proved but 2 books that are alike; so that I conjecture the little french Bookseller gave me but one home with me; and afterwards sent the other.†1 Mr Collins took one with him and I presume will write to you of it.
The inclosed to Mr Coste is all you will have farther at present from
Sir
Your very humble Servant

Rotterdam 5 October 1703
Sir
I am favoured with your Most obliging lines at bottom of My Lady Masham's, as my father was with your former, in which you exprest so great a concerne for me, in my great Sicknesse, out of which I am now God be thanked by your and other my good Friends advice, next to the Grace of God, quite restored againe, for which I am most highly obliged to you, but am ashamed I have
― 68 ―
not ere now owned it, and do not know what apology to make for my self, but hope your good nature will pardon me, all our family I thank God is also pritty well; but my father has been indisposed for some days, or elce he would have given himself the honour of writing to you, tho is now a little better again, he returns you his Humble thanks that you take his offer of making a present of the Mum to yourself as kindly as if you consented to it, and intreats you kindly to do it for the present, yett so that you will not thereby be any ways hindred to take the liberty to honour us with your further Commands, assuring you nobody shall be more ready to comply therewith then we—

For news I refer you to my Lady's letter
My Lord Shaftesbury has been very much afflicted with the news of his Sisters death,†1 but is now pritty wel again—

Sir
Your Most Obliged humble Servant—

3339. Locke to [Richard King], 27 September 1703 (3333, 3346)
The Remains of John Locke, pp. 13-14. For the identification of King as the person addressed see no. 2846, head-note. The letter was sent through Peter King: p. 71. Answers no. 3333; answered by no. 3346.
Oates, 27 Sept. 1703.
Sir,
I am sorry to find, that the Question which was the most material, and my Mind was most upon, was answer'd so little to your Satisfaction, that you are fain to ask it again. Since therefore you ask me a second time, What is the best Method to study Religion? I must ask you, what Religion you mean? For if it be, as I understood you before, the Christian Religion in its full Extent and Purity, I can make you no other answer but what I did, viz. That the only way to attain a certain Knowledg of that, is, the Study of the S. Scripture. And my Reason is, because the Christian Religion is a Revelation from God Almighty, which is contain'd in the Bible; and so all the Knowledg we can have of it, must be deriv'd from thence. But if you ask, which is the best way to get the Knowledg of the Romish, Lutheran, or Reformed Religion, of this or that particular Church, etc. each whereof entitles it self to be the true Christian Religion, with some kind of Exclusion or Diminution to the rest; That will not be hard to tell you. But then it is plain, that the Books that best teach you any one of these, do most remove you from all the rest; and in this way of studying, you pitch upon one as the right, before you know it to be so: whereas that Choice should be the Result of your Study of the Christian Religion, in the Sacred Scriptures. And the Method I have propos'd, would, I presume, bring you the surest way to that Church, which, I imagine, you already think most conformable to the Word of God. I find, the Letter you last honour'd me with, contains a new Question, and that a very material one, viz. What is the best way of Interpreting the Sacred Scripture? Taking Interpreting to mean Understanding, I think the best way for understanding the Scripture, or the New Testament (for of that the Question will here be, in the first place) is to read it assiduously and diligently; and, if it can be, in the Original: I do not mean, to read every day some certain number of Chapters, as is usual; but to read it so, as to study and consider, and not leave till you are satisfy'd, that you have got the
― 70 ―
true meaning. To this purpose, it will be necessary to take the Assistance of Interpreters and Commentators; such as are those call'd the Criticks,†1 and Pool's Synopsis Criticorum;†2 Dr. Hammond on the New Testament,†3 and Dr. Whitby,†4 etc. I should not think it convenient to multiply Books of this kind, were there any one that I could direct you to, that was infallible. But you will not think it strange, if I tell you, that after all, you must make use of your own Judgment, when you consider, that it is, and always will be, impossible to find an Expositor, whom you can blind-fold rely upon, and cannot be mistaken in following. Such a Resignation as that, is due to the Holy Scriptures alone; which were dictated by the infallible Spirit of God. Such Writings also as Mr. Mede's†5 and Dr. Lightfoot's,†6 are very much conducing to lead us into a true Sense of the Sacred Scriptures. As to the Method of reading them, Order requires, that the Four Evangelists should, in the first place, be well study'd and thorowly understood. They all treating of the same Subject, do give great light to one another; and, I think, may, with greatest Advantage, be read in Harmony. To this purpose, Monsieur Le Clerc's,†7 or Mr. Whistons' Harmony of the four Evangelists†8 will be of Use, and save a great deal of Time and Trouble in turning the Bible. They are now both in English, and Mr. Le Clerc's has a Paraphrase. But if you would read the Evangelists in the Original, Mr. Le Clerc's Edition of his Harmony in Greek and Latin will be best.†9 If you find, that by this Method, you advance in the Knowledg of the Gospel; when you have laid a Foundation there to your Satisfaction, it will not be hard to add, what may help you forwards in the Study of other parts of the New Testament. But I have troubled you too much already, for which I beg your Pardon; and am,
Sir, Your most Humble and most Obedient Servant,
JOHN LOCKE.
― 71 ―
3340. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 27 September 1703 (3337, 3348)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 300-1.
Oates 27 Sept 03
Dear Cosin
On Saturday last†1 I writ to you and the same day that to which the inclosed to your Cosin†2 is an answer came to me under a cover of yours, but you put not in a word concerning him as I expected you haveing in your last to me promised me to discourse him on the subject.
By mine you will see how the matter stands, and I begin to be in some doubt thereupon whether any good can be done. You know him better than I and if you have don as you should have discoursed him, and are able to judg whether the inclosed will be of any use to him and fit to be deliverd, for I would not perplex him to no purpose, but if his temper etc: so require it would send him an answer that might turn it off an easier way, and so set him at rest in a lesse troublesome and thoughtfull course, which might be better for him, if he cannot with attention labour and study apply himself to the findeing out the truth. My humble service to him and your father. All this family salutes you. I am
Dear Cosin
Your most affectionate Cosin and servant
J LOCKE
Pray convey the other inclosed to Sir Walter,†3 I know not how to direct to him
You are asked whether you can hear of a good morgage in those parts.
Address: For Peter King Esqr member of Parliament at his fathers house in Exeter Frank.
Postmark: SE 29
Endorsed by King: Mr Locke September 27. 1703.

3341. Dr. Pieter Guenellon to Locke, 28 September/9 October 1703 (3325, 3371)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 11, ff. 114-15.
Monsieur,
Je repons un peu tard a votre derniere avec la quelle j'ay recu le pacquet de fleurs de Bellis Major. nous vous remercions bien humblement pour le present, et pour votre grande affection. m'a femme s'en sert; mais pas regulierement. elle a de la repugnance a prendre tant de liqueur, et son estomac est fort affoibli, et souvent douleureux par les frequentes c<o>ncussions†a que la tou lui cause. tant que ses poulmons produiront tant de phlegmes provenant apparemment d'un fond alteré, et qui sont tousjours fort recuits, et comme purulents, je ne vois pas de f<i>n†a a sa tou. sa santé ne paroit pourtant pas reculer, ses forces se soutiennent assez bien, sa couleur est plus vive, elle est la pluspart du tems sans fievre, sort en slee,†1 et ne manque pas de courage: cepandant il faut un peu d'Opium pour le repos de la nuict, car la situation dans le lict et la chaleur, la font tousser davantage. nos herboristes m'ont assuré qu'autour de la Haye le Bellis Major croit en nombre. J'auray soin l'année prochaine d'en faire ceuiller, si nous en avons besoin.
Monsieur Daranda nous est venu voir depuis peu. je l'ay chargé d'un petit traitté en flamend pour vous, il traitte de la solide et veritable joye pour la felicité de cette vie. l'auteur s'appelle freder. van Leenhof, ministre de l'eglise publique de Swol. il a fait autrefois, si vous vous en souvenez, quelques objections a l'extrait de vos essays sur l'entendement. c'est un grand Mathematicien, et je croi que vous trouverez dans ce livret plusieurs endroits qui vous agreeront, et qui sont un peu hardis pour un ministre.†2 j'en ay envoyé aussi un a Monsieur Limburgh, qui poura vous ayder a le faire entendre. toute la famille vous presente ses tres humbles respects, et je suis sans reserve,
Monsieur
Votre tres humble et tres obeissant serviteur
P. GUENELLON.
d'Amsterd. ce 9 Oct. 1703
― 73 ―
Address: For Mr John locke at Mr. Powlings over against the plough in Little Lincolns inne feilds. London
Postmark: OC 4
Endorsed by Locke: Dr Guenellon 9 Oct 03 Answered 10
October
3342. Locke to Anthony Collins, 1 October 1703 (3332, 3351)
The Carl H. Pforzheimer Library, New York. Transcribed from photostat. Printed in A Collection of Several Pieces, pp. 263-5.
Oates 1° Octob. 03
Sir
You are a good man and one may depend upon your promise. This makes me pass my days in comfortable hopes, when I remember you are not far off. I have your word for it, and thats better than citty security. But for fear vilanous business, should impertinently step in again between you and your kind purposes to us here, give me leave to beg the favour of you that if you write again before I have the happyness to see you you will doe me the favour to send me a note of what you have laid out for me, that I may pay you that part that I am able of what I owe you, and may not have soe much to interrupt the advantages I am to reape from your conversation, when you honour me with your company, as an apologie to be made, if I am not out of your debt before we meet.
Does Monsieur le Clercs N: Testament make any noise amongst the men of letters or Divinity in your town? The Divines of Brandenburg or Cleve have got the King of Prussia to prohibit it in his dominions.†1 And the Wallon Divines in Holland are Soliciting the same at the Hague, but 'tis thought will not prevaile.†2 I have not yet heard what are the exceptions made in particular either by the one or the other. If there be need of Authentick Interpreters of the word of god. what is the way to find them out? That is worth your thinking of. unless you would have every one interpret for himself: And what worke would that make? Betwixt these two
― 74 ―
find something if you can: For the world is in want of peace, which is much better than everlasting Billingsgate. I thought not to have troubled you with hard questions, or any thing that should have required a serious thought any farther than what day you should pitch on to come hither. But everlasting wrangleing and calling of names is so odious a thing that you will pardon me if it puts me out of temper a little. But I think of you and some few such as you in the world and that reconciles me to it, or else it would not be worth the staying in an hour. I am
Sir
Your most humble and most obedient servant
J LOCKE
All here remember you with great respect and impatiently look for you
3343. John Bonville to Locke, 2 October 1703 (3334, 3386)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 4, f. 133.
London Oc: 2d: 1703
Honoured Cousin
Sir yours the 24 of September I Received, I have sent this day by Lenham, one bundle which I packt up as cearfull, As I Could, In this bundle There Is A bundle of books, sum Tea In A box, A 1/2 pint Teapott, 6 pound of pouder, Two pair of stockeings, my wife bought the pouder of a friend of hers one That make It I hope It will pleas you, It Cost 7s shillings, what price you use to pay I cannot tell, but If you Think it to deare That I shall be glad to know, the stockeings: 3s:6d, there was great ceare In buying of Them, According to your desire, I wish It May pleas you, Then we shall know, how to doe, the Like Occation, Deare Cousin I doe heartely wish that I had Injoyd your Good Company Two dayes Longer, for when your servant with me Came to Mr Cleeves,†1 Ongar Coach was at Mr Cleeves dore, but he had prevented my Journey And
― 75 ―
Tould The Coachman that I should Not goe before Munday, for said he, I brought you from London, A I will see you safe home Againe, and he was as good As his word, Sir I shall be glad to heare That these things Com to you safe, My wife Gives you Her Humble Servis with My Self Heartely, The Lord be with you.
I Am Deare Cousin,
Your Faithfull Kinsman An Humble Servant
JNO: BONVILLE
I did seale this bundle where I fastened the Knott.
Address: For Mr Locke [at Oates, by Joslyn, Bishops Stortford]
Postmark: OC 2
Endorsed by Locke: J: Bonville 2 Oct. 03

3345. John Churchill to Locke, 9 October 1703 (3335, 3366)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 6, ff. 12-13.
London Oct 9 1703
Sir
I have yours of the 30th past and 5th present and herewith send the two books you ordered and in one of them twenty five pounds in Gold. viz. 23 Guineas and 5/6d silver, was loath to tarry till my Brother could have an opertunity of wayting on you he haveing bin 7 or 8 dayes in the West Country and will be the end of the month er'e he return. I alsoe send a parcell which received from Mr Pawlin.
There is none of the Oxon Bibles printed on a finer paper than that sent you.†1 —Shall take your Gregorys Astronomie if you please to return it—As to the other parts of your Letter must referr you to my Brothers return. Dr Molyneaux in a letter I had from him gives you his humb service—Mr Fletcher caled to inquire of your Welfare and sends his service alsoe.
I am Sir your most humb servant
JOHN CHURCHILL
Address: For John Locke Esqr
Endorsed by Locke: J: Churchil 9 Oct 03 Answered 22
3346. Richard King to Locke, 9 October [1703] (3339)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 13, ff. 4-5. Year from Locke's endorsement. Answers no. 3339.
Exon Oct: 9.
Sir
The method you propose for the right understanding of the S Scriptures I am very well satisfyed is the best. To read Them daily with close application of mind and with sincere intention of heart to make Them the Rule of Life, I am sure is that simple and honest way to attain the true sense of them, which their great Author will never fail to bless. To turn over many Commentators will rather distract than enlighten; but to use none is too much to rely upon our own understanding, and will make us guilty of willful ignorance whilst we depend alone upon our own knowlege. since therefor
― 77 ―
it is necessary to take the assistance of some Interpreters; such as are least bigotted to a Party will be most likely to give the fairest Interpretation. and such as are generally esteemed the best of every party will sett before our eyes the sense of all Parties, whereby we may have a view of their several opinions and be the better able to exercise our judgements concerning what is true; which, in the scripture Phrase, is to try all things, and to hold fast what is good.†1 Amongst the Commentators which you have not named I esteem no one more for Integrity and candour, great Learning, witt and judgement than Erasmus, whom our first Reformers highly respected. The comparing of Editions is another way to come to the true meaning of the Text; but the best of all (as you advise) is to read the several Gospels in Harmony; which indeed is to make the S Scripture a comment upon it self.
Now Sir I must heartily thank you for the extraordinary favour you have done me by answering all the Questions my importunate desire to be informed by you did move me to ask you, and your peculiar readiness to oblige did comply with. you have dispensed with your own satisfaction to accomplish mine, whilst you have interrupted those Studies your wisdom has made choice of, to compleat the scene of a Life acted by so much reason purely to gratify me in my enquiries after knowledge. which I the rather learn from you because the experience of many years and the knowledge of men and things have made you the perfect Socrates of the Age. and to you I come with the same intention of mind as the young Gentlemen of Greece went to that great man, or the young Romans to Cato Major, to be instructed in those things that would make them wise and happy. and what they sought after from them I know is to be obtained from you, Cui sunt
Compositum jus, fasque animi, sanctique recessus
Mentis, et incoctum generoso pectus honesto.†2
Sir
your most obliged et most humble servant
RIC: KING.
Address: For the much Esteemed John Locke Esquire
Endorsed by Locke: Ric: King 9 Oct: 03

3347. Elizabeth Clarke to Locke, 13 October 1703 (3317, 3400)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 6, ff. 165-6. Printed in Rand, p. 590.
Chipley October the 13th. 1703.
Sir
No distance Shall ever hinder My inquireing after your health the perfect enjoyment of Which I Sincerely wish You. This place affords nothing worth your hearing, for which reason I'll trouble you no Longer than whilst I present you my Fathers and Mothers Most humble Service and assure you that with the Same respect and affection which I ever profess'd I am
Dear Sir
Your most Obedient humble Servant
ELIZ CLARKE
All this family Joyne in the presentment of their Service to You and all the good Company at Oates.
My Father I thanke God is mighty well, and My Mother pursues Dr Colebatch's directions,†1 which I hope will be to her advantage. be pleased to deliver mine to Mrs Masham.
Address: To John Locke Esqr at Oates

3348. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 21 October 1703 (3340, 3349)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 302-3. Answered by no. 3353.
Oates 21 Oct 03
Dear Cosin
I hope you got safe and well to town, what resolutions you took concerning the malt tickets at the place where you called by the way†2 I doe not know But upon serious and sedate consideration of the whole matter since the departure of you and the rest of the company here I judg it best they should be sold, But still I must as I doe leave it to you who are upon the place, and have sight of things which I have not here to determin as you would for your self in the case
― 79 ―
I have also here inclosed sent you a receit for 6 months interest due from Mr Conyers 3d May last which I must desire you to speak to Mr Marton for and which I presume he has had long since ready by him. There will also be 6 months more due within this fortnight, which I desire you soe to order the matter with him that I may not hereafter stay for one half years interest till an other is due which I was not wont to doe when I received it immediately from Mr Conyers himself. I should give Mr Marton something but I doe not think it reasonable whilst he uses me so ill, yet I leave it to you to manage the matter as you think fit. This I am confident that the Interest is duely paid in time by the Morgagor. I am
Dear Cosin
Your most affectionate Cosin and humble servant
J LOCKE
Address: For Peter King Esqr member of Parliament. To be left at Mr Potmans in the Middle Temple London Frank.

Sir
Since I came to Town I have a security offered me for Two Thousand and Odd pounds, which hath the appearance of a good one, The Odd Hundred pounds I shall be able to furnish, and The 2000 l. I design for you, You know by our account what mony I have of yours, and how much more you must add to make it up 2000 l. If We go on—The Security is from Mr Hopwood of or by Colchester, who married my Lady Dier, I Suppose Sir Francis knows him well—The Land proposed to be morgaged by him is the Mannour of Stanaway about three miles from Colchester, worth 315 l. per annum, about 6000 l. to be sold, The said Mannour Or The Greatest part thereof is of his own purchasing, and I believe, he borrowed part of the mony to make the purchase, for which he Morgaged the premises, and now takes up Mony to satisfy that

Sir

3350. Dr. Robert Pitt to Locke, 21 October 1703 (3077, 3359)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 17, ff. 177-8.
Sir
It appears by the Account you give me of the Gentlemans Illness,†2 that it is, as you judge it, Rheumatick: and of the Kind of the vagi scorbutici Rheumatismi. There can be nothing added to
― 81 ―
the exectness†1 of this Diet. If he drinkes sometimes the Mault Drinkes, it may be reasonable to omit them, and use water and wine in stead of them. I direct every week at the Hospital†2 to a great number of the in and out Patients with this Disease. There happen several together of the many Classes of them. I have observ'd nothing effect so much in a little time and more safely, then Calomel, with purging it of. I advice your Patient to take 15 or 20 gr. of it with Diascordium, in a Bole over night, and to take a Bole the next morning of El Lenit ʒii res. Jalap. gr. viii vel x cum Crem. Tartar. ʒi Syr Rosacei Solut q s. This may be repeted thrice in a fortnight or twice a week. The other days I recommend a Tea of Trifolium Paludosum dry'd to be dranke pretty freely. and besides a solemn Draught morning and Evening of Decoctum Guiac. and Sassaphras in Aqua Calcis. I have mett with several who have us'd the cold Bath without the expected Advantage. I present my service to my Lady Massam and the young Gentleman and am
Your most humble servant
R PITT
Oct 21. 1703.
Address: For Mr John Locke to be left at Mr Joslyn's in Bishop Stratford Essex
Postmark: OC 21
Endorsed by Locke: Dr Pitt 21 Oct 03 Answered 26
3351. Anthony Collins to Locke, 21 October 1703 (3342, 3361)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 7, ff. 3-4. Allusions in this and other letters show that Collins visited Oates between 1 and 20 October. Answered by no. 3361.
Lincolns Inne. Octob. 21th/03
Sir
I have too pleasant a relish of the Conversation at Oats to resist my inclination to continue it. Besides I owe you my thanks for the greatest favour I can receive, the Friendship you have shewn towards me in so freely communicating your thoughts to me concerning severall particulars of great moment wherein I wanted the satisfaction which I acknowledg in great measure to have receiv'd: so that I hope with this additionall light that you have given me
― 82 ―
to go on in the search after truth, and dispell those mists that my own Inability and the Authority of others have cast in my way. Truth in the love of it is what I aim at, and it is to your discovery of my regard to this only that I can attribute your willingness to inform me, as well as your extream civility to me; obligations not to be repay'd by all the services I can do for you. I am too sensible of the disadvantages I am under from my way of education, want of conversation in the world, and a naturall Incapacity, to think I can afford you any entertainment by my company or letters, and can only wish it was in my power to be as agreable to you, as you are to me.
I deliver'd your letter to Dr Pitt the night I came to Town and he promisd to write to you by the next post. Yesterday I gave my Lady Mashams Letter and your Gregorys Astronomy to Mr Churchill.
I have already met with Kirchers concordance of the Septuagint†1 and will send it with your other Books unless you desire it sooner.
Since I came to Town I have heard of the Exception upon which the Divines of Brandenburg sollicited the King of Prussia to forbid the Sale of Mr Le Clerc's Testament in his Dominions. Tis for translating the word Proskunein Prosterner in the 2d Chapter of St Matthew.†2 Notwithstanding He hath the Authority of Calvin†3 (with whom I did not think any one could err so as to incur the censure of any of the Reformd Establishd Churches abroad) This renders his performance suspected of Heterodoxy while those they are otherwise secure of may be indulgd in the same Liberty.
I am Your most Obedient and most Humble Servant
A. COLLINS.
I beg my most humble service to Sir F. my Lady and the rest of the Family with my thanks to them for all their favours.
Address: To John Locke Esq [at Oates, by Joslyn, Bishops Stortford]
Postmarks: OC 21; GC
Endorsed by Locke: A. Collins 21 Oct 03 Answered 29

3352. Philippus Van Limborch to Locke, 22 October/2 November 1703 (3200, 3557)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 14, ff. 155-6. Copy by van Limborch in Amsterdam University Library, MS. R.K., III D 16, ff. 223-4. Forwarded by Francis Limborch on 30 October: p. 99.
(English)Vir Amplissime
Nunquam evidentius expertus sum, quam verè Magnus ille Græcus, rogatus quâ ratione tanta ac tam magnifica confecisset, responderit, μηδὲν ἀναβαλλόμενος, quam hoc anno.†1 Ego totâ hac æstate ad te scripturii: Verum impedimentum aliquod impetum meum aliquamdiu retardavit, scriptionemque differre suasit: exin, uti plerumque fieri solet, de dië in diëm procrastinando in scribendi officio cessator fui. Culpam agnosco ac deprecor: illius à solitâ tuâ bonitate veniam peto. Nunc à filio de valetudine tuâ præ senio admodum debilitatâ monitus, calamum μηδὲν ἀναβαλλόμενος arripio. Quando considero ætatem tuam, corpus macilentum, et astmate jam ab aliquot annis fractum, frigus hyemis instantis valetudini tuæ inimicum, valde de te sollicitus sum; non tam tui causâ, quem in voluntate divinâ acquiescere, et contemtâ sæculi hujus vanitate jam dudum æternitatem anhelare novi, quam mei,
― 84 ―
imò totius orbis Christiani, quem doctissimis ac utilissimis lucubrationibus egregiâ cum παῤῥησίᾳ erudire, ac saluberrimis instruere monitis non cessas; piâque hac sedulitate ac labore indefesso te fidelem illum demonstras servum, quem Dominus, cum veniet, reperturus est vigilantem. Talis non horret mortem, sed quietem ab operibus, statumque illum beatum, in quo omnibus hujus sæculi molestiis defunctus operum suorum fructum reportaturus est, desiderat, et bonæ conscientiæ testimonio securus in voluntate divinâ acquiescit. Nec ego mihi, utpote jam septuagenario,†1 ætatem meam considerans, multos annos polliceri possum: licet enim valetudo pro ætatis ratione vegeta admodum sit, anni tamen diem obitus non procul abesse docent: imo nimius ille corporis mei vigor subitam mihi minari videtur mortem. Jam denuo hebdomade proximè elapsâ gravem ac valde periculosum inveterati mei mali paroxysmum sensi. Nondum duo à postremâ venæ sectione menses elapsi erant, et ecce gravissimas cordis palpitationes, pulsusque arteriæ alternis vicibus nunc intermissionem, nunc vehementissimam agitationem sensi, quæ adhibitâ venæ sectione ad aliquot quidem horas cessavit; at mox recruduit,†a adeo ut nondum elapso biduo iteratâ venæ sectione opus fuerit. Sanguis crassior erat, eaque fuisse videtur causa quod per venas
― 85 ―
angustiores difficilior fuerit illius circulatio; alias nihil in illo desiderari potuit nisi nimia copia: colore erat rubro, non tantum in superficie, sed et dissectus, adeo ut Medicus affirmaverit, se nunquam vidisse similem. Tales paroxysmi me fragilitatis meæ admonent, et quam lubrico in statu sit mea vita docent. Quoniam itaque ætatis, et corpusculi utriusque nostrum constitutio dissolutionem terrestris hujus tabernaculi non procul abesse monent, non possum quin sinceræ nostræ amicitiæ memor grates quas possum maximas tibi agam pro benevolo erga me meosque affectu, proque omnibus è tam puro fonte derivatis beneficiis, quorum memoriam, si me tibi superstitem velit Deus, nulla ex animo meo delebit ætas. Si utrique vitam continuet benignum numen, occasionem etiam mutuum porro testandi amorem dabit: Sin aliud de nobis statuerit, in beatâ resurrectione denuò nos conjunget, non ultra per mortem separandos, ut in gloriosâ filiorum Dei universitate perfectè unum simus cum capite nostro Christo, omnibusque sanctis, corporis ipsius mystici membris, Deusque omnia sit in omnibus: Quæ votorum nostrorum summa est.†1
(English)Equidem quando considero labores tuos eximios, quos jam ab aliquot annis paci ecclesiasticæ affectu sincero et nunquam satis
― 86 ―
laudando destinasti, cœlestis illius charitatis ac unionis ideam in Ecclesiâ verè catholicâ nobis exhibere voluisse videris. Legi maximâ cum voluptate, nec minore cum fructu, eximium illum, Quod religio Christiana sit summè rationalis, tractatum à D. de Coste ex tractatibus Anglicis, te autore (uti communis fert opinio) conscriptis, linguâ Gallicâ elegantissimè concinnatum; cujus mihi exemplar sive tuâ sive D. de Coste liberalitate quam primum prodiit donatum fuit; pro quo liberali datori, serò licet, gratias quas possum maximas ago.†1 Tarditatis meæ nullam adfero excusationem, tu eam pro solitâ tuâ humanitate condonabis. Unum dico: me cum autore tam eximii tractatus plane idem sentire: nihil enim mihi, Scripturam seposito omni, quantum fieri potest, affectu ac præjudicio legenti, evidentius est, quam hanc unam, quam ille perpetuò urget, imò unicam à quâ Christiani denominemur fidem exigi, quod Jesus sit Christus, seu per Prophetas promissus Judæis Messias. Huic salutari veritati, ab omnibus qui Christiani vocantur unanimiter agnitæ ac constanter professæ, si alii nulli adderentur articuli, non perinde in Scripturâ expressi, nec eorum confitendorum æqualis exigeretur necessitas, haberemus Ecclesiam verè universalem atque Apostolicam, in quâ omnium fidelium esset
― 87 ―
cor unum atque anima una: unusquisque sibi fidei ac vitæ regulam in Scripturâ quæreret: nemo alteri credendi legem esset præscripturus: nemo ob dissensum, salvâ hac veritate universali, fratri anathema dicturus, aut Ecclesiæ separationem moliturus. Sic fidei nostræ norma erunt non placita humana, non voces aut phrases ab hominibus, et quidem plerumque in æstu contentionis, fabricatæ, sed sola Scriptura, phrasesque ac verba quibus Spiritus Sanctus Apostolos eloqui docuit. Sanctissimis illis tuis laboribus Deus benedicat, ut alma illa pax postliminio in Ecclesiâ vigeat ac floreat, intimaque illa ac perfecta animorum unio instar ac idea sit universitatis filiorum Dei, quæ in futuro sæculo erit consummata. Quod ardentissimis, à Deo pacis, Filioque ejus Jesu Christo rege pacis, suspiriis ac votis petere nunquam desinam.
(English)Honoratissimum Comitem Shafburiensem, virum omni honore dignum, ante duas hebdomadas in ædibus meis vidi. Narravit ille, nuper aliquos in Parlamento decretum quoddam contra dissentientes, libertati conscientiarum valdè inimicum, esse molitos: sed illius rogationi obstitisse Episcopos ad unum fere omnes: imprimis Salisburiensis laudabat constantiam, qui egregiâ oratione cordate ac fortiter illi obstitit.†1 Moderatiora consilia Ordini
― 88 ―
Episcoporum placuisse gaudeo. Utinam eadem semper ipsis placeant! Vix enim credo ipsis invitis ullum atrocius contra dissentientes edictum unquam esse conditum, aut in posterum condendum. Concordantiam Græcam Vet. Test. Kirche<ri>†a ut et Biblia, à D. Clerico mihi tradita, primâ quæ nunc se offert occasione ad filium mittam, porro ad te quamprimum transmittenda. Vale, Vir Amplissime. Deus, cujus te Tutelæ commendo, omnia tibi animæque tuæ salutaria plenâ manu largiatur. Salutant te uxor ac filia. Salutem officiosissimam dices honoratissimo Domino ac Dominæ Masham, nec non dignissimæ filiæ, ac filio, ut et D. Coste, ab uxore, filiâ, ac me
Tui Amantissimo
PHILIPPO À LIMBORCH
Amstelodami 2 Novembris
inline image
(Latin)Excellent Sir,
I have never experienced more clearly than in the present year how truly that great Greek, on being asked how he had accomplished so many and such honourable things, answered 'by not putting anything off'.†1 All this summer I have wanted to write to you, but some obstacle checked my impulse for a while and persuaded me to defer writing; then, as is wont to happen, by procrastinating from day to day, I was a sluggard in the duty of writing. I acknowledge and deprecate my fault, and beg pardon from your wonted goodness. Now, informed by my son about the great weakness of your health owing to old age, 'not putting anything off' I seize my pen. When I consider your age, your body, thin and now for some years broken by asthma, [and] the cold of the winter nigh at hand, so adverse to your health, I am exceedingly anxious about you: not so much for your sake, for I know that you acquiesce in the will of God and that, despising the vanity of this world, you have long been panting for eternity, as for my own, indeed for that of the whole Christian world, which you do not cease to teach, [p. 84] with excellent freedom of speech, by very learned and useful lucubrations, and to instruct by most salutary admonitions; and by this pious diligence and unwearied toil you show yourself to be that faithful servant whom the Lord, when he comes, will find watching. Such a one does not dread death, but desires rest from labours and that blessed state in which, delivered from all the troubles of this world, he will obtain the fruit of his labours; and, secure in the testimony of a good conscience, acquiesces in the will of God. Nor can I, as being now a septuagenarian,†1 when I consider my age promise myself many years; for although my health is very sound for my age, yet my years tell me that the day of death is not far off: indeed that excessive vigour of my body seems to threaten me with sudden death. Again last week I felt an oppressive and exceedingly dangerous paroxysm of my inveterate trouble. Two months had not elapsed since the last bleeding and lo! I felt very oppressive palpitations of the heart and, by turns, now an intermission, now a very violent motion, of the pulse of the artery; after I had been bled this ceased indeed for some hours, but it soon broke out again, so that when two days had not yet elapsed another bleeding was necessary. The blood was thicker, and that seems to have been the reason why its circulation through the narrower veins was more difficult; in other [p. 85] respects nothing could be found wanting in it, apart from its excessive quantity; it was red in colour, not only on the surface but also when dissected, so that the physician asserted that he had never seen the like. Such paroxysms put me in mind of my fragility and teach me in how uncertain a state is my life. Since then our ages and the state of our frail bodies warn us that the dissolution of this earthly tabernacle is not far off, I, mindful of our sincere friendship, cannot but thank you most heartily for your benevolent affection towards me and mine, and for all the kindnesses drawn from so pure a source: if God wills that I outlive you time will not blot them from my mind. If God in His goodness should continue both our lives He will also give us opportunities of further testifying to our mutual love; but if He has decided otherwise for us He will join us again in the blessed resurrection, no more to be separated by death, so that in the glorious company of the sons of God we may be perfectly one with Christ our head, and with all the saints, the members of his mystical body, and so that God may be all in all: this is the sum of our prayers.†1
(Latin)Indeed, when I consider your notable labours which, with a sincere passion that is beyond all praise, you have now for some years devoted to ecclesiastical peace, you appear to have wished to show us a pattern [idea] [p. 86] of that heavenly love and concord in a truly catholic Church. I have read with very great pleasure, and not with less profit, Que la Religion chrétienne est très raisonnable, that notable treatise put together very elegantly in French by Mr. de Coste from English treatises that, according to common opinion, were written by you; when it first appeared a copy was presented to me, whether by your generosity or Mr. de Coste's; although late in the day I thank the generous donor most heartily.†1 I plead no excuse for my slowness; you of your wonted good nature will pardon it. I say only one thing: I am altogether of the same opinion as the author of the so notable treatise; for when, having set aside, as much as possible, all passion and prejudice, I read the Scripture, nothing is clearer to me than that this one belief which he perpetually urges, indeed the only belief in virtue of which we are named Christians, is required: that Jesus is the Christ, or rather the Messiah promised by the Prophets to the Jews. If to this saving truth, unanimously acknowledged and constantly professed by all who are called Christians, there were added no other articles that are not expressed in Scripture in like manner, and if an equal necessity of confessing them were not required, we should have a truly universal and Apostolic Church, in which there would be one heart and one soul in all the faithful: each one would seek in Scripture a rule [regula] of faith and life for himself; no one [p. 87] would prescribe a law of belief to another; no one would call his brother accursed on account of disagreement, save in [the matter of] this universal truth, or would endeavour a sundering of the Church. So human ordinances will not be the standard [norma] of our faith, not words or phrases framed by men, and indeed for the most part in the heat of strife, but Scripture alone, and the phrases and expressions which the Holy Spirit taught the Apostles to speak with. May God bless your most sacred labours, so that that bounteous peace may subsequently thrive and flourish in the Church and so that that intimate and perfect concord of spirits may be the likeness and pattern [idea] of the company of the sons of God, which will be perfected in the world to come. And this I shall never cease to seek with most ardent sighs and prayers from the God of peace and from His son Jesus Christ, the prince of peace.
(Latin)Two weeks ago I saw in my house the most honourable earl of Shaftesbury, a man worthy of all honour. He told me that some men had recently endeavoured in parliament to enact a decree against dissentients, a decree exceedingly adverse to freedom of conscience, but that the bishops almost to a man had opposed the bill; he praised especially the steadfastness of the bishop of Salisbury, who opposed it wisely and stoutly in an excellent speech.†1 I am glad that more moderate counsels have seemed good to the [p. 88] bishops' bench; would that they may always do so! For I scarcely believe that any more cruel edict against dissentients has ever been established against their will, or ever will be in time to come. With the first opportunity that now offers I shall send to my son, to be sent on to you as soon as possible, Kircher's Greek concordance of the Old Testament, and also the Bible, that were delivered to me by Mr. Le Clerc. Good-bye, excellent Sir. May God, to Whose keeping I commend you, grant you in abundance all things healthful to you and to your soul. My wife and daughter send you their greetings. Please give their best respects, and mine, to the most honoured Sir Francis and Lady Masham, and to their most worthy daughter and son, as well as to Mr. Coste.
Your most affectionate
PHILIPPUS VAN LIMBORCH
Amsterdam 2 November
1703
Address: Amplissimo ac summe Honorando Viro D. Joanni Locke.
Endorsed by Locke: P: Limborch 2° Nov 03

3353. Peter King, later first Baron King, to Locke, 23 October 1703 (3349, 3355)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 12, ff. 202-3. Answers no. 3348; answered by no. 3360.
London. Octob. 23. 1703.
Sir
As for the Malt Tickets I was Inclined before the receit of your letter to sell them, and intimated so much to my Cosin,†1 but yet still desired him to do therein as he Would do with his own, which he hath promised me to do, and I doubt not will perform—I have not since my Last heard of Mr Hopwood, I suppose he is trying to get it at 4l. 10s. per Cent.—Mr Conyers man will pay me your bill Munday morning next, When I have received this bill I will then talk with him about what is behind—Here is no news— Last Sunday The Doctor who preach'd before the Queen preach'd against the Scots, and for the Occasional bill, which, they say, did not please†2 —It is probable that there will be a Recommendation to the next Session to Regulate the Liberty of the Press.†3
I am Sir
Your Most affectionate Cosin and Humble Servant.
P. KING
Endorsed by Locke: P. King 23 Oct 03 Answered 29
3354. James Seamer to Locke, 23 October 1703 (3344, 3368)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 18, f. 66.
London 23th. Oct: 1703.
Sir
The Tumbler of halfe a pint will weigh about 9 oz. at 4l. 6s. per ounce with the fashion (for the gold is better than 4l. 1s. per ounce) which does come to 38l. 14s. but if you please it may be made for less about one ounce in gold, and be pretty serviceable notwithstanding. The plainest fashion gold plate that is made is no less than 4l:6s: per ounce, for melting gold will wast it unless
― 90 ―
it be full fine and fileing and fashioning of it will occation wast in the melting down the pieces againe and limill.†1
for the fashon of a silver thimble I give but 4. pence and in gold 4s. 6d. the fashion and soe for a paire of shoe buckles 12d. or 18d. but in gold 12. shillings the making; which sumtimes weighes not above 3l. in gold.
In anything in my way [I doe respect your Worth soe much]†a that you may depend upon kind useage from
Sir
Your most humble servant
JAM: SEAMER.
Address: To John Lock Esqr to be left at Mr Jocelins a shopkeeper in Bishop-Startford
Postmarks: OC 23; GC
Endorsed by Locke: Jam: Seamer 23 Oct 03 Answered 26
3355. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 26 October 1703 (3353, 3357)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 304-5. Answers no. 3349.
Oates 26 Oct 03
Dear Cosin
I was glad to find by yours of 19th that you got safe to town. You will see by mine of the 21 that I thought it convenient to sell the malt-tickets before I received yours of the same date, soe that if upon farther lookeing into it you approve well of the Colchester security which you mention in yours of 21 I shall be able to make up my quota, but pray tell me in your next what is the whole summ must be lent upon it. I have enquired of Sir Francis concerning the man, who says he knows him, and that he never heard any thing to the contrary but that he is an honest man. The copy of the survey you sent me I suppose you are satisfied with since you doe not put me upon enquiring about it. However if any body chance to come here that can give me any light I shall not fail to aske questions, but otherwise to send thither I am as much farther off from it than you as it is from hence to London.
― 91 ―
And Benjamin Furly,†1 a quaker in Colchester is the onely man there that I know or can write to about it. But if there be any thing concerning the man or his manner that you desire my help or any I can get here to satisfie you in beyond what information you can get at London upon any direction from you I shall doe my best in it. When you write any thing that requires hast you know how to direct your letters to Mr Harrisons at the Crown in Harlow that they may come to me the next day. In your next pray tell me when will the money be expected if this business goes on. I am
Dear Cosin
Your most affectionate Cosin and humble servant
J LOCKE
My Lady and all here give you their service
Pray send the inclosed by the next post
I just now received a note from Mr Spranger that he and his folke will be here with me the later end of this week about the £140 I promised to lend his sons in law.†2 I shall draw a bill upon you for it and therefore write to you of it, that you may have the money ready in town against my bill comes to you, which I believe will be monday or Tuesday next
Address: For Peter King Esqr member of Parliament. To be left at Mr Potmans in the Middle Temple London Frank.
Postmark: OC 27
Endorsed by King: Mr Locke. Oct. 26. 1703.
3356. Edward Spranger to Locke, 26 October 1703 (3251, 3417)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 18, f. 183.
Sir
Mr Aylett gave mee your letter last Friday att Epping and I retorne you thanks for sending of it to mee, I told you the summe desired and the persons that were to give the security for it, the younger Brother is att his habitation beyond Chelmsford and I have wrote to him to come to mee next Friday or Saterday, if hee comes on Friday wee purpose to bee with you on Saterday, if hee comes not till Saterday then it will bee monday before wee shall
― 92 ―
bee with you, I suppose your money is at London, if you could gett your notes against wee come it may save A Journey to London because I can gett a Retorne on teusday att Epping faire or on Wensday if not the Journey is not much for A young man, I thought it butt civill to give you notice of the receipt of your letter And to conclude I am very well assured and knowe that your money will bee honestly paid againe or els I would not have Recomended the security to you, I would not willingly deceive any man in such case as this is Butt to you I thinke my selfe obliged to provide that security that cannott reasonably bee questioned and shall att all opportunities endeavour to approve myselfe
Sir
your very humble servant
EDW: SPRANGER
26: oct: 1703.
Bee pleased to spare mee the present State of England†1 till I see you againe
Address: These To John Locke Esq present att Otes:
