3358. James Tyrrell to Locke, [25?] October 1703 (3324, 3392)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 22, ff. 161-2. Written by an amanuensis, apart from the signature and address. In view of the postmark, 25 October is probably the correct date of the letter.
Shot: October 26th:†a 1703
Dear Sir
I received Yours at a time, when being newly return'd from a Journey out of Cheshire and Staffordshire, I began to reflect upon the Letter I had wrote you, and fear'd that your want of Health or some worse accident had hinder'd you from returning an Answer,†1 and I am sorry to find by your last that your Lungs did not continue in that good state I thought I left them in the Spring, but I hope your Cough is rather better than worse, the weather having bin hitherto so mild, and I heartily wish you may pass the Winter with less trouble from your old Enemie then you did the last.
As for the small Affair you have trusted to my managment, I desire you would understand the meaning in my last to be, that I saw your name enter'd before the Books you gave, but not in the Book of Benefacters, for it is particularly provided by Sir Thomas Bodely in his Statutes, that none shall be capable of that honour, unless such as give Books or money to buy them to above the value of 40s. so that if you desire to be enter'd as a Benefactor in the Library Book, you must encrease your Benefaction somewhat more, tho' to what Degree is to be left to your
― 94 ―
discretion: but I saw Dr: Hudson yesterday, who desir'd me to let you know, that if you have any thoughts of giving some more Books, you would be pleas'd to let him know, either in a Letter to himself or else by me, the Titles of them, and the Editions, and then he will let you know, whither those Books are already in the Library or not, that if they should happen to be the same with those they have, you might think of making up your Benefaction with some others. but if I may give you my Advice in this Affair, I know you may have many Physick Books which now you do not use, and perhapps in Divinity and History, which if they prove such, as are not there already will be altogether acceptable as Books bought new out of the Shops; I hope to be in Town my self within this month, and if you please to send me your Answer within that time, with the names of the Books you think fit to present, I will communicate it to Dr: Hudson, or you may write to him yourself directed to University Colledge. so with my humble Service to all the good Company with you and my hearty Prayers for your health: I remain
Your most Affectionate Friend and humble Servant
J TYRRELL
My Daughter is well and presents her Service to you, my Son is still in Ireland. I hope you will pardon my writing to you by the hand of my Amanuensis the business being of no private concern and he writing some other Letters for me.
Address: For John Lock Esqr [at Oates, by Joslyn, Bishops Stortford]
Postmark: OC 26
Endorsed by Locke: J: Tyrrell 26 Oct. 03 Answered Nov. 12
3359. Dr. Robert Pitt to Locke [29? October 1703] (3350, 3363)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 17, ff. 179-80. The letter appears to be a response to Locke's lost letter of 26 October (no. 3350, endorsement) and is perhaps that mentioned in no. 3363 of 30 October. Locke's date, 29 October, may depend on something now lost; it does not appear to be a date of receipt.
Sir
I have sent ℥iiij of Cortex and as much of Diascordium, and ℥s of Calomel. and lb s of Sassaphras. I have omitted sending any
― 95 ―
purging Pill, because you take notice of the young Gentleman†1 being very easy to purge, and the stronger Pills might injure him, and the weak be not active enough to draw the matter prepar'd by the Calomel to the Evacuation. I propose therefore, that he may the next Day take a Portion of the Infusion of Sena and Rhabarb and Syrup of Roses. The Sassaphras will be made into a Tea by as much boyling as is us'd to Coffee. The other Herb to be infus'd as Tea. Both to be dranke separately. I have Known both very usefull. As for the Dispensary it increases in numbers of ordinary Patients, but the Rich, quibus inutilis sua libertas est,†2 as Celsus observes upon another occasion, do only now not expose and insult it. We have brought 'em to Cartesius,†a his Philosophy to doubt.†3 We are sure of our Point in time. especially when even our Freinds the Irish have given our Parliament a good Example. They are in Parliament considering of an Act to regulate the Practize of Phys. Chir. and the Apothecaries.†4 I have sent to the Press a Treatise,†5 the last I shall trouble the People with in this Controversy, to lay open the danger of taking Physick without the careful application: to expose their being advis'd for nothing by the Apothecary, which is the great Greivance, and, which will surprize you, that our present Physicians are the most ignorant in the World, because they have been forbid by the Ap. these last 40 years, from preparing Medicine in great Cases and therefore do not understand the true distinguishing Qualities and Vertues of any one Simple.
Sir I am
Your most humble servant
R PITT
Address: For Mr John Locke in Oates
Endorsed by Locke: Dr Pitt 29 Oct 03
― 96 ―
3360. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 29 October 1703 (3357, 3364)
B.L. MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 306-7. Answers nos. 3353 and 3357; answered by no. 3365.
Oates 29 Oct 03
Dear Cosin
Not being of late used to receive any letters from you in the packet of news papers I neglected to open them till after my letter was gon which was the reason you had in my last noe answer to yours of 23d. But I guess it makes noe great difference in the case for by mine which I writ in answer to yours of 21 the next oportunity after I received it and which I hope came to your hands Wednesday last†1 I said all I could about Mr Hopwood, who I guess by yours of the 26 that came to my hands but just now, is returnd to you again. The half years interest that you enquire after will be due the 3d of Novemb next. But you know the man must be dealt gently with and it will not look very well to goe about to draw the money out of his hand soe of a suddain, you know his temper and it will not be safe to give him matter of offence. The security that money is upon I look upon to be a very good one, and I think it is like to lie, and unless this new one that offers it self be a very tempting one and like to be of continuance I am a little doubtfull whether it be best to mention any thing to Mr C†2 about takeing that in. If I knew the conveniencys of this New one I should be better able to judg. In the mean time Between that of Mr Hopwood and this new mans I hope you will have a good security for two or for one. If you had told me the conveniencys and the continuance of this last offerd security I should have been a little forwarder about drawing out of Mr Cs hands. What is best now to doe you can best judg, but pray take care not to stir in it if it may give him the least matter of exception nor unlesse this new Security be every way very promiseing and suitable to my purpose. If you write again about it pray informe me the best you can. I am
Dear Cosin
Your most affectionate Cosin and humble servant
J LOCKE
― 97 ―
Address: For Peter King Esqr member of Parliament. To be left at Mr Potmans in the Middle Temple London Frank
Postmark: NO 1
Endorsed by King: Mr Locke Octob. 29. 1703.
3361. Locke to Anthony Collins, 29 October 1703 (3351, 3372)
The Carl H. Pforzheimer Library, New York. Transcribed from photostat. Printed in A Collection of Several Pieces, pp. 271-5. Answers no. 3351; answered by no. 3372.
Oates 29 Oct 03
Sir
You in yours of 21 Say a great many very kind things, and I beleive all that you say, and yet I am not very well satisfied with you. And how then is it possible to please you? will you be ready to say. Think that I am as much pleased with your company, as much obleiged by your conversation as you are by mine, and you set me at rest and I am the most satisfied man in the world. You complain of a great many defects and that very complaint is the highest recommendation I could desire to make me love and esteem you and desire your freindship. And if I were now seting out in the world I should think it my great <happyness>†a to have such a companion as you who had a true relish of truth, would in earnest seeke it with me, from whom I might receive it undisguisd, and to whom I might communicate what I thought true freely. Beleive it my good Friend to Love truth for truths sake is the principal part of humane perfection in this world and the seed plot of all other virtues, and if I mistake not you have as much of it as ever I met with in any body. What then is there wanting to make you equall to the best, and a freind for any one to be proud of? Would you have me take upon me because I have the start of you in the number of years and be supercilious conceited for haveing in a long ramble traveld some countreys which a yonge voyager has not yet had time to see, and from whence one may be sure he will bring larger collections of Solid Knowledg? In good earnest Sir when I consider how much of my life has been trifled away in beaten tracts where I vampd on with others onely to follow those that went before us I cannot but think I have just as much reason
― 98 ―
to be proud, as if I had travelled all England and (if you will) France too, onely to acquaint my self with the roads and be able to tell how the high ways lye wherein those of equipage and even the herd too travel. Now me thinks (and these are often old mens dreams) I see openings to truth, and direct paths leading to it wherein a little industry and application would setle ones mind with satisfaction even in those matters which you mention and leave noe darkeness or doubt even to the most scrupulous. But this is at the end of my day when my sun is seting. And though the prospect it has given me be what I would not for any thing be without, there is so much irresistible truth beauty and consistency in it, yet it is for one of your age, I think I ought to say, for you your self to set about it as a worke you would put into order and obleige the world with. You see whether my just thoughts of you have led me. and that I shall have no quarrel with you if you will cease to set me as you doe on the higher ground; and to think that I have not as much pleasure and satisfaction from your company as you have from mine. If I were able to live in your neighbour hood in town I should quickly convince you of that, and you scape being haunted by me onely by being out of my reach. A little better acquaintanc will let you see that in the communication of truth between those who receive it in the Love of it, he that answers is no less obleiged than he that asks the question: and therefore you owe me not those mighty thanks you send me for haveing the good luck to say something that pleased you. If it were good seed I am sure it was sown in good grownd and may expect a great increase. I think you have a familiar ready to dispatch what you undertake for your freinds. How is it possible else you should soe soon procure me Kirchers Concordance? I am not soe much in hast for it, but it will†a- come time-†a enough with the other books.
Shew me the man and I will shew you his cause†1 will hold now adays almost in all other cases as well as that of Proskunein, and yet they must all be thought lovers and promoters of truth. But my letter is too long already to enter upon soe copious a subject. All this family give you their humble service and thanks for the favour of your visit, particularly my Lady, to which she addes the new one of your so quick delivery of the letters to Mr Churchil
― 99 ―
and Dr Pitt, who writ as he promised you. I owe you a thousand thanks and am
Sir your most humble and most obedient servant
J LOCKE
Address: For Anthony Collins Esq at his Chamber in Searls Court numb: 6 in Lincolns Inne London
Postmark: NO 1
3362. Francis Limborch to Locke, 30 October 1703 (3331, 3377)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 13, ff. 221-2.
London 30 October 1703
Honoured Sir
Yesternight came in a Dutch maile and thereby the Inclosed from my father†1 who hath had againe a violentt Fitt off the palpitation off the heartt which he tells me by his letter hath att present left him againe and that he is pretty well againe; j know nott whether he mentions any thing in his letter to you off itt, butt he tells that the phisician had ordered him some herbs on old hock which he uses every other day, and by which he findes himself better, j know nott better Sir butt when he Complained off itt the last time butt you advised him to abstaine from wine; I wish there could be found outt any remedy to prevent itt which really I fear one time or other will sweep him away, he being very Corpulent and very hearty in his dyett; My Wife Jointly with me returns her most sincere and hearty thanks for your very kind present which I layde outt in 3 Castors and given her yesterday in your name, for which we both acknowledge our selves infinitely oblidged to you, butt particular more my owne self who really am ashamed off not haveing made uze better off the Just obligation I owed you; I confes I was afraide off your just Censure, because I knew nott whatt to say for my self in my defence, neither know itt as yett, butt humbly to confesse my fault and in all sincerity to begg you'll look upon itt as a folly off my youth and to be assicured itt hath nott been by want off due respect which j for ever shall owe to your person and memory. by my Continuall busines I really have neglected to send your flannell to Mr Pawlings
― 100 ―

butt itt shall be done next munday withoutt fault; these letters bring no Considerable news Landau holds outt still the fleet is gone to Sardinia and some private letters mention that Germans in Italy were fallen upon the French Marching towards savoy and killdm 4000 men butt this wants Confirmation.†1 The king off spain is with the first fair winde expected,†2 God graunt him good succes and that he through his mercy preserve your worthy person is the most sincere wishes and prayers off
Honoured Sir
Your Most oblidged and devoted servant
FRAN: LIMBORCH
Address: For John Locke Esquire These
Endorsed by Locke: F: Limborch 30 Oct 03
3363. Dr. Robert Pitt to Locke, 30 October 1703 (3359, 3378)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 17, ff. 181-2.
Sir
I write this for a more perfect Direction, where the Medicins you desir'd me to send you may be deliver'd. I sent my servant twice to the place appointed by you; where they did not own any such Person. My letter to you†3 is joynd with the Parcel. I hope I shall have the Answer to send it on Wednesday. You may order the Butcher to send a Porter to me for it, it shall be at any time deliverd to him. I forgott to note, what they cost. I am sorry the Accident has occasion'd this delay. Sir, I am with my service to my Lady Massam and her son
Your most humble servant
R. PITT.

3364. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 1 November 1703 (3360, 3365)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 308-9. Answered by no. 3367.
Oates 1 Nov 03
Dear Cosin
My two last letters haveing been writ to you in hast I forgot to enquire concerning Mr Newton whether you saw him and what he said to you on occasion of what you delivered to him. Pray a word or two of this in your next And also Mr Lydes answer to those particular matters you discoursed him about when you were in the country in relation to my affairs in his hands for I cannot well write to him till I know what he said thereupon. Mr Spranger has been just now with me and I have given his Son in law william Fuller a bill on you for an hundred and forty pounds at sight which I desire you to pay him. I have here inclosed set you his name in his own hand writeing that you may know him when he comes to you
The letters are come and I finde noething from you today but the printed news. I expected also to have heard from Mr Seamer that the Tumbler was done. I desire you to call upon him for it, pay him for it, and with your own hands deliver it to my Lady with the inclosed letter the first oportunity. but pray say noe thing of it to any body. I orderd it should come to £50 as near as they can make it, and that it should be well shaped, and be put into a hadsome box coverd with leather and lined with bays or the like as such things use to be. As soon as it is ready pray deliver it, for I have some reason to desire that it should not be delaid. If my Lord and Lady lye in town you will find her at home any day, if they are at Parsons green†1 Mr Collins (whose children are
― 102 ―
there)†1 will carry you thither any holy day. I beg you it may not be delaid. I would not have the Gent himself whose coach you goe in know any thing of the matter. Get it as cheap as you can
If the thousand pound security does not need the money very very speedily I beleive before the end of this weeke I may be able to send you a positive answer and therefore though the first should goe on yet pray doe not put it by if they can stay so long for an answer. I am
Dear Cosin
your most affectionate Cosin and humble servant
J LOCKE
Address: For Peter King Esqr member of Parliament. To be left at Mr Potmans in the Middle Temple London Frank.

I am Debtor for severall letters you have favoured me <with>†a since I have bin in Dorsttshire, from whence I returned last weeke. 3367. Peter King, later first Baron King, to Locke, 4 November 1703 (3365, 3369)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 12, ff. 208-9. Answers no. 3364; answered by no. 3369.
Inner Temple. Novemb. 4. 1703.
Sir
I†b- have this day paid your bill of 140 l. to Mr William Fuller-†b— I wish Mr Spranger had been here with him, That I might have
― 104 ―
known, where the Representatives of Capt. Thompson†1 live; from whome I have not yet heard any thing—The Tumbler is not yet finished, It is promised to be finished by Saturday in the afternoon, but I Question it, Assoon as it is, I shall follow your directions— Mr Newton had your papers from my own hand, he is very well pleased with your design and performance, and will Critically peruse what I delivered him,†2 I had a great deal of discourse with him upon the subject, he was very free and easy—As for mr Lydes affairs—The Warrant sent by me to distrain on Mary Cooks estate was not good, but Mr Lyde hath a letter of Atturney dated the 3d. february 1695, which will be a sufficient authority to distrain, Horwood is very poor, but being frighted, hath promised to pay the Mony at Pensford fair,†3 which if he doth not, mr Lyde will distrain—Gullock hath paid 14s. towards the arrears, and promises to pay the rest in a little time—Veale is a good Tenant, and therefore best continue him without Endeavouring to raise his rent—I am Sir
Your most affectionate Cosin and humble Servant
P. KING.
The Queen was not at the house too day, The Parliament is prorogued till Tuesday next.†4
Herewith you have mr Lydes account.

3369. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 8 November 1703 (3367, 3370)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 310-11. Answers nos. 3365 and 3367; answered by no. 3370.
Oates 8° Nov. 03
Dear Cosin
What you write to me in yours of the 2d concerning Mr Hopwood gives me noe occasion to say anything more to you upon that matter. when you know his resolution it may not be amiss to let me know it.
I thank you for answering my bill to William Fuller. Mr Spranger when he was here with him told me he should be in town this weeke and then he would give you an account of Captain Thomsons representative and that affair.
Mr Seamer in his of the 4th writes me that all would be ready the next day. If you got it before the end of the last week I hope
― 106 ―
it is deliverd with my letter before this time. If it be delaid any longer, it will be convenient to lay the blame of keeping my letter so long (for it is dated Nov. 1.) upon the slowness of the workeman, for I imploid you to get it as soon as you returned from the circuit. But pray however let it be deliverd as soon as possible may be. I have great reason to desire it. and pray send me word how it was accepted
I am glad you grow into a familiarity with Mr Newton. You will find him a very extraordinary man
I thank you for Mr Lydes account which you sent me, If what you writ besides be all that he answerd to my questions I have noe more to aske you upon that article. I am
Dear Cosin
Your most affectionate Cosin and humble servant
J LOCKE
The discharge being now ready my Lady Masham expects her mony from Capt Reves†1 this or the next weeke and then she will have £ 1000 by her which she desires to put out together, and to that purpose is enquireing after a security. I tell you this that if we make noe use of that in Somerset shire, you may give her notice of it in a line or two and there in very much obleige her, but of this you must be sure not to say a word to Sir Francis
Address: For Peter King Esqr at his Chamber in the Temple London
Endorsed by King: Mr Locke Novemb. 8. 1703.
3370. Peter King, later first Baron King, to Locke, 9 November 1703 (3369, 3373)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 12, ff. 210-11. Excerpt printed in Campbell, Lives of the Lord Chancellors, iv. 575-6. Answers no. 3369; answered by no. 3375.
Inner Temple. Novemb. 9. 1703.
Sir
I know not whether The Queens Speech will come out too night, and Therefore I shall Trouble you with a short account thereof, tho, The Speech its self was pretty long and very good,†2 Her
― 107 ―
Majesty told The Parliament, that she hop'd, she had Improv'd their Confidence in her, for that tho, No provision had been made the last session for the augmentation Troops, and the Expedition to Portugal had been already expensive, yet the funds of the last year held so well, That with them and the Produce of the Prizes, she had discharged that expense, That she was now entred into a League with Portugal and other allies to rescue the Monarchy of Spain from the house of Bourbon and restore it to the house of Austria, That This would require farther and speedy supplies, That The Duke of Savoy was come into the Confederacy, That Tho, The Revenue of the Civil List was diminished by the war, yet out of it she had sent a Considerable support to the Circle of Suabia, who had been under very great pressures, and That she would so manage her own revenue, as to apply some part of it to the Ease of her People, That she wanted words to express her desire for the peace and union of her people, and that every One of her subjects should live easy, and Therefore Intreated that there might be no rents or divisions amongst us—On The Report of the Speech to the House of Commons It was moved for an address of thanks to the Queen for her Speech, and in our address to specify The Alliance with Portugal, The support of the Circle of Suabia and the other things mentioned in her Speech, and to thank her for them particularly, Sir E— S—r†1 was only for a general address of thanks, and not to thank particularly, till the house had first considered the particular matters of the Speech, But It was carried with a swing to thank her Majesty particularly for all those things mentioned in her Speech, and to assure her, that we will stand by, support, and maintain all her alliances already made or to be made— The Address is to be drawn up too morrow morning—I hope We shall do well enough against our Common Enemy—
Mr Seamer did not end his busyness the latter end of the last Week, but now it is done, I hope by the next post to give you an account, that I have done too—In hopes to have Mr Hopwoods security I Let go that in Somersetshire, and I am afraid I shall lose Mr Hopwoods too—Sir You may depend upon it That I will mind what you write about My Lady Masham, I have a great many Obligations to her upon my own account, and if that were out of the way, Yet the Kindness she hath shown you will always oblige me to serve her and her family in any thing I can—I have
― 108 ―
but one thing more to add to this long Epistle, and that is to desire you in the next to let me know how you do, and, as my father used to tell me, to take care of your self, Christmass is hastning and It will be very great joy to me to find you then well at Oates— I am
Sir
Your most affectionate Cosin and Most Humble Servant
P. KING.
Since the writing of My Letter I have heard from Mr Hopwood, That He will have the mony—I will Endeavour to find out a security for my Lady Masham
Endorsed by Locke: P. King 9 Nov 03 Answered 12
3371. Dr. Pieter Guenellon to Locke, 9/20 November 1703 (3341, 3431)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 11, ff. 116-17.
d'amsterd ce 20 Nov. 1703.
Monsieur,
Nous sommes tres sensiblement obligé a l'ardeur avec la quelle vous prenez interest au restablissement de la santé de ma femme. elle s'est mise en train de vous obeir. elle prend regulierement de son infusion de bellis major le matin, tout d'une suitte, comme vous l'avez premierement ordonné; mais pour la theriace et de se tenir captive au lict, pendant quelques jours, c'est a quoy elle ne scauroit se resoudre. je vous diray que le lict est son ennemi, c'est la ou elle souffre le plus, et outre qu'elle a de la difficulté a prendre des remedes, elle est d'un temperament si sec et si bilieu, que je crain meme que la theriace l'offenseroit, c'est un remede chargé d'aromate et de drogues echauffantes, qui luy font tousjours du mal: et si l'opium qui y entre cause le calme que cela donne, je ne voi pas pourquoi l'opium seul, bien preparé et simplement dissoute dans l'eau, et purgé de sa resine, pris dans une quantité qui ne passe pas celle qu'une dose de theriace contient, puisse faire plus de mal. ma femme ne scauroit s'en abstenir. quand elle essaye de le faire, elle essuye constamment une tres meschante nuict. tant elle tousse sans relache. je suis fort de votre sentiment que toute liqueur fermentée comme le vin, luy sont nuisibles. elle s'en abstient aussi entierement. plus j'ay d'experience, plus j'observe ce que le
― 109 ―
fameu Malpigius repete dans ses ecrits. Naturam scilicet, esse magis solicitam in coercendis ferm<e>ntationibus,†a quam in excitandis.†1 j'ay fait prendre depuis ma derniere que j'ay eu l'honneur de vous ecrire, pendant un mois entier, a ma femme le vitriol de Mars en pilules, avec le suc de reglisse, tous les matins a juin, deux grains. je ne scay si je dois attribuer a ce remede l'amendement ou elle s'est trouvée depuis. ces mois†2 qui ont esté supprimez plus d'un an, ont reparu depuis. sa couleur est plus vive. elle marche gayement, elle n'a plus de fievre, point d'Asthme. sa tou en general est effectivement moindre, et ses phlegmes moins recuits. je croi qu'on juge mal en general de l'effect du fer et de l'acie<r>.†b on dit qu'il est aperitif. pour moi je le croi tousjours adstringent. et que dans la cachexie le sang etant sereu et sans consistence, ce remede a une proprieté a le restablir dans sa consistence naturelle. je me sers egalement du vitriol de mars et du crocus Martis aper ou†c- la fine rouillure de fer-†c dans les hemorrhagies comme dans les retentions de menstrues: et son effet est plus prompt pour les hemorrhagies que pour les suppressions. dans l'un il n'a besoin <que>†a de rendre le sang plus lié, et qu'il se contienne m<ieux>†a dans ses bornes; et dans l'autre il semble que le sang a besoin de sa consistence naturelle pour produire le mouvement quil faut pour produire le flu des menstrues. tous les esprits volatils que la chymie nous fournit, surviennent a quelques symptomes, mais ne guerissent presque aucune maladie. le spir. cochleariæ pris méme en quantité et longtems ne guerit point le scorbut. et le suc seul et recent de cet herbe, pris jusqua 4 onces par jour, guerit a vue d'œil, et en brefle scorbut le plus grand et le plus inveteré. et dans la paralysie et autres maladies froides du cerveau le mouvement, les frictions et une decoction d'herbes appropriees feront cent fois plus que tous les esprits et sels volatils que les chymistes nous vantent tant. voyez l'effect admirable du kinakina! n'est il pas vraysemblable quil agit principalement par son adstriction? dans l'epidemie qui regne parmi nous presentement, qui est une fievre double tierce, fort opiniatre dans les enfans, j'en ay sauvé plusieurs avec cet ecorce, dont quelques uns etoient atteint de
― 110 ―
gangrene a la bouche. quelle dissolution ne fait pas l'usage de l'eau de vie a ceux qui en abusent? quel dereglement ne voit on pas de toutes ces sausses de haut gout qui echauffent le sang et le rendent fievreu? vous le scavez mieu que moi Monsieur et le bon usage que vous faitte de toute chose vous soutient sans doute contre l'effect de tant de maladies que vous avez essuyez. on nous a dit que vous vous portez assez bien presentement. Dieu vous le continue le reste de vos jours! c'est ce que nous souhaittons ardemment. toute la famille vous presente ses treshumbles respects. Monsieur Veen a eu une attaque de paralysie a la langue graces a Dieu cela se passe. ma femme vous remercie encore de vos bienfaits, de votre tendresse. du soin que vous avez de contribuer efficacement a son restablissement. je suis d'un tres profond respect
Monsieur,
Votre tres humble, tres oblige et tres obeissant serviteur,
P. GUENELLON.
Address: For Mr. John Locke at Mr. powlings over against the plough in little lincolns inne feilds London
Postmark: NO 22
Endorsed by Locke: P Guenellon 20 Nov. 03 Answered Dec. 22
3372. Anthony Collins to Locke, 10 November 1703 (3361, 3381)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 7, ff. 5-6. Forwarded by Awnsham Churchill on 17 November: p. 121. Answers no. 3361; answered by no. 3384.
Lincolns Inne. Novemb. 10th/03
Sir
You in yours of the 29th of Oct: perfectly come up to all the Characters that I could ever frame to my self of a Friend from whom I propos'd to reap any advantages by his Instructions, For I could never hope the continuance of his acquaintance unless I had a better place in his thoughts than I deserv'd, as I'm sure I have in yours, Because I believe all the kind things you say of me do proceed from your perswasion that they are true, though without vanity I cannot take 'em to my self. Let not this difference between us give you any dissatisfaction, since I can no more help disagreeing from you in this, than I can agreeing with you in every thing else, and accept of my thanks (the only return I can make you for all your
― 111 ―
favours) for those assistances I have had from you in my Enquiry after truth, since they take not of from my acknowledgment to the divine goodness for the great blessing of your friendship.
You have set me a very agreable task, and I heartily wish my ability was equall to my inclination to do justice to Truth, and particularly that of Religion which I so much long to see done that I could freely give you half those years my age gives me a prospect of enjoying to enable you to perform what I really think you only are capable of, and could easily have faith enough to believe you are reserv'd for, if you would set about it, For if the extent of my Knowledg in men or Books was to be my Rule in the Case, I could affirm that there are innumerable truths to the benefit of us as Men, and as Christians which will be lost to the world whenever it is deprivd of you. You see whither my just thoughts of you have led me, who notwithstanding what I have said, have a regard to you as well as to truth, and would not have your health injur'd, for all the benefit's that might accrue from it; But of this you are the fittest Judg, who will be more apt to determine to your own prejudice, than I should be, when the good of mankind is concern'd.
I have sent you all your books except the Annotat. in Vet. Test. etc†1 which I did intend to have staid for, and to have sent with the rest. when it comes from Holland you shall have it by it self. You will also find in the Packet the Dedication to the Queen of the second vol. of the Earl of Clarendons Hist etc†2 which was subscribd by the Earls of Clarendon and Rochester in that book that was presented to her Majesty. It hath made too much noise in Town, that I need make you any excuse for taking the liberty to send such a curiosity, which you might not otherwise have seen. I desire the favour of you to return it when you have done with it. a few days agoe I received some papers from Mr Bold containing an answer to so much of the first part of Broughtons Psychologia as you are concernd in.†3 I need not tell you that he keeps up to that clearness, and strength of reason that his former works show him
― 112 ―
to be Master of. if you have a mind to see 'em I will get 'em fairly transcribd for your use. The smallness of his hand, <the>†a frequent blotting and interlining will give you too much trouble, should I send 'em to you as they are. I desire my most humble service to my Lady and the rest of the family. I am with a perfect esteem and respect
Your most Humble and most Obedient Servant
A. COLLINS
Address: To John Locke Esq [at Oates, by Joslyn, Bishops Stortford]
Endorsed by Locke: A Collins 10 Nov 03 Answered 17

Sir
I have presented Your Letter and Gold Tumbler to the Countess of Peterborough, with whome I had a great deale of discourse, She Received your present with great expressions of respect and kindness for you, and saith, that she will come down to Oates and see you there in a little time, She told me That she should always keep with the greatest care any mark of mr Locks favour, but that without this kind remembrance she should never have forgot a Gentleman, who deserv'd so well of every one, and particularly of her, She told me long storys of her bringing you over from Holland, and your obliging her with a ramble in an open Calash for 150 miles,†1 and at last told me that for your sake she should study to do me any kindness that lay in her power—Mr Seamers note came just to fifty three pounds, which I†b have paid him and have his receit. 3374. John Shute, later Barrington, later first Viscount Barrington, to Locke, 11 November 1703 (3074, 3394)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 18, ff. 99-100.
Sir
I have venturd to send you a Pamphlet, which I know very well is too inconsiderable, for you to have given your Bookseller any Commission about.†2 And tho' it has not so much as Novelty to recommend it, is yet, I think, in no great danger of being among your Duplicates. That you are troubled with it now is owing to your condescension in talking over my affairs, and inviting me by those peculiar marks of your Friendship, to let you know that I was the Author of it. Which is a Confession I have not made to many, or that I thought many wou'd care to hear. The Regard you were pleas'd to convince me you had for my concerns, made me think you wou'd pardon me for troubling you with so useless a Secrett. And the Value, I have allways seen, you put upon an honest design for the good of the Public, gave me an Inclination to acquaint you, that it had had power enough over me, to make me do what I coud to serve it. I think I coud not have us'd a more proper argument to convince you of my Love of my Country, or of my Regard to your Beleif of it, than by owning a Book, that is so very Incorrect, and tempting You to Revise†3 it. Which whenever I do my self, I find it more likely to hurt my Reputation, than
― 114 ―
to serve an honest Cause. But I wont be so impertinent as to transcribe my Preface. That will inform you, that some of the Faults are owing to Hast. Those which you find (and I know none can escape your notice) Good Nature and Friendship which so happily tempers the Quickness of your Penetration will forgive. If you meet with any <thing>†a that is not a Fault it belongs to You Sir. For I'm sure I'm the likest to You, and copy you the most exactly of any Man, I ever had the honor to know, when I think as an Englishman or as a Christian ought to do. There are but two things, that set me at tolerable terms with my Self for having ventur'd my own credit, and that of my cause by appearing in Print: The one is, that I have had the Thanks and Approbation of a Family abroad, for whose Service it was chiefly design'd:†1 And the other is, that You have given me reason to flatter my self, that You will think me more worthy to be
Sir
Your most Humble Servant
J. SHUTE.
Lincolns Inn Nov. 11. 1703.
Endorsed by Locke: J: Shute 11 Nov. 03
3375. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 12 November 1703 (3373, 3376)
B.L. MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 312-13. An excerpt with passages from no. 3380 is printed in King as a single letter, 15 November: p. 261. Answers no. 3370; answered by no. 3382.
Oates 12 Nov. 03
Dear Cosin
I thank you for your kind letter of the 9th. I am glad to find you in so good temper god continue it. My Lady Masham takes what you say to her very kindly, believes it all and returns you her thanks. I need not repeat any thing to you about Parsons green. I shall be glad to hear that it is done and how taken. If Mr Hopwoods business goes on I know I shall hear from you, and then shall write to you as there shall be occasion. If upon your askeing
― 115 ―
me with so much concerne as you doe how I doe, I should answer that I have noe more reason to brag than you had lately of your tooth ach and swelld face, would you not think me a little morose? Though you say noe thing of it yet I tell you that the surest way that I know in the world to get a cold and all the mischeifs that follow it is to have ones room washed and then sit in it. I never knew it faile, and you will doe well to write it down with a Probatum est. As to my lungs they goe on their course, and though they have brought me now to be good for noe thing I am not surprised at it. They have lasted longer already than the world or I expected. How much longer they will be able to blow at the hard rate they doe I cannot precisely say. But in this race of humane life when breath is wanting for the least motion one cannot be far from ones journeys end. But I am obleiged to your concerne and am
Dear Cosin
Your most affectionate Cosin and humble servant
J LOCKE
If Mr Hopwood goes on you remember that the Malt tickets must goe to the makeing up of my quota. Pray send me word when he must have the mony

Frank.

3376. Peter King, later first Baron King, to Locke, 13 November 1703 (3375, 3380)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 12, ff. 214-15. Answered by no. 3380.
Inner Temple. Nov. 13. 1703.
Sir
I gave you an account by my last of the discourse I had with My Lady Peterborough, since which I have met My Lord, who in his jocular way thinks that he hath reason to suspect an Intimacy between you and his Lady, if he were not sure that you follow the rules of true Philosophy, He is in a very good humour, and thinks himself much obliged to you, and saith he will speedily
― 116 ―
give you a visit at Oates—I Should be very glad of an opportunity to wait upon you before Christmass, My Inclination is to be with you at Oates, and if at any time You should be willing to see me there before the course of my busyness regularly permits me, On The Least Intimation of your mind I will not faile to wait upon you—God Knows what scene will open next in the affairs of Europe. A storm seems to be coming from Turky,†1 and all Europe is like to be thrown into war and Confusion, I hope this will however make way for that Kingdome, that shall not be moved†2 —Here is no news here—
I am
Sir
Your Most affectionate Cosin and most humble Servant
P. KING.
Endorsed by Locke: P: King 13 Nov. 03 Answered 15
3377. Francis Limborch to Locke, 13 November 1703 (3362, 3447)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 13, ff. 223-4.
London 13 November 1703
Honoured Sir
The occasion off my troubling you with these few lines is to acquaint you that itt hath pleased Almighty God to deliver my wife yesterday morning halfe an hour past one off a sonn,†3 which I intended to have my self acquainted you with, butt itt being upon our Holland fleets departure which is Fallen down this day to Gravesend itt hath proved impossible to me, and necessitates me to doe itt with these few lines, by which j presume also to begg off you most sincerily both for my wife (who j thank God is pretty well) and me that you will favour me so much as to stand Godfather to itt, my Lady Masham to who I write letter to this purpose and desire you to name him and to appoint the time when you please he shall be Christened; upon which I begg the favour off a line or two; by my last letters from Holland My Father is perfectly
― 117 ―
well againe also is Md S.†1 very well; and j heartily wish j may hear the same from you Sir and with our most sincere respects I subscribe my self jn all sincerity
Honoured Sir
Your most oblidged humble servant
FRANCIS LIMBORCH

3378. Dr. Robert Pitt to Locke, [13 November 1703] (3363, 3399)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 17, ff. 183-4. Date from postmark and Locke's endorsement.
Sir
Since Mr Massam had so many stools with his Potion, I thought he would not soon want another Purgative, which perswaded me to defer sending the Pills till Wednesday, when they shall be deliver'd to the Person you appoint. The Vomiting was occasion'd by the Calomel, we often bid our Patients expect it, and besides it disposes to be laxative for some days. By both it affects the blood to a greater Degree. Half a Pound of lime stone may be us'd with a Gallon of Water, and six Ounces of Guiacum and Sassaphras each, or Mr Massam may pretty often drinke the Sassaphras Tea only. He may observ, which agrees best with him. and the Buck-bean may come in in its Turn. The Decoction of the Woods ought to be at least half an Hour. I hope your next will acquaint me, that Mr Massam finds himself more easy, to whom and my Lady I present my service and am
Sir
Your most humble servant
ROB: PITT.
Address: For Mr John Locke at Mr Joslyn's House in Bishop Stratford

3379. James Johnstoun to Locke, 14 November 1703 (2884, 3497)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 12, ff. 45a-45b.
Twittenham 14 Novr 703
Sir
I am ashamed that you have prevented me by yours which I have just nou received. My constant resolutions to wait upon you (and which will still hold) keeped me from writing. I was this summar contrary to my inclinations obliged to goe to Hinton in a manner to wait on My Lady Pembroke who passed a moneth there;†1 so that my work was behind at home, which hath keept me so long here: and, pour me confesser a vous, I have got into such an innocent course of lyfe and thoughts; that its a pain to me to goe to London and if others would not impute my not going to spleen or humour, of which I was never so free, (for I never had such health and pleasure of lyfe) I would not goe at all: but I am just nou in the hurry of going this day but least I should be hindered there, I would not delay to write to you the sense both Mrs Johnstoun and I have of your kind remembrance and to assure you that we doe heartily accept of any token of your friendship that you are pleased to send us: for tho we need no help to make us remember you; you will be remembred and more thought on when both you and we are gone then you are nou; tho few men so bold to do good and be useful in their tyme as you†a- have been more-†a lucky in escaping the malice and envie that living virtue is subject to; nay you have lived to have justice done you by all those in whose thoughts one would desire to have any place. I myself doe envie (and so you may pardon others) amongst many other good qualityes; the tranquillitye in which you persevere to possesse yourself; it seems nothing troubles you but the poor old horse,†2 hou to provide too for the remainder of his days. Noe doubt your support is the reflexion that you have done all the good you could; sweet is the remembrance of a well-spent lyfe sayeth the old Cato;†3 there is an excellent sermon printed of the
― 119 ―
conformist Calamy of our Saviors going about to doe Good.†1 I am (as we speake in Scotland) in a manner called to doe a very considerable worke and if ever I doe it as it should be done; I promise to my self to die more easye then its like I otherwise shall. My call is that noe man is or can be in the circumstances that I am in to doe it but of this at meeting for I am nou in a hurry.†2 Our respects to My Lady Masham. I suppose Sir Francis is nou in toun. so praying for one more winter at least I rest
your true friend and humble servant.
J. JOHNSTOUN.
Endorsed by Locke: J: Johnstown 14° Nov. 04 Answered Jun. 5
3380. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 15 November 1703 (3376, 3382)

Dear Cosin
I thank you for the delivery of the tumbler as I desired. And take very kindly your offer of comeing hither. Your kindeness makes me very willing to see and enjoy you, but at the same time it makes me the more cautious to disturb your business. However since you allow me the liberty you may be assured if there be occasion I shall send for you.

I am troubled at your news from Turky, For though I think I shall be gon before any storm

from thence can reach hither, yet you and my freinds and my country whilst I have any thought will be dear to me. I am
Dear Cosin
Your most affectionate Cosin and humble servant

3381. Locke to Anthony Collins, 16 November 1703 (3372, 3384)
The Carl H. Pforzheimer Library, New York. Transcribed from photostat. Printed in A Collection of Several Pieces, p. 275. Identifiable as the 'short note' which Locke dispatched on the morning of 17 November: p. 122. On p. 125 he says that he wrote both the present letter and no. 3384 on the same day; he was writing loosely. Answered by no. 3385.
Oates 16 Nov. 03
Sir
If I aske you how you doe it is because I am concerned for your health: If I aske you whether you have sent me any books since you went to town it is not that I am in hast for them but to know how the carrier uses me. And if I aske whether you are of Lincolns Inne it is to know of what place you write your self which I desire you to tell me in your next and what good new books there are. My Lady and this whole family are your humble servants. In particular doe me the justice to beleeive that I am with a perfect sincerity
Sir
Your most obleiged friend and most humble servant

3382. Peter King, later first Baron King, to Locke, 16 November 1703 (3380, 3388)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 12, ff. 216-17. Answers no. 3375.
Inner Temple Novemb. 16. 1703.
Sir
I Thank you for your letter of the 12th instant, and the account therein of your health, about which I had been in some pain, Sir Francis having acquainted me, that you had been somewhat out of order,—I was at Mr Newtons last Sunday night, where was other Company, and so could not freely discourse of your papers, but whispered me, that he had read them half over—Mr Hopwood is yet a little uncertain, There is something yet sticks, There is a Thousand pound on the state, which the People who have it, are not willing to take in, and I do not care to lend mony on an estate, Unless I have the whole security at my Command, Nothing hath been done these two days in the house of Commons but the forms of voting a supply, and a division about printing Our address, The voices were equal 95 on each side, The Speaker determin'd in favour of the Noes, however The Address was printed by authority,†1 and is here inclosed,
I am
Sir
Your Most affectionate Cosin and most humble Servant.
P. KING.

3383. Awnsham Churchill to Locke, 17 November 1703 (3366, 3390)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 5, ff. 202-3. Locke's endorsement shows that the letter reached him on the day on which it was written.
London Nov. 17th. 1703
Sir
I send herewith 2 Esop interlinary†2 and some things mr Collins left for you†3 and mr Stephens book of Comunion.†4
― 122 ―
I have delivered Secretary Johnston the pacquet, he will shortly come downe to see you. I am very sorry to hear by Sir Fra. Masham that you are not very well, I hope this will find you perfectly recovered.
I shall draw out your account next weeke. I am very busy just now publishing my Collection of Voyages.†1 Ile send you one by Lenham Saturday and I begg youl send me the volumn you have sticht†2 —
Sir
Your most assurd Servant
A CHURCHIL
Address: To John Locke Esquire present at Oates

3384. Locke to Anthony Collins, 17 November 1703 (3381, 3385)
B.M., Add. MS. 4290, ff. 25v-26v. Copy made by or for Desmaizeaux. Printed presumably from this text, or a copy of it, in A Collection of Several Pieces, pp. 276-7. Evidently written late in the day, after no. 3383 and its enclosure had arrived. Answers no. 3372; answered by no. 3389.
Oates. 17. Nov. 03.
Sir
I†a- sent you a short note by the way of Bishop Startford this morning.-†a†3 The books I received from you to night with the kind letter accompanying them far more valuable than the books give matter of enlarging my self this evening. The common offices of freindship that I constantly receive from you in a very obliging manner give me scope enough and afford me large matter of acknowledgment. But when I think of you I feel something of nearer concernment that touches me, and that noble principle of the love of truth which possesses you makes me almost forget those other obligations which I should be very thankful for to another. In good earnest Sir You cannot think what a comfort it is to me to have found out such a man, and not only so but I have the satisfaction that he is my freind. This gives a gusto to all the good
― 123 ―
things you say to me in your letter. For though I cannot attribute them to my self (for I know my own defects too well) Yet I am ready to perswade my self you mean as you say, and to confess the truth to you I almost loath to undeceive you so much do I value your good opinion. But to set it upon the right ground, you must know, that I am a poor ignorant man and If I have any thing to boast of it is that I sincerely love and seek truth with indifferency, whom it pleases or displeases. I take you to be of the same School, and so imbrace you. And if it please god to afford me so much life, as to see you again, I shall communicate to you some of my thoughts tending that way. You need not make any Apology for any book that is not yet come: I thank you for those you have sent me; they are more I think than I shall use, for the Indisposition of my health has beaten me almost quite out of the use of books, and the growing uneasiness of my†a distemper makes me good for nothing — —— ——
Dear Sir
Your —
3385. Anthony Collins to Locke, 18 November 1703 (3384, 3387)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 7, ff. 7-8. Answers no. 3381; answered by no. 3387.
Lincolns Inne. Novem. 18th/03
Sir
If I tell you that I am very well, or that I write myself of the Middle Temple, it is to comply with yours of the 16th, but if I tell you that I sent you some books which you ought to have had before the Date of yours, it is not to acquaint you how the Carrier, but how Mr Churchill uses you, to whom I deliver'd 'em on the 10th or 11th, with orders to send 'em by the first opportunity, which I hear he hath neglected.
As for the Books that come abroad I shall from time to time give you an account of the best of 'em. at present wee have but few worth taking notice of, and they are these: Bishop of Meauxs Critique upon Simons version of the New Testament.†1 This being a Book printed at Paris there came but a few of 'em to England,
― 124 ―
which are all sold of. I can lend it to you if you have a mind to see it. Wee have Mr Bayles New Book come over, of which you will see an account in the Republique des Lettres for November.†1 Mr Fletcher hath publish'd the speeches he made in the last session of the Scots Parliament to prevent mistakes in the affairs to which they relate.†2 I can think of nothing else printed of late worth your notice but what I presume you know already. Mr Newtons Theory of Light and Colours is Printing in English in quarto. It will make about forty sheets of the same character with his Principia etc.†3
Next week comes abroad a Book of Dr Davenants in Part against the Bill to prevent Occas. Conformity where if he doth not speak his own sense, he doth that of her Majesty, who I can assure you upon good evidence declared in Councill that she could not look upon those to be her Friends that should again promote it.†4
I heard from Mr Bold yesterday who gives his service to every body at Oats. I am their most Humble servant and in particular with a perfect sincerity
Sir
Your most obligd humble Servant
A COLLINS
Address: For John Locke Esq [at Oates, by Joslyn, Bishops Stortford]
Postmarks: NO 18; GC
Endorsed by Locke: A Collins 18 Nov. 03 Answered 22

3387. Locke to Anthony Collins, 22 November 1703 (3385, 3389)
The Houghton Library, Harvard. Transcribed from photostat. Answers no. 3385; answered by no. 3391.
Oates 22 Nov 03
Sir
I have yours of 18th, and if you have since the writeing of it received an other from me writ the same day with that to which yours is an answer, you will be satisfied that before I went to bed that night I found where the books you did me the favour to send me and your letter that accompanied them had stuck by the way. Mr Churchil does me an hundred good offices But he haveing a great deale of business of his own I am not to take it a misse if that sometimes makes mine be a little postponed, therefore pray
― 126 ―
doe not chide him too much, for that the books that you put into his hands for me came a little later than you intended, and I beleive he kept them to send them by a very safe hand that was comeing directly hither
If the Bishop of Meaux critique upon Simons version of the New Testament be onely in relation to the Vulgate or controversies of their church I would not give you the trouble to send it me But if it gives any light into the true sense of the S Scripture by establishing the Greek text or explaining the sense of any obscure or difficult passages I shall be glad to see it. But I expect not much of that kind from that Gent: Mr Fletchers speeches which you mention I desire you to send me, and pray in your next say a word of Mr Bayles new book for I should much more relye upon your account of it than what is to be found in the Republique des Letres though that of November is not yet come to my hands. I suppose Mr Newtons book will be done before Christmas. The peice of News you close your letter with I heartily thank you for. I am without complement
Sir
Your most humble and most obleiged servant
J LOCKE
My Lady and the company here give your their humble service
Address: For Anthony Collins Esquire at his Chamber No: 6 in Serle Court in Lincolns Inne London
Postmark: NO 24

3389. Anthony Collins to Locke, 23 November 1703 (3387, 3391)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 7, ff. 9-10. Answers no. 3384.
Lincolns Inne. Nov. 23./03
Sir
I received Yours, together with the Dedication I sent you,†1 on the 18th, which I had sooner acknowledg'd, but that I waited for some better news from Oats than was contained in it, which since I have had the satisfaction to hear. This gives me new life. I believe as I would have it, and my hope overcomes my fear for you. Indeed you are so necessary to me, that I cannot entertain the thought of parting with you, whose Friendship only I can think it worth living for to enjoy. If I do not take all occasions of waiting on you, when you give me so great encouragements as the communicating any of your thoughts to me, Think that nothing but the greatest Love and affection for you can keep me from you, who now I am at a distance can consider how I made you spend your self in discoursing with me, and that I promoted it, without reflecting enough on the injury it might do you. Believe me, Sir,
― 128 ―
I can take nothing amiss from you for any regard to your self, but the more care you have of your own health the greater obligation you will lay on
Dear Sir
Your most Affectionate Friend and most Humble servant
A. COLLINS
My most Humble service to my Lady and the rest of the Family at Oats.
Address: To John Locke Esq [at Oates, by Joslyn, Bishops Stortford]
Endorsed by Locke: A Collins 23 Nov: 03

3391. Anthony Collins to Locke, 25 November 1703 (3389, 3422)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 7, f. 11. Answers no. 3387.
Lincolns Inne. Nov. 25/03
Sir
I had yours of 22d Yesterday, and have now sent you Mr Fletchers speeches together with proposalls that just now came to my hands for printing a New book.†3 I have not troubled you with the Bishop of Meaux's Critique etc: which I have look'd into since I wrote you word about it, and find only to relate to the Vulgate and the Socinian Controversy, and to have no tendency to any of the good purposes you speak of, being only remarkable for a great deal of disingenuity, which Orthodoxy every where stands in need of. As to Mr Bayles Book. The subjects he handles are very many but few of any importance, and will not admit of being treated in such a manner as to give much entertainment to the publick, and he himself hath been (as I imagine) at little more pains than to read a great many trifling Authors, and to write. I would not be so free with Mr Bayle, But when you ask me my Opinion, I must speak as I think. You see I am a severe Judg of Books. The relish of the Conversation at Oats hath deliver'd me from the satisfaction I took in reading so many bad ones, and
― 130 ―
is a sufficient recompence for the loss of it. You have my wishes for the continuance of your health and had long since my heart. I am with perfect sincerity
Dear Sir
Your most Humble and most Obedient servant
A COLLINS
My Humble service to my Lady and the rest of the family at Oats
Address: To Jn. Locke Esq at Oats in Essex
Endorsed by Locke: A. Collins 25 Nov. 03
3392. James Tyrrell to Locke, 27 November [1703] (3358, 3477)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 22, ff. 163-4. Year from Locke's endorsement.
London Novem: 27.
Dear Sir
I received your last, and as to your proposal about the books you mean to present to the Bod: Library; I shewd it to Dr: Hudson; who presents his service to you, and bids me let you know; that the books whose Titles you mention, are not in the Library, so that if you please to present them, they will be very acceptable: but if to those you give of your owne, you would likewise add, your letters to Mr: Proast, the Present would be yet more valueable, since that controversy is no where so well handled;†1 but as for this I onely tell you my Judgment I must leave it to your Discretion; and therefore shall say no more as to this Point, then that when ever you please to order Mr: Churchil to send the Books to Oxon, I will see them deliver'd to the Library Keeper and your name inserted in the great Book of Benefactors.
I have bin in Towne these 4 days, and when I went last to the Club was very glad to hear from worthy Sir Francis the good news of your amendment from that languishing distemper you labourd under, and hope this mild season will restore you to that good state of health in which I left you in the spring, so with my
― 131 ―
hearty wishes for that and such another comfortable meeting as wee had the last spring I remain
Your affectionate Freind and humble Servant
J TYRRELL
I pray give my humble service to your good Lady Lady. and to her son and daughter.
Mr: Collyns and all the rest of the Club from whence I direct this are your servant
I hope you will remember to get the marginal notes transcribed upon the sheets I sent as you were pleased to promise me.†1 last night there arose so feirce and a violent storme between 1, and 2, which continued till near 6, that it has beat downe the roofs of a great many houses, and killd near 20 persons; the streets were this morning all filld with the Rubbige of Tiles and brickbats, it was so terrible, that most People got up: but for my owne part, not knowing how to be safer, I lay abed, and read, till I was weary, not being able to sleep for the horrid noise of the fall of Tiles, and Bricks into the Court yard where I lye, and I much feard the falling in of the Chymneys, or roofs which happend in divers places, wee have not as yet heard what mischeif has bin done at sea. but fear a great deal, for in a short Gust of wind that happend the night before the York frigate of above 60 Guns was drove from her Anchors and stranded, and near 300 seamen perishd. this is to be reckon'd among rare Accidentia in nature, for were such things to happen often the Huricanes of America w<ere>†a to be preferd.†2
Address: For John Lock Esqr [at Oates, by Joslyn, Bishops Stortford]
Written by Sir Francis Masham (?): Frank Fra: Masham:
Postmarks: NO 27; GC
Endorsed by Locke: J: Tyrrel 27 Nov. 03 Answered Dec. 14

3393. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 29 November 1703 (3388, 3395)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 316-17. Answered by no. 3395.
Oates 29 Nov. 03
Dear Cosin
I am glad you scaped so well in the storm. It has done no less ravage in the country here abouts than in the town everyone that one sees brings some new story of mischeif received. Though the damage upon Land is, I doubt not, exceeding great yet my greatest fear is of what we shall hear from sea for there goe ships goods and mens lives to boot in shoales together.
You send me the Votes and I thank you, But Sir Francis furnishes us, and so you need not give your self that trouble, those that you have sent me already you may have again if you want them to compleat a set
In my last I desired some things of mine that are at Mr Pawlings. among the particulars intended to be set down this was forgotten, a parcel of fine holland which you will finde in a flanen bag in the black trunk. this I desire may also come with the rest
I am just now writeing a letter into Holland to a person to whom my last miscaried, this brings to my mind my desire I mentiond to you of speakeing to Sir Thomas Frankland of the abuse of his post officer at Bishop Startford, who of late has got a trick to require of me and Mr Coste 4d a peice for every letter we send to Holland which extortion serves onely to make the letters miscary (as it happend in this which I now mention) and to cheat the office by suppresseing of such letters. I am
Dear Cosin
Your most affectionate Cosin and humble servant
J L
My Lady and all here greet you well
Address: For Peter King Esqr member of Parliament. To be left at Mr Potmans in the Middle Temple London Frank.
Postmark: DE 1
Endorsed by King: Mr Locke Novemb. 29. 1703.

3394. John Shute, later Barrington, later first Viscount Barrington, to Locke, 30 November 1703 (3374, 3553)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 18, ff. 101-2.
Sir
You have ever since I have had the honor to be known to you discover'd a great deal of Concern and Kindness for me; but your last gives me a peculiar Instance of Tendernes and Friendship. You saw me perfectly out of countenance at a freedom I had ventur'd to use, and because you saw I meant well, You were pleas'd to give me those Encouragements, that wou'd have made me too vain, had my bashfullness been counterfeited, or your design mistaken. But tho' an Instance of your pity and Condescension was never more seasonable, nor any man so desirous of Anothers good Opinion, as I am of yours; yet the things you are pleas'd to say are so much to my Advantage, that they prove like a Strong perfume to weak Spirits. I own Sir, I'm allmost afraid to read over some passages in your Letter; tho' others give me more just pride and pleasure, than a Patent for the best favor a Court cou'd bestow. I confess I vallue my self as much more for being Mr Lockes Favorit, than for being that of a Prince's, as the bounds of his strong sense exceed those of the others Empire. I say this with the more justice Sir, since you have done that in your Province, which they have but attempted in Theirs. You alone have vindicated the Rights and Dignitys of human nature, and have restor'd Liberty to Mens Consciences from the Tyranny of human Laws and their own Passions, Whilest they how well soever supported by Leagues and Alliances, have barely contented themselves with maintaining the freedom of their own Countrys from some degrees of foreign force.
You must not wonder then Sir, that a parcell of People desirous of Liberty and threatned with hardships; Defenceless and Expos'd but Secure in a Pen which has baffled the boldest Champion of Slavery,†1 and expos'd the Sophistry of a more Refind Scheme of Persecution,†2 shou'd fly to You for Protection: and beg of You once more to reasume your pen, and vindicate the Liberty of Ocasionall Communion from the Objections of a last Week's
― 134 ―
Author:†1 who is but a Second to two other Adversarys, over whom you have had a glorious triumph.†2 Forgive me Sir for saying you have done all this, since I say it, because I think no other capable to have done it. Take then Sir an honor reserv'd for you, and dont deny a Body of People your Aid, who make it their Suit, because they know there's none so Capable to manage it to the Silencing and the Shame of their Enemys.
I'm sensible this is not a fit petition for a Single man to subscribe: and tho' I'm not sure Numbers will Justify it, yet I know when it comes in their name, I shall at least be skreen'd from the Censure to which such a Presumption wou'd expose me; and can be only thought to have acted the part of their Secretary, and of a Man who is with all imaginable respect
Sir
Your most obedient Servant
J: S:
London November 30th. 1703.
Endorsed by Locke: J. S 30 Nov 03 Answered Dec. 18

3395. Peter King, later first Baron King, to Locke, 2 December 1703 (3393, 3397)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 12, ff. 220-1. Answers no. 3393; answered by no. 3397.
Inner Temple. Decemb. 2. 1703.
Sir
I have been at Mr Pawlings to look out the things you desired, but he not being at home, I could not come at them, I will mind what you write, and either send them to you, or bring them down with me, Mr Collins is no Barister,†3 I believe he will come with me, If I Let him know when I come down—I have acquainted Sir Thomas Frankland with what you writ, he will mind the officer, but he saith, that you must pay in the country the port to London, that is to say 2d. for a single and 4d for a double letter—As for
― 135 ―
mr Hopwood he doth not deal ingenuously with me—I†a- Sold your Malt tickets at 12 l. 05s. per Ticket, which makes up about 5 l. per Cent. Interest,-†a They cost you 12 l. a ticket and are now fallen to 11 l. 18s. a ticket, They must of necessity rise again, unless all be lost, That which you now give 119 l. for will about August 1706 bring in about 148 l. I believe till we can find a morgage, It will be for your advantage to buy in Malt Tickets again, They are likely to be more profitable than any thing else, but I will not do any thing of this nature without your Order, If you will leave it to me, I will do as for my self, and as I would do, were it my own— Melancholy news every day by reason of this storm, It did not reach the North but hath ravaged in the West especially in Devonshire and Somersetshire—I am
Sir
Your Most affectionate Cosin and humble Servant.
P. KING.
Endorsed by Locke: P: King 2 Dec 03 Answered 4
3396. Edward Clarke to Locke, 2 December 1703 (3298, 3402)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 6, f. 147. Printed in Rand, pp. 591-2. Answered by no. 3402.
London December the 2d. 1703.
Deare Sir
I am out of countenance that I have been 10. dayes in Town, and not tendred you my service, nor made you acquainted with the state of my own, my Wifes, and my Childrens Healths, which you have bin allwayes with soe much Kindnesse and Affection soe heartily concern'd for, But indeed the constant hurry I have been in ever since I came to Town, and my steady Attendance on the House, with the Interruption occasion'd by the late violent Storm, and the Consequences of it, will I hope in some measure Excuse mee to you, tho I shall never forgive my selfe, for not makeing a particular inquiery of your selfe by Letter after your health, notwithstanding I have had a pretty good Account of it by Sir Francis Masham, of whom I have dayly inquiered after it, and hope the Answer to this will give mee satisfaction therein,
― 136 ―
by a Confirmation of the Good account I have lately Received from Him and others in that Poynt, and shall heartily Pray for the continuance thereof in all Respects etc:
From Chipley you have the most hearty wishes of my Wife, and yours, as well as all there for your health, And desires to mee to assure you That Mrs Clarke is much in the same state of Health as when you last saw Her, And that all my Children are in perfect Health, All desireing to have theire humble services presented to you and my Lady, and the rest of theire Freinds at Oates, and I am sure I shall bee Quarrell'd with for not doeing of it sooner; I thank God, by following your good Advise, my health is in a great measure Restored to mee, soe that I am now able to doe you any Service you shall command mee, tho at present a little dissabled by the sad Account I had the last Post from my Wife of the greate Dammage done mee by the late violent Storm, which I beleive will not amount to lesse then fower hundred pounds, But since Providence has preserved my Wife, my Children, and my whole Family from any Personall hurt, I Blesse God and am Thankfull, what ever other Dammage I may have sustained, To Discend to particulars would bee tedious and troublesome, and therefore shall reserve That 'till I can have the satisfaction of waiteing on you, which I intend with the first opportunity, in the mean time, desire you to make my services Acceptable to my Lady, and bee assured that I am
your most Affectionate and most obleiged servant
EDW: CLARKE
I have noe News to send you but what you will find in the Prints, But heartily congratulate the miraculous Preservation of Mr. Masham at Sea:†1
Endorsed by Locke: E: Clarke 6 Dec 03 Answered 6
― 137 ―
3397. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 4 December 1703 (3395, 3398)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 318-19. Passages printed in King, p. 261. Answers no. 3395.
Oates 4 Dec 03
Dear Cosin
I am sorry to find by yours of 2d that the things at Mr Pawlings will give you the trouble of going twice thither. Mr Collins is a very good man and if it be as you say I guess I shall have the benefit of his good company here with you and you the benefit of it here and on the road too. If Sir Clously Shovil and the men of Warr that went out of the downs with him are lost,†1 and the storm has that effect upon us and the Dutch that the King of Spain cannot goe between this and Christmas to Portugal as was concerted, what other thing can be reasonable to be done but to keep ready money by you for any exigence that may happen? There you have in short my measures. And therefore I am not sorry for Mr H—ds†2 disingenuity and that you have at present mony in your hands and not tickets. I would not I confess part with a penny, for parchment or paper securitys of any kind till I could see what is like to come of this terrible shock. If it leave the maritime powers well upon their leggs to goe on with the common cause then use your discretion. If they should be disabled, (which god forbid) must not the securitys you speak of sink? and then will not mony be hard to be got any where? and may not a man who is rich in summs due to him even upon unquestionable securitys starve for want of money? When that fear is over (and I think you cannot be long in suspense) I say again, use your discretion. Pray when you come hither doe me the favour to bring me twenty Guineas.
My Lady and the rest of our fires side give you their service. I am
Dear Cosin
Your most affectionate Cosin and humble servant
J LOCKE
If anyone bring you a parcell of Chocolate for me pray let it come
― 138 ―
with my other things or with you or some way or other you shall not fare the worse for it whilst you are here
Address: For Peter King Esqr member of Parliament. To be left at Mr Potmans in the Middle Temple London Frank.
Postmark: DE 6
Endorsed by King: Mr Locke Decemb. 4. 1703.
3398. Peter King, later first Baron King, to Locke, 4 December 1703 (3397, 3401)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 12, ff. 222-3. Answered by no. 3401.
Inner Temple. Decemb. 4. 1703.
Sir
I hope now to wait on you in a little time, when I shall inform you what I meant by my new scene,†1 It being not convenient to say any thing of it by letter.—I am like to be offered a morgage for 1000 l. of 800 Million Lottery tickets, of a Talley of 300 l. and of a Mannour, where all The Tenements are granted out for lives and the head rents reserved are not above 4 l. or 5 l. per annum but the Reversion is worth 1000 l.—I believe it will be a good security, but however I do not think to medle with it without your order, which I desire you to do forthwith—I am
Sir
Your Most affectionate Cosin and Servant.
P. KING.
Endorsed by Locke: P: King 4 Dec 03 Answered 6
3399. Dr. Robert Pitt to Locke, 4 December 1703 (3378, 3408)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 17, ff. 185-6.
Sir
I am very glad Mr Marsham is more easy of his Pains. At the present you may ease him of the purgings, which lately wrought pretty largely. The Calomel always intends†2 the operation of
― 139 ―
Purgatives: and frequently move a Vomit or 2, especially of Phlegm. I desire He will continue the Alteratives. But what thinke you at this time of giving 4 spoon fulls sometime in the morning and afternoon of an Ounce of the Barke infus'd in a Quart of Canary, shaken sometimes, and pour'd of when us'd from the subsidence? It may be of the greatest service to him. With my humble service to Him and my Lady I am
Your most humble servant
ROB: PITT
Dec. 4. 1703.
Address: For Mr John Locke [at Oates, by Joslyn, Bishops Stortford]
Postmark: DE 4
Endorsed by Locke: Dr Pitt 4 Dec 03 Answered Jan 17
3400. Elizabeth Clarke to Locke, 4 December 1703 (3347, 3430)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 6, ff. 167-8. Printed in Rand, p. 592.
Chipley December the 4th: 1703:
Dear Sir
You oblige Me by your Letters more than you can imagin, and your Last, gave me an extraordinary Satisfaction, bringing Me an Account that Your descent (which word, is notwithstanding Melancholy,) is gentle; May you with as much health as you can wish, be Long, e'r'e you Come to your Periode.
My perticular inquiry is at present, how You, and the rest of the family escaped the Most Dreadfull Storm that perhaps was ever known in England; I hope Well, but indeed the Sad Storys I daily hear, of the Destruction it has Made on Person's, as well as Churches, Houses, Trees, Mow's, etc. I owne it makes Me Shake to thinke what are become of My Distant friends, from whom I cannot hear as yet, amongst which number You are not the Least of My Concern. The Damage Done in this Country is very great, and of the number of Those that have Lost their Lives, The Bishopp and his Lady (of Bath and Wells)†1 are two. May you have
― 140 ―
escaped this danger, And May You never be Molested by any, is the Sincere Wish of
Sir
Your most affectionate friend and humble Servant
ELIZ: CLARKE
All our Service waites on you, Sir Francis, My Lady, and the rest of the good Family.
Address: To John Locke Esqr at Oates
Endorsed by Locke: El: Clarke 4 Dec. 03
3401. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 6 December 1703 (3398, 3405)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 320-1. Answers no. 3398.
Oates 6 Dec. 03
Dear Cosin
You will by what I writ you by the last post see how little I am disposed to lend any money just at this time. Unlesse perhaps a morgage of £1000 or under, very tempting, offerd it self. That which yours of the 4th mentions is most upon publique funds and if I venture my money upon such sort of security I like better what you say in your former concerning malt tickets. For the purchaseing of them does some what reward the risque by the greater advantage of profit. But I thinke as I said in my last that it may be best to stay and looke about a little at least till we have news from Holland how they scaped, if you think otherwise pray write me word. This is in hast in answer to yours of the 4th which I received just now. I am glad I shall see you here in a little time. I am
Dear Cosin
your most affectionate Cosin and humble servant
J LOCKE

Frank.

3402. Locke to Edward Clarke, 6 December 1703 (3396, 3406)
B.L., MS. Locke b. 8, no. 158. Badly faded. Printed in Rand, pp. 593-4. Answers no. 3396; answered by no. 3406.
Oates 6 Dec. 03
Dear Sir
At a time when one could hear noething on all hands but sad storys it was noe smal satisfaction to me to receive from you the good news of the recovery of your health. Tis what I have long heartily wished and desired And it was a charitable kindeness to give me an assurance of a thing you knew would be so pleaseing to me to divert my thoughts a little from the sad objects it had every where else. I shall not so far indulge my owne melancholy or furnish matter to yours as to trouble you with the reflections which the ravage made by this storm at sea and land does suggest. I rather choose to congratulate to you the happy escape of your wife children and the rest of your family from the danger the Hirocane threatned.†a Tis a deliverance that ought to be rejoycd in with thankfulness as you doe. and I am very much rejoycd to find you make so small an account of the other great dammages done you by this tempest, since it satisfies me you are got clear of that distemper which over whelmd you when you parted from me last. This makes me the <m>ore†b sensible of the favour I shall receive in the visit <you>†b intend me, When you will restore to me the enjoyment <o>f†b my whole freind again. For the last time methought I had but the outside and shell, the better half was away and wanting.†1 I returne you my thanks for the kind offer you make me of doeing me any service. I shall thereupon presume to beg the trouble of you to Pay Mrs Smithsby twelve pounds and eleven shillings for me which is mony she has laid out for things I wanted here. You need not at all constrain your self about it, any time betwixt this and Christmas will be time enough, when you find it most convenient for you, goeing to or comeing from the house, to call at her lodging.
When you write to Chipley I must beg you to return my service and thanks to your Wife for her kind remembrance of me. But that
― 142 ―
is not all you must say to mine, I received an obleigeing letter from her of 13th of October.†1 And a post or two after the receit of it I returnd her my thanks in an answer I writ her. It was addressed to you and since your letter makes noe mention of it I conclude it lost. P<ray>†a in your next to Chiply let her know that though I am gr<own>†a one of the lazyest creatures in nature, yet I could not forbear returning her my acknowledgments for the great civilities and kindness I found in her letter. She being assured of this she has noe loss by the miscariage of m<ine>.†a
My†b Lady returns you by me her thanks to you and Mrs Clarke and the <rest>†c that soe kindly remember her and says she hopes you will give her the oportunity of doeing it by word of mouth to you your self here at Christmas at which time she hopes she shall have the happiness of your company. I am
Dear Sir
your most humble and most faithful servant
J LOCKE
My service to the Batchelor
Address: For Edward Clarke Esquire member of Parliament. To be left at Richards Coffee house within Temple Bar London Frank
