3403. Samuel Locke to Locke, 10 December 1703 (3281, 3476)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 14, ff. 223-4.
Sir
The inclosed from my Son,†2 as it came open so I sende it you: who I persieve doth Entertayn you with som Indian occurrences. and would be glad if he could Render you any manner service. I know hee highly esteems your lines in which please to let him have your Commands, wherewith, if you please to honnour him; his
― 143 ―
Brother (my second son Samuel)†1 will be the bearer. who God permiting is upon his departure in 8. or 10 days for India by the ship Rochester (newly lanched) burden 360 ton 30 Guns. on whome my said Sam is to goe Chief Supra Cargo. on a stock which cost about £15000: with which hee goes a tradeing voyage and hope to Returne within 20 months tyme:†2 Tis on the separate Stock:†3 and goes on a verry hopefull experrimented voyage on which at the perswation of good friends I was prevaild to send him: who for divers years hath made it his buysinesse to be verry well verst in all India affairs and commoditys: I hope God will blesse his undertakeings. Tis not unlikely but that hee may bring home his Brother John with him: which if please God I may Live to see; will be no small comfort to mee.
Sir I hope you will excuse my presumtion in troubling you with these Remote matters. It would much Rejoyce me the continuance of your good health. I ad my hearty Respects. and am
Sir
Your affectionate Cossin and servant
SAM LOCKE
London 10th December 1703.
Sir I have ordred to sende you some of the best white wine that is to be had, though not so good as I could wish: About 2 months hence hope to recieve som curiouse wines both white and Red.
Address: To the Honorable John Locke at Oates.
Endorsed by Locke: S: Lock 10 Dec. 03 Answered 14

3407. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 15 December 1703 (3405, 3409)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 324-5. A sentence is printed in King, p. 259; it is interpolated there in the rendering of no. 3216.
Oates 15 Dec 03
Dear Cosin
I very much thank you for your news of yesterday. My Lord Peterborow and my Lady and Lord Mordant with them were with me here Monday morning†1 but staid not much above an hour but went away at 12 that my Lord might be in town the next day. I was much in pain about their geting to town now the days are so short, your letter saying no thing of them makes me presume they got safe it would else have made a noise. Never the less I must beg you to wait upon my Lady to thank her and my Lord for the extraordinary honour they have done me and let them know I have desired you to wait on them to know how they doe after their journy and whether they got well to town. and tell her Ladyship that tis some consolation for not being able to keep them that afternoon from the trouble and danger of an ill late journey, that my Lords goeing was not in vain.†2 Pray make my complements also to my Lord Mordant in the house if you meet him not when you wait on my Lady
Pray tell me in your next how your stomach stands to malt tickets or the like?
My Lady gives you her service and thanks. I am
Yours
J L
I think every day with satisfaction on the approaching time that I shall see you and your companions my service to them

Dear Cosin
Your haveing taken Bishop Starford coach for Saturday next week (as you write me in your last) means I hope that you have taken it wholy to your selves to bring you directly hither, this being as near a way to Bishop Starford (as I have heard Sir Francis often say) as by Harlow.†1 If you are to be set down at Harlow, you will, I conclude, let Sir Francis know it, if he comes before you, that we may know here what farther care is to be taken to bring you hither. 3410. Robert (later Sir Robert) Eyre to Locke, 21 December 1703 (3411)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 8, ff. 88-9. The writer, 1666-1735, was the eldest son of Sir Samuel and Lady (Martha) Eyre (nos. 316, 2552, etc.). He was M.P. for Salisbury from 1698 to 1708. He was appointed solicitor-general in 1708; judge in 1710, when he was knighted; Lord Chief Baron 1723; Lord Chief Justice of the Common Pleas 1725: D.N.B. See also no. 3086.
Most Honoured Sir
I receiv'd your first Letter and the Paper enclos'd with all the respect due to such a particular mark of your favour and confidence, and had return'd it long ago if the trust you design to raise had not requir'd a more than ordinary care and a very nice consideration to set it clear of all future dispute; You have gone as far towards it as 'twas possible for one who had not made the law his particular profession, and with some few alterations it will stand right upon your Scheme, But I was unwilling to return the paper, hoping there was no immediate occasion for it, 'till I had review'd, more than once, the alterations I had presum'd to make, and therefore took the Liberty to keep it by me longer than I should have done in case it had requir'd a present dispatch. This morning I intended to have deliver'd it to Sir Fr: Masham who told me he was to go into the Country about Christmass; But am forced to acquaint you, with the Utmost confusion, that I miss the paper in the place where I expected to have found it, and have not been able to recover it yet, which is a misfortune that gives me all the uneasiness imaginable, and when I consider whose concern it is, and that you have kept no Copy of the paper, I can never sufficiently
― 149 ―
lament the loss of it; I never had any accident of this kind before, and therefore hope it may be retriev'd, but if it should prove otherwise, I am so well appriz'd of the matter, that I can make a new draught of it, which I will certainly do, and send it you by the next post. In the mean time I humbly beg that you will not impute this misfortune to any disrespect, or negligence in your service; but continue the good opinion you have been pleas'd to entertain of me, which will always be esteem'd as a great Honour, by
Sir
Your most humble and most obedient servant
ROBERT EYRE
21 December 1703.
Address: For John Locke Esquire [at Oates, by Joslyn, Bishops Stortford] Frank R: Eyre
Postmark: DE 21
Endorsed by Locke: R: Eyre 21 Dec. 03
3411. Robert (later Sir Robert) Eyre to Locke, 23 December 1703 (3410)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 8, ff. 90-1.
Most Honoured Sir
I am asham'd and blush to say that I can't recover your Paper; But I have endeavour'd to repair the loss as well as I can, by sending you here enclos'd another Draught of the Trust you design, as well as I could recollect it; and I hope it will hit your meaning; At least, the Draught will furnish you with the Legal form, under which, you will express it much better than I can: But before I take leave, I must beg you once more to believe, that nothing but the greatest regard to your service made me keep your paper so long, and 'twas meer accident that has taken it from me I know not how: for I shall always think your service a matter of the greatest weight and consequence to my own satisfaction, being deeply sensible of your many favours to my Father and the Family, and the particular Honour you have done me by your late Correspondence. If I might presume to hope for your pardon, and that you would give yourself the trouble of another Letter; I would
― 150 ―
desire to be further serviceable, if there be occasion; I heartily wish the recovery of your Health, and am with great respect
Sir
Your most obliged, most humble and most obedient servant
ROBERT EYRE
23 December 1703.

3413. Sir Walter Yonge to Locke, 24 December 1703 (2681, 3492)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 23, ff. 176-7.
London December 24th. 1703.
Sir
The only reason why I have so long delayed my Answer to your kind letter brought me by Mr King, was the hopes I have hitherto had that I might find your note of the price of the books you were so kind to buy for me in Holland,†1 and consequently might be enabled to return you an answer to your proposition in the way you make it; But all my search hitherto having proved ineffectuall, I dispair of finding that mislaid paper and am therefore unwilling to leave you longer without such an answer as I can give it. And in the first place give me leave to assure you that I never looked upon you as my Debtor for those books, I knew they were in the best hands that could be, I am much more indebted to you for the Friendship you have so long exprest for me, and shall be very glad to make a present to you of those books, if you please to accept of them. But if that be not agreable to you, I shall then be as ready to accept the other part of your proposall and pay you what you have disbursed for the continuation of those Journal's des sçavans, which you have since collected, and you may please to return Les entretiens d'Ariste et d'Eugene†2 with the Sentimens de Cleante upon it.†3 Sir, I hope those Friends who have the Opportunity of being some times with you may yet have the pleasure and advantage of your good conversation for a long time, and that we who are placed at a distance, may have the Happiness of hearing sometimes of you. That you may be continued amongst us (as long as God see's fitt) for the good of Mankind, and be for ever Happy is the sincere prayer of
Sir
Your very affectionate friend and most humble servant
WALTER YONGE
Address: For John Locke Esqr. At Oat's in Essex These
Endorsed by Locke: Sir W. Yonge 24 Dec. 03 Answered 4 Mar

3414. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 5 January 1704 (3409, 3415)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 328-9. Answered by no. 3419.
Oates 5 Jan 03/4
Dear Cosin
I hope you and your good company got well to town, I thank you for makeing me so many holydays. Our Ladys here are mightily in distress for want of Saturday last's news papers they expecting in them all the particularitys of the interview, enterteinment, and other famous acts and deeds relateing to the King of Spain.†1 Tis desired therefore that your man would look after them to get them sent if they were neglected by Saturday post and the post since, and to send them yet if they have not been sent already
Looking over your draught†2 since you were here I find these following different expressions
1. That they the said A B and C and the Survivors or Survivor of them shall put out at interest etc
2 That then the said A B and C and the Survivors or Survivor of them their or his assignes shall convey assign transfer and pay etc.
3 That the said A B and C the Survivors and Survivor of them their or his assignes shall convey etc
4 That then my said Trustees shall pay the Interest etc.
Pray tell me now whether all these several expressions are equivalent and if they be which of them is the best forme to be used. If they be of different purport pray be at the pains to explain them a little to me that I may know how to apply them right and use them in their due places. I have been long of opinion that I did not in Lawyers language understand the difference between And, and Or and you confirme me in it by your useing them as it seems to me promiscuously as you will find by compareing the second and third of the formes I have sent you, wherein I have marked Or and And as I think soe used. I doe not say this as a Critick to laugh at the exactness of the forensick stile which I have sometimes heard the men of the robe†3 sometimes boast of. But I doe it
― 154 ―
to write right and unexceptionably in my present case. I hope monday was noe loss to you. I am
Dear Cosin
your most affectionate Cosin and humble servant
JOHN LOCKE
All our fires side salute you.
Address: For Peter King Esqr member of Parliament. To be left at Mr Potmans in the Middle Temple London
Endorsed by King: January. 5. 1703. Mr Locke.
3415. Peter King, later first Baron King, to Locke, 6 January 1704 (3414, 3416)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 12, ff. 224-5. Answered by no. 3418.
London January. 6. 1703.
Sir
When I was at Oates I did but just hint to you a certain matter, which I will now explain more at large—Mrs H—n is to be married to an Herefordshire Gentleman,†1 and a friend of yours hath a proposal of the like kind Made to him of the daughter of Mr Seys a Glamorganshire Gentleman the son of Serjeant Seys,†2 The Gentleman hath two sons and this daughter who is near eighteen years old, an agreable woman, thriftily and carefully bred, and against whome your friend hath no objection but likes her very well, She hath for some time liv'd in Town with a Maiden Aunt, who is rich, and promiseth, that if she dys before her Niece, she will particularly and principally consider her, but will not engage her self to any thing, The fortune that is proposed to be given with this Lady is four thousand pounds, and the like summ is expected from your friend,—Your friend thinks that tho, perhaps by waiting he may meet with a greater fortune, yet it will be a question, whether ever he shall meet with a woman and other family Circumstances that will better please him, However he is determined not to proceed herein without your approbation, and therefore for that reason this letter is writ to lay this matter before you, and to
― 155 ―
desire your thoughts and opinion hereon, which is desired to be had by the first opportunity—I Do Believe that if this affair succeeds, the Aunt, who is now in Lodgings, will be willing to live with her Niece, which It may be may not be amiss, Your Thoughts are desired thereon, and Your friend hopes That This being a matter of so great Concern, You will excuse him in laying this before you, and give him your directions and opinion thereon—
The Parliament is not like to sit long, but however will do all its busyness, before it rises, If no heats arise after the calling over of the house,†1 when the house is full, I hope things will go on smoothly, The Observator is got out of the way, but still writes on,†2 They are printed twice a week, That which came out yesterday is herewith sent you—I am Sir
Your Most affectionate Cosin and Humble Servant.
P. KING.
I have made your Compliment to the Bishop of Worcester by his son†3 —You may see Dr Mills, if you please, Assoon as I have the book I will send you the variæ lectiones on the .1. Rom.†4 —I Believe the Bishop expects again the Chronology of our Saviours life†5 that Mr Collins left with you, he hath but that one fair Copy, which he hath promised to lend to my Lord Pembrook, If it be too big to send by one letter by post, You may put it into two, and so It will come free to me—They will not allow above two Ounces free—The Bishop makes our Saviour to have been at five Passeovers,†6 —I believe a proper letter from you to the Bishop on this occasion would be very acceptable, and do you no disservice.
Endorsed by Locke: P: King 6 Jan. 0 3/4 Answered 10

3417. Edward Spranger to Locke, 7 January 1704 (3356, 3420)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 18, f. 184.
Sir
I was butt very little in London last terme butt in that little time I was there enquired for Captaine Thompsons widowe att the house of one that married his daughter and there I heard shee was in London and lodged in Fleetstreet butt the servants being
― 157 ―
onelie in the way and not the master could not tell mee where to find her, butt I left A letter with directions for her to goe to Mr King and hoped shee had observed my directions, I will knowe next weeke whether shee bee in London or in the Countrey, if in London shee shall have further directions to waite upon Mr King, if in the Countrey I will make A stepp to her which will bee butt 5 or 6 hours riding. Thus with all service and thanks I wish you A happy new yeer and many of them and am
Sir
your most humble servant

3418. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 10 January 1704 (3416, 3419)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 332-3. Answers no. 3415; answered by no. 3419.
Oates 10 Jan 03/4
Dear Cosin
I wish Mr Collins or you had plainly made me understand when the book was put into my hands that my Lord Bishop of Worcester expected it back again. I should then have made use of it better to the purpose for which I imagin it sent me, and returnd it by you. Whereas now not apprehending how he could have but this one fair copy I presumed this given me and accordingly, I read it superficialy at broken times resolveing to set apart some time afterwards to study and examin it very exactly as I thought the worke of so great a master in that sort of learning as his Lordship is deserved. His favour I acknowledg was great either way whether he gave it me or sent it me onely to peruse, and I must not make that ill use of it however coviteous I am of it to keep it a moment longer than he allows. The way you propose of sending it by the post I think not safe for it will not goe in lesse than three packets and if any one of those miscarry the whole is spoild, besides that the folding will injure the book. I have therefore not taken that course haveing the hopes of sending it entire by a safe hand from
― 158 ―
hence within three posts. But if my Lord will not stay till I have the oportunity I expect but will have it ventured by the post I will punctualy obey your commands as soon as I receive them and in the meantime keep it safe in my study.
The Gent you mention knows the relations, likes the Lady, and is pleased with the family circumstances so that to all this there can be no thing said by one who is wholy a stranger to every particular of it. The onely question that remains is about the Aunt, and the management of that depends upon the knowledg of her character. whether she be to be pressed to any positive promise or to be left to her self without saying a word cannot be said but by one who is well acquainted with her and can make some judgment from th<e>nce†a what is to be expected. The best advice I can give the Gent upon the whole matter is that he get some freind to debate and transact for him, For in all such affairs there are great inconveniencys for the person himself to manage the negotiation, wherein he cannot in many cases that will arise either say or doe with any grace what is fit to be said or done. One thing I think will be of great consequence viz to sound the yonge ladys minde, and see whether she will be content to set out privately and in a meaner way at first. If she comes with an expectation of a coach and equipage and shew at first, it will have one of these ill consequences, That either she will be discontented to find her self disappointed, which is a dangerous begining: or else our†b freind†b may be engagd in too great a way of expense before he†c- sees where he is,-†c which afterwards he†d cannot quit, and so will drag on uneasily. Another thing I would advise him to take care of in the setlement upon her children. Let the quantum in the whole be what is convenient, But let him keep the power in himself to prefer, which of their common children he thinks fit, to the greater portion.
There is a messenger from London expected here who returns this week by him I intend to send the Bishops book. If he fails Tom the footman goes to town next weeke, and he shall bring it you.
I hope you have found the key of the Standish right
Pray in your next send me in short the forme of a Codicille. must it be sealed? When I had <wr>it†a so far I received your answer about And and Or but not to my main question in that letter
― 159 ―
which was which was the best forme†a to be used all through. You say nothing every trade I see has its mysterys. I am
Dear Cosin
your most affectionate cosin and servant
J LOCKE
Address: For Peter King Esqr member of Parliament. To be left at Mr Potmans in the middle Temple London
Frank.
Postmark: IA 12
3418A. Mrs. Mary Clarke to Edward Clarke, 10 [January] 1704
B.L., MS. Locke b. 8, no. 159. Printed in Rand, pp. 594-5. Mrs. Clarke's date is obviously wrong; the month from the postmark and Clarke's endorsement. Clarke does not allude to the contents of this letter in his letter to Locke of 13 January (no. 3421) and it is unlikely that they were ever communicated to him. Locke's latest extant letter to Mrs. Clarke is dated 19 August 1699: no. 2611.
December the 10th. 1703/4;
My Deare
I receved yours with the Cirtificate for J Lane which I have delivered to J Spreat to Convay to him as he desired, etc: I was in hopes your Journey to otes where you sed you was soe earnestly invited had bin in order to some new proposealls, pray was there not one word sed of that matter; and how stands the a faire of mr Boulstrode and mr. Collins.†1 I should be Glad to know where mr Frek have ever sed any thinge to you of the Gentleman he writt of in the summer and who it was; I heartily wish there would some body or other offer that you and she Could both aprove off, for methinks she begins to Grow past the best and soe Conceited with-all that I fear it ont mend.
I hope you will pardon me If I have sed any thinge in this or my last letter that I should have kept to my selfe; but it is very hard to have a Great deale of all sorts of troble and noe body to
― 160 ―
speake to, therefore it will burst out sometimes, doe what I can that am
your Truley affecttionate and Faithfull wife
M CLARKE
Pray what is become off Ward that I heare nothing of him my Blessing to my Children and my service to my Frends it is Extreeme Cold, but fine wether heare;
my Cozen tom Cross†1 his wife is heare and lookes for her Husband every day to Come and feth her;

3419. Peter King, later first Baron King, to Locke, 13 January 1704 (3418, 3423)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 12, ff. 226-7. Excerpt printed in Campbell, Lives of the Lord Chancellors, iv. 578, with wrong date 13 June 1703. Answers nos. 3414 and 3418; answered by no. 3423.
London January. 13. 1703.
Sir
I thank you for your last letter, and the several kind hints in it, I believe the Aunt will not come under any legal obligation for futurity, but she promiseth well, As to the Young Lady, She hath wit and sense, and will, I believe, be very easy in all those things you mention—I have tried the Key of the Standish in the presence of Mr Clark and Mr Collins, who will both inform you that the Key will not do, being too big, If you please I will send you the Key by the post, or by Thomas when he returns—I will take care to satisfy the Bishop of Worcester about his Copy till you have a Conveniency to send it, He will be here with me Saturday morning next. As to the And or Or The Best form is The Said A B and C. the Survivors or Survivor of them, Their or his assigns—As for
― 161 ―
a Codicill there is no certain form, any thing to show the partys Intent is sufficient, this following form is a good one—I A.B. being of sound and perfect mind do this day of in the year make this Codicill, viz. I give unto And I desire that this my Codicill may be annexed to my above will (or foresaid will) and taken as part thereof, and do not hereby intend to revoke or alter my said will farther or otherwise than as in and by these presents the same is altered, but as to all other matters in my said will contained and not hereby altered, I do hereby ratify and Confirm the same—There is no necessity to seal the Codicill, but it is advisable to seal it, and have 3 witnesses.
I have got 40 Malt Tickets for you at 11 l. 16s. 00d. which comes to about 500 l. I hope to get other tickets at the same price for the residue—tho, I am not without hopes to get a good security— The House of Lords have made rampant votes against the Commons,†1 which I will send you by the next, as also the Ballad on the Conformity bill†2 —I am Sir
Your most obliged Cosin and Servant
P. KING.
Endorsed by Locke: P. King 13 Jan 03/4 Answered 17

3421. Edward Clarke to Locke, 13 January 1704 (3406, 3436)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 6, f. 150. At the time of writing Clarke had received Mrs. Clarke's letter of 10 January: no. 3418A.
London January the 13th. 170 3/4.
Deare Sir
I acknowlidge the Favour of yours by the last Post, and should most readily have sent down your Mapp in the manner Proposed in your Letter, had I sent down a Messenger for the Mastive-Dogg Sir Francis gave mee, as was once thought of, But Mr. Harrison at Harlow has Directions from mee to send a Messenger from thence to fetch the dogg from Oates to Harlow, and then to send Him up from thence by the Dunmow-Waggon which comes every Weeke to London, soe that I cannot convey your Mapp to you in the Method you proposed, but shall gladly doe it some other Way, as soon as I can find an opportunity etc:
The Lords have this day Voted an Addresse to bee made to Her Majestie upon Heads directly contrary to what was contayned in the last Addresse from the House of Commons,†1 whereby I thinke it will bee rendred impracticable for this Parliament ever to meete againe
The inclosed Speech is said to have bin made by the Earle of Peterborow;†2 I am,
Yours intirely,

3422. Anthony Collins to Locke, 15 January 1704 (3391, 3435)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 7, ff. 12-13. Answered by no. 3435.
Lincolns Inne. Jan. 15/03
Sir
I am arriv'd to that degree of Esteem for you, and Gratitude for the greatest favour I can receive, the continuance of your Friendship which my late visit to you gave me an opportunity of experiencing, that I can easily satisfy my self that you believe me to be so constantly sensible of my obligations to you, that I need not make an Apology for this tardy acknowledgment of what I have allways upon my mind. I build my assurance on my sincerity, which I cannot suspect but you must discover, though disguis'd by awkward habits which one less penetrating into human nature than your self might not.
I have here sent your Dr Geddes's Church Hist.†2 and the Annotat. in V. Test etc,†3 and with 'em a Book which the Author of it presents you with.†4
― 164 ―
There is nothing publish'd of late in England worth acquainting you with.
Wee had lately from Holland Mr Le Clercs Bibl. Choisie 3d Tom. Grotius de Iure B. et Pacis cum Comment: Vander Meulen in 3 vol in folio,†1 and a promise of a Thesaurus Antiq: et Histor: Italiæ,†2 a project of Grævius's by way of continuation to his Thesaur: Rom: Antiquitatum.
Mr Clark gives you his service, and desires me to acquaint you that the speech he lately enclos'd in a letter to you was made by the Lord Haversham and not by that Noble Peer to whom by Misinformation he attributed it.
You have my wishes for the Continuance of your health, and may be assur'd that your Commands on any occasion will be acceptable to me who am with perfect esteem and sincerity
Your most obligd Humble servant
A. COLLINS
My humble service to the whole Family at Oats. I am obligd to 'em for all their favours.
Address: For John Locke Esq at Oats in Essex.
Endorsed by Locke: A Collins 15 Jan. 0 3/4 Answered 24
3423. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 17 January 1704 (3419, 3425)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 334-5. Answers no. 3419; answered by nos. 3428 and 3429.
Oates 17 Jan 03/4
Dear Cosin
You haveing tried the key in the presence of Mr Clarke and Mr Collins you have two substantial witnesses to convince me by oath if there were need that the key you have is too big. I must confess I am wonderfully amazed at it And must desire you to consider and recollect whether you have not any where changed
― 165 ―
this key by any mistake, since I cannot see how I could mistake it carrying it always by it self in a little purse in my pocket un mixed with any other keys. Another thing I would desire of you is to discribe the standish to me and send me the breadth and the length of the lid of it. In the meantime if Sir Francis or either of his sons†1 or any body else comes down hither I would desire you to send me the key by them sealed up in a letter. If it be so left at Mr Churchils there it will be most in their way.
I counted upon three sure messengers I should have to send the Bishops copy†2 by this weeke who will all fail. But I have light on an other who will hold. Mr John Lukin is here now with his father†3 he has promised I shall see him before he goes which will be some time this week, by him I shall send it. I cannot but wish I had one of them to study, for a single reading though very attentive is not enough for such a treatise, it should be examined, the quotations considerd, and the parts laid together to be master of it and profit by it. But tis not fit I or any body from me should make any such request to the Bishop. You know the persons and the circumstances of the book, what you think you can or in good manners may doe upon your owne interest to procure me one of them I leave to you. Perhaps I should not say so much as this did I not apprehend it will not be to be bought in my life time
I thank you for the forme of a Codicil you have sent me so much just as I wishd. But why must there be three witnesses to a codicill when two serve to a will? I see by your dealing in malt tickets that you have changed your thoughts about the Kings of Sweden and Prussia. Of such purchases pray send me the time when they were made and the number of the tickets. I wish well to anything that may be for your happyness and am
Dear Cosin
your most affectionate cosin and servant
J LOCKE
May it not be adviseable to hear of our Fleets arriveing in Portugal†4 before you deale any farther in Malt tickets Or if the two houses chance to fall out will not that sink the price of those tickets
― 166 ―
etc: and soe it may be best to delay a little. I write in the darke and onely suggest to your thoughts
Address: For Peter King Esqr Member of Parliament. To be left at Mr Potmans in the Middle Temple London Frank.
Postmark: IA 19
Endorsed by King: Mr Locke January 17. 1703.
3424. Benjamin Furly and Arent Furly to Locke 17/28 January 1704 (3286, 3450; 3338, 3503)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 9, ff. 192-3. Forwarded by Francis Limborch on 4 February: pp. 187, 190.
[Benjamin Furly:]
Dear Sir
I hope this will find you restored from the great indisposition I lately understood you laboured under, and should be heartily glad to understand it.
I have taken the liberty to send (for your perusal, if your health will permit it) a letter I have writ to Sir John alias the Laird Swinton, who (since he has been beNighted) it seems has forgot his old acquaintance, that was so tender to him in the day of his adversity.†1
You will therein find the Contents of a letter of his wrote 25 July in answer to mine to him, of the 25 May 1691†2 which you did me the favour to cause to be conveyed to him by a friend of Madam Lockhart, the originall of which letter you were pleasd to keep there, not thinking it fit to be adventured over the sea, It may possibly now be of some service, if not to prove how great my debt is, yet to prove that he lookt upon himself as my debtor, and would be just to me, and do all he could to accomodate my occasions, and consequently that he knew me with a witnesse.
If your health permits not your reading so long an Epistle, and so forrein to your inclinations, as my pain in my right arm, which I have had now nigh 3/mo that I cannot lift it to my head, stretch it right out, nor much less bend it backwards, did not permit me to
― 167 ―
write it with my own hand, be pleasd but to return it to mr Limborgh who will take care to send it forward to Edinburgh.
There is a Grecian calls himself Seraphim,†1 who in his passage through this Town, from England, towards Germany, visited me, and I found to be the most rational of any that I ever met with of that Nation. Who is now gone over to England again, and being stopt at the Briel by contrary winds, all his stock was spent, so he wrote to me to let him have f15—and withall desired my letter to yourself, intending to make you a visit: But it was my ill luck to be in the Hague, so that I could not timely supply him, with either, as otherwise I should, If he come to visit you pray present my respects to him, and let him know what I write.
My wife, self and sons do all present our humble service to your good self, and all that honorable family and particularly to Monsieur Cost remayning
Dear Sir
Your most humble servant
BENJAMIN FURLY
Rotterdam 28 January 1703/4
[Arent:]
Sir
I beg leave to assure you in particular of My humble respects to your Good self and All at Oates. I hope you are recovered of your late indisposition: I am on all occasions
Sir Your Most Humble and Most Obedient Servant
ARENT FURLY
[Written by Benjamin Furly:]
I send by this bearer to mr Limborgh a small thing in Canvas which came from his father. For Sir Francis Masham.

3426. Brook Bridges to Locke, 18 January 1704 (2641)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 4, ff. 155-6.
Sir
I am not a little pleased to understand by yours of the 14th instant, that I have yet a place in your esteeme, and that you think me capable, at least willing, to serve you to my power. I will not forfeit your opinion, and am therefore newly returnd from getting the best information I could at the Bank, in Order to satisfy your demands, for I dare not trust my memory. I am there told, that since the 25. March 1702. the following dividents have been made, vizt
25 March 1702 a Divident of 4.15.0. per Centum proffitts
July 6. a Divident of 5 - - per Centum principall
September 29—a Divident of 4.15.0.proffitts, and of 2.10.0. principall
 
1703. 	March 25.—A Divident of 2.15.0.principall. and of 4.15.0— proffitts 
July. 13. A Divident of 2.10.0.principall
September 29. a Divident of 4.5.0.principall, and 4.15.0. proffitts
Soe that including the divident made 25. March 1702, the proprietors to this time have received 17l. per Centum part of theire principall, and 19l. per Centum on Account of proffitts, in both 36l. per Centum†1
The 100l. Bonds you mention, are I suppose what we call Bank Sealed Bills, with an allowance of 2d. per diem. These are not to bee had at the Bank, they haveing some time since made an Order for issueing none, but in exchang to those, who at the yeares end, would renew them, therefore I know no way of obteyning them now, unless by purchaseing them out of theire hands, who are possest of them, which I believe may bee done at about 2s.6d. a Bill, tho some months since they were bought at 8. and 10d. a Bill. had I any of my own, you should freely comand them, but I seldom keepe any, when exchequer fonds are open, tho
― 170 ―
the interest given of late has not exceeded 5l. per Centum. My wife and sonns joyne in sending you theire services, and in wishing you all health and hapiness, by
Sir
Your very obliged humble servant
B BRIDGES
18. January 1703.

3428. Peter King, later first Baron King, to Locke, 20 January 1704 (3427, 3429)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 12, ff. 228-9. Answers no. 3423.
London January. 20. 1703.
Sir
I am ashamed to tell you, that I mistook the Key, I have now the true Key, which will open the Standish, I will take care to take out what you desire, and send it by the first—I thank you for your care in sending back my mistaken letter—The same Resolutions of the house of Lords†1 with some other busyness was design'd for you by that post, but it seems there was a reciprocal mistake, the paper which you should have had is gone into hands, where it should not have been—The King of Spain with the fleet is come back to Torbay, some say much shatter'd, others say, that there is no great damage, but that he will be going again with the first wind†2 —next post I will write more at large about your other affairs —The Bishop of Gloucester presents you his humble service— As to the number of witnesses to a will or Codicill Two will serve where the devise is only of Chattels, but where it is of lands, there must be three, and therefore I advised to three witnesses, because then there would be certainly witnesses enough, whatever the devise was, It is no harm to have too many, It may be to have too few—
I am Sir
Your most affectionate Cosin and Servant
P. KING.
Endorsed by Locke: P: King 20 Jan 0 3/4 Answered 24
3429. Peter King, later first Baron King, to Locke, 21 January 1704 (3428, 3434)

London January. 21. 1703.
Sir
I have opened your Standish, and found therein only One Key, which was the Key of your square deal box, in which I
― 172 ―
found the thick quarto of Pamphlets bound together in Parchment, Indors'd on the back, Unitarians,†1 and also a manuscript concerning the Conduct of the Understanding†2 —In the deal box there was a bag, wherein are several Keys, but there is not amongst them the Key of the large trunk, that stands in your chamber, so That I could not open that—Sir Francis will be so kind to deliver you the above quarto and manuscript with the Observators†3 you want, which are all made up in one packet, As for the news Sir Francis will give you a better account, than I can, As for the Malt tickets you cannot have an answer before Tuesday next, for that My Cosin Freke will not be in Town before next Munday—I am

3430. Elizabeth Clarke to Locke, 21 January 1704 (3400, 3507)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 6, ff. 169-70. Printed in Rand, pp. 596-7.
Chipley January the 21st: 170 3/4:
Sir
My Joy at the account My Father has given me, of your health is not to be conceal'd, and I owe you more then words can express for your perticular and kind inquiry after me when he was at Oates. Hee likewise informs Me that you are pleas'd to make me another offer of Bookes, which is So great a Mark of your favour that I can no Waies reject it; and yet at the same time I blush to be always on the taking Side. but what Shall I Say! I want not the utmost Sense of, and gratitude for all your extraordinary friendship, and Civility to me. it is then Manifest, that 'tis my misfortune, and not My fault, that I make noe Sutable returns.
You Sir have furnish'd me with most of the bookes I have; it will therefore be needless to Send you a Catalogue of them, in order to your judging what Sort I want; and besides; I remember when I had the honour to see you Last, You persisted in it, that
― 173 ―
I should Chuse for My Selfe. These then, are What I Shall take the Liberty to name, and if you approve them not, I hope you will be so kind as to Correct My judgment. Dr Tillottson's Sermons,†1 Plutarch's Lives,†2 and a Ladys travels into Spaine, which I thinke is intitled Memoirs of the Court of Spaine.†3
Wee hear that the King of Spaine is driven back by contrary winds to Torbay, and tis Said that of 300 and odd Ships that embark't with him, there is no certain knowledge of More than twelve; but 'tis hoped they are all safe. indeed the weather here since Munday Last, has been dreadfull, A perpetual Tempest which still continues, and which nothing can exceed, but the Late terrible Storm on the 27th of November. I pray God to Alay it, and to protect You from all the ill consequences that May attend it. Thinke all I would Say to express My good wishes for you, and be assur'd I am Dear Sir
Your most affectionate humble Servant
ELIZ: CLARKE
My Mothers service in perticular waites on you.
Hers, and my most humble service with the rest of this family attend on My Lady, and all at Oates.
Mrs Masham has my thanks for her Letter.
Address: To John Locke Esqr at Oates
Endorsed by Locke: El: Clarke 21 Jan 0 3/4
3431. Dr. Pieter Guenellon to Locke, 21 January/1 February 1704 (3371, 3554)

d'Amsterdam ce 1 fevr. 1704
Monsieur,
nous avions desja appris avec bien de la douleur que vous etiez fort malade, et en danger de mourir. cela nous a fait beaucoup
― 174 ―
de peine, et quoi que depuis on nous a dit que vous etiez un peu mieu, nous n'avons eté r'assurez que par celle quil vous a plu nous ecrire. Dieu veuille que vos forces s'augmentent de plus en plus, et que vous puissiez survivre cette année et plusieurs autres avec joye et contentement. Ma femme continue sur le bon pied, reste tousjours reglée. le grand froid l'a un peu incommodée, elle eut méme un acces ou deux de fievre; mais Graces a Dieu cela n'a pas eu de suitte. elle continue constamment son infusion de bellis major et son Vitriol de Mars. nous avons recu le second paquet de fleurs, dont nous vous sommes tres redevables. elle aura peine a consumer le tout, beuvant a son ordinaire tres peu, elle s'en rebuteroit facilement, sans la grande foi quelle y a, a votre persuasion. je ne croi pas que l'Opium puisse guerir la cause du mal de sa poitrine, mais je reconnois que le repos que cela luy donne, met la machine en etat de se mieux retablir, et je suis persuadé que dans un semblable cas, ou la nature ne scauroit se retablir qua la longue, il faut necessairement avoir egard au symptomes pressans, comme est celuy de sa tou. j'espere avec vous que si elle r'attrappe l'eté, elle se tirera d'affaire. le ministre Leenhof, dont je vous ay envoyé un petit traitté en flamend, se trouve attaqué de toutes parts de plusieurs Ministres, qui a mon sens s'eloignent de l'etat de la question. il me semble n'avoir eu principalement en vüe que de faire voir en quoy consiste la felicité de cette vie; et eux rapportent tout a celle qui est avenir, et ne s'accommodent pas de sa philosophie.†1
toute la famille vous fait la reverence. Monsieur Veen se porte assez bien pour son age. Ma femme tousjours accablée de vos bien faits, ne scait comment repondre a vos honnestez, et votre lettre obligeante. elle vous demande encore un peu de tems pour vous repliquer. je suis avec un tres profond respect,
Monsieur,
Votre tres humble, tres obeissant et tres obligé Serviteur
P. GUENELLON.
Address: For Mr John Locke at Mr Powlings over against the plough in little Lincolns inne feilds London
Postmark: IA 2—

3433. Dr. Robert Pitt to Locke, [c. 22 January 1704] (3408, 3582)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 17, ff. 189-90. Date from postmark and Locke's endorsement.
Sir
I am glad Mr Massam is pretty well. You may please to conclude the method by Degrees, by giving at greater and greater Distances
― 176 ―
the Purge and the Alteratives in lesser Quantities and seldomer. I have sent a Note to Mr Churchill for the Barke. I will call on him for the price of the Pills, and will acquaint him, that, when you send a Note for any number of Medicines, I will sign it, by which it will instantly be made up. The Lawyers have oblig'd us to this nicety, to obviate the Clamours of the Shops, whose Practice is yet conniv'd at. I am oblig'd to prepare you for the reading a Peice, which will come out next week: the Antidote;†1 The Publick not interposing in the Controveries,†a you will not doubt but the Language as well as the Passions of the Parties will be exasperated. I presume every part of it is true and absolutely necessary to preserve the People and the Profession. I present my humble service to my Lady Massam and Mr Massam. I am
Your most humble servant
R PITT

3434. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 24 January 1704 (3429, 3437)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 340-1.
Oates 24 Jan 03/4
Dear Cosin
Soon after you went hence I writ to Mr Spranger to enquire where Capt Thompsons Representative, (which it seems his Widow is) was to be found. He sent me noe answer to this the cheif business of my letter, but excuses why he waited not upon you concerning this matter in Michaelmas-term last as he promisd me. I writ to him again and then he told me that one Mr Baron a linin Draper just within Algate had maried Captain Thompsons daughter and there was the place to be informed where the Widow was to be found when she was in town. He also told me he had sent a letter to Mr Baron concerning this matter the week before but had not then an answer and that the day before he writ to me he had sent
― 177 ―
him an other with one inclosed for his mother in law desireing her to apoint a day and place for her paying of the interest, whereupon he desires me to suspend my own sending till I heard from him the next, which is now the last weeke. He writ to her that he had order to receive the interest, which is more than I gave him. Intending as he says at the same time to see it paid to you. Since that letter which was dated 13 Instant I have heard noe thing from him. I begin not to like this matter. The money was lent 3d July 1702. There has never been any more interest yet paid but £15 in January 170 2/3. I must therefore desire you to look a litle quick after this matter. I forgot it till the inclosed†1 was sealed which is the reason you have two letters in stead of one. I am
your affectionate cosin and servant
J LOCKE
Address: For Peter King Esqr member of Parliament. To be left at Mr Potmans in the Middle Temple London
Endorsed by King: Mr Locke January 24. 1703
3435. Locke to Anthony Collins, 24 January 1704 (3422, 3438)
The Carl H. Pforzheimer Library, New York. Transcribed from photostat. Printed in A Collection of Several Pieces, pp. 278-80. Answers no. 3422; answered by no. 3438.
Oates 24 Jan 03/4
Sir
Till your confidence in my friendship and freedome with me can preserve you from thinking you have need to make apologies for your silence when ever you omitt a post or two when in your kind way of reconing you judg a letter to be due you know me not so well as I could wish; nor am I so little burdensome to you as I desire. I could be pleased to hear from you every day, because the very thoughts of you every day afford me pleasure and satisfaction. But I beseech you to beleive that I measure not your kindness by your opertunitys of writeing nor doe suspect that your freindship falters when ever your pen lyes a little still. The sincerity you profess, and I am convinced of, has charms in it against all the little phantomes of ceremony. If it be not so that true freindship sets one free from a scrupulous observance of all those little circumstances
― 178 ―
I shall be able to give but a very ill account of my self to my freinds, to whom when I have given possession of my heart I am less punctual in makeing of legs and kissing my hand than to other people to whom that outside civility is all that belongs.
I received the three books you sent me. That which the author sent me deserves my acknowledgment more ways than one and I must beg you to return it. His demonstrations are so plain that if this were an age that followed reason I should not doubt but his would prevail. But to be rational is so glorious a thing, that two legd creatures generaly content them selves with the title, but will not debase so excellent a faculty about the conduct of so trivial a thing as they make themselves†1
I am indebted to Mr Clarke for his care to set me right about the author of the speech though upon the first sight I wa<s>†a throughly satisfied it was not his who was first named
There never was a man better suited to your wishes than I am. You take a pleasure in being troubled with my commissions, and I have no other way of commerce with you but by such importunitys. I can onely say that were the tables changed, I should being in your place have the same satisfaction, and therefore confidently make use of your kind offer. I therefore beg the favour of you to gett me Monsieur le Clerc's harmony of the Evangelists in English printed by Buckley in 4to†2 bound very finely in calf, guilt and letterd on the back and guilt on the leaves. So also I would have Moliere's workes (of the best edition you can get them) bound. These books are for <Ladys>†b and therefore I would have them fine, and the leaves guilt as well as the back. Moliere of the Paris edition I think is the best if it can be got in London in quires.†3 You see the liberty I take. I should be glad you could find out some thing for me to doe for you here. I am perfectly
Sir
Your most humble and most obleiged servant
J LOCKE
Address: For Anthony Collins Esq at his Chamber numb: 7 in Serle Court in Lincolns Inne London
― 179 ―

3437. Peter King, later first Baron King, to Locke, 26 January 1704 (3434, 3439)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 12, ff. 232-3. Answered by no. 3439.
London. January. 26. 1703.
Sir
I Remember to have discoursed with you on the 1 Corinth. 2. 6.7.8. about the Princes of the world, and their wisdome there mentioned, I have met with an Interpretation in a Sermon preach't before the house of Commons in 1645 by Thomas Goodwin,†3 which may possibly please you, viz. that by the princes of the world are meant Caiaphas and the other rulers of the Jews, and also Pilate, and by their wisdome, worldly Interest and designs to preserve their grandeur and power, for god so ordered it by his providence that Reason of State and worldly Interest were the chief motives to those rulers as well Jewish as Heathen to crucify Christ—for the Jewish rulers See 11. John. 49.50. If we let him thus alone, all men will
― 180 ―
believe on him, and the Romans will come and take away both our place and nation, and saith the High Priest, Ye know nothing at all nor consider that it is expedient for us etc. Us Priests, they were jealous that Christ would get the people from them, and that which is here said to be a prophecy by Caiaphas is in the 18 John. 14. said to be his counsell,—and then as for Pilate it was state Interest that caused him to condemn Christ. 19. John. 12.13.—So that in this Text The Princes of the world are not all the Jews, but the rulers of the Jews and Pilate who from a principle of worldly wisdome crucified Christ, which they would not have done, if they had known the wisdome of god—I have not yet had an opportunity of talking with the Bishop of Worcester,†1 but hope to have it in a few days—As to the malt tickets I have an account of—
19. in the 101st. payment at 11l. 16s.each.
17. In the 119.
> at——11l. 13s.†a
5 In the 117.
>There is a bargain made for some others for you, but my Cosin†2 cannot yet get them from the Gent. who sold them to him—I am
Sir
Your Most Affectionate Cosin and humble Servant.
P. KING.
Address: These For John Locke Esquire at Oates.
Endorsed by Locke: P: King 26 Jan 03 Answered 28

Lincolns Inne. Jan 26/03/4
Sir
That I may give you satisfaction for making Apologies, and show how little Ceremonious I am to you, I have sought an occasion to give you some trouble, and will offer no excuse for it. I desire the favour of you to give the inclosed Papers to my Lady Masham from Mr Shute who left 'em with me to put into the first packet I should send to Oats.
― 181 ―
It is but for you to employ me to make me succeed in any thing. I have already met with Molieres works in Quires of the Paris Edition, and I believe the only one in Town. You say you should be glad you could find out something to do for me at Oats. 3439. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 28 January 1704 (3437, 3441)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 342-3. Answers no. 3437.
Oates 28 Jan: 03/4
Dear Cosin
I have the draught you approved and returnd†1 and yours of the 26th. I return you my thanks for both, particularly the remark on the place in the Corinths. Reading lately the history of the passion with attention I was inclined to think that the cheif governors of Judea who had a hand in our Saviours death were meant and soe Pilate ought to be taken in too, The note you have sent me confirms me in that opinion and I take it to be the right. If the way proposd for raiseing of men goes on, consider whether all that are the sons of those who had a right to give their votes in the election of parliament men, or are actualy the menial servant of such should not be exempted; Next whether their obligation to serve should not cease as soon as the warre they are raised for is at an end; and thirdly whether they ought not to be at liberty to leave
― 182 ―
the service and be lookd on to be as free as disbanded as soon as ever they are landed either in England or Ireland. The reasons for these restrictions I think so clear that there need not any explication of them to you.†1 I am
Dear Cosin
Your most affectionate Cosin and humble servant
J LOCKE
I writ to you last post concerning Capt Thompsons representative. I have heard noe thing more from Mr Spranger

3442. Peter King, later first Baron King, to Locke, 29 January 1704 (3441, 3445)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 12, ff. 234-5. Answered by no. 3445.
London January. 29. 1703.
Sir
I have sent my man To Capt.Thomsons' son in law, who is his Executor, he married the Capts. Daughter by a former wife, and there is now a suite in chancery between him and his Mother in law and her children, who claime a thousand pounds out of his estate by vertue of a judgment given for securing that summ, the Judgment, he saith, was given since your morgage, I had no satisfactory answer from him, but hope to have the opportunity next week to speak with him my self—There is six hundred thousand pound to be borrowed upon the Malt at 5l. per Cent.†1 — and an act is passing for taking up mony on the hereditary excise, which I believe will be at 6l. per Cent.,†2 What if you did lay out some mony there,—I Never knew good Land securitys so scarce as now they are, I desire you to let My Lady Masham know that I do mind her concerns with Mr Marton, Mr Coniers told me this afternoon that it should be done—I think it is all safe—
I am Sir
Your most affectionate Cosin and Servant.
P. KING

all Good, the man of home I bought them, tould me, that noe better was In London, pray my Deare Cousin Axept of them, If I could bought better for mony, you should have had them,

Your Faithfull Kinsman And Humble Servant

3445. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 1 February 1704 (3442, 3448)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 349-50. Answers no. 3442; answered by no. 3448.
Oates 1 Feb 03/4
Dear Cosin
By the last post I sent you Mrs Thompsons answer to Mr Spranger and his to me which all does not amout to so much as you learnt there, though that is not itt seems so full as it should be. What is farther to be done in that case I must leave to you
If you think fit to lend upon either of the borrowing funds if the security of the hereditary excise be as good, that security as you state it is certainly to be preferd to the malt as much as 6 is to be preferd to 5. I am for a Land security whenever you can light on any that you approve of. That being my aime I would not have my money out upon any of your borrowing acts unless the tallys can be sold and it be usual to buy and sell them that so upon the offer of a Morgage I may have my mony as it were at a call. Another thing I would have care taken of is not to lend it upon tallys that come in late espetially care is to be taken not to come within the danger of difficiencys. These cautions observed I leave it to your discretion to doe with my money in your hands as you would doe with it were it your own. But as soon as any part of it is disposed of pray let me know the particulars how it is. I am
Dear Cosin
Your most affectionate Cosin and humble servant
J LOCKE

3449. Locke to Anthony Collins, 7 February 1704 (3438, 3456)
B.M., Add. MS. 4290, ff. 28-9. Copy made by or for Desmaizeaux. Printed presumably from this text, or a copy of it, in A Collection of Several Pieces, pp. 281-2. The quotation in no. 3467 shows that some words have been omitted by the copyist. Answers no. 3438; answered by no. 3456.
Oates. 7. Febr. 03/4
Sir
Tis with regret I consider you so long in Essex without enjoying you any part of the time. Essex methinks (pardon the extravagancy, extraordinary passions and cases excuse it) when you are to go into it should all be Oates, and your journey be no wither but thither. But lands and tenements say other things, whilst we have carcasses that must be clothd and fed; and books you know the fodder of our understandings cannot be had without them. What think you, are not those spirits in a fine state that need none of all this luggage, that live without ploughing and sowing, travel as easy as we wish, and inform themselves not by a tiresom rummaging in the mistakes and jargon of pretenders to knowledg, but by looking into things themselves? Sir I forgot you had an estate in the Country, a Library in Town, Freinds every where, amongst which you are to while away as pleasantly I hope as any one of this our planet a large number of years (if my wishes may prevail) yet to come, and am got I know not how into remote visions that help us not in our present state, though they shew us something of a better. To return therefore to my self and you, I conclude by this time you are got to Town again, and then in a little time I shall hear from you etc— I am
Sir
Your—
3450. Benjamin Furly to Locke, 8/19 February 1704 (3424, 3478)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 9, ff. 194-5.
Rotterdam†a- , 19 February 1704-†a
Dear Sir
I wrote you the 28th past by 2 Germans that were going from hence to seek a retreat for 100 Protestant families in some of the
― 190 ―
English Plantations, especially Carolina, which had been most of all praised to them, I sent by them to mr Limborch†a a small packet from his father to Sir Francis, And a bundle of letters and accounts to yourself, relating to my Scotch Debtor†b and that Scotch affaire, which is a Scotch affaire indeed, in the acceptation of that word in these Provinces, who call every that is od and otherwise than it should be, Scotch,†1 as the Germans do everything that is so in their Eye Spanish. I feare I trespassd too far upon your patience to desire your reading of my long letter to my Sir John and that other to the Gentleman that offers me his service†2 —I understand†c by mr. Ashley,†3 as also by mr Limborch that they are arrivd and have deliverd their letters, so that I have reason to beleeve that mine are ere this wel come to your hands, as unwell come, or Scotch as it may be to you, to be put upon such pennance. However having understood by monsieur Costs letter to my son Arent, that you were much better than you had been for some time past, I hope you wil have so far at lest been able, and willing to peruse them, as to see what I aime at, and give your helping hand, or good counsell how to get them forward safely to Edinburgh—
I am now requested to write to you, at the desire of Monsieur Hubers of uytrecht,†4 whose merit I know is not unknowne to you, being one of the most polite Esprits of this Age, but extreamly valitudinair, he requests his Brother Shepheard of this towne mr. Joseph Shepheard, my neighbour, and brother to mr. Sam. Shepheard of London,†5 to intreat your Judgment concerning the Vertue and operation of Irish slates,†6 in what cases it is good to be made use of, and how; especially if yourself have had the experience of it.
It being very much recommended to him, if not for himself, at lest for a sister of his that keeps his house, out of which he stirs not, nor has for severall years, he is an old Vryer†7 as your good
― 191 ―
self Sir, that never durst venture upon the bonds of Wedlock—I must cry peccavi I am better informd he is an old Widdower, mr Joseph Shepheard and he had 2 sisters, and so comes in the Brotherhood—
He writes not to me, because I have never had the honour of being acquainted, but I have heard so much of him by myn Heer Vacesen†a and Paats,†1 and his brother that I resolv God willing this summer to go with his brother to visit him.
This is the Gentleman, of whom I beleeve I have told you, who, hearing monsieur Jurieu at the request of myn Heer Paats, describing his God, with a great deale of windy Ecclesiastick fast,†2 and pomp—Defined him to be a Sovereign Will, whose will was the rule of all righteousness, who therefore could creat, and dispose of his creatures, how innocent soever according the Soveranity†b of his will and pleasure, some to everlasting blisse, others to everlasting torment, without any regard to any thing good or evill that any of them should do, or any reason but because his will was so.
Askt him when he was done, Very gravely, if this was his Idea of God, And he answering yes, Replyed with a smartness, which such a declamation against God deservd. This has allways been my Idea of the Devill.
Tho I beleeve you know this, yet I now write it again, that it may stand registred in my Copie book, in perpetuam rei memoriam,†3 what God Monsieur Jurieu adores
I have nothing further to add, but that if you should think fit to write to the Gentleman himself, that you would be pleasd to let your letter come open, that my wife may also profit by it— And so with her, mine, and our s<on>s†c humble service to your good self, Sir Francis and my Lady and the rest of that honourable family rest
Dear Sir
your most humble servant
BENJAMIN FURLY
― 192 ―
P.S.
pray give my service to mr. Cost, and tell him when he writes agen to my Lord Sh.†1 he needs not do as this last time write to mr. Bail,†2 to give it to mr. Leers, to give it to my Lord. my Lords letters from all parts Germany as well as England coming directly to me under a blanck covert or a droiture†3 to his Lordship at my house—my pain in my arms continues and makes writing Irksome to me—you may think then how useless I am grown.
We have no news but of a late conspiracy to displace the Magistrates of Middleburgh, and introduce the old taskmasters of Egipt The Statolderians—executed in one houre and repelled†a in another, whithout shedding one drop of blood, which has establisht the goverment so firmly, and shews what spirit is striving to get up again, that its like now to be quasht to purpose. The heads are fled, and prosecution began against them, while the poore misled burgers are pardond†4 —One of the Assembly of the States for the Town of Zierickzee having receivd the news at a place where he was, said, This is the day we have expected, now we shall see their blood gush forth—The States have desired Z.Zee to draw in his Commission, as being unwilling to set with so bloodthirsty a man—But they refuse, ordring their other members to do nothing where he is not present, pretending it an imposing on their right to send whom they please. The States have again desired them not to send him and the city of middleburgh if they will not forbeare, resolve to forbid him coming into <t>heir†b Jurisdiction†5 —
Address (written by Arent Furly?): To John Locke Esquire Att Sir Francis Masham's At Oates Near Bisshopstarfford Essex

3452. Francis Limborch to Locke, 12 February 1704 (i) (3447, 3453)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 13, ff. 227-8.
London 12 February 1703/4
Honoured Sir
Just now is sent to me the packett off Mr Furly under Covert off your most acceptable favour off yesterday;†2 this evening j intend therewith to waite on Mr Micklethwaite to see whether we can finde Mr Fletcher, who was to Jnforme us aboutt the Character off this Mr Simpson jn conformity to Mr Furlys desire. I received
― 194 ―
amongst some books and other things off mine for you Sir Corn: Kircheri Concordantiæ Veteris Testamenti in 2 volumes in quarto neatly bound†1 and a latin bible unbound,†2 aboutt the forwarding off them I desire your directions; I had lately orders for some Cacao Nutts for some friends so j finde'm very good j tooke some more and the person off who j had 'm being a man that owed me money and being the goods j sold him were fallen upon that account unwilling to pay me j took 3 hundredweight†a off him in very good nutts by which j came his debtor and so settled my account with, they are all garbled and the faulty ones and rotten all picked outt off'm, and very clean, so that upon that account j was willing to putt a quarter off hundred by for you and whenever you think fitt to accept off'm they shall be att your disposall, My parents sister and wife Joyne their most sincere respects with those off
Honoured Sir
Your Most oblidged Humble servant
FRANCIS LIMBORCH

London 12 February 1703/4
Honoured Sir
Just now returning from Sir Francis to whome I delivred a letter to you whereby I owned the receipt off Mr Furlys packett; j received your most acceptable favour off this day;†3 in answhere to which humbly serves that j shall keep the packett by my till j hear the Character off Mr Simpson, and according to Mr Furlys order acquaint you therewith and expect your directions, about the forwarding off the same into Scotland; I spoke with Mr Micklethwaite this night butt he hath nott yett an answhere off Mr Fletcher, butt promises to lett me know itt as soone he hath itt,
― 195 ―
which then j will nott faill to acquaint you with, iff in the mean while you desire these papers againe to peruse'm further j ll send'm you on your first order, j wish with all my heart Mr Furly may recover part off this debt, butt according the description Mr Joseph Wright makes off him j fear he'll never see a farthing and iff he goes to law fling good money after bad; Mr DAranda who is returned home from the Bath is very ill and so trouble with the Cholick that he Can neither lay nor sitt to sleep but is now and then oblidged to be tyed to Chair and so to sleep standing, or be kept between two men, so that they are in greatt fear for him; I hope My Lady received the seeds and expecting your orders about the books I most humbly remaine
Honoured Sir

3455. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 16 February 1704 (3454, 3457)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 353-4. Answered by nos. 3457 and 3460.
Oates 16 Feb: 03/4
Dear Cosin
Though the large letter you promised me in your last†2 not being yet come gives me noe matter of writeing to you, yet haveing this oportunity by a neighbour that goes directly to you I cannot forbear to tell you that in my last to you I desired you to put Mr Marton in minde of my half years interest that has been due to me ever since the begining of November last, and desired you also to see you if possible here before your journey. Methinks you may steale down with Sir Francis on Saturday next and return Monday. I have abondance more to say to you than I can put into a long letter. If you come down with him pray bring me twenty Guineas. If you doe not pray seale up twenty Guineas in a paper and deliver them to Sir Francis for me. But it would be much better you Should come your self. I cannot say how long or how little I shall live. But this I thinke it would be much better I should not be surprised and dye before I see you. I am
Dear Cosin
your most affectionate Cosin and humble servant
J LOCKE
Send me word whether your chamber be not in Tanfeild court

3456. Anthony Collins to Locke, 16 February 1704 (3449, 3461)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 7, ff. 16-17. The letter, which answers no. 3449, was sent in a parcel of books which Locke did not open until 24 February or a day or two later: no. 3474, which answers it.
Lincolns Inne Feb. 16/03/4
Sir
I can easily comply with you in your wish that all Essex was nothing else but Oats, that contains all that I know to be truly valuable there: I'm sure I spend no time any where else with more pleasure and it is my fault if I cannot say advantage. Lands and Tenements say nothing against this; they are necessary to but low degrees of satisfaction in comparison of what I enjoy with you, who have so happyly imitated the glorious state of those spirits you so well describe by looking into things themselves and by your friendship make me wait with patience for it. Wee that have others to do the drudgery's of life for us might approach very near to that state and make our selves as wee are certainly design'd to be very happy here, for wee the herd exceed our Leaders in numbers and consequently our true, substantial pleasures depend on such a liberty as wee can give our selves, which is only to be happy here that wee may be happy hereafter, else wee are deservedly answerable to our Creator for being Irrational Unhappy Creatures who has put it in our power to be Rational Happy ones: but the full cry is against those who are for the wellfare of all men by the Interest of some and the stupidity of the rest and the tide too high to be controul'd by the too few Partisans of truth, and the best wee can expect is that the Leaders will never agree one among another, and this way there will be some Liberty though against the consent of 'em all.
I hope Le Clerc's Harmony and Moliere's works are bound to your satisfaction. I have endeavour'd to oblige the Ladys and consequently you by speaking to the Binder to be particularly careful about 'em.
There is no 8vo Bible to be met with without marginal notes and references but there is lately printed a very fair one in large twelves without 'em,†1 wherefore I desire your further directions.
Dr Pitt has made a new attack upon the Apothecaries,†2 and
― 198 ―
Dr Coward has publish'd a Book to show that no such thing as Immaterial substance exists in nature and that all matter has originally a principle of self motion in it. his arguments are very far from proving either and are too mean to give you any account of.†1 Mr Toland has a book in the press upon these subjects. The power of prejudice. The History of the Immortality of the Soul. The Original of Idolatry. An answer to Spinoza's system of Philosophy and a discourse to prove that motion ought to be part of the definition of matter.†2 I should be glad to see more books publishd besides Mr Newtons Opticks†3 that would be a fit entertainment for you. I am with the greatest esteem and respect
Your most Humble and most Obedient serva<nt>†a
A. COLLINS
My most humble service to all the family at Oats
Address: For John Locke Esq at Oats in Essex
Endorsed by Locke: A: Collins 16. Feb 0 3/4 Answered 28
3457. Peter King, later first Baron King, to Locke, 17 February 1704 (3455, 3459)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 12, ff. 239-40. Answers no. 3455; answered by nos. 3459 and 3464.
London february. 17. 1703.
Sir
I have spoke with Mr Marton about your half years Interest, and he assures me, that he hath not yet received it, but believes he shall next week, or the week after—Before I Received your letter I did design to wait upon you Saturday fortnight—I cannot wait upon you next Saturday,†4 because there is busyness of great Concern, wherein I am principally employed, appointed to be tried Munday morning next at Guildhall—If you would have me come down next week, and not stay till Saturday fortnight, The least Intimation thereof shall bring me down, and I will wait upon you again Saturday fortnight also—I have Malt tickets for about 1200 l. and I was promised the residue this day, but my Cosin†5
― 199 ―
did not come according to his promise, so cannot give a full account of that matter till Saturdays post—I have now a title by me, which hath the appearance of a good morgage for 1200 l. If on farther examination It proves to be so—I will lay out your mony thereon before I go out of Town—I am
Sir Your most affectionate Cosin and Humble Servant.
P. KING.
Endorsed by Locke: P. King 17 Feb 03/4 Answered 21
3458. Dr. (later Sir) Hans Sloane to Locke, 17 February 1704 (2986, 3466)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 18, ff. 126-7. Answered by no. 3466.
London Feb. 17. 1703.
Sir
I have been extreamly pleased to hear of Your good health which I have not neglected inquiring after though I have not had the good fortune to see you or to hear from you. I mett the other day with a catalogue of books of Yours which Mr. Courten had forwarded to you†1 and amongst the rest one Richerius de Belleval about the Montpelier garden.†2 If you have that book I should be very much obliged to you for a sight of it and you may be sure I will take care of it and return it to you in a week. I know the value of scarce books so well as to take more then ordinary care of them and to restore them soon to their owners. I need not tell you that I should be glad to have it in my power any ways to serve you nor that I would take it as a favour to have Your commands being with great sincerity
Your most obedient and most humble servant
HANS SLOANE
I see Mr. Churchill every week so that when you please to send it him, I shall in a week return it him to be sent you again.

B.L., MS. Locke c. 7, ff. 18-19. Answered by no. 3465.
Lincolns Inne / Feb. 18/03/4
Sir
I sent you on thursday last Le Clercs Harmony and Moliere's Works by the Bishopstafford Coach, on which day after I had sent 'em to the Coach Mr Tyrrell came to me and desird me to acquaint you in my next letter to you that he had not yet receiv'd the letter (the contents of which he guessed at) you gave to Sir F. Masham for him, but beleiv'd it might be at his house in the Country: that he intended to go out of town on this day and would take with him the Books that were left at his Lodgings by your order, and after about a fortnights usage in the Country
― 202 ―
deliver to the proper person at Oxford.†1 Mr Bold gives his service to all the family at Oats. I am going to put to the press a Book of his entitul'd Two Discourses 1 Of the Immateriality of Created thinking substance 2 Of the Resurrection of the same body.†2 I am promisd by a Friend at Oxford a particular account of the Proceedings of the Heads of Colleges with relation to their forbidding any of your Books to be read in the University.†3 I should be very glad so considerable a recommendation might for the benefit of Mankind be made publick to the World, for what they have done plainly shows that in the way of reason they are not to be dealt with which they very well approve of when it serves their purpose. You know the world too well not to expect such treatment from the Traders in Opinions and me too well not to believe that I am with a perfect Esteem and Respect
Your most Humble and most Obedient servant
A. COLLINS.

3464. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 21 February 1704 (3460, 3469)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 357-8. Answers nos. 3457 and 3460.
Oates 21 Feb 03/4
Dear Cosin
I received the Guineas by Sir Francis. but needed them not till you came, nor had I any desire to have you come just then or precisely at any other time. I had said noething of all that if you had told me sooner that you intended to come down before your western journey. Now I lay hold on your promise where in you say that whenever I please to have you come you will assuredly doe it. I would then have you come on Friday seventh night.†4
You told me in your last of your prospect of a good Morgage for £1200. Pray let the obligation be if you can get it to repay me as much standard silver as is now in £1200 according to the rule of the mint at present. For I meet with the suspition that the raiseing of our money will be again attempted.†5 I am sure it is but just that the Morgagor should pay back as much silver as he received doe you word it as softly†a as you can, and let it be lent in Mr Churchils name
I here inclosed send you a receit for the 1/2 years interest to be received of Mr Marton that if you and he approve the forme it
― 205 ―
may be ready against the mony is ready. If anything be to be amended in it you may send it down to me corrected For you may remember Mr Marton desired the last time that the receit might name him since he is accountable for the money
Dr Gastrel left at Mr Pawlings for me one of the sermons he lately preachd before your house.†1 Pray send to Mr Pawlings for it and let your man bring it. I am
Dear Cosin
your most affectionate Cosin and humble servant
J LOCKE
Have you enquired of Mr Pawling for the key of my trunk. The parliament is selling of annuitys.†2 What think you of laying out money upon any of them and which is best
