3465. Locke to Anthony Collins, 21 February 1704 (3461, 3467)
The Robert H. Taylor Collection, Princeton, New Jersey. Transcribed from photostat. Printed, omitting a sentence, in A Collection of Several Pieces, pp. 282-5. Answers no. 3461; answered by no. 3467.
Oates 21 Feb†a 03/4
<Sir>†b
I must acknowledg it as an effect of your zeale to serve me that you have sent le Clercs Harmony and Molieres workes by the Bishop Startford Coach and I return you my thanks as much as if it exactly answerd my purpose. I ought not to think it strange that you in town amidst a hurry of business should not keep precisely in minde my little affaires when I here where I have noe thing to disturb my thoughts doe soe often forget. When I writ to you to doe me the favour to get those books for me carefully bound I think
― 206 ―
I made it my request to you, I'm sure I intended it, to write me a word when they were done and then I would acquaint you how they were to be disposed of, for the truth is, they were to be disposed of in town. But whether I onely meant this and said noe thing; or you forgot it the matter is not much, I expect to receive the books tomorrow and shall doe well enough with them. Mr Churchil has made the same mistake about the books gon with Mr Tyrrel into the Country.†1 I should not have taken notice of this to you at all, did I not intend it for an excuse for an ill mannerd thing very necessary in business which perhaps you will find me use with you for the future, which is to repeat the little circumstances of business, which are apt to be forgotten, in every letter till the danger be over. This if you observe to doe will prevent many cross accidents in your affairs, I assure it you upon experience.
I desire you to stop your hand a little and forbear puting to the press the two discourses you mention they are very touchy subjects at this time and that good man who is the author may for ought I know be crippled by those who will be sure to be offended at him right or wrong. Remember what you say a little lower in your letter in the case of an other friend of yours That in the way of reason they are not to be dealt with. It will be a kindness to get a particular account of these†a proceedings but therein must be conteined the day, the names of those present and the very words of the order or resolution and to learn if you can from whence it had its rise. When these particulars are obteind it will be fit to consider what use to make of them in the meantime I take what has been done as a recommendation of those books to the world. as you doe, and I conclude when you and I meet next we shall be merry upon the subject. For this is certain, that because some men wink, or turn away their heads and will not see, others will not consent to have their eyes put out. I am
Dear Sir
Your most obleiged and most humble servant
J LOCKE
Address: For Anthony Collins Esquire at his Chamber Numb: 7 in Serle Court in Lincolns Inne London
― 207 ―
3466. Locke to Dr. (later Sir) Hans Sloane, 21 February 1704 (3458, 3473)
B.M., Sloane MS. 4039, ff. 243-4. Printed, with wrong date 24 February 1704, in Forster, pp. 73-5. Answers a lost antecedent and no. 3458; answered by no. 3473.
Oates 21 Feb 03/4
Sir
I should not have been so long indebted to you for a letter without returning you an answer were I not through lazyness or indispotion call it which you please grown in that respect perfectly a Bankrupt. And yet had you staid but some few days longer I had prevented you and made an offer at redeeming my credit with you. The particular esteem I have for you and <t>he†a obligations I have to you, made me resolve some time since to send you the continuation of my register of the air, wherein there was one obstacle to be got over. Mr Churchil printed the former part of it in Mr Boyles general history of the air†1 which he told me some time since he intended to publish a new with additions and thereupon I promised him the remainder of my register to be added to what he has already printed But seeing his other additions not ready, and concludeing mine if there be any use of it will be lost if not published in my lifetime, I have prevailed with him to consent to give the remainder of my register to you to be published in the Philosophical transactions upon condition your Bookseller who prints the transactions will acknowledg the right of the register to be in him so that he may print it when he will. The matter standing thus and Beleiveing this trifle of mine would not be utterly unacceptable to you I had got one year transcribed before I received yours of the 17th instant. with an intention to send it you very speedily.†2 Thus much to alleviate my fault and to convince you that though I did not write to you yet I did not wholy forget you. To come now to the business of your letter I can onely say to you in short that wherever the mistake is, Richerius de Beleval about the Montpelier Garden is a book that I <never>†a had nor to my knowledg never saw in my life. If I had it you might command <it as much>†a as any other book in my study not onely for a week but for as long a ti<me>†a as you had occasion to use it. I return you my thanks for the Transactions <which>†a
― 208 ―
from time to time I receive from you. I wish there were any thing wherein I could serve you here. I am
Sir
Your most humble and most obedient servant
JLOCKE
Address: For Dr. Sloane at his house near Southampton house in Bloomsbery Square London
3467. Anthony Collins to Locke, 22 February 1704 (3465, 3470) enclosing William Wright, jnr., to [Collins?], 19 February 1704 (3485, enclosure)
B.L., MSS. Locke c. 7, ff. 20-1, and c. 23, f. 109. Its date and contents indicate that Wright's was the letter forwarded by Collins. Collins's letter answers no. 3465 and is answered by no. 3470.
William Wright, c. 1659-1721, was a son of Alderman William Wright (no. 615). He matriculated from Trinity College in 1674, became a barrister, and was elected recorder of Oxford on 22 October 1688. Like his father he was a strong whig: Foster; Wood, L. and T.; Hearne, Remarks and Collections, (Oxford Hist. Soc.), vii. 222.
Lincolns Inne. Feb 22/03/4
Sir
I return you my thanks for the kind excuses you make for my mistake which upon a Review of your letters I acknowledg my self to have been guilty of. I can only say that whenever I do not do my best to serve you and am not ready to own a mistake in any kind that I am guilty of I am contented to forfeit the most valuable Friendship that ever I contracted. was my ability to serve you equall to my Zeal, that would prevent the trouble of being more particular in your orders to me which I have reason to hope the continuance of, since it is not I see in my power to disoblige you so long as you think so well of my intentions, wherein I assure you, you do me but justice. That you may for the future have a measure of my understanding and so know how to apply to me I beg leave to transcribe from your letters all that relates to this matter. In yours on Jan.24th you desire me to gett you Le Clercs Harmony etc. and Molieres works I need not transcribe what concerns the Editions, binding etc: and in y'rs of Feb. 7th you say you conclude by this time I am got to Town again and then in a little time you shall hear from me that the Books etc. are done. I have here
― 209 ―
sent you a letter I yesterday received from Oxford, when I hear any thing more relating to the same matter I shall communicate it to you.
I have already put those books I mention'd to you to the press, but they shall never come abroad so long as you think the Author is in any danger.
If you please to return Le Clerc and Moliere to me with directions to dispose of 'em in Town. Pray do me the favour to direct to me at Sir F. Childs at Temple Bar,†1 because I am just now going out of Town to see my own Father and shall not return to Town till munday next. I am with the utmost esteem and respect
Dear Sir
Your most Humble <and>†a most Obedient servant
A. COLLINS
Address: For John Locke Esq [at Oates, by Joslyn, Bishops Stortford]
Endorsed by Locke: A Collins 22 Feb 03/4 Answered 24
[Enclosure:]
19 February 1703 Oxford.
Sir
Since my return to this place I have inquired into the procedings of the Heads of Houses concerning Mr. Locks books, and am informed that at a meeting towards the end of November last they agreed to prohibite all scholars to read any of those books, or the books of Mr Clerke;†2 and ordered a Programma to be prepared for that purpose against their next meeting, and adjorned to another day. at the day of their Adjornment Dr. Edwards†3 was present, and advised them not to publish the Programma, but rather to let all the Heads of houses admonish the tutors not to read to their Pupills, nor to suffer their Pupills to read any of those books, and so the matter fell, and no notice hath been taken of it since. if any Minutes were taken by the Register of the procedings of either of those meetings, I hope I may be able to send them to you in a short time. the Doctor, who prevented the issuing out of the Programma, had been as likely to promote it as another; but he
― 210 ―
expects preferment from a noble friend of that great man.†1 your promise to communicate to Mr <Edward>†a Clerke what you should receive from me upon this occasion, will I hope excuse me from giving him the trouble of a letter: pray present him with my humble service, and accept it your selfe from Sir
Your most humble servant
WILL. WRIGHT.
Endorsed by Locke: W. Wright 19 Feb. 03/4
3468. Jean Le Clerc to Locke, 22 February/4 March 1704 (3319, 3559)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 13, ff. 148-9. Printed in Bonno, Le Clerc, pp. 128-9.
A Amsterdam le 4 de Mars 1704.
Je ne doutes pas, Monsieur, que Mr. Limbourg le fils ne vous ai fait remettre, il y a long tems, la Bible de Schmidt, et la Concordance de Kircher. Je trouvai cette derniere, par hasard, dans une auction, et comme elle étoit assez bien conditionnée, je ne la voulu pas laisser aller. Il doit y avoir une auction au mois d'Avril,†2 où sont les Conferences de Voiture et de Costar,†3 que je ne manquerai pas de faire acheter, car il y a long-tems qu'on ne les trouve plus dans les Boutiques. Pour la Bibliotheque de Du Pin, on m'avoit dit chez Mrs Huguetan que l'on avoit sous la presse le dernier volume, qui est imprimé à Paris in 8. Mais ils m'ont avoüé depuis peu qu'il n'en étoit rien, et que ce qu'ils avoïent d'imprimé, depuis le XII. Tome, étoit il y a long tems en vente à Londres.†4 Ces gens vont quitté la Librairie, pour faire seulement le Change, comme ils le font depuis quelque tems. Ils contribuent à faire payer les armées de France, et pour cela l'ainé est allé à Geneve, où il s'est mis sous la protection du Résident de cette Couronne.†5 Si vous
― 211 ―
souhaitez que je vous envoye ce qu'ils ont d'imprimé, depuis le T.XII. je ne manquerai pas de le faire, et je l'aurois fait d'abord, sans la parole qu'ils me donnerent qu'ils alloient incessamment imprimer le dernier Tome. S'ils vendent leur fonds, ceux qui acheteront cette Copie, ne manqueront pas de le publier. Il ne se passe rien ici de nouveau dans les Lettres. On verra seulement cet Eté le livre d'Eusebe et de S. Jerôme de locis Hebraïcis, avec les notes de Bonfrerius et les miennes, qui ne sont pas grand'chose. Mais le livre est très-bon en soi.†1 On pourra le joindre avec le Sanson,†2 quoi que cet Ouvrage ne vaille pas ce que l'autre vaut; à cause de l'egalité de la forme et du peu d'épaisseur de l'un et de l'autre. Si vous en êtes curieux, je ne manquerai pas de vous l'envoyer. On va achever de tirer les planches de ma Philosophie, imprimée ici pour la 3 fois.†3 J'aurai soin de vous l'envoyer par la premiere occasion. J'y ai corrigé et ajoûté quelque petite chose. Cependant vous trouverez joints ici deux exemplaires du 3 Tome de la Bibliotheque Choisie, l'un pour Vous et l'autre pour Madame Masham, à qui je vous prie aussi de faire rendre cette lettre. J'apprends de Mr. Limbourg que son Fils lui a écrit que vous vous étiez assez bien porté cet hiver, dont je me réjouïs. Je prie Dieu qu'il vous fasse passer le plus doucement qu'il sera possible le tems qu'il vous veut encore laisser avec nous, et que nous puissions profiter jusqu'à la fin de vos excellentes lumieres. Je suis, Monsieur, entierement à vous
J LE CLERC.
Je remercie Mr. Coste de sa Religion des Dames, quoi que la 2. Partie ne vaille pas la premiere.†4
Address: A Monsieur Monsieur Locke A Oates.
Endorsed by Locke: Le Clerc 4 Mar 03/4
― 212 ―
3469. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 24 February [1704] (3464, 3471)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 359-60. Year from King's endorsement and contents.
Oates 24 Feb
Dear Cosin
You are at last like a worthy member of Parliament come to a good resolution for tomorrow seventh night, pray remember to bring with you the late address of your house to the Queen†1 that you may read a little upon it. And if any more chocolate come to your chamber for me pray bring that too For I know not but a little of that may goe down and relish well enough after an address or in the midst of it. If the morgage be a good one doe not bauke†2 it for a forme, though if you can get the forme I propose or an equivalent it would not be the worse. I hope the Bishop of Worcesters copy†3 will come with you. Pray let your man goe to Mr Churchils before you come out of town to bring what he hath for me. Of the rest when I see you.
I am Dear Cosin
Your most affectionate humble servant
J LOCKE
Have you yet found the Key of my trunk at Mr Pawlings? Pray remember to bring from him Dr Gastrels sermon which he has for me
Address: For Peter King Esqr member of Parliament. To be left at Mr Potmans in the Middle Temple London Frank.
Postmark: FE 27
Endorsed by King: Mr Lock february 24. 1703.

3470. Locke to Anthony Collins, 24 February 1704 (3467, 3474)
The Carl H. Pforzheimer Library, New York. Transcribed from photostat. Printed in A Collection of Several Pieces, pp. 285-7. Answers no. 3467; answered by no. 3479.
Oates 24 Feb 03/4
Sir
You know me not yet as you ought if you doe not think I live with you with the same confidence I doe with my self and with the same sincerity of affection too. This makes me talke to you with the same freedom I think which though it has not all the ceremony of good breeding, yet it makes amends with something more substantial and is of better relish in the stomach. Beleive it therefor that you need not trouble your self with apologies for haveing sent the books hither you have obleiged me as much by it as you could by any thing of that nature which I had desired neither need you be concerned for the future. Tis convenient to make it a rule not to let ones freind forget little circumstances whereby such cross purposes some times happen, but when they doe happen between freinds they are to be made matter of mirth.
The Gent. that writ you the letter which you sent to me is an extraordinary man and the fitest in the world to go on with that enquiry.†1 Pray let him at any rate get the precise time, the persons present, and the minutes of the Register <taken>†a of their proceedings, and this without noise or seeming concerned to have them, as much as may be, and I would beg you not to talk of this matter till we have got the whole matter of fact, which will be a pleasant story and of good use.
I wish the books you mentiond were not gon to the press and that they might not be printed for when they are printed I am sure they will get abroad and then it will be too late to wish it had not been so. However if the fates will have it so and their printing can not be avoided yet at least let care be taken to conceale his name. I doubt not of his reasoning right and makeing good his points, but what will that boot if he and his family should be disturbd or discased.†b
― 214 ―
I shall as you desire send Molliere and le Clerc back to you by the first oportunity and address them to Sir Francis Childs. I am with perfect affection and respect
Dear Sir
Your most humble and most obedient servant
J LOCKE
Address: For Anthony Collins Esq To be left at Sir Francis Childs within Temple Bar London
Postmark: FE 25 (?)
3471. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 26 February 1704 (3469, 3513)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 361-2.
Oates 26 Feb. 03/4
Dear Cosin
I writ to you the last post to desire you not to decline the morgage you mentioned for want of their complying with my desire of stipulateing to pay the like quantity of Silver if you approve of the morgage otherwise if it be executed before you come out of town pray remember to bring with you a declaration of trust thereupon from Mr Churchil. Be sure to be here early on Friday that we may have the more time, for I know not but this may be the last time I may see you. I speak not this from dispondency of mind but from what I feel in my body. If the comeing about of the wind to the south or west does not releive me tis probable my journey will be at an end before your circuit. I am
Dear Cosin
your most affectionate Cosin and humble servant
J LOCKE
Remember to bring your addresse†1 and any thing else that is good

3472. Edward Clarke to Locke, 26 February 1704 (3436, 3482)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 6, ff. 153-4. Printed in Rand, pp. 597-8. Answered by no. 3482.
London February the 26th. 1703/4.
Deare Sir,
I acknowlidge the Favour of yours, with the inclosed Directed to the Bishop of Glocester, to which I should have Return'd you an Answer by the last Post, had it been possible for mee to have transacted, what was desired, with the Bishop of Glocester, But Hee being at present under some Indisposition which renders Him unable to attend the Publick Service, or to bee spoken withall, I could not 'till this morning, with all my importunity, get his Lordships Hand to what was desired; Sir Francis was pleased to Refuse to doe any thing towards it, (not soe much as to stepp over the threshold) tho I thought it was more His Businesse then mine, to have done some thing in it, espetially when Hee Knew I was more then ordinarily Engaged to Dissable mee, in poynt of time, to Attend the Bishop to gett his Hand; Sir I am (in hast)
Your most Affectionate and obleiged servant
EDW: CLARKE:
My humble service to my Lady etc:
Pray give the inclosed to Mrs Masham from your Wife etc. and pardon this hasty scribble:
Address: For John Locke Esq. [at Oates, by Joslyn, Bishops Stortford] Frank: Edw: Clarke:
Postmarks: FE 26; GC
Endorsed by Locke: E: Clarke 26 Feb: 03/4 Answered 4
3473. Dr. (later Sir) Hans Sloane to Locke, 26 February 1704 (3466, 3489)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 18, ff. 128-9. Answers no. 3466.
London Feb 26. 1703.
Sir
I would have answer'd your kind letter sooner but that I resolved first to speak to the bookseller,†1 who readily agreed to the proposall
― 216 ―
that the copy†1 of Your register should remain to Mr. Churchill. Therefore pray send it up when you please. I am sorry you have not the book I mention'd to you. I have long wanted to see it and thought by a catalogue I found amongst Mr Courten's papers you certainly had it amongst your books. Wee have been here very much diverted by Mr. Fontenay a Jesuit from China†2 and one who says he comes from Formosa and is a Japonese.†3 I could make you very merry with what I have heard from them which I will leave till our next meeting in the mean time I am
Your most obedient servant
HANS SLOANE
Address: To John Lock Esqr: at Sir Francis Massams at Oats near Bishop-Startford Essex.
Postmark: FE 26
Endorsed by Locke: Dr Sloane 26 Feb 03/4
3474. Locke to Anthony Collins, 28 February 1704 (3470, 3479)
B.M., Add. MS. 4290, ff. 32-3, a copy made by or for Desmaizeaux, with an additional paragraph from Messrs. Maggs Brothers' Catalogue no. 433, 1922, item no. 3442. The original letter was again in Messrs. Maggs Brothers' Catalogue no. 449, 1924, item no. 272, and was sold at auction in 1956: Libreria Antiquaria Hoepli, De Deschamps à Proust, sale-catalogue, Geneva, 23 and 24 April 1956, no. 85. The letter is printed from the incomplete British Museum text, or a copy of it, in A Collection of Several Pieces, pp. 288-90. Answers no. 3456; answered by no. 3479.
Oates. 28 Feb. 03/4
Sir
I saw the packet was exactly well made up, and I knew the books in it were well bound; whereupon I let it alone and was likely to have sent it back to you unopen'd; but my good genius would not suffer me to lose a letter of yours in it which I value more than all the books it accompanied. Since my last therefore to you I open'd the packet, and therein found yours of the 16th instant which makes me love and value you if it were possible more than I did before, you having therein in short so well described wherein
― 217 ―
the happiness of a rational creature in this world consists, though there are very few that make any other use of their half employ'd and undervalu'd reason but to†a- bandy†1 against it.-†a Tis well, as you observe, that they agree as ill with one another as they do with common sense. For when by the influence of some prevailing head they all lean one way, Truth is sure to be born down and there is nothing so dangerous as to make any enquiry after her, and to own her for her own sake is the most unpardonable crime. You ask me how I like the binding of Moliere and Le Clerc. You will wonder to hear me say, not at all, but you must take the other part of my answer, which is, nor do I dislike it, Tis probable that this yet does†b not satisfy you after you have taken such special care with your Binder that they should be exactly well done. Know then, that upon moving the first book, having luckily espied your letter, I only just lookd into it to see the Paris print of Moliere, and without so much as taking it out of the paper it was wrapped up in cast my eye upon the cover which lookd very fine and curiously done and so put it up again hasting to your letter. This was examining more than enough of books whose binding you had told me you had taken care of, and more than enough for a man who had your letter in his hand unopened.
Pray send me word what you think or hear of Dr Pitts last book.†2 For as for the first of the other Authors†c you mention by what I have seen of him already I can easily think his arguments not worth your reciting. And as for the other†d though he has parts yet that is not all which I require in an Author I am covetous of, and expect to find satisfaction in. I†e- am glad to hear there is a good bible in 12° without marginal notes. If it be a fair print and good paper I desire you to send me one of them and redy bound if you can find any one that is tolerably so for strength and use, guilt or not guilt on the leaves ...-†e
Pray forget not to write to your freind in Oxford to the purpose I mention'd in my last to you.†3 I am
Dear Sir
Your most etc.
― 218 ―
3475. Locke to [Cary Mordaunt, countess of Peterborough], 28 February 1704 (2320)
Moscow, State Historical Museum, Count G. Orlov Collection. Transcribed from photostat. The letter probably belonged at some time to Voltaire: Correspondence, ed. T. Besterman, 1953-65, no. 14883; the note on the cover is perhaps in his hand. Orlov was in London in 1818 and 1821, but there is nothing to show how he acquired the letter: Times Literary Supplement, 1964, p. 456; information from Mr. J.S.G. Simmons. The person addressed from the contents. The letter was taken to her by Anthony Collins: pp. 231, 234.
Oates 28. Feb 1703/4
Madam
About two months†1 since I had the strangest and most pleaseing vision in the world. All of a suddain I saw enter into my study, your Ladyship, my Lord Peterborow and my Lord Mordant and had the honour of this company the length of a pleaseing dream. Though I am not very credulous that way, yet it was most certainly a vision, I could not else have so much failed in the common dutys of humanity to mortals of that rank, as not to have made them eat and drink with that hospitality which is due to strangers who were travellers and fasting: But Spirits when they appear take up the whole mind, and banish all ordinary and regular thinking; espetialy spirits like these, of that high elevation and brightness, that one cannot approach them even when cased in flesh and blood, without the respectfull discomposure of an extraordinary veneration and astonishment, that leaves no liberty to mind other things. All this I have said to my self a thousand times: but yet the impression made upon my mind at that time was so strong and so lively, that I cannot to this day get quite rid of the suspition that it might realy be you your selves: And this suspition would at last undoubtedly prevail, were it not too great a complement for me to put upon my self, to suppose, that such persons, at that time of the year, should take a toylsome journey, in unpasseable ways, to doe an honour to the decaid†a shell of an old servant, and make a glory about a breathless skeleton just droping into the darkeness of the grave. Whatsoever therefore may be in my thoughts (as there will in regard to the persons mentioned be always something too great for my words to utter) I dare not owne the vanity of returning
― 219 ―
thanks. Amongst other things that passed then, your Ladyship took notice of Moliere among my books.†1 If you were really here (for who can set bounds to the actions and goodness of you superior Spirits) you remember it. Him therefore, now I have got him into a fit dress to wait upon your Ladyship, I beg you to receive as a witness, that I doe not overlook or forget so mighty an honour don me, though it be too great for me to take to my self, or presume to own. This life 'tis said is but a dream. This I am sure the visions, which by your bounty I have had of this kind, have made mine realy happy, and realy glorious: Be pleased therefore I beseech you, Madam to allow me to assure your Ladyship that I am with all the powers of the poor life I have, and with wishes beyond them Madam
Your Ladyships most humble most obleiged and most obedient
servant
JOHN LOCKE
Marked on cover in a later hand: lettre de loke
3476. Samuel Locke to Locke, 28 February 1704 (3403, 3629)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 14, ff. 225-6. Perhaps sent through Bonville: p. 228.
Sir
I owe answer to your acceptable letter of 14th. december. wherein was one for my son John at Surat, which I sent forward by my son Samuel, who departed from Portsmouth the 6th. of January and hope by this Tyme may be at least 1/2 way to the Cape of Good Hope.
Sir It being not only your favour but your order to send my son John a presant, I did accordingly do it; to the vallew of £3. in wine. which my son Sam will take care with all due Respects to performe. And I hope will bring his Brother home with him, who I hope may live to Returne you personall thanks for that and all your favours.
I begin to be weary of the India Trade. since the Parliment are pleas'd to lop off so many of the best branches off it: upon pretence of gratifying a Rude Multitude of Lazy and Idle weavers.†2 etc. I ad
― 220 ―
my hearty wishes of your good health and with the tender of my Respects am
Sir
Your most humble servant and kinsman
SAM: LOCKE.
London. 28 february 1703/4
Sir my son John in his last letter in January 1702/3 did Intimate to mee that those Warrs from the Savaguzzees†1 then about Surrat was occation'd by a son of Aurenge Zebe's. who was then supposed to be dead or dyeing.†2 But wee have had no Confirmation of it as yet: Vale —
yours S.L.
Address: To The Honorable John Locke att Oates
Endorsed by Locke: S: Locke 28 Feb. 03/4
3477. James Tyrrell to Locke, 28 February [1704] (3392, 3511)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 22, ff. 165-6. Year from Locke's endorsement. A sentence from this letter is printed in King, p. 193, as part of no. 3511.
Shot:†3 Feb: 28
Dear Sir
On Thursday, a few days after my return home, I received a letter from Mr: church;†4 and on saturday a second with one enclosed from your self; which whether I had received or not, I should not have faild to have sent up the parcel of books which he sent to my Lodgings before I left the Town, and which I then imagin'd were the same you intended for the Bod: Library, since I remember in your last yow writ to me before Christmass, you mentiond these new volumes of Travels†5 as part of your intended
― 221 ―
present to that Place, and whether you continued or not in the same mind as to that, I might have seen, had I bin so lucky as to have received your last, which Sir Francis Masham beleiving me to be here, when I was not, directed hither, tho by some mistake or carelessness of the People at the Posthous<e>†a it be miscarryed, so that if you have any thing further to communicate to me which was in that letter, I desire you would again repeat it in your next. In the mean while give me leave to tell you, that had I not begun to read the last volume of those Travels before I came down, and had not finishd it till yesterday, I had certainly put it out of my power to have taken them back with any good Grace, since I had put them into Dr: Hudsons hands last week: but I shall now send them up to Mr: church: by my carrier on wednesday, and since I have made some use of them am contented to loose what it will cost in bringing them down; but tho' I have perused that one volume, yet I hope it has bin with that Care as†b- will no-†b way prejudice the sale of them either to your self or any body els; and I can further tell you, that if the precedent volumes are as considerable as that of the Travells of Dr: Gemelli Carreri almost round the world, they will prove a very valuable Collection, and as such, I recommend to your view; and when you have perused†c them, or what part of them your leasure shall permit, they may prove a valueable present to the place you I suppose still design either those, or some others of the like value; For I cannot beleive you can change your Resolution as to that point upon so inconsiderable a cause as the Report, which I suppose Mr: Collyns has sent you, of the Heads of Houses making an order that your works should not be read by Tutors to their Pupils; in which Report tho there is indeed so much of Truth, as that such a Thing was started by somebody among them (whose name I cannot yet learn not having as yet spoken with any Head of a Colledg) yet it ended onely in a secret agreement among some of them that the Tutors they recommended should be advised neither to read your Essay, nor Le Clercs Physics nor Logick to their Pupils, since those works had much discouraged the noble Art of disputation, (or Hogshearing†1 as wee call it) tho even as to this tho I have discoursed with some Tutors in New Colledg and university yet I do not hear
― 222 ―
there are any such Instructions given in those Colledges, tho' their Heads are high enough in their way:†1 but granting the thing to be true, I hope you will not impute the indiscreet zeal of a Few men to the whole university, any more, then wee should lay the failing of the Bishops to the Church: especially, since you have allready by your writing to me made me tell Dr: Hudson you intended a farther present of Books so as to be entred as a Benefactor; which when you shall think fit to do, I will (if you please again to trust me) see faithfully performd, and am however your most humble servant
J TYRRELL
I hope this will find you in as good health as you have bin of late, if not better, since you say nothing to the contrary; I pray give my humble service to the worthy Lady, and her daughter in Law,†2 and the yong Gentleman, and his Ingenious Tutor.
I hope to receive an answere to this at your leasure.
Address: For John Lock Esqr at Sir Francis Mashams house at Otes. Essex.
Endorsed by Locke: J: Tyrrell 28 Feb: 03/4 Answered Apr. 3.
3478. Benjamin Furly to Locke [29 February/11 March 1704] (3450, 3503)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 9, ff. 196-7. Furly states in no. 3503 that this letter was written on 11 March (N.S.). It was forwarded by Francis Limborch on 9 March: p. 235.
Dear Friend
I received yesterday your most acceptable lines of the 12th past,†3 under cover of Mr Limborchs of the 15th or 18th in which he mentions not the receit of those papers, which this night I order him to return to be amended. I am a certain kind of a lazy companion, loath to be got to work, especially about old accounts where I must have mony. And therefore when I am at it I am apt to overdoe, being willing to say all at once, that I may have no more occasion to think of it, at lest in a great while, and so do say
― 223 ―
more than needs, as you shew me in my letter to Sir John.†1 You say right that I Aym at the prevailing with him upon the Topick of his preserving his Credit. And I know not what I can take hold of him by than that, if he has any left, as he seems to have among his Countrymen, by the station he is in. But how I can urge that Argument, if I turn all upon that thred bare pretence of the mistake of mr. Simson, as to the name Farley for Furly. Which I desire you to consider again. and give me your thoughts of, upon second thoughts. He cannot but be satisfyed that he has exposed himself to that man, and therefore that's impossible to be conceald from him. But my argument proceeds upon the interest, and concern he has to take up, repaire that weak step, that it may be known to nobody else but mr Simson: whom therefore I do earnestly caution tho I let him read my letter, not to divulge it. As to that part relating to mr.Fl.†2 I confess, if he should have been on the oposit side he may make an ill use of it. But do you think he can doe so with that passage that relates to my forbearance of him, upon the ill success of the Darien affaire. I am not sensible how he can take my letter to him to be an angry letter, which, to me, seems so smooth as I can possible write, fully of care for him, and promising all forbearance to him, that he can, in any reason propound to himself. I know of what operation such a letter would be on my understanding. But it is usuall for standers by to see more than the gamesters, they say, for I never was proficient in that art, as to know it by experience. In order to purge my letter of all superfluous matter, I have ordred it to be returnd me. And therefore would gladly have your second thoughts about the first point above mentiond, it being impossible, as it seems to me, to hide that blind side of him from mr Simson, whose friend has describd him by his father, as to his station in the state, and change of Religion a little before the Restauration, and his own change of Religion a little after the Revolution, from which he, judiciously, concludes it must needs be the same J.S. that is my debtor.†3 If
― 224 ―
that letter could be found, it might be well, but my giving him the substance of it so particularly will prevent his denying of it.
Your thoughts that I should be well assured of mr Simsons character before I put the thing into his hands, is what I had taken care about, and to that purpose, by mr. Micklethwaite causd to be deliverd a letter to mr. Fl— informing him that D.S. was recommended to me, for that affaire, and begging his information if he knew him, ordring mr. L. not to send the papers forward to Scotland, till mr. M— or mr. Fl— should approve it. But to this day mr. M. can get no account of him from mr. Fl—, who not knowing his Character, has wrote to Scotland, but got no answer, and there it sticks†1 —Besides as by Dr. Hays†2 letter to mr. Simson you see I intended to join with him (supposing him not sufficiently (being but a writer) verst in marchants accounts) mr. Gilbert Stuart marchant in Edinburgh whom I well know, he having dwelt here many years.
As to the pain in my arm, it continues—theres nothing in the world to be seen on it, nor redness, nor swelling, nor any thing more than on my left arm—but a pain I feel in the outside of my arm, a hand breadth beneath the shoulder—
Some suppose it a cold got in it. And would drive it away with bags of hot sand. Others think it a sprain, and that that would inflame and do it harm. Now this I find that if I set too near the fire with that side, the heat causes pain tho I move it not, which makes me fear to use hot sand. Otherwise I have no pain but when I move it too far right out, too high, or bend it backwards, so that as cold as it has been, and is, it puts me to a sweat, to get up my breeches when I have been at the necessary place—Others will suppose it the gout, because I feel it sometimes in the place above described, and sometimes, upon stretching my self out in my bed, in the joint of my shoulder.
I have used some months a wild Cat skin in my wastcoat sleeve and shoulder—Then I tooke that out, and by advice of one that had experimented both, lined my wastcoat with red bay dyed in mather—And now lately I have applyed a playster some time since taught my wife, by a good acquaintance of hers; which she says has been long used with good success in their familie against
― 225 ―
the Gout, sprains or aches by Cold, and I think I gain ground, and can move my arm further with less pain than some time since—I am right glad the mum came so well to your hand, uncorrupted, and proves so good.
I remain obliged to you for this your kind letter with respect to both my person and estate, as also for the concern you have always shewd to my children—salute the family where you are. My Lord†1 is well lives next doore to me converses almost with nobody, walks out if faire weather twice a day, otherwise is always at home, tho never to be found there, but by my family—he had by some exercise in his chamber a little spraind his arm, but by swathing, as I also do mine, he is got shut of it, tho I cannot—I am
Dear friend
your humble and obliged friend and servant
BENJAMIN FURLY
My Lord salutes your self and that worthy family and says he has his health better than some time past,
My dearest who has been abroad this afternoone, which seldom happens without my Company, being come home, salutes you, Sir Fr. and my Lady, and desires you by all means to send her your plaister, if perhaps hers should faile: Or directions how to make it, which she rather begs, as an addition to her treasury
Address (written by Arent Furly?): For John Locke Esq. Att S. Francis Masham's At Oates Near Bishopstarford Essex
Endorsed by Locke: B Furly Mar 03/4 Answered Mar 11
March
3479. Anthony Collins to Locke, 2 March 1704 (3474, 3483)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 7, ff. 22-3. Answers nos. 3470 and 3474; answered by no. 3483.
Lincolns Inne. March 2/03/4
Sir
Though you are satisfied with my Endeavours to serve you as much as if I exactly answerd your purpose and out of your extreme civility prevent my Apologies in giving so good a turn as you do
― 226 ―
to my mistakes: Yet give me leave to be better pleasd when I can exactly comply with you, which Mr Churchils slowness gives me now an opportunity of doing with respect to the two treatises that I wrote you word were sent to the Press.
I have already in my answer to the Letter I sent you beg'd the favour to be inform'd upon those heads that you desire and I shall communicate to you what I shall receive. I wish for Dr E——s sake†1 the Letter I sent you could be shown to the Person of Quality mentiond in it as well as for the sake of the Person of Qualitys Friend.
The Bible I send you by Mr King costs three shillings in Quires.†2
I am told by a Physician that is of the Dispensary as well as Dr Pitt that his late book is nothing but an Invective. There is lately published Strauchii Brev. Chron. in English considerably improved by one Mr Milner that was esteem'd when I was at the University a Good Chronologer.†3
A day or two ago I received Mr Norris's second part of his Theory of the Ideal World.†4 I will let you know in a little time how far you are concernd in it.
That I may comply with you in finding out something for you to do for me at Oats; instead of writing myself to Mr Coste, I would beg the favour of you to give him my service and desire him to write to some Friend in Holland for an account of the Qualifications of the Magistrates and Officers there with respect to Religion (I believe Mr Le Clerc if it be thought proper to trouble him from his general insight into things both at home and abroad would give the most pertinent one with relation to the Occasion of the Enquiry) and that you would be pleas'd to give him such Instructions whereby matter enough may be obtain'd to see whether there is any just ground for a parallel to be made between the Practice in Holland and what was lately propos'd here by the
― 227 ―
Bill to prevent Occasional Conformity. I can make you no returns for the kindness and affection wherewith you treat me in your letters, that I can approve of my self, which are both beyond my desert and acknowledgment: but that I am with the utmost respect
Dear Sir
Your most Humble and most Obedient servant
A. COLLINS
Address: For John Locke Esq at Oats in Essex
Endorsed by Locke: A. Collins 2 Mar 03/4 Answered 6
3480. John Bonville to Locke, 2 March 1704 (3443, 3510)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 4, f. 137.
London March 2d: 1703/4
Honoured Cousin
Sir This†a- day I waited on mrs L——†1 And she paid me fiveteen pounds which was due Lady day Last, I thought It would been three yeares due Next Lady day,-†a I desired To see her Receits, which she did, The Last mony you Received your self, was July: 12: 1700, Then, she tells me, there was none due, but what was from Lady day, Too the Time you Received It your self, Then <Au>gust†b 7th: 1701: And march the 15: 1702/3, And This day march the 2d: 1703/4: thes three I have Received, soe I find she Is In the right, soe there will be Another yeare due next Lady day, which she tells me that It shall be soone paid, and Then for the futur she will make beter pay, I went to mr Locks Two or three Times before I Could Know, your debt, he Tould me It was three pounds which I have paid, This day I went throw the Temple, I Cald on my Cousin King, he Tells me he purpose to be with you next satarday, Deare Cousin I shall be very Glad to heare how you Are In Respect of your helth, The Ever Blesed God, Long preserve your Life In helth If It be his Blesed will, My wife Give you her Humble servis, she have been very Ill Above 3 munths. I did not
― 228 ―
Exspect her Life too this day, but now I bless God she Is pretie well Againe,
I Am Deare Cousin your Faithfull Kinsman and Humble Servant
JNO: BONVILLE
This Inclosed Came from mr Locke†1

3481. Cornelius Lyde to Locke, 4 March 1704 (3444, 3502)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 15, ff. 179-80.
Stanton weeke the 4th march 1703/4
Sir
Yours I Received of the 18th of february. as soone as I understoode by the tenant of the Falle of the trees I sent you word but had not opertunety to take notice of them what they weare but since I have beene at beluton there is 8 Elms downe by the house two Beale†2 trees and weshnut†3 and one Elm downe In the Ground at Carsbrook they are tymber but of the meanest sort the Elms some of them may measure 18 Inches square at the butt and may be neare some of them 20 Foote Long but what they will yeald I Cant tell theare is soe many downe In the Country I beleife we have above 200 downe In our parish and I dont heare of but two sold and they choise maiden Elms†4 and but for 6d per Foote the prisse that is Common In the Country is 4d per Foote wee being soe far from any towne I have spoke to one of bristoll to buie yours who have promised to Come if I send to him which I thinke is the best Corse to sell them if they will give something Answerable to the worth for to Cutt to board will Cost pretty deale and I doubt board will be as cheape after the rate as timber I†a- have Received of Geo: Horwood 10s in part and also of Jos: Hanny 10s with promises soe faire of the rest as soone as they can-†a (upon Examination I finde them Verry Poore and much in debt
― 229 ―
but doe hope they will pay the remainder) which have hinder me in takeing distres upon them will Gullock is much the same Condittion verry Poore but doe promise that he will pay part of the arears at Ladie day Sir I Am your obliegd friend and ser
COR: LYDE
you may dirrect to me to be Leaft at mr James Lydes merchant in Corne Street in Bristoll
Address: For John Locke Esquire At mr. Pawlings over against the Plough in Little Lincolns feilds London London
Postmark: MR 6
Endorsed by Locke: C: Lyde 4 Mar. 03/4 Answered Apr 7
3482. Locke to Edward Clarke, 6 (?) March 1704 (3472, 3521)
Rand, pp. 598-9. The date should perhaps be 4 March: see no. 3472 endorsement and Clarke's date of receipt. Answers no. 3472.
Oates, 6th March, 1703-4.
Dear Sir,
I am sorry that the letter which you returned me signed by the Bishop of Gloucester cost you so much trouble at a season when you had no time to spare. The refusal of the Baronet†1 not to do in the case what you desired of him I confess looks strange. But to ease you a little in that matter I can assure you it was not out of any personal regard to you, but out of I know not what shyness to meddle in that business. For never since you have been in the trust could he be got to go one step or speak one word to anybody concerning that affair, though he were in town and my Lady had nobody else but strangers to apply herself to and make use of in a matter they were strangers to, and it is possible had the same thoughts about that I find in his letter.
I know you see Sir Walter Yonge every day. I know not his lodgings, but besides that I must beg you to pay him £1 13s. 5d. for me, which I am in his debt. This matter being done, I beg the trouble of the enclosed, and if before you go out of town you will at your convenience pay Mr. Churchill an hundred pounds for my account, and then state the account between us and send it me signed, I will return to you the duplicate signed by me, that
― 230 ―
so it may be adjusted before you go into the country. I hope your health is improved with a daily increase. I am,
Dear Sir,
your most faithful and most humble servant,
J. LOCKE.
My Lady gives you her thanks and service.
Endorsed: Mr. Locke to pay Sir W. Yonge £1 13s. 5d., and to pay Mr. Churchill £100 etc. Received the 6th March, 1703. Answered the 17th in part.
3483. Locke to Anthony Collins, 6 March 1704 (3479, 3485)
Universitets Biblioteket, Uppsala, Waller collection. Transcribed from Xerox print. Copy, omitting everything after 'inclosed to her, and my most humble service', made by or for Desmaizeaux, in B.M., Add. MS. 4290, ff. 34-34v. Printed with the same omission and presumably from this text, in A Collection of Several Pieces, pp. 291-3. Answers no. 3479; answered by no. 3485.
Oates 6 Mar 03/4
Sir
Were you of Oxenford it self, bred under those sharp heads which were for damning my book, because of its discourageing the staple commodity of the place, which in my time was called hogshearing,†1 (which is, as I hear, given out for the cause of their decree) you could not bee a more subtil disputant than you are. You doe everything that I desire of you with the <utmost>†a care and concerne: and because I understand and accept it so, you contend that you are the party obleiged. This I think requires some of the most refined logique to make good, and if you will have me beleive it I think you must forbid me too to read my own book, and obleige me to take to my help more learned and scholastick notions. But the mischeif is I am too old to goe to school again, and too resty†2 now to study arts, how ever authorised or wherever taught, to impose upon my own understanding. Let me therefore if you please be sensible of your kindeness, and I give you leave to please your self with my interpreting them as I ought, as much as you think fit. For it would be hard in me to deny you so small
― 231 ―
a satisfaction, where I receive so great and real an advantage. To convince you that you are not like to loose, what you so much value, and is all you can expect in our commerce, I put into your hands a fresh oportunity of doeing something for me which I shall have reason to take well. I have this day sent back the bundle of books by Sir Francis. They are directed to you and will be left at Mr Churchils where it was most convenient for Sir Francis to leave them. I have taken what care I can to secure them from any harme that might threaten them in the cariage. For I should be extreamly vexed that books so curiously finished by your care should be in the least injurd, or loose any thing of their perfect beauty, till they came to their hands for whom they are designed. You have you see by your kind offer drawn upon your self a farther trouble with them, which was designed for my Cosin King. But he seting out for the circuit to morrow morning, I must beg you that may be my excuse for takeing this liberty with you. Molieres works are for the Countess of Peterborough, which I desire you to present to her from me with the inclosed to her and my most humble service. I think my Lord Peterboroughs house, where he now lives, is some where towards Berkeley house†1 wherever it be it is I conclude much easier to find than to goe to. doe not I beseech you think I am without some feeling of shame for sending you so far on my errand. Monsieur le Clerc's Harmony is for an other lady, which I shall direct you to, when you have taken a little breath and are recoverd from the other journey.
I am glad to hear the two treatises you mentioned are not yet in the press, there is time now to consider, how open and safe the way lies, and what is fit to be done
I hope you will receive a full, exact, and authentique account of particulars from the person you have writ to, he is the fitest person one could have wished to imploy in the case, for he has admirable good sense, and understands how to doe business.
I have deliverd your message to Mr Coste and the answer he here inclosed gives will I suppose so fully satisfie in the point I think enquired after, that perhaps there will be noe need for him to send into Holland. If any farther light be desired, which he cannot give you himself, he is ready to obey your commands and aske what other questions you please to direct.
― 232 ―
Sir
When you were here last you spoke to me concerning a sight of the Fundamental Constitutions of Carolina†1 and as I take it I promised you a copy. But you that never forget your freinds, are not so carefull of your self. If I did not then promise you a copy I now tell you I have one for you and if I did not send it with the other books by Sir Francis, you will see a reason when you see the book, but pray doe not forget it an other time
I thank you for the Bible you sent me. It is a good one of the kind. I wish I had an other of them so bound as that you sent me and an other of their ordinary binding but strong and that will open well.
What ever I have more to say to you tis time to put an end to this long letter, and respit any farther persecution at present from one that professes respect for you for in truth I am
Dear Sir
Your most humble and most affectionatly faithfull servant
J LOCKE
Address: For Anthony Collins Esquire at his chamber number 6 in Serle Court in Lincolns Inne London
Postmark: (G.P.O., defective?)
3484. William Grigg to Locke, 6 March 1704 (3214, 3605)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 10, ff. 188-9. Probably the letter forwarded by Awnsham Churchill on 11 March: p. 236.
Sir
Before I left London I had an Opportunity of speaking with Mr Laughton about the Catalogue of Greek Authors,†2 which he promis'd to gett ready at his Return to Cambridge; At the same time he spoke as if he design'd you a visit in his way hither. I was taken so ill at London with a cough that as soon as I was able to get on
― 233 ―
horseback I came the nearest way home. I thank God I am pretty well recover'd and hope to wait on you about the latter end of this month with the Greek Testament which is binding here and I believe will be as well done as it could have been any where.†1 There is in our press besides Suidas,†2 Anacreon,†3 the Geoponicks†4 and Cæsar's Commentaries.†5
I am
Sir
Your most oblig'd and humble servant
W: GRIGG
Jes: Coll: March 6th 1703/4
Address: For Mr Locke at Oates near Bishop Stortford in Essex
Endorsed by Locke: W: Grigg 6 Mar 03/4
3485. Anthony Collins to Locke, 9 March 1704 (3483, 3488) enclosing William Wright, jnr., to [Collins?], 3 March 1704 (3467, enclosure)
B.L., MSS. Locke c. 7, ff. 24-5, and c. 23, f. 110. Its date and contents indicate that Wright's was the letter forwarded by Collins. Collins's letter answers no. 3483 and is answered by no. 3488.
Lincolns Inne March 9/03/4
Sir
My subtility extends no further than to assure you, that you may employ me very freely in your business,†a that I am obligd to you for it, and is all founded on the affection and esteem I have for you. If I have distinguish'd away any obligations that you may think you have to me, it is that you may be without any feeling of shame when you send me on your errands, and not call what you
― 234 ―
think fit to write to me Persecution: when I know no greater Persecution than to be without the satisfaction of your letters now I am us'd to 'em. If you will overvalue my service and so spoil my Hogsheering, I design to use none of the Arts of the Men of Subtility and Distinction towards you: whether you agree or disagree to what I at any time say to you I shall never be disposd to take up with the dernier resort of the Hogsheerers.
The Books you sent by Sir Francis came safe to my hands and I this day deliver'd Molieres Works at the Countess of Peterboroughs with your service and letter. My Lady was somewhat indispos'd and so I did not see her.
I have inclosd another letter relating to the Proceedings you desire to be inform'd of, and I shall not fail to do my Endeavour to get you a full account of 'em.
I am oblig'd to you for Mr Costes letter. I shall return him my thanks for it this post with a Query or two for further Information.
I shall be glad to see the Fundamental Constitutions of Carolina and shall take the liberty to put you in mind to send it me.
Your wishes shall be comply'd with, and in a little time you may expect two Bibles according to your directions.
Whenever you have any business to be done pray think of the Subtilty of
Dear Sir
Your most Humble and most Obedient servant
A. COLLINS
Address: For John Locke Esq [at Oates, by Joslyn, Bishops Stortford]
Postmarks: MR —; GC

Sir
I have endeavoured to get a more particular account of those procedings, but cannot learne who were present, nor can get any copy of the first days order, the Register disowns that he knows or ever heard of the designe, others say that at the first meeting only a verball order was given to prepare an instrument for the purpose you have heard; I perceive all of them are ashamed of the matter, and think it no credit. to them to own there was ever any such

intention. if I meet with any particulars worth your notice you shall have the trouble of another letter from

Honoured Sir
In conformity to your desire by your most acceptable favour off the 3 I heere inclosed send you an account so as itts booked by my and j shall take care att the due time to discharge one hundred pounds on that bond as you order, owning my self in the mean time infinitely oblidged to you for the Use off itt so long; I doe nott doubt butt the Cacaonuts I last delivred to your order when made up will please you. the jnclosed I received last tuesday from Mr Furly†1 he <or>ders†a me to send his papers back by a good secure oppor<t>unity,†a so j intend to deliver m to morrow to a shipmaster that goes a Munday with the fleet; My parents I humbly thank you and we heere are all well and heartily wish you might enjoy your health as well as any off us. 3488. Locke to Anthony Collins, 13 March 1704 (3485, 3490)
The Henry E. Huntington Library, San Marino, California. Transcribed from photograph. Printed with omissions in A Collection of Several Pieces, pp. 293-6. Answers no. 3485; answered by no. 3493.
Oates 13 Mar 03/4
Sir
If the Disputers of this world were but half soe good at doeing as you the Mart of Logique and Syllogismes would noe doubt be the onely place for the yonge frye ad capiendum ingenii cultum†1 (Pardon me I beseech you this Scrap of Latin. My thoughts were in a place that authorises it, and one can not chop logique half soe well in unlearned modern vulgar languages.) But the traders in subtiltie have not your way of recommending it, by turning it into substantial solidity, whereby you prevaile so much on me, that I can scarc avoid being perswaded by you that when I send you of a jaunt beyond Picadilly†2 you are the person obleiged and I ought to expect thanks of you for it. Excuse me I intreat you, if, for decencys sake, I stop a little short of that: And let it satisfie you that I beleive, nay such is the power of your logique, that I can not help beleiveing that you spare noe pains for your freinds, and that you take a pleasure in doeing me kindness. All that remains for me to aske of you is to doe me this right in your turn to beleive I am not insensible of your favours, and know how to value such a friend.
Though you saw not my Lady when you deliverd Moliere and my letter at her house, yet had you no message from her? or did you not goe in or stay when you heard she was indisposed?
Mr le Clercs Harmony the remaining book, is for Mr Secretary Johnstouns Lady. I know not whether either he or she be in town or where they lodg, If you cannot without trouble learn that of Scotchmen that come in your way, a note by the penny post to Mrs Lockhart at her house in St. James street beyond petty-France†3
― 238 ―
will procure you that knowledg, and when you are informed that he is in town, the book sent to his lodging with a note to informe him that it is for his Lady from me will doe the business. So that for this errand I am glad that your servant is sufficient, without sending you. For you must give me leave some times on such occasions to be a little stingy and spareing of my favours
I perceive by the inclosed you did me the favour to send me That those worthy heads are not yet grown up to perfect infallibility. I am sorry however that their mighty thoughts wanted utterance. However I would very gladly know the true matter of fact and what was realy proposed, resolved, or done, this if possible I would be assured of that I might not be mistaken in what gratitude I ought to have.
Mr Limborch's Vita Episcopii will, I suppose, informe you concerning several particulars relateing to the matter whereof you have writ to Mr Coste. If you have not the book I will send it you, it is worth the reading. But I have said nothing of this to Mr Coste because I beleive it may be for your satisfaction to have your questions resolvd from some knowing men in Holland and therefore I would doe noe thing to hinder his sending them thither
As to the Fundamental Constitutions you say you shall take a liberty to mind me to send you, I must informe you that I have two Copys the one in ordinary paper and stichd the other in large paper in velom covers, guilt, fit to be kept as I suppose you intend. I would have you have the better of the two as fitter for my freind and your library, but it is a very large thin folio not easy to be caryed so that I scarce see any other way but that you must come and fetch it your self. Could I have contented my self with your haveing the stitchd one in ordinary paper I should have folded it up and sent it you before this time.
You baulkd my haveing the Bishop of St Asaphs sermon†1 by telling my Cosin King that I care not for sermons, and at the same time you send my Lady plays. This has raised a dispute between her Ladyship and me which of us two it is you think best of. Methinks you are of opinion that my Lady is well enough satisfied with the unreformed stage: But that I should be glad that
― 239 ―
some things were reformed in the pulpit it self. The result is that my Lady thinks it necessary for you to come and appease those broyles you have raisd in the family. I am Dear Sir Your most humble and most obleiged servant
J LOCKE
3489. Locke to Dr. (later Sir) Hans Sloane, 15 March 1704 (3473)
B.M., Sloane MS. 4039, ff. 259-70. Letter and weather-diary 9 December 1691—31 December 1692. The letter is on ff. 259-60, with the address on f. 260v. The first and last paragraphs of the letter are marked with crosses, probably not by Locke, and to indicate that they were not to be printed. The diary is a copy made by an amanuensis; it bears notes in Locke's hand. The diary and Locke's explanatory notes were published in Philosophical Transactions, xxiv (1705), no. 298, pp. 1917-37. Here the diary and all except one of the explanatory notes are omitted.
Oates 15 Mar. 1703/4
Sir
I herewith send you the copy of my register of the weather to the end of the year 1692. If you find it worth your acceptance (for†a- it is-†a bare matter of fact) my man shall if you desire it goe on to transcribe it and when he has done an other year I will send it you. If you think it fit to be published in your philosophical transactions tis fit I explain a litle to you some things in the table for the better understanding of it
I have often thought that if such a register as this or one that were better contrived with the help of some instruments that for exactness might be added, were kept in every county in England and so constantly published many things relateing to the air winds health fruitfulness etc might by a sagatious man be collected from them, and several rules and observations concerning the extent of winds and rain etc be in time established to the great advantage of man kinde. Whether you will think it worth the Royal Societies consideration and promotion I leave it to you. From this solitary one there is little to be collected. The pains is so little that I indulged my curiosity when it cost me the writeing not so much as a line a day For though besid<es>†b the ordinary observation which I set
― 240 ―
down commonly every morning, There seldom happend any rain snow or other remarkeable change which I did not set down but as you see a few letters and figures doe it, such as it is I send it you and if it may be of any use to any one I shall be glad. I am
Sir
Your most humble servant
JOHN LOCKE
If you intend to publish it in your philosophical Transaction If you will send me down the proof of the first half sheet of the register I will set it right and return it to you again. For it is not well as it is in Mr Boyles history of the air. You may direct it to Sir Francis Masham and Frank it
[Locke's note to the Diary:]
NB That the Thermoscope marked 4 which I made use of till 7 Mar 1701 was one of those sold by Mr Tompion where in 0 marked Temperate and the figures from thence increaseing both upwards and downwards shewd the increase of heat and cold from Temperate. Sept. 2. 1701 I began to use a new scaled Thermoscope adjusted to a scale made by Mr John Patrick†1 in the Old Bayly who places 0 at the top supposeing it to be the heat under the line and so the figures increase downwards with the increase of cold. Temperate being placed at 45. This Thermoscope is marked 5 in my register
Address: For Dr Sloane at his house in Southampton square London
3490. Anthony Collins to Locke, 15 March 1704 (3488, 3493)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 7, ff. 26-8. Answered by no. 3498.
Lincolns Inne. March. 15/03/4
Sir
The Liberty you allow me, and your kind acceptance of all my intentions towards you would take away all excuse from me,
― 241 ―
should I not keep the promise I made you, to send you some account of Mr Norris's New Book Intituled the 2d part of the Theory of the Ideal world,†1 so far as he concerns you in it; how much awe soever I am under from what I am able to say, as well as from what is fit to be said to you; which is somewhat abated by a New Liberty that I assume in telling you, you need not read what I say, after I have told you that I have here sent you the two bibles you desir'd,†2 and that I am ready for any further service in my power. Here therefore you may leave off, since it relates not to any business you are pleasd to employ me in.
In his Preface he attaques the late address, as he calls it, of a Well Known Author to disparage Syllogism; but I think his defence of Syllogism amounts to no more than this, that there is no argument but may be reduc'd to this form; which is all the Usefullness that he attributes to it. But this has no relation to the Question of its Usefullness to all those ends that are pretended to by Others, and is deny'd by the Well Known Author: Therefore his conclusion that to disparage Syllogism is as Paradoxical, as to speak against reasoning itself, cannot be allowed, till he has prov'd there can be no reasoning without the Use of that form; which I despair of, since he has not as yet offer'd to show it to be preferable to that more natural (as I think) order lay'd down in the Additions to the fourth Edition of the Essay of H.U.†3
He makes an exception to this Proposition in those Additions, that to infer is nothing but by vertue of one Proposition lay'd down as true to draw in another as true;†4 which is that no conclusion can follow from the premisal of one single proposition, which I wonder he should affirm, since the Intermediate proposition only shows the Inference to be rightly drawn from the premis'd proposition, and as it is not the proposition, from whence the Inference is drawn, so it is not excluded from being us'd to show the Inference to be just, as is plain from the words subsequent to the passage cited from the Additions etc.
In his first chap. he undertakes to answer the third L. to the Bishop of W. upon the subject of the Immateriality of Soul; and
― 242 ―
in the second maintains that Brutes are meer machines; which is such a contradiction to the testimony of our senses that it must disserve any Hypothesis it is allowed to be a consequence of. For if in order to the determination of this Question, Whether God can superadd to matter a power of thinking, it was agreed on both sides, that Brutes are Material, and what remaind to prevent its being resolv'd in the affirmative, was only the question, whether Brutes have any degree of thought, I should think the Controversy would be at an end, if there was no fondness for an Hypothesis, or resolution never to part with any opinion once received for truth: for the certainty that wee have of Brutes thinking, seems to me equall, to that I have of Mr: Ns thinking; and his Theory of the Ideal world may be as well accounted for from Waterworks etc. (by which he endeavours to show that Machines may perform all the Operations wee see perform'd by Brutes) as the Operations of Brutes.
I think Mr N. is more easy to be answer'd than others, because he writes more clearly and owns the consequences of his Hypothesis. He has not touch'd on the difficultys objected in the third L. to the Bishop of W. but only affirms that motion, vegetation, life etc are containd within the Idea of matter, at least by way of consequential result, and that thought not being contain'd within the Idea matter but being more excellent than Motion Vegetation life etc must belong to another kind of substance.†1 So that the very thing in dispute is taken for granted, and upon this supposition all that he says is founded; and yet if it be not prov'd that motion, vegetation, life etc belong
― 243 ―
to matter as Matter, but are superadded to it, then thought may be superadded too upon his own principles; since motion, vegetation, life etc must according to him be more excellent from their absence from matter than those qualitys contain'd in the Idea of Matter. That motion, vegetation, Life etc are not containd in the Idea of matter I think is plain, because there are parcels of it without 'em. Since I have taken this liberty with you I will propose a thought of my own to you to be consider'd: whether Quality's of different kinds can be said to be more or less excellent one than another, or can admit of any comparison one among another; since each, for all wee know, may be as proper for the end design'd with relation to the System of the Universe as the other. Nay whether a degree of the same quality can be said to be more or less excellent than a greater or less degree, but only with relation to accidental circumstances. One degree of motion may be more proper for a ship in a hasty voyage than a less degree, where there is not the same occasion; and it is with relation to these ends only the terms more or less excellent can be applied; which can be no rule to know whether they are more or less excellent one than another in the System of the Universe; since the same things this way may be at different times both more and less excellent. If this be true, I see not how motion and thinking can be compar'd, and consequently there can be no argument to exclude thinking from being superadded to matter, but must exclude motion too.
In the seventh Chapter he undertakes the examination of Mr Lockes principle of Sensation;†1 upon which I presume to propose to you a Scheme, that I a good while since thought of; whereby in a very little compass the Essay of H.U. may be vindicated against all opposers, and make a particular application of it to what is said by Mr Norris.
1 That there can be nothing advancd contradictory to the design of the Essay of Human Und. but on the Principle of Innate Ideas, in that sense they are refuted by the Author in the first book.
2 That Mr Lee,†2 Mr Lowde†3 etc., who pretend to maintain Innate Ideas, and by using the terms improperly, may seem to those, that will not examin, to be of a different opinion from him, do,
― 244 ―
notwithstanding their inclination to contradict him, state the Question so, as to leave nothing contrary to what the Author has said.
Mr Norris denying Innate Ideas†1 as well as the Author has advancd nothing contradictory to the design of the Essay. He defines Idea thus the Omniform Essence of God†2 and for the manner how Ideas become the objects of the Understanding, he affirms that wee see all things in God by which from what I have read in Mr N. and P. Malebranch, I understand, that upon the occasion of the presence of objects God exhibits Ideas to our Minds.†3 Whether this be true or false I undertake not to show, how much soever I am of the mind it is. I only observe that how curious soever some may think these enquirys are, as they carry no opposition to the design of the Essay, so neither are they pertinent to the Authors design: for how does the knowing the nature of Ideas concern one who only considers 'em as objects of the Understanding? and how does the question whether the senses are the real or occasional causes (for it is granted that wee can know nothing without em) of our Ideas contradict a matter of fact about the Original of our Ideas, that may be true, whether the senses be either the real or occasional causes of 'em. Malebranch and his followers as well as those that account for the mode of Human Understanding after the 4 other ways assignd by him do agree that wee cannot have Ideas of Sense without our senses and so agree to as much as the Author of H.U. affirms or is necessary to his design, and Mr Norris himself after all his answering at the end of his book confesses p 516 If Mr Locke means that our Ideas are occasion'd by the Impressions which are made by Objects on our organs of sense, that may be ordinarily true but then he observes that there is nothing peculiar in this account; for it may be applied to any Hypothesis, since let our Ideas be what they will, it may be said of them according to this sense that wee ordinarily have 'em from our senses.†4
But not to tire you any longer about matters you understand so well. I hope you will bear with this Impertinence since the liberty I take shall not be an introduction to any further trouble
― 245 ―
of this kind, for when I reflect on this I shall never have assurance enough to attaque you again after this manner.
I am Dear Sir with the utmost esteem and respect
Your most Humble and most Obedient Servant
A. COLLINS
My most humble service to all the family at Oats.
Endorsed by Locke: A Collins 15 Mar 03/4 Answered 21
3491. John Wheelock to Locke, 15 March 1704 (1642, 1998)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 23, ff. 91-2.
March the 15th 703 Beeby†1
Sir
My Lord†2 commanded me some time agoe to present his service to you And desird you to doe the same for him to all his Friends at Oates. In the same Letter he Orderd me to pay you to Christmas last.
In obediance to which Commands I desire you would please to send your Acquitence to mr King. I will waite upon him with the money and take up your Acquitence. Who am with all due regard
Sir
your most humble servant
JNO WHEELOCK
I shall be at Chelsey†3 in 8 or 10 days.
Address: To John Locke Esqre at Sir Francis Marshams at Oates in Essex present
Postmarks: MR 23; London Penny Post: W WES
Endorsed by Locke: J: Wheelock 15 Mar 03/4 Answered 27
