3492. Sir Walter Yonge to Locke, 15 March 1704 (3413)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 23, ff. 178-9.
Escott March 15th. 1703/4
Sir
It is now a week since I left London, and it was but the night before that I received from Mr Clark the favor of your kind letter of the 4th. instant and†a- 1l. 13s. 5d. which you were pleased to reimburse me for the Journals des sçavants:-†a I am glad an end is put to that small Affair (which has given you but too much trouble) in the way that you like best; and I esteeme my selfe obliged by your acceptance of that other trifle of a book with so kind a Remembrance of one, who values your Friendship as he ought, and is only unhappy that he can not sometimes enjoy the advantage of your conversation, as formerly when your better health permitted you to see the Town. Your not adventuring so far of late leaves us very little hopes, and yet I am unwilling to dispair, of seing you once in these parts, where you have many Friends; but to none of them would your Company be a more valued Favor then to
Sir
Your most affectionate humble servant
WALTER YONGE
My Wife, who is your very humble servant, resolving to spend the month of May in the Country, claimes your promise of sending her the receipt of the plaister, which you once bestowed upon her for a pain in my shoulder, She desires to make the salve, and will performe the condition on her part, hoping you will accept of a share of it.
Address: For John Locke Esq. At Oat's These.
Endorsed by Locke: Sir W: Yonge 15 Mar. 03/4 Answered 31
3493. Anthony Collins to Locke, 16 March 1704 (3490, 3495)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 7, ff. 29-30. Answers no. 3488; answered by no. 3498.
Lincolns Inne. March 16/03/4
Sir
I received yours of the 13th on Wenesday†1 just after I had sent
― 247 ―
the Two Bibles to the Bishopstartford Coach. The letter that went with 'em will surfeit you with chopping of Logick, wherefore I think wee had as good compromise the present difference between us. I will endeavour to believe that you ought to be oblig'd to me if you will use no reserves in your commands and reprove me when I am faulty in the execution of 'em. I wish I have not given you a just occasion to reprove me with respect to My Lady Peterborough. When I carried Moliere and your letter to her house, I was had into a Parlour where I staid about a quarter of an hour, before My Ladys woman came to me, to whom I deliver'd my Message. She told me my Lady was indispos'd and fal'n asleep and was not to be disturb'd, whereupon I desir'd that if my Lady pleas'd to return any message by me that notice might be given me and I would wait upon her for it. I wish with all my heart I had staid longer for an answer to have prevented my own uneasiness when I do not perform your commands as I ought, for you are too kind to allow me to think I can disoblige you.
This morning for the pleasure of a walk I deliverd Le Clercs Harmony to Mr Secr: Jonstoun for his Lady from you. I believe you will soon hear from him.
I am obligd to you for your offer to send me Limborchs Vita Episcopii. I have the book and will read it upon your recommendation.
When I have heard my Lady Masham and you chop logick upon the Question in dispute between you, I will undertake the charitable office of a Moderator and shall not think my pains ill bestowd though they succeed no better than in the Controversy of fire-making.
I am Dear Sir
Your most Humble and most Obedient Servant
A. COLLINS
My most humble service to all the family at Oats.
Address: For John Locke Esq [at Oates, by Joslyn, Bishops Stortford]
Endorsed by Locke: A Collins 16 Mar 03/4 Answered 21
― 248 ―
3494. Humfry Smith to Locke, 17 March 1704 (3321)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 18, ff. 132-3. Answers no. 3321.
Dartmouth Mar. 17. 1703.
Worthy Sir,
The Account you were pleasd to send me of Dr Pocock some months ago, I have often perusd with much satisfaction. Tho I have had many letters relating to his Character, from several Persons who were intimately acquainted with him, I must acknowledge that I have a clearer and more distinct Idea of his great Worth, from Yours, than from any other hand. I do now beg you therefore to accept my hearty thanks for the great assistance you have given me. And I should, as I was obliged, have done this much sooner, but that, ere I troubled you again, I was willing to perfect my Review of all the papers I have by me relating to the Doctor, in order to the proposing a few questions more about some Passages of his life, which are yet obscure to me. Some of these I will here add, and do request you, Sir, if it be not too great a trouble to you, to favour me with such Answers as you can give to them, when your leisure will permit you: as also with any other things concerning that Excellent man which may happen to come to mind: In doing which You will yet farther very much oblige
Sir, Your most humble servant
HUMF: SMITH.
1 Whether You ever heard who was his Tutor, first in Magd: Hall and after in Corpus Christi Colledge?
2 Whether he ever mentioned to you the assistance he had in Oriental Learning from Mr William Bedwell:†1 and if he did, where it was and about what time?
3 Whether you know any thing of his translating the Credentials of Ambassadors from Africa or the East? I find from a letter of Mr Williamson (afterward Sir Joseph Williamson) dated Apr.4. 1668 that an Epistle of the King of Morocco to King Charles 2. was then sent to him from Whitehall, for that purpose.†2
― 249 ―
4 Whether You can remember any thing as to the time, nature and circumstances of his long sickness, which was attended with the tumor in his thigh that causd his lameness?
5 Whether his paralytical trembling came on him with that sickness, or at what time, and on what occasion?
6 What Forreigners eminent for Learning, you can remember to have visited him, or Written to him? Most of the letters of such Persons to him, which he had preservd, having been accidentally lost since his death.
Address: For the much Esteemed Mr John Lock
Endorsed by Locke: H. Smith 17 Mar 03/4 Answered May. 12.
Locke to Anthony Collins, 21 March 1704
The letter was not completed until 24 March, and is placed under that date: no. 3498.
3495. Anthony Collins to Locke, 21 March 1704 (3493, 3498)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 7, ff. 31-2. Answered by no. 3498.
Lincolns Inne. March. 21/03/4
Sir
I am glad that you know me so well as to think I make you no compliment when I offer you my service. I wish for my own sake as well as yours that I could be more usefull to you though my affection to you will never allow me to think I can do you such service as will bear any proportion to what you deserve of me.
I met with the Epistola de Tolerantiâ in quires and have sent it to you sticht up by my self; if you have a mind to have one bound you may please to return this to me, or give me orders for another. The Person of whom I bought this has near 20 of 'em by him.†1 You will pardon me if I tell you, that I believe I can read so much of the Title page as to find out the Authors name and his country.†2
I have look'd over the Article Rorarius in Mr Bayles dictionary†3 which is a very long one but very entertaining. it allmost all relates
― 250 ―
to the question Whether Brutes have reason? on the occasion of a book of Rorarius's wherein is endeavour'd to be prov'd that animalia bruta ratione utantur melius homine.†1 Your Essay is only mentiond in one place of this Article after this manner (Le Docteur Locke s'est declaré contre ceux qui ne donnent point aux bêtes le raisonnement. Vous allez voir en quoi consiste, selon lui, la diference entre les hommes et les bêtes.) and then quotes B.2. C.11 p. 170 and 171 of Mr Costes translation.†2 I need not transcribe the passages from Mr Bs Dictionary since you will find 'em in the pages referr'd to. He adds in the margin. (C'est un excellent ouvrage, et qui meritoit d'être traduit en Francois aussi bien qu'il l'a eté par Mr Coste.) Your Essay is mentiond in two other places of his dictionary, which I will give you an account of if you desire it.†3 The second Edition is sold for four pound ten shillings. I am with the utmost respect
Dear Sir
Your most Humble and most Obedient servant.
A. COLLINS.
Address: For John Locke Esq at Oats in Essex.
Endorsed by Locke: A Collins 21 Mar 03/4 Answered 24
3496. Francis Limborch to Locke, 23 March 1704 (3486, 3509)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 13, ff. 232-3.
London the 23 March 1703/4
Honoured Sir
I received butt last Friday your most acceptable favour off the 13th. Upon the receit of your direction I shall pay the money where you please to order me, that letter for mr Furly I jnclosed in mine the very same day. I am very much satisfied to finde the Cacao nuts please you, and iff you incline to have any more off
― 251 ―
the same they shall be att your service, My father hath had lately his intermission in his puls againe and was bleeded for itt, butt within 3 or 4 dayes perfectly well againe, I hope in god he may Continue so long and wish most sincerely the same to you, j was in hopes off getting some stockfish butt could nott have now expect some by the first fleet which by this faire wind we expect every day when j shall not faill to provide you; My Wife joynes her most humble respects with these off
Honoured Sir
Your most oblidged humble servant
FRAN LIMBORCH
I hope the books I sent yesterday seavennight by the butchers are come safe to your hands.
Address: For John Locke Esquire These
Endorsed by Locke: F: Limborch 23 Mar 03/4 Answered Mar. 27
3497. James Johnstoun to Locke, 23 March 1704 (3379)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 12, ff. 44a-44b.
London 23 March 70 4/3
Sir
I have past this winter with a constant resolution to see you which keept me from writing to you; but when I was going twise, I found others were before me and of late some Lords friends or relations of mine are here from Scotland to beg home their own plot, which they complain they are robbed of, and they in a manner keep me prisoner.†1 I have not so much as been at Twittenham one night these 4 moneths. I am mighty glad to hear that there is ground to hope, we shall have one year more of you. Mrs Johnstoun gives you many thancks for your present by Mr Collins;†2 she says you could not have given her better help to follow you in case you goe before her for she has been ill this winter having a sorte of flying gout, which is one day in her head and another day in her foot; she gives her compliments to My Lady Masham and bids me tell you both, that when the ways are good, if I doe not goe, she will make the visit for me. I should be verry easy, were her
― 252 ―
health as good as mine; for mine is dayly better, but in order to what I know not; if it be to inable me to help to recover the almost irrecoverable state of affairs in Scotland, I shall value it the more, but it is my lott never to be called for, till matters be desperate; In short I have a new argument that the p.p. of wales†1 is not a Stewart, for he is luckier then they use to be; for this winter our friends, his enemies here have done him more service as to his pretensions in Scotland then his enemies could ever have done. The whole proceedings about the Scotch plott and the addresse to the Q. to settle the succession there†2 with Atwoods ridiculous book†3 and such other Incidents one would thinck were all directed by the Court of St Germains to hinder the settling of it; for it is equally true that the Lords are heartily and sincerely for the settling of it and that nothing can hinder it more then what they have done, so God in his providence plays (ludit) with humane affairs says Grotius having observed that the dutch in their wars had least successe, when they had most hopes of it†4 — To break off from such a malancholy subject I hope you still read books to divert you and therefor pray get the True-Tom double†5 (I know not hou to send you it) it is written by some unknown pen, which rises far above the most part of writers both for stile and thought: and so entertaining that My Lady Masham and you will owe me a great dale of pleasure when you read it.
As to news; I have alwyse had a good opinion of our Kingmaking in Spain; Mr Fletcher would have gone with Coll. Stanhope if he would have stopt at unmaking.†6 As to Italy, unlesse the
― 253 ―
Emperor can augment the body of men at Ostiglia to draw the French that way, I fear the D. of Savoye†1 will have his hands full, for the French will come over the Alps and be on his back—I consider not Flanders—The French intend to attack the lines of Stolhoffen and will carry them if the troops of Hanover be not nou there; if so they will take Franckfort and Nuremberg; I trust to this weather—the Emperor still persecutes in Silesia farewell.
In the monethly register for February see a curious paper the french K's letter to the Pope against the D. of Savoye.†2
Endorsed by Locke: Answered May.5
3498. Locke to Anthony Collins, 21 [and 24] March 1704 (3495, 3500)
The Hyde Collection, Four Oaks Farm, Somerville, New Jersey. Transcribed from photograph. Printed, omitting the postscript, in A Collection of Several Pieces, pp. 296-302. The main part of the letter answers nos. 3490 and 3493, and was written on 21 March, as their endorsements show. The postscript answers no. 3495; according to Locke's endorsement he answered it on 24 March; this date, a Friday, tallies with the London postmark 27 March. The letter is answered by no. 3500.
Oates 21 Mar 03/4
Give me leave to tell you Sir that you are mistaken in me. I am not a yonge Lady a beauty and a fortune. And unlesse you thought me all this and designd your addresses to me how is it possible you should be affraid you acquitted not your self well in my commission beyond Piccadilly. Your waiting in the parler†a a quarter of an hour was more than any reasonable man could demand of you and if either of us ought to be troubled in the case, it is I because you did so much, and not you because you did too little. But the reality of your freindship has so blended our concernes into one that you will not permit me to observe whether I doe or receive the favour in what passes between us, and I am almost perswaded by you to beleive that siting here by the fire I trudg up and down for you in London. Give me leave however to thank you as if you had deliverd Mr le Clercs harmony to
― 254 ―
Mr Secretary Johnstoun for me and sent me the two bibles which I received. As for the rummageing over Mr Ns late book†1 I will be sworne tis Not I have done that for however I may be mistaken in what passes without me I am infallible in what passes in my owne mind, and I am sure the Ideas that are put together in your letter out of him, were never so in my thoughts til I saw them there, What did I say, Put Ideas together, I aske your pardon tis put words together without Ideas, Just as I should suspect I did if I should say you disparaged a very good strait ruler I had if you told me <it>†a would not inable me to write sense though it were very good and usefull to shew me whether I writ strait or noe.
Men of Mr Ns way seem to me to decree rather than to argue.†2 They against all evidence of sense and reason decree Brutes to be machins onely because their hypothesis requires it, and then with a like authority suppose, as you rightly observe, what they should prove viz that whatsoever thinks is immaterial. Cogitation says Mr N is more excellent than motion or vegetation and therefore must belong to an other substance than that of matter in the Idea whereof motion and vegetation are conteined. This latter part I think would be hard for him to prove viz that motion and vegetation are conteined in the Idea of the substance of matter But to let that pass, at present, I aske whether if this way of argueing be good, it will not turn upon him thus? If the Idea of a spirit doe not comprehend motion and vegetation, then they must belong to an other substance than a spirit and therefor are more excellent than cogitation or the affections of a spirit, for if its greater excellency proves any mode or affection to belong to an other substance, will not its belonging to an other substance by the same rule prove it to be more excellent. But this is onely to deale with these men of Logick and subtilty in their own way, which use the terme excellent†3 to prove a material question by without haveing as you remarke a clear and determined Idea of what they meane by more or less excellent. But not to wast your time in playing with the arguments of men that examine not strictly the meaning of the words they use I†b- will shew-†b you the fallicy whereby they impose
― 255 ―
on themselves, for such talkers commonly cozen them selves as well as others. Cogitation say they is not comprehended in the Idea of extention and solidity, for that is it which they mean when they say the Idea of Matter, from whence they might conclude right that cogitation belongs not to extension and solidity or is not included in either of them or both together but this is not the consequence that they draw, but infer a conclusion that is not conteined in the premises and is quite besides them, as Mr N if he would make use of syllogisme to its proper purpose might see. Extension and Solidity we have the Ideas of and see that cogitation has no necessary connection with them nor has any consequential result from them, and therefor is not a proper affection of extension and solidity nor does naturaly belong to them, but how does it follow from hence that it may not be made an affection of or be annexed to that substance which is vested with solidity and extension, of this substance we have noe Idea that excludes cogitation any more than solidity. their conclusion therefor should be the exclusion of cogitation from the substance of matter and not from the other affections of that substance. But they either over look this which is the true state of that argument or else avoid <to>†a set it in its clear light least it shew too plainly that their great argument either proves no thing or if it does it is against them.
What you say about my Essay of H U that no thing can be advanced against it but upon the principle of Innate Ideas is certainly so and therefore all that doe not argue against from innate Ideas in the sense I speake of innate Ideas, though they make a noise against me yet at last they so draw and twist their improper ways of speaking which have the appearance and sound of contradiction to me that at last they state the question so as to leave noe contradiction in it to my Essay as you have observed in Mr Lee and Mr Lowde and Mr N in his late treatise. It is reward enough for the writeing my book to have the approbation of one such a reader as you are. You have done me and my book a great honour in haveing bestowd so much of your thoughts upon it, you have a comprehensive knowledg of it, and doe not stick in the incidents which I find many people doe, which whether true or false make nothing to the main designe of the essay which lies in a little compass and yet I hope may be of great use to those who see and follow that plain and easy method of nature to carry them the
― 256 ―
Shortest and clearest way to Knowledg. pardon me this vanity it was with a designe of enquireing into the nature and powers of the understanding that I writ it, and nothing but the hope that it might doe some service to truth and knowledg could excuse the publishing of it
I know not whether I ever shewd you an occasional scatch of mine about Seeing all things in god†1 if I did not, If it please god I live to see you here again I will shew it you and some other things. If you will let me know before hand when you designe us that favour it will be an addition to it. I beg your pardon for <hold>ing†a you so long from some better imployment, I doe not you see willingly quit your conversation. If you were nearer me you would see it more for I am
Dear Sir
Your most affectionate most humble and most obleiged servant
J LOCKE
Just as I was goeing to seale this I received yours of the 21 wherein I find new matter of thanks. I would be glad you would buy me two more of the Epistola de Tolerantia in quires for me and then tye them up in a paper and lay them by for me. My Lady returns you many thanks with her humble service. I have not time to read over this long letter to you and so must leave you to correct the faults in it
Address: For Anthony Collins Esq at his chamber Numb: 6 in Serle Court in Lincolns Inne London
Postmark: MR 27
Marked on cover: +H: N: (?)
3499. John Sowter to Locke, 25 March 1704
B.L., MS. Locke c. 18, ff. 177-8. The writer was of Swedish extraction or had some Swedish connection; and was a merchant or perhaps a goldsmith in Exeter: W. Nisser, Michael Dahl, etc., 1927, pp. 8, 173, etc.
London 25 March 1704
Sir
You may please to remember I had some discourse with you as you sate for your picture to Mr Dahl;†2 and afterward at your
― 257 ―
Lodgings about the Manufacture of Copper etc: But I could not from soe smale an Acquaintance presume to trouble you with this, and obtaine the favour of an Answer, And therefore desired My Good freind Mr King to introduce me, which is the bussines of the enclosed.
The Occassion of this Sir is, That a very studious thoughtfull, freind of mine about 100 miles from London is soe very positive that he has found out the Longitude soe as to be usefull to Navigation; that I cann noe longer refuse him my assistance, in first enforming my self and then him, If there be not severall Donations in England and else where allready setled, or promised to be given to him or them that shall first discover the Longitude soe as to be of use to Navigators etc:†1 And if soe how to take such Measures as may secure the rewards; I know Sir you cann if your time will permit, answer me hereto, and I doubt not but you will, with your first leasure.
I would advise my freind for secureing the rewards, to make the discovery to a Second able skillfull person that should first examine into the truth of his pretensions; and if they were found good to let this second person share the reward a quarter or a third, This my freind is willing to doe, to such a person as your self, but he dare not trust (as he saies) any but my Lord Pembroke or Mr. Lock.
pray favour me Sir with a Supposition that he has made this discovery of Longitude; and your thoughts in answer hereto and you will greatly oblige
Sir Your most humble Servant
JOHN SOWTER
My Lettres has noe other adress then Merchant in London
Address: For John Locke Esqr. to be left at Mr Jocelines In Bishopstafford.
Postmark: MR 25
Endorsed by Locke: John Sowter 25 Mar 04 Answered 8
― 258 ―
3500. Anthony Collins to Locke, 30 March 1704 (3498, 3504)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 7, ff. 33-4. Answers no. 3498; answered by no. 3504.
Lincolns Inne March. 30/04
Sir
Give me leave to tell you: that I am not mistaken in you, for though you are not a Young Lady a Beauty and a fortune Yet I have more satisfaction and pleasure in your friendship than I can hope for from those qualifications only, and therefore you may easily conceive it possible for me to be afraid that I do not acquit my self well in your commissions. If I fall into the strain of a Lover, it is from motives that arise within me, and that are not in my power to prevent. You must cease to be what you are; nay you must never have been what you have been, to destroy that disposition of mind that has so much merit for its foundation.
I rejoyce that our concernes are blended into one, for I cannot Imagine, that you will lay aside any business in Town when you can do it yourself and at the same time sit by the fire at Oats. Though you have some grudgings when you employ me, yet sure you will have none when you employ yourself, who while in Town are so Young Lusty, and Willing.
I am extreamly pleas'd with those parts of your Letter that relate to Mr Ns Book. You have cleard what I had puzled and even made me understand my own thoughts better than I did before, and have given me a new argument against presuming so far again upon the liberty you allow me.
I shall be oblig'd to you for a sight of any thing that you have thought fit to write, and do esteem that a sufficient motive for a longer Journey than it is to Oats: but when I have so many I think my self oblig'd to lay hold on the first Opportunity (which I am affraid will not be this month yet, having engag'd to visit two or 3 friends at Oxford about ten days hence where I shall not forget any particulars of your late letters to me) of declaring by word of mouth to you how much I am
Dear Sir
Your most Humble and most Obedient Servant
A. COLLINS
Address: For John Locke Esq [at Oates, by Joslyn, Bishops Stortford]
Endorsed by Locke: A: Collins 30 Mar. 04 Answered Apr. 3.
― 259 ―
3501. Edward Fowler, bishop of Gloucester, to Locke, 30 March 1704 (2814, 3633)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 8, ff. 147-8.
Sir
What with indispositions and hurrys, I have too long delayd giving you thanks for yours: The sight of your hand was very wellcome. Blessed be God that I have a no more uncomfortable Account of you. I hope we shal now soon see an End of this very worst of weathers. I know not that I ever so longed to visit Oates. It will be a great Uneasiness to me to be disappointed of my hope of being with you (for 2 or 3 days) when the ways are Tolerable. Sir Francis has been naught this Session; I never had him at my Table. He is not one of those Commons that is Angry with another House. You'l be surprized to see, how the Lords have behaved themselvs at winding up the bottoms. All's to be Printed. The 7 Lords Report of the Plot too. There's an Admirable Reply to the Commons Answer to our Representation.†1 With our humble and Affectionate Services to yourself, and my good Lady, et. Praying most heartily for a Renewal of your Strength; which will be most Wellcome to,
Sir
Your Affectionate, Humble Servant,
E G
March 30 — 704
Humble Services to Sir Francis, if with you; or when he comes.
Address: To John Lock Esqr [at Oates, by Joslyn, Bishops Stortford] E Glocest. frank
Postmark: MR 30

3502. Cornelius Lyde to Locke, 30 March 1704 (3481)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 15, ff. 181-2.
Stanton weeke the 30th Mard: 1704
Sir
Yours I Received of the 4th Instant and also one of the 18th of February to which I sent you an account of your trees and also
― 260 ―
of the debts due from Gullock Hanny and horwood which I hope you have Received. for the trees I have had a chepman but will give but 4d per Foote they being but ordinary timber but am not willing to dispose of them soe without your order. as in relation of the Taxes you mention in your Last the Reson that it is more then was in the former yeare the Tax is now brought upon Every tything the same that it was 1692 according to the Act: Parliament soe some tythings are taxd more then they was in the former yeare for in 92 they taxd them selfes upon oath Soe the Commissioners have agreede to raise the Tax that way it being according to the Act of Parl†1 and as to the Lords rent the tything of Publo Taxd the Lord for all his rents theare soe the tenants Cold not Expect An abatement they being soe much the Less chargd as was chargd upon the Lord but the Last yeare the Lords baylie wold not suffer the tenants to charg the Lord for his rents soe the tenants did According to the Act take up after the rate of 4s. per li. I have this day spoke with John Pearce that married Nat: Sumers daughter aboute the furzes who ses that they are verry much damnified by the Poore Inhabitants and the unruely boyes in makein bonfires upon dayes of rejoceing, he thinke they are verry deare at the rent they give now and will not give more for it. I finde if they dont have it there is noe body will theare being noe bakers at Pensford but them selfes and Tho: Summers who gave downe the trade to his son. I did in my Last give you an account of 10s I Received of Hanny and 10s of horwood and with promises to pay the rest in short time which promise they have renewed this day and will Gullock doe promise to pay what is behinde but they are all verry poore and much in debt as I understand. Sir I understand that the Queene have setled the tenthes to augment the maintenance†a of Poore vicors†2 now Sir you Know Publo and St Thomas the maintenance†a is soe Little
― 261 ―
not above, one 16 li per†a annum†a and the other 4 li that there have beene noe setled minister many yeares†1 if you Cold doe them any Kindnes in that afaire I hope it may be a meanes of good to the place for now it is verry populus and verry wicked with all due Respect wishing you all Good I Remaine your obligd friend and ser
COR: LYDE
I did in my Last Let you Know how to direct to me at mr James Lydes merchant in Corn Street Bristoll
Address: These For John Locke Esquire Att mr. Pawlings over against the Plough in Little Lincolns feilds London
Postmark: AP 3
Endorsed by Locke: C: Lyde 30 Mar 04 Answered Apr: 7
3503. Arent Furly and Benjamin Furly to Locke, 31 March/11 April 1704 (3424, 3626; 3478, 3597)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 9, ff. 198-9. Sent through Francis Limborch: pp. 262, 268.
[Arent:]
Rotterdam 11 Aprill 1704
Sir
The occasion of these few lines is to acquaint you, that after a diligent inquiry At the most of our booksellers for the little book you wrote for, to witt Epistola de Tolerantia,†2 I have not been able to meet with it any where: but Accidently speaking with Mr. Bayle about it, he promis'd me, he would do his endeavours to get you said book: At last he has Found it in the library of a Friend of his, who, at his request, gave it him for what it cost him, which is a small triffle, not worth taking notice of: The book is at present yet at Mr. Bayle's, (who desired me to give his humble service to you) from whence I shall fetch it and send it per First opportunity to Mr. Limborch. Sir If I am capable of doing you any Further service, in buying of any books in Auction, or any thing elce, I shall think my self very happy to be honoured with Your
― 262 ―
Commands. After my humble and due Respects to your Good self and All at Oates I subscribe my self
Sir Your most humble and most obedient servant
ARENT FURLY
Pray sir excuse this scribling, it being in haste and very late at Night.
[Benjamin Furly:]
Rotterdam 11 April 1704
Dear Sir
I received the honour of yours of 11/22 past, and accordingly have since received my papers from mr Limborch, and wrote another, to the purport of yours to Sir John.†1 But now I am at a stay how to send it to him, for want of a character of mr. D. Simpson which I had to mr. Flr.†a about, but to this day, receive no answer— And mr. Simpson writes that that Gentlemans state grows worse and worse. I have been again this day sollicited on behalf of mr Hubers of uytrecht, for your judgment about the use of the Irish slates, which are with this Convoy arrivd for him, I wrote to you about it the 8/19 february, whereas the letter you answer, which it seems was forgot to be dated, was wrote the 11 march†2 — and doubtless was sent under covert of mr Limborch as that you answer, and this comes — I hope it did not miscarry, but lest it should, the onely import of it, was, to desire on behalf of that worthy Gentleman, your Judgment concerning the vertue of Irish slate, in what cases, and how it is to be used, and particularly what effects you have at any time seen of it, to which I intreat your answer; As to my arme, it is much mended by my wives plaister, so that I can now put on my cloths with much adoe without help, but I cannot bend it back so as to pull them off, By the continuance of the plaister my arm came to itch so, and my skin to grow so red and tender that I have left it off, but am afraid I must make use of it again, in this Cold weather we have got of anew. My wife desires, as the plaister you have sent to mr. Limborch, so the receit of it, and information in what cases it is serviceable, for she desires to increase in all serviceable knowledge, and
― 263 ―
by it she helps many. My Lord†1 is very well, he honoured us with a visit 2 or 3 days agoe. He is mightily retired, visits scarce anybody, nor receivs any but from my family, and sometimes from mr. Bayle who is his Counsellor for books in Auctions—pray give my wives mine, and my sons service to Sir F. my lady and the rest of that honourable family, I am
Dear Sir
your obliged humble servant
BENJAMIN FURLY
My service to mr Coste
Address (written by Arent Furly): For John Locke Esquire Att Sir Francis Masham's Memb: of Parliam At Oates Near Bisshopstarford Essex
Endorsed by Locke: B: Furly 11 Apr 04 Answered 15

3504. Locke to Anthony Collins, 3 April 1704 (3500, 3506)
The Carl H. Pforzheimer Library, New York. Transcribed from photostat. Part printed in A Collection of Several Pieces, pp. 303-5. Answers no. 3500; answered by no. 3506.
Oates 3 Apr. 04
In good sooth Sir you are an obstinate lover, there is no helpe for it you must carry your point. Only give me leave to tell you that I doe not like the puleing fit you fall into at the lower end of the page, where you tell me I have given you an argument against presumeing so far again upon the liberty I allow you. That is to say you may give me books, you may buy books for me, you may get books bound for me, you may trudg up and down with them on my errant to Ladys, but my book you may not presume to read, use your judgment about, and talke to me freely of. Though I know noebody that understands it so well, nor can give me better light concerning it. Away with this squeamishness I beseech and be assured that among the many good offices you dayly doe for me in London there is none whereby I shall reap so much profit and pleasure as your studying for me and let us both without scruple or reserve help one an other on the best we can in the way to truth and knowledg; And when ever you find me presume that I know all that belongs to the subject of my owne book and disdain
― 264 ―
to receive light and instruction from an other though of a much lower forme than you conclude that I am an arrant coxcombe and know noething at all.
You will see by the inclosed†1 that I can find business for you at Oxford as well as at London. I have left it open that you may read it before you seale and deliver it. In it you will see what he writ me on that affair. He is well acquainted among them in the university and if he has not, may be prevaild on by you to fish out the bottom of that matter and informe you in all the particulars of it But you must not take his conjectures for matters of fact but know his authors for any matter of fact he affirms to you. You will think I intend to engage you in a thousand disputes with him. Quite the contrary. You may avoid all dispute with him if you will but say after him, though you put him upon things that shew you question all he says
Dr Gastrel sent me his sermon upon the fast for the Tempest, It is indeed an excellent good one, and this will serve me to some purpose when my Lady you and I meet but this is not a place for that controversie. The Doctor You will finde either in Lincolns Inne or Christchurch. I beg you to give him my most humble service and thanks for his most accepta.†a present and let him know I read it over with extreme satisfaction.†2 When my name is up between you, you may as from your self enquire concerning the particulars of the Oxford affair perhaps he will be free in it for I take him to be a friend to me and my book too.
If Mr Wynne of Jesus Colledg who epitomised my book be in the University tis like you will see him and talke with him of that matter. Pray give him my service. But be sure forget me not with all manner of respect to Mr W—†3 for whom I have as I ought a very peculiar esteem. I hope you will be pleased with me for you see I have cut out worke for you and that is all that is left for me to doe to obleig you. I am
Dear Sir
Your most humble and most obleiged servant
J LOCKE
― 265 ―
Pray doe me the favour to send me word where Mr Secretary Johnstown lodges that I may know how to direct a letter to him
I shall envy: Oxford if it keeps you too long from Oates
Address: For Anthony Collins Esq at his chamber Numb: 6 in Serle Court in Lincolns Inne London
Postmark: — 5

3511. [James Tyrrell] to Locke, [c. 17 April 1704] (3477, 3575)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 22, ff. 167-8. Perhaps written as a draft; many alterations, etc. Ink faded and worn. Printed in King, pp. 192-3, with omissions, and a sentence from no. 3477 added at the end. Apart from Locke's endorsement the writer is identifiable by the writing and the contents. Date: Locke wrote to Tyrrell on 3 April: no. 3477, endorsement. His letter is identifiable as that which Collins hoped to deliver to Tyrrell on 13 April: pp. 264, 265. Tyrrell wrote the present letter presumably within the next few days: It was perhaps carried to Oates by Collins, who was there early in May, and perhaps earlier: pp. 258 (intention to visit), 284-5.
Dear Sir
In answere to yours received by our good Freind Mr: C.†1 the best Information wee†2 can give you concerning the forbiding the reading of your Essay is as follows: that in the begining of November last there was a meeting of the Heads of Houses then in Town. The†a- certain I cannot tell it not being registred or any publick Act, onely it was there-†a proposd by Dr: Mill†3 and seconded by Dr: Maunder†4 that there was a great decay of Logical Exercises in the university, which could not be attributed to any thing so much as the new Philosophy which was too much read; and in particular your Book and Le Clercs Philosophy, against which it was offred that a Programma should <be>†b publishd forbiding all Tutors to read them to their Pupils: this was like at first to have passed: till it was opposed by some others there present and particularly by Dr: Dunstar,†c †5 who not onely vindicated your book, but sayd that he thought the making the Programma
― 270 ―
would do more harm then good, first by making too much noise abroad as if the University went about to forbid the reading of all Philosophy save that of Aristotle next that he thought that instead of the end proposd it would make yong men more desirous to buy and read those books when they were once forbid then they were before: then at another meeting their Resolution upon the whole was, that upon Dr: Edwards's proposeal,†1 they agreed instead of a Programma that†a all Heads houses should give The Tutors private Instructions not to read those Books to their Pupils, and to prevent their doing it by themselves as much as lay in their power: and yet I do <no>t†b find after all that any such thing has bin put in Execution in those Colledges in which I have any acquaintance, as par<ti>cularly†c in university, Maudlins, New Colledg and Jesus, all which have Heads that are sufficiently for the High Charch Party:†2 so that I beleive they finding it like to have little†d Effect they have thought fit to let it drop:†e and besides this there are to my knowledg several of that Party who having read your book themselves do not onely very much approve of it but allso encourage their Pupils after they have done with their Logick, as particularly Mr: Sacheverel of Maudlins†3 as I am <told>†f by one of was, not to mention Mr. Percyval the son of your old Acquaintance, who when he was of Christ Church (being lately gone into Ireland) not onely read your book himself but encouraged his Pupils to do it.†4 and now you will be best able by what wee have written what seems best for you to do in the affair about the books you writ to me you once intended to bestow:
Endorsed by Locke: J: Tyrrel April. 04
― 271 ―

3513. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 21 April 1704 (i) (3471, 3514)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 363-4.
Oates 21 Apr. 04
Dear Cosin
The weather has been here of late as you describe it in yours of 15th from Exeter extremly fine and warm, but I find it not, as you hope, at all Balsamick to my lungs. They goe on the same train and if I mistake not the reconing will soon be at an end
Pray look over Sir Francis's late morgage to me which you left in your mans hands to be finished when you went out of town, that if there be any fault or mistake in it, you may bring it down with you to be remedied presently
I sent to your Cosin Freke the last week to turn the Malt ticket whereof you left a list with me into money presently. He promised me it should be done this week and the money paid to Mr Churchill as I orderd it. Pray inform your self what is done in it.
Bring me if you please 40 or 50 Guineas with you. if you have not so many to spare by you you may take them of Mr Churchil. Delay not to come for besides that I desire to see you, I think I am not like to be here long. I am Dear Cosin
Your most affectionate Cosin and humble servant
J LOCKE
If he has not already done it pray press your Cosin Freke to dispatch that business
Address: For Peter King Esqr member of Parliament at his chamber in the Inner Temple. To be left at Mr Potmans in the Middle Temple London Frank.
Endorsed by King: Mr Locke April 21. 1704.

Dear Cosin
If the money the malt tickets were sold for by Mr Freke be paid as was promised on Monday or Tuesday next to Mr Churchil that matter is at an end and you need say noe thing of it. 3516. John Bonville to Locke, 22 April 1704 (3510, 3552)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 4, f. 139.
London Aprill 22th: 1704
Honoured Cousin
Sir yours of the 15 Instant I Received, I Am heartely vext About the 6 l of powder, I Am sur you are wronged Either by Lenham, or sum Others, I Am sur non can buy of The same under 18d per l, The more reson I Am to beleeve you are wronged, Is, because you mention Two sorts: my wife doe say for A Truth It was all Taken out of one Glace, for she stood by while It was waide, I would not Take Any Noteis of It To Lenham This day, Tell I have heard from you, I doe beleeve, upon Examination we shall find, Either Lenham, or sum other, have made An Exchang, for I am sur better was not to be bought for mony, for my wife Thought Non Could be To good for you, I have sent by Lenham This day 6 l more: 5 l parfumed one pound plaine, cost: 4 l: 11d, for securitie I have sealed Every pound particuler, And Allsoe the paper which containe the whole, As for Orenges they are Not fitt for your purpose, and what Is off them are 24s per cwt, but they doe Exspect ships In dayly. when Any Com I will be sur To send you sum of the first, I went to the Other End of the Towne This Morning Adoning,†1 As I came Homard I call Att Richards Coffee
― 275 ―
house, There I saw Mr Clark and mr Frek,†1 Mr Clark desir me to Give you his Servis, My wife Give you her humble Servis,
I Am Deare Cousin
your Faithfull Kinsman And Humble Servant
JNO: BONVILLE

3520. Samuel Bold to Locke, 27 April 1704 (3326)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 4, ff. 45-6.
Steeple 27th Apr: 1704
Honoured Sir
I do heartily bless God that He supported you the last winter, and has brought you thus near to another summer. I pray with all my heart that your Friends may have many yearly repeated occasions to give God thanks for continuing you in a good measure of Health and Ease, to their great Joy, and the singular advantage of all who love and follow after Truth. Your letter of the 10th of this month came to me Saturday last. The sight of It did mightily refresh me. I was in great pain some time agoe by a letter mr Collins writ to me, wherein He intimated that you was very much indisposed; But I thank him, His concern for my ease was such, He took the first opportunity He could obtain to assure me He had good information you was wonderfully recovered. I wish you many a good year, not that I find this world to be so very desirable a place, I can wish a long continuance in It, either for my self, or my friends, without calling in some other Considerations to countenance and support such desires. I am so sensible of my own Insufficiency and unserviceableness, that considering how much I am sometimes fatigu'd, I can hardly forbear taking sanctuary in Hoping that in a little while, the time wil come when I shal be at Rest. The principal Reason which sways me to desire my Friends may have their lives and their Health continued long, is, that they may do much Good, and contribute a great deal to the enlightening and amending of a foolish and evil generation. The world, in it's present posture seems not to me very likely to yeild a delightful
― 278 ―
prospect to a Considering man. But what have I to do with the world? I leave the government of it to Him, who at his pleasure can make every thing appear with a new and beautiful face, and out of the greatest Confusion, can set every thing in the Exactest Order. In the winter I was for some weeks confined to my chamber by a sort of lameness; This spring I have bin somewhat indisposed, At present both my head and bowels are in some disorder. I think no man would be more glad to see you than I; But so many things concur to hinder my waiting on you the begining of this summer, they are too numerous to be mentioned now. I wil endeavour all I can to wait on you before the summer concludes. I pray you present my most humble service to Sir Francis and my Lady Masham, to Mrs Masham, and Mr Francis Masham, and to Mr Coste. I thank you heartily for all the Respect and kindness you express in your letter, and am with all my heart
Your most Humble and Obliged servant
SA: BOLDE
I am just now told of a considerable change that is made.†1 If it be begun upon a right foot, if it be carried on, not by halves, but with resolution and vigour, if they go throwstitch with It, I hope the consequences wil be very desirable and fortunate.
Address (not written by Bold): For Jn Lock: Esqr [at Oates, by Joslyn, Bishops Stortford]
Postmark: MA 6 (?)
Endorsed by Locke: S. Bold 27 Apr. 04

Dear Sir
I return my thanks to <you>†a for yours of the 11th instant and to my wife for hers inclosed in it.†2 I had done it sooner but that
― 279 ―
the increase of my distemper indisposeth me to every thing and my life goes away in painfull short breathing which I have reason to expect will quickly come to an end since this warm fine weather we have lately had has been so far from releiveing me that I have been far†a the worse for it. But what ever it shall please god to doe with me I shall always be concerned for <you>†b and your family whilst I am in this life and wish all happiness continued to you when I am gon. I am
Dear Sir
your most affectionate humble servant

3522. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 2 May 1704 (3515, 3524)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 368-9.
Oates 2 May 04
Dear Cosin
I thank you for your kind visit and hope you got well to town. Since you went here begins to be a talk of a new Parliament. You in the town have the news and the money we poor campaniards†1 are glad when you will impart to us that which costs you no thing
The hollow mentioned to be made in the middle of the seat of my chair I would not have very deep, and so not wholy without stuffeing. But the stuffeing should not raise the seat so high in the middle as it is about the sides. This moderate depression in the middle of the seat (which the upholsterers understand,) makes it both easier for siting, and makes the Cushion lie better without slipping off. I am
Dear Cosin
Your most affectionate Cosin and humble servant
J LOCKE

3524. Peter King, later first Baron King, to Locke, 6 May 1704 (3522, 3525)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 12, f. 243. Answered by no. 3525.
Inner Temple. May. 6. 1704.
Sir
Mr Pawling found the Key of your trunk but it would not open it, so that I was oblig'd to force it open where I found your morning gown and lining, which when dip't will serve for your easy chair, which I have Ordered to be made, and am promised to have it the latter end of next week or the beginning of the week after, and assoon as finished shall send it to Oates, I believe that about the latter end of next week a Messenger will come down to Sir Francis and My Lady, I desire you to tell them of it, that they be not out of the way, for the Settlement shall be perfected by a Commission without putting them to the trouble of Coming up to Town— All Things here are quiet, No New Secretary yet declared, The Vogue†2 of the Town is, that it will be Mr Harley. Several others had determined and resolved to lay down with the E. of. N.†3 if new measures were not taken, but his success†4 seems to have abated the resolution of the others—My service to all—I am
Sir Your most Affectionate Cosin and Servant.
P. KING.
Endorsed by Locke: P: King 6 May. 04 Answered 8

Dear Cosin
I thank you for what you have done in compliance with my desires, as you write me in your of the 2d.

If you were not geting money I should say we passe our time more pleasantly here than you doe there

3526. Locke to Edward Clarke, 8 May 1704 (3521)
B.L., MS. Locke b. 8, no. 161. Two separate leaves: letter and enclosed statement of account. Printed in Rand, pp. 600-1.
Oates 8° May 04
Dear Sir
Mr Churchill in a letter I received from him the last week acquainted me that you had paid him an hundred pounds for
― 283 ―
my account. Whereupon I have drawn up the state of the account between us and sent it you here inclosed signed. If you find it right I desire you to returne me the duplicate signed. I remember noe thing that you have paid for me since our last accounting but what I have set down. If I have slipd any thing rectifie the account and send it me that I may return it to you signed. Thus much to comply with our usual method. But now you must give me leave to tell you that I am much troubled with what I hear of you from different hands. I am told that your health is out of order and your spleen begins to be in ferment again. You will pardon me therefor if I interesse my self again in that matter. Sir Francis acquainted me that when he left the town he invited you hither and <I hoped>†a before this time to have seen you here. The air and company here I flatter my self might be some releif <to you and therefor you will>†a pardon me if I wish to see you here both as a present good to you <and a satisfaction to me in enjoying>†a your company and an oportunity of discourseing to you and prevaileing upon you to doe what farther I may think fit to be done for the recovery and preservation of your health. This I cannot but say that allowing your self too much time of persecuteing your self with your own melancholy thoughts in town is what I think not any way convenient for you neither the air nor unactive leisure of that place can I imagin good for you. In such a state the body and the mind revenge themselves one upon an other and in their turns increase the evil. I would therefore wish you were in a way that gave constant exercise to your body and diversion to your thoughts that soe you might receive respit and help on both sides.
If I interess my self thus far and talke soe plainly you will beleive it is because I am without complement
Dear Sir
Your most affectionate faithfull and humble servant
J LOCKE
My service to all at Chipley
All this family salute you and them

Dear Cosin
You will here inclosed have a receit for Mr Marton of a fresher date. Why I yet put it back as far as the 2d instant this is the reason because the 3d an other half year is due and I would not give occasion by a date after that to suppose I had mistaken and said the 3d of November last instead of the 3d of May, and so bring in question 1/2 years interest. I hope it will serve to receive the money upon, if not you must tell me. The family, Mr Collins and all wish

JOHN LOCKE

3528. Peter King, later first Baron King, to Locke, 11 May 1704 (3527, 3531)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 12, ff. 244-5. Answered by no. 3531.
Inner Temple May. 11. 1704.
Sir
I Believe The Company at Oates are very fond of one another, Mr Collins being not yet returned—The Tailour will have your Cloths ready by next week—The Gazette saith that the News of the French Fleets being out was a false alarm,†1 however that be, Sir Cloudsly Shovel sail'd yesterday morning to the Westward with a good wind and a sufficient force—I desire you to let Sir Francis know that the writings etc. cannot be ready till next week, and that I hope they will be with him Munday next—My humble service to all the Good Company—I am Sir
Your Most Affectionate Cosin and Most Humble Servant.
P. KING.

3529. Francis Limborch to Locke, 12 May 1704 (3519, 3561)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 13, ff. 238-9.
London 12 May 1704
Honoured Sir
I finde before me your most acceptable favour off the 10th. Instant by which j was very glad to see my staying some dayes
― 286 ―
longer after my promise was no prejudice to you, j have yett circa two hundred weight off the same Cacao Nutts and you may Command off'm whatt quantity you please. they are att present £12 per hundredweight†a and so as the markett goes you shall have these when ever you please, j cannott see there will or can come any alteration in'm unlesse we succeed in Spain for then there will be a demand for'm; butt as long we Continue under the same uncertainty itts likely twill continue aboutt this price; the packett which went from Harwich with letters off the 25th. is taken all the other mailes on this side, we have againe a report off a squadron off 20 french men off war off of Scilly, butt the generall opinions are its the Scandroon Fleet,†1 which we heartily wish itt may be, for in Case of the first that rich fleet runs 99 PC risicq,†2 as for our fleets putting to sea we hear nothing yett, the Esquadron fitted for the West Indies with the Merchandmen under their Command, being nott strong enough to engage'm, is returned and so lost this fair opportunity off the Easterly wind, My parents I thank god are very well, they alwayes remember their most sincere respects to you Sir Francis My Lady and Mr Masham— I was in hopes to have send you some stockfish tomorrow butt the greatt hurry off businesse att the Custome house so the Master could nott send itt, butt hope Certainly to doe itt by wednesday or saturday next; heere with some books for Mr Coste, which are sent to me this week; pray Sir Lett Sir Fran My Lady Mrs Masham and Coste may finde our sincere respects In these and to beleeve me
Honoured Sir
Your most oblidged humble servant
FRAN LIMBORCH
Address: For John Locke Esquire att Oates jn Essex
Endorsed by Locke: F: Limborch 12 May 04

3530. Locke to Anthony Collins, 14 May 1704 (3506, 3537)
The Houghton Library, Harvard. Transcribed from photostat.
Oates 14° May 04
Dear Sir
You may justly think by the very little other use I make of them, that I trafick in books onely to trouble you with them. But you are good, and forgive all, and take delight to be so served. And you have met with a man for your purpose. The convenience of this conveyance hath tempted me to send you up two Bibles The English one I desire may be very well bound in Calves leather. Whereby I mean the top lines of the pages all set even of the same length, the margents all large, the Pastboards strong, exceedingly well beaten,†1 and so well sown and orderd in the back that it will lye open any where, and letterd on black on the back BIBLE. The Latin one true folded and sewed upon bonds†2 and put in marble paper will serve my turne well enough, and this any ordinary binder may doe. I must also desire you to get another Epistola de Tolerantia stitchd in marble paper and sent by the penny post to Mr Limborch at his house in Castle Court†3 in Budge row. If you onely write it is by my order sent him it is enough.
Your mention of a footmans walkeing by has put me in mind to desire you that with the bridle for my Coach horse may be sent down a leading rain, with which a servant on foot or on horse back may lead the horse, when one has a mind to it, and so save those in the Chaise†4 the trouble of driveing. And now I wish the chaise finished with dispatch that I may be soon happy in your company here and tell you how much I am
Dear Sir
your most affectionate and most humble servant
J LOCKE
Address: For Anthony Collins Esquire at his Chamber number. 6. in Serle Court in Lincolns Inne London
― 288 ―

Oates 15° May 04
Dear Cosin
Your message to Sir Francis in yours of the 11th is deliverd.
One word more to my Tayler. I would have him with my Clothes send me a pair of drawers of the best fustian or as I thinke they call it Dimity
Will it not be best if they be ready near the same time to send the Chair and my clothes together by the same carrier, twill I think have every way the least trouble in it, my Cosin Bonville must be consulted herein
Pray send the inclosed directly to Dartmouth or to your Cosin King at Exeter†1
I have sent you by Mr Collins (to whom I am mightily obleiged for his company here) a dimity Wastcoat†2 which I would desire might presently be sent to my Tayler to make another by that both the Wastcoats as well as the drawers may come down in the box with my clothes. I am
Dear Cosin
Your most affectionate Cosin and humble servant
J LOCKE

Oates 15 May 03
Dear Cosin
Sir Francis just now tels me that of necessity he must be in town tomorrow morning very early. And therefore staying in expectation of your messenger and the writeings here today he must ride all night. I write you this that if the Messenger with the writeings comes not out of town to day you will stop him and let him not come till after to morrow, for Sir Francis intends to be back again tomorrow night. I am
Dear Cosin

3533. Peter King, later first Baron King, to Locke, 15 May 1704 (3532, 3534)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 12, ff. 246-7. Supplementary instructions show that the deeds related to a fine to Edward Haberfield, gent., in respect of the manors of Oates and Matching Hall and their appurtenances, with further property in Surrey: B.L., MS. Locke c. 16, ff. 79-80. Answered by no. 3534.
Inner Temple. May. 15. 1704.
Sir
The Bearer hereof comes with the deeds and Commissions for the fines and recoverys—As for the deeds, Sir Francis must sign and seal to the first seale on the left hand, and My Lady to the second seal, And on the back of each deed the witnesses to the signing, sealing and delivery must put their hands—You will find an Indorsement for that end on the back thereof—As for the Captions†1 of the fines and Commissions for the Recoverys, The Bearer hath full
― 290 ―
instructions for that, You are nominated for a Commissioner, and therefore when My Lady acknowledges the fines You must take her apart, and privately examine her whether she doth it Voluntarily —Our Priviledge of franking is now determined, which is the reason I did not send you the news last Saturday, but have sent it you by the bearer—The franking priviledge will revive again in a little time, till which time I shall not send any more news unless you order otherwise†1 —Both the deeds sent must be Executed, and let them be sent back again by the bearer—
I desire you to send me by the bearer the body of Ecclesiastical laws framed by the 32 Commissioners in the time of H.8. and Edw.6.†2 It may be of use to me in a point that I am to argue this term before all the Judges at Serjeants inn,†3 and therefore I desire you not to faile of sending it me by the bearer—Here is no news besides what is in the papers—The Easy chair, and Cloths will be both sent you this week—My Ladys Morgage will, I believe, be perfected this week, when, I think, it will be proper to make some gratification to Mr Marton, either five guinys, Or a Guiny for every hundred pounds. I am Sir
Your Most Affectionate Cosin and Humble Servant.
P. KING.
The Bearer must lodge at Oates this night and Come away too morrow morning
Address: For John Locke Esquire at Oates.

3536. Peter King, later first Baron King, to Locke, 18 May 1704 (3534, 3538)

Inner Temple. May. 18. 1704.
Sir
Your Tailour hath not been so diligent and quick, as I expected, and he promised, The excuse is, that My Lord Coleraine hath unexpectedly employed him in making a great deale of mourning,†1 so that he cannot end your Clothes before Tuesday next or Thereabouts, Assoon as he hath done 'em, You shall have 'em sent together with the stockins, drawers, Wastcoat, and easy chair— I†a- have Cancelled your old receit to Mr Marton, have not yet received the mony from him, but it is ready, I Intend to pay your Tailour with it,-†a—In the fine and Recovery sent down we followed the old fine, and added 150 acres to it, As it was sent down it would have done well enough, but however it is now altered, and we have put in enough—

― 293 ―
character of a very honest and punctual man—It will be my Interest to continue the mony at Interest, A word of your advise whether it will be prudent to take a morgage from him for it—I am in hast so can only add my service to all, and that I am

The Bearer hereof hath directions as to the fines and recoverys

3537. Locke to Anthony Collins, 19 May 1704 (3530, 3539)
The Carl H. Pforzheimer Library, New York. Transcribed from photostat. Printed in A Collection of Several Pieces, pp. 306-8. Answered by no. 3546.
Oates 19 May. 04
Dear Sir
No thing works so steadily and effectualy as freindship. Had I hired a man to have gon to town in my business and paid him well my commissions would <not>†a have been soe soon nor soe well dispatchd as I find by yours of the 16th they have been by you. You speake of my affairs and act in them with such an air of interest and satisfaction that I can hardly avoid thinking that I obleige you with imploying you in them. Tis noe small advantage to me to have found such a freind at the last scene of my life when I am good for noe thing, and am grown soe useless that I cannot but be sure that in every good office you doe me you can propose to your self noe other advantage but the pleasure of doeing it. Every one here finds himself obleiged by your late good company. As for my self if you had not convincd me by a sensible experiment I could not have beleived I could have had soe many happy days together. I shall always pray that yours may be multiplyed. Could I in the least contribute any thing there unto I should think my self happy in this poor decaying state of health which though it affords me little in this world to enjoy, yet I find the charmes of your company makes me not feel the want of strength or breath or any thing else. The Bishop of Glocester came hither the day you went from hence, and in noe very good state of health. I find two
― 294 ―
groaning people make but an uncomfortable consort. He returnd yesterday and went away in some what a better state. I hope he got well to town. Enjoy your health and youth whilst you have it, to all the advantages and improvements of an innocent and pleasant life remembring that merciless old age is in pursute of you and when it overtakes you will not faile some way or other to impair the enjoyments both of body and mind. You know how apt I am to preach. I beleive it is one of the diseases of old age. But my freinds will forgive me when I have noe thing to perswade them to but that they should endeavour to be as happy as tis possible for them to be, and to you I have noe more to say but that you goe on in the course you are in. I reflect often upon it with a secret joy that you promised I should in a short time see you again. You are very good and I dare not press you, But I cannot but remember how well I passed my time when you were here. I am
Dear Sir
your most humble and <most>†a obedient servant
J LOC<KE>†a
The whole family greet you with a pecu<liar>†a respect and Frank is infinitely†b your servant
Address: For Anthony Collins Esq at his Chamber Numb: 6 in Serle Court in Lincolns Inne London
3538. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 20 May 1704 (3536, 3540)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 380-1. Answers no. 3536; answered by no. 3540.
Oates 20 May. 04
Dear Cosin
I returne you by Mr Smith†1 Your Deeds Captions and Commissions all Executed I hope secundum artem for I in every step consulted and followed the Rubrique. They are all sealed up
If my clothes be well done and with my chaire come safe it will be well enough for I am not in hast for them and so can stay till next week
― 295 ―
If you treat with him yourself and he offer you a morgage I doe not see how you can well refuse it if you transact with Mr S—s†1 by a mediator he may perhaps prevaile for the money instead of a morgage which I thinke better. For such relations are often nice to deale with, expect forbearances, and cannot be pressed as others and therefore I thinke any dealeings of that kind are best avoided if it can handsomely bee. But if you like the other circumstances you must take care rather to prevent his offering a morgage, than to give him any umbrage by refuseing it when offerd
You say noe thing to me about the morgage of £700 you spoke to me of. I hope it goes on, pray let it be taken in Awnsham Churchils name.
I wish good success to your affair and am
Dear Cosin
Your most affectionate Cosin and humble servant
J LOCKE
My Lady and all here give you their service
I am glad the book was that you wanted

3539. Anthony Collins to Locke, 20 May 1704 (3537, 3542)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 7, ff. 37-8. Answered by nos. 3542 and 3544.
Lincolns Inne May. 20/04
Sir
I have hitherto defer'd writing to you about the affair of a Chaise, that I might take the more time to make my inquiry among the Coachmakers, and so be the better able to serve you. The most convenient sort as well as the cheapest are those that fall back tho they be not quite so ornamental as those that have fix'd tops. They insist upon twenty pound, and I believe will not make one much cheaper. if you continue your inclination to have one I desire your further direction whether you would have any brass on the Harness and whether you would have the top only fall back upon two standing corner pillars, or so far that you may
― 296 ―
have as full a prospect behind you as before you. I have the particulars that you requir'd written down and shall take as much care to have 'em perform'd as if I had a Canon,†1 that I was sworn to maintain, to direct me. I am with the greatest esteem and respect
Dear Sir
Your most Humble and most Obedient Servant
A COLLINS
My most humble service to all the family.

3540. Peter King, later first Baron King, to Locke, 20 May 1704 (3538, 3541)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 12, ff. 250-1. Answers no. 3538; answered by nos. 3541 and 3543.
Inner Temple. May. 20. 1704.
Sir
The Messenger is returned safe, I have not yet look't upon the Captions, Commissions etc. but shall this evening assoon as the Atturny comes to me, I hope all is well done—The Chair is finished, It looks handsome and I believe will please you, The morning gown did the feat without the Lining, The Lining hath been scoured and looks well, which shall be sent you together with the Chair, that so you may dispose of it as you think fit, Assoon as the Taylour hath done All shall be sent you—And I hope to see you with my Cosin Freke this day fortnight†2 —I had forgot to write you by My last letter, that the 700 l. morgage was finished this week, The mony was paid in the Country last Munday, and I paid it here again this week, I take it to be a good morgage, It is in Hampshire, It was procured for me by a Country Atturny, to whome I think some gratification should be given, when he comes up to Town, if you think fit, It is an old Morgage, and I have taken it in my name, It was perfectly out of my mind to take it in mr Churchills name, however if you would have it so, It shall be assign'd to him, tho, I think it will be better that it should continue as it is, but as it is your mony, I will do just as you please—
― 297 ―
I had last night another security offered me for the same summ in Wiltshire, I Design that for my self if it goes on, but if you please, You shall have that taken for you in Mr Churchills name, and I will have the other—
Here is a great deal of bad news in Town, The French have join'd the Bavarians, The D. of Savoy retreats before the D. of Vendome,†1 The Spaniards have Invaded Portugal and taken two Towns, but this last seems not well Confirmed—I am
Sir
Your Most affectionate Cosin and Most humble Servant,
P. KING.

3541. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 25 May 1704 (3540, 3543)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 382-3. The ink of the first part is bad. Answers no. 3540; answered by no. 3545.
Oates 25 May 04
Dear Cosin
If the chair and my clothes be sent by the Dunmow Carrier as my Cosin Bonville uses to send me all Bulkie things, then as he knows they must be directed to be left at Mr Harrisons at the Crown in Harlow. with a letter to him to desire him to take them in and pay what is agreed for their cariage, and to send me a letter which must be inclosed in his to give me notice of it. There must care also be taken in loading the chair upon the wagon, that it be not so placed as to be broken or injured by the things contiguous to it†2
The Morgage in <Hampshire>†a (I wish you had put down the mans name as well as the county) for tis by the Morgagers name I take notices and make my memorandums of them) being taken in your name I shall not alter any thing in it. But Dear Cosin permit me as a freind to tell thee, that it is always a satisfaction
― 298 ―
to one of my age, whether he says anything of it or noe, to find what he has desired remembred since tis some assurance to him that what he desires will not be forgotten after he is dead. It would have been a satisfaction also to have known what money you have taken of Mr Churchil and the time. For this keeps my accounts in order, and my affairs in vew before me. I doe not when I say this forget your multiplicity of business, nor would mention it to you would it cost you more than a line in any one of your letters. You know pretty well my opinion of the world we are in and how suitable it would be to it, to have always by me such a knowledg of the present posture of my little affairs that I might be able presently upon any occasion without sending for information, to give orders about them. Pray remember to bring with you a declaration of Trust for this last morgage signed and sealed and if Mr Churchil be in town let him be witness to it. You will remember also to bring with you Sir Francis's morgage to me which you took back with you, with a declaration of Trust for that too. I desire you also to bring me four and twenty Guineas, and let the cloake in my trunk at Mr Pawlings and some linin I have there viz shirts and napkins and a remnant of New Holland be put up in the box with my new clothes. There is also in that trunk or the box or chest a steel truss which you must needs have taken notice of. I should be glad it could be new coverd before my clothes come away that it might come in the same box. There is one Mrs Bernard a Truss maker that lives in the middle row between Temple bar and st Clements church yard in that street which goes from Temple bar to Clements Inne,†1 who uses to doe it for me But doe not pudder your self about it, I have given you trouble enough besides and if it cannot conveniently be done now, it must be deferd to an other time. When you goe to Mr Pawlings pray remember me to him and aske him whether he did not some time since receive a letter from me by Mr Clarke. I know not whether Mr Clarke be in town, I writ to him about a fortnight or 3 weeks since and inclosed one to Mr Pawling since when I have not received a word from either. If those letters be miscaried I must write again. I wish for Saturday come seventh night because you promise then to be here. This warme weather doth yet very little releive me, from whence I
― 299 ―
make my conclusion. And therefore I would have as much of your company as I can. If you bring your Cosin John Freke for a pretence to goe away quickly again I shall be angry for I make account to have you here all the Whitson week. Order your affairs so as not to fail me, perhaps it may be the last time. I am
Dear Cosin
Your most affectionate Cosin and humble servant
J LOCKE
If you come in a coach can you not bring me a basket or two of Cherys for we have very little or noe fruit here, and I think some Cherys if they could be brought without bruseing would doe me good but I must have enough for me and the company.
Address: For Peter King Esqr, To be left at Mr Potmans in the Middle Temple London
Postmark: MA 26
Endorsed by King: Mr Locke May. 25. 1704.
3542. Locke to Anthony Collins, 25 May 1704 (3539, 3544)
The Carl H. Pforzheimer Library, New York. Transcribed from photostat. Part printed in A Collection of Several Pieces, pp. 309-10. Answers no. 3539; answered by no. 3546.
Oates 25° May 04
Dear Sir
When you come to my age you will know that with us old fellows Convenient always carrys it before ornamental And I would have as much of the free air when I goe abroad in it as is possible. Only I aske whether those which fall back soe as to give as free a prospect behind as before be as easily managed and brought over you again in case of need (as in a shower) as one that fals back upon two standing corner pillars? and next whether that which fals back so well, does when it is drawn up over you, come so far over your head when it is erected as to shelter it from the dew without shutting you up from the free open air? For I think sometimes in the evening of a warm day to sit abroad in it to take the fresco, but would have a canopy over my head to keep the dew off. If this be soe I am plainly and without balanceing for that which falls flatest. One question more and I have done. Pray what place
― 300 ―

is there for a footman in any of them? Most of my time being spent in siting I desier special care may be taken in makeing the seat broad enough and the two cushions soft plump and thick enough. You know I have a great likeing to be Canonical but I little thought that you of all others was the man to make me soe. I shall love it the better for your sake and wish that Canonical were ready that you might have the hanseling of it hither speedily. Collonel Masham†1 will be in town to the end of this month and if you goe that way may be heard of at the Gant Coffee house by St James's gate.†2 I tell you this because he perfectly knows the fashion of the Chaises that the Queen hunts in and the man that makes them†3 and some convenience or other may be observed in every sort. If I did not take you for my self as you have taught me to doe I should not be thus free with you. Count me in your turn all your self except my age and infirmities, those I desire to keep to my self. all the rest of me is Yours John Locke
All this family remember you with great esteem and affection
Have you not a word to say of Psalmanassar?†4
My Cosin King who comes hither the latter end of the next weeke will bring my Greek testament if it be done but let it not be done in hast for that
Address: For Anthony Collins Esq At his chamber numb: 6 in Serle Court in Lincolns Inne London
Postmark: MA 26

3544. Locke to Anthony Collins, 26 May 1704 (3542, 3546)
The Carl H. Pforzheimer Library, New York. Transcribed from photostat. Printed in A Collection of Several Pieces, pp. 311-12. Answers no. 3539; answered by no. 3549.
Oates 26 May 04
Dear Sir
My letter yesterday went away without an answer to one of your demands, and that was whether I would have any brass on the harness. To which give me leave to tell you, that in my whole life I have been constantly against any thing that makes a shew, noe maxime being more agreeable to my condition and temper than Qui bene latuit bene vixit.†2 I like to have things substantialy good of their kind and usefull, and handsomely made and fitly adapted to their uses, for if either were necessary I had rather be taken notice of for some thing that is fashionably gaudy than ridiculously uncooth, or for its poorness and meaness remarkeable. Therefore if you please let the harness and all the whole acoutrements be of as good materials and as handsomely made and put together as may be, but for ornaments of brass or any such thing I desire it may be spared. One question more comes in my mind to aske you and that is whether the back of those that fall down soe
― 302 ―
flat are soe made that when it is up one may lean and loll against it at ones ease as in a coach or a chariot for I am grown a very lazy fellow, and have now three easy chairs to lean and loll in, and would not be without that releif in my chaise. You see I am as nice as a yonge fond girle that is comeing into the world with a face and a fortune, as she presumes, to command it. Let not this however deter you for I shall not be so hard to be pleased. For what you doe will be as if I did it my self. I am
Dear Sir
your most humble and most obedient servant
J LOCKE
Address: For Anthony Collins Esq at his chamber in Serle court in Lincolns Inne London
Postmark: MA 29
3545. Peter King, later first Baron King, to Locke, 27 May 1704 (3543, 3551)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 12, ff. 252-3. The letter did not reach Locke until he had written to King on 1 June: p. 309. Answers no. 3541.
London May. 27. 1704.
Sir
The time for franking of letters being again revived, You have herewith a recontinuance of the News—The Tailour hath now finished your clothes, which you will have together with the Chair by the first waggon, I will likewise send with them the several things mentioned by you in your last letter, which I have all found except the steel truss, which I have seen and is in one of the boxes, I will take care that that shall come with the rest, or I will bring it with me—As to the letter sent by you to Mr Pawling, he did receive it, I told him that an answer to it would be acceptable to you, Mr Clark is still in Town, but very melancholy—I thank you for your kind advice, I will not excuse my self, but endeavour to do that which is Better, viz. profit by it and amend, on the other side is an account of what mony I have Received and paid on your behalf since our last account made up—I had this evening a goldsmiths note for 37l. 10s. due to you 2 days ago, for half years interest of 1500l. lent mr Jolliff, It is a good note, I have given you credit for it—Tho, we have very warm weather, yet there are but
― 303 ―
few cherrys,†1 I hope by next week there will be more, and if there be I hope to obey your orders—I am very sorry that the warm weather doth not more relieve you, I hope without faile to see you this day sevennight, I will to be sure stay with you as long as I can—I hear nothing yet of my Ladys morgage, My Lord Chief Justice Trevor†2 would not sign till the morgageor had first signed, Mr Morley carried it with him into the country for that end, but hath not yet returned it—my Humble service to Sir Francis, My Lady and the whole family—I am
Sir
Your Most affectionate Cosin and Humble Servant,
P. KING.
The Names of the Morgageors of the Hampshire Lands are Richard Mason and Thomas Mason both of Vernham Deane. in Southampton.†3
They are father and Son
