3546. Anthony Collins to Locke, 27 May 1704 (3544, 3548)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 7, ff. 39-40. Answers nos. 3537 and 3542; answered by no. 3548. In his endorsement Locke gives the date of his answer as 9 June; his extant letter to Collins of that date (no. 3556) deals with matters discussed in this letter, but is not immediately an answer to it.
Lincolns Inne May 27/04
Dear Sir
I received two of yours.†1 The Kindness and Civility you show to me for doing nothing but endeavouring to be as happy as I can is so excessive that I can no more make you any sutable returns in Expressions that I can give you the Happiness I enjoy in your Friendship. Could I contribute to your health as much as you do to That, I would never be absent from you, and would at least preserve you here as long as myself. I cannot help having my own Interest and Advantage in View, when I am acting in any of your affairs, and to confess the truth I am so far from doing any Office for you for the sole pleasure of doing it, that you give me fresh motives every day and put that out of my power which I could otherwise do with the greatest satisfaction.
That which falls back so as to give you as free a prospect behind as before is as easily managed, and brought over again, as one that falls back upon two standing corner pillars, and comes, when it is erected, as far over your head, and is not to be distinguish'd by the outside from the other sort. I have not met with one that has any place for a Footman, but am told it may be contriv'd with the greatest ease imaginable. I shall do my best to make you as Canonical as I can with a broad seat and Cushions soft plump and thick, and shall be glad to see you in it with what you call disease upon you. I have a long time taken you for the Great Lay Priest among us. I was yesterday at the Gant Coffee house to inquire for Colonel
― 305 ―
Masham and was inform'd that he was upon duty at Kensington and would continue so till Sunday. I shall endeavour to wait upon him on munday and go with him to the Queens Chaisemaker and if I want any further information from you upon what I may observe, shall write to you. When I have full instructions from you I intend to bespeak one for you at the Queen's—unless you please to direct me elsewhere.
Poor Psalmanassar has very ill luck, he has no party to cry him up. I never knew a Rogue of his parts, and that took this method, but any Religious Sect among us would defend him. But I see a Convert from Paganism is not of half the Consequence as a Convert from one sect of Christianity to another.
You will have your Greek Testament by Mr King. I am sorry I must prepare you to expect to see it not so well bound as it should be. besides not observing my directions of only lettering it on the back the Binder has not squar'd it well and consequently cut the margin too close. I am
Dear Sir
Your most oblig'd Humble servant
A. COLLINS
My most humble service to all the Family.
Address: For John Locke Esq [at Oates, by Joslyn, Bishops Stortford]
Postmarks: MA 27; GC
Endorsed by Locke: A Collins 27° May 04 Answered Jun. 9
3547. Samuel Clarke to Locke, 27 May 1704 (3309)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 6, f. 189.
Honnoured Sir
I have by the Commande of Mr Kin<g>†a the Consler†1 Mad youe A Cloth sutt of Clothes and sent them Last Tusday to Mr Churchell whoe will Tacke Care: to send them safe which I hope theaye will fitte and plese you
Sir Mr King hath payid mee my bill for which I humbly†b Thancke you and This small bill I shall send to Mr King As soone As posable
― 306 ―
wishing you A manney manney whitssin Tides is the heartie Desir of Sir
your Faithfull searrtt To Commande
SAMUEL CLARKE

3548. Locke to Anthony Collins, 29 May 1704 (3546, 3549)
The Carl H. Pforzheimer Library, New York. Transcribed from photostat. Part printed in A Collection of Several Pieces, pp. 313-14. Answers no. 3546; answered by no. 3555.
Oates 29° May 04
Dear Sir
How should I value the Chaise you take soe much pains about if I could hope I could have your company with me abroad in it every two or three days. However it wears the signature of your freindship, and so will always have some thing in it to please me
I know not whether it be worth while to clog it with any thing to make a place for a foot man. That must I suppose make it bigger and heavier which I would avoid. and I think upon the whole matter there will be noe great need of it. But when I hear from you again I shall know that. In the mean time all the rest I think is resolvd for I suppose of course you will choose a cloth for the lineing, of a dust colour. That is the proper colour for such a priest as you mention in your letter. Pray trouble not your self to hunt out Collonel Masham, tis enough if he fals in your way and you your self have both skill and care enough to manage an affair of much greater importance than this
If poor Psalmanassar be really a convert from Paganisme (which I would be glad to be assured of) he has very ill luck not to heard any where among the variety of sorts are among us. But I think it is soe that the partys are more for doeing one an other harme than for doeing any body good. I am
Dear Sir
Your most humble and most obedient servant
J LOCKE
― 307 ―
My Lady Masham who sits by me whilst I am writeing this gives you her humble service And the rest of the family are your devoted

3549. Anthony Collins to Locke, 30 May 1704 (3548, 3555)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 7, ff. 41-2. Answers no. 3544; answered by no. 3556.
From Mr Kings Chamber. May 30/04
Dear Sir
You in yours of the 26th which I received this afternoon start a very material question. I must leave it to your resolution after I have told you how the matter stands. There is no rest for the head in any those chaises, that fall back flat, that I have seen, the movable part being, when it is erected, at least two inches behind the rest for the back. The Coachmakers say they can make the back as straight as that of a Coach or Chariot, but then I believe it will not be so easy to the head as a perfectly stuff'd back, you can judg of this best for yourself and therefore I desire your directions. Colonel Masham and I were yesterday at the Queens Chaisemakers but could not meet with ever a chaise ready made, or the Master at Home.
I shall inclose in a packet that I intend to give Mr King for you a new book of Mr Fletchers being the substance of a discourse between Sir Ed. Seymour Sir Christop. Musgrave and himself about the Scotch affairs.†1 I am Dear Sir
Your most Humble and most Obedient servant
A COLLINS
My most humble service to all the family
Address: For John Locke Esq [at Oates, by Joslyn, Bishops Stortford]
Endorsed by Locke: A Collins 30 May 04 Answered Jun. 9

3550. Martha Lockhart to Locke, 30 May 1704 (3190)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 15, ff. 117-18.
May. 30th. 704
You are to suppose if you pleas that your humble servant has past some months in scotland or some remoter corner beyond the reach of post's, messengers, etc but yet I have not been without the pleasure to hear of you but that inquiry's have been made at all hands that I thought could informe me how you have been in this long, long time, that I have not heard from you. And now I must tell you that next to the knowing that you have not injoy'd that perfect degree of health I allways wish you it has been an additionall mortification to me to kno that you have not thought me worth one single inquiry of what might have been the reason of my unaccustom'd sillence whether it was occasion'd by my death or blindness or what other misfortune might have hap'ned to me this great indifference from you seem'd to me a willingness in you to drop an old useless aquaintance; and will save you the trouble of hearing more at large the reasons that really has hinder'd me which I dare venture to say are sufficient to clear me to you of any sort of forgetfullness of so agreable a part of my duty as writing to you allways was. or more from the common sin of ingratitude, nay or laziness it self my more notorious failing. now I have brought my self to a resolution to try whether this will have the good fortune to remember you that once you had known such a name as now troubles you and according to my success in this attempt you will hear farther from me, And so much for what concernes my self. In the next place I am to tell you from your frend Mr Johnston that he complains violently of your sillence to some letter or letters he has lately writ you†1 and if You'le doe me the favour to inclose an answer to him in a letter to me I shall charge my self with the safe delivery of it; And soon after let you kno why your doing so will at this time be a service to Sir
Your affectionate, and ever faithfull frend, Oblig'd and humble
Servant
M LOCKHART
my service to my good lady Masham, and all the familly tho
― 309 ―
I have some reasons to think that wholl name has forgot me. Could I tell you how passionately my Sister Forfar longs to se you and shew her boy to you. I am sure you have so much good nature as would at least make you wish she had that satisfaction.
Address: For Mr Locke [at Oates, by Joslyn, Bishops Stortford]

3551. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 1 June 1704 (3545, 3560)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 386-7. Printed, omitting the first paragraph, in King, pp. 261-2.
Oates 1 Jun. 04
Dear Cosin
If I had had notice of it one day sooner I might have had the convenience of bringing my Chair by Sir Francis's wagon which goes to morrow to Bow to bring coles on Saturday from Mr Boswells in Stratford,†1 But that is now too late. For Clarke my Tayler has writ me word that my clothes are ready and sent to Mr Churchils, so that I make account to hear to morrow that they are at Mr Harrisons, Chair, Box, and all
I have received no letter from you since yours of the 20th.†2 I remember it is the end of a Terme†3 and a busy time with you and you intend to be here speedily which is better then writeing at a distance. Pray be sure to order your matters soe as to spend all the next weeke with me. As far as I can impartialy guess it will be the last week I am ever like to have with you. For if I mistake not very much I have very litle time left in this world. This comfortable, and to me usualy restorative season of the year has noe effect upon me for the better: on the contrary my shortness of breath and uneasiness every day increases, and my stomach, without any visible cause, sensibly decays, so that all appearances concur to warne me that the dissolution of this cottage is not far of. Refuse not therefore to help me to pass some of the last hours of my life as easily as may be in the conversation of one who is not onely the nearest but the
― 310 ―
dearest to me of any man in the world. I have a great many things to talke to you which I can talke to noebody else about. I therefore desire you again, deny not this to my affection, I know noething at such a time so desireable and soe usefull as the conversation of a friend one loves and relyes on. It is a week free from business or if it were not perhaps you would have noe reason to repent the bestowing a day or two upon me. Make hast therefore on Saturday and be here early. I long till I see you. I writ to you in my last to bring some Cherrys with you. But I fear they will be combersome to you. And those things that entertain the senses have lost with me a great part of their relish. Therefore give not your self any trouble about them. Such desires are usualy but the phansy seekeing pleasure in one thing, when it has missed it in another, and seeks in vain for the delight which the indisposition of the body has put an end to. When I have your company I shall forget those kind of things. I am
Dear Cosin
Your most affectionate cosin and humble servant
J LOCKE
Address: For Peter King Esqr member of Parliament. To be left at Mr Potmans in the Middle Temple London
Postmark: IV 2
Endorsed by King: Mr Locke June 1. 1704.

3553. John Shute, later Barrington, later first Baron Barrington, to Locke, 2 June 1704 (3394)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 18, ff. 103-4. The letter relates to Shute's The Rights of Protestant Dissenters, in two parts. First, being the Case of the Dissenters review'd; the second, a vindication of their right to an absolute toleration, from the objections of Sir Humphrey Mackworth in his treatise intitled Peace at Home. Part I. It is listed in the May issue of The History of the Works of the Learned (p. 318). L.L., no. 2665. Atterbury, writing on 6 June, mentions it as 'a very notable book ... in behalf of the occasional conformists ... supposed to be written by Locke', and on 10 June adds that the dedication to the queen 'is in answer to that of my Lord Rochester before the second volume of Clarendon; and reflects upon that noble Lord with great freedom': Epistolary Correspondence, iii. 198-9, 201-2.
Lincolns Inn June 2. 1704.
Sir
I find Mr Collins has betrayd me at Oates: And therefore I have nothing to do but to make my terms as well as I can. My Unwillingnes to be known to be the Author of any thing that so great a Judge as you are to read, would not allow me to ask your censure of it by that hand. But since he has put me out of my pain, I am much the easier, and cant find in my heart to complain of the usage. For how much soever I vallue your Esteem, I had yet rather lose a part of it by being guilty of a peice of Forwardness and Impertinence, than to forfeit the least share I possess of your Friendship by any omission or neglect. Besides Sir I very well know, that if No Body knows so well as you how to find fault, No Body knows better how to make allowances. Besides the extraordinary ones I hope youll be so kind to make for me, I beg you would not forget those that are due for Indispositions and Interruptions. However I am something the more easy in this matter, since the Mistakes of this Pamphlett will excuse me for my last peice of presumtion, and convince you that
― 312 ―
I was not wholly in the wrong to suffer my Self to be engag'd to Request it of You in the names of Some Others to undertake the defence of this Cause; Since I easily guess'd, the want of Leisure and Inclination those Gentlemen have to defend their own would put em upon retaining a very sorry Advocate, if you coud not be prevail'd on. The best thing I thought I coud do in these circumstances was to write upon those Principles which you Sir have so well establishd. If theres any thing that is not wrong in these Sheets They are but Deductions from the Incomparable Letters of Toleration, and an Accommodation of them to this particular case: And I dont know whether I approve of those Principles more, or disapprove of my own way of Inferring from 'em. I have only this Resource, that when you have an idle hour to look em over in, tho you cant lay aside your Exactness you'll be as little able to forget the Friendship you have allways expressed for
Sir
Your most obedient Servant
J SHUTE.
Endorsed by Locke: J: Shute 2 Jun. 04
3554. Dr. Pieter Guenellon to Locke, 2/13 June 1704 (3431, 3622)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 11, ff. 120-1.
Monsieur,
quoy que je n'aye pas recu responce a celle que j'eu l'honneur de vous ecrire au commencement de cette année, je ne devois a la verité tarder si long tems a vous rendre compte de l'etat de ma femme qui vous doibt une si grande partie de son restablissement. elle a pris assez longtems de votre bellis major et a continué son vitriol de Mars et l'opium jusqu'a demi Mey et se trouve a cette heure raisonnablement. j'espere que le bon air de la Campagne achever<a>†a de la guerir. elle y est depuis quelque j<ours.>†a les vents du Nord qui ont regné icy longtem<s>†a l'ont retenue en ville, outre que sur la fin du mois d'avril elle eut quatre acces de fievre tierce. je m'enqueste en toute occasion de l'etat de votre santé. j'espere quelle se soutient mieux dans cette bonne saison. nous voudrions quelle put durer longtems. quand vous aurez un moment de loisir,
― 313 ―
faite nous l'honneur de nous ecrire. vous ne scauriez croire, Monsieur, Combien cela nou<s>†a resjoüit. Monsieur Leenhof autheur du livret de la felicité de cette vie a eté condamné au dern<ier>†a synode de sa province a une suspension de sa charge pour deux mois, et que pendant ce tems il retracteroit son livre par un escrit public. cepandant le Magistrat de Swol qui le protege n'a pas voulu quil s'abstint de precher et a sindisposer le consistoire a luy continuer son rang dans les fonctions eclesiastiques.†1 quel fatras! je le plain. il est de mes anciens amis. je le crois tres honéte homme et tres vertueux il a afaire a un mechant parti, vous le scavez par experience. Dieu veuille nous laisser la liberte de conscience et de vivre en paix le reste de nos jours. toute la famille vous presente ses tres humbles respects. je suis avec un tres profond respect,
Monsieur,
Votre tres humble et tres obeissant serviteur
P. GUENELLON.
d'Amsterd. ce 13 juin 1704

3555. Anthony Collins to Locke, 6 June 1704 (3549, 3556)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 7, ff. 43-4. Answers no. 3548; answered by no. 3556.
Lincolns Inne. June. 6./04/
Dear Sir
Upon considering the matter I am of your mind that there will be no need of any place for a footman; besides the advantage of having it less and lighter I think your servant may as well go on foot to open the Gates for you in the fields, as ride behind.
I cannot think my self sufficiently instructed till I have an answer from you to my last. I am very much concern'd for your ease and
― 314 ―
have therefore left it to you to determine upon what I have said to you with respect to that sort that is easiest for leaning and lolling in. I hope I shall be able to wait upon you in it about three weeks hence and taste of those joys, that I make a shift to subsist on the remembrance of, but in vain expect to meet with any where else. Your particular friendship gives me a great interest to wish and pray for your most valuable health. I am Dear Sir
Your most Humble and most Obedient Servant
A. COLLINS
My most humble service to all the family and to Dear Mr King

3556. Locke to Anthony Collins, 9 June 1704 (3555, 3558)
The Carl H. Pforzheimer Library, New York. Transcribed from photostat. Part printed in A Collection of Several Pieces, pp. 314-15. Answers nos. 3549 and 3555; answered by no. 3558.
Oates 9 June 04
Dear Sir
I might number my days (and it is a pleasant sort of Almanake) by the kindnesses I receive from you. Your packet by my Cosin King†1 I received and have reason to thank you for all the particulars in it. However you thought fit to prepare me for being disappointed in the binding my Greek Testament,†2 there is noe thing in it that offends me but the runing of his pareing knife too deep into the margent, a knaveish and intolerable fault in all our English Bookbinders. Books seem to me to be pestilent things, and infect all that trade in them, i e all but one sort of men, with something very perverse and brutal. Printers. Binders, Sellers and others that make a trade and gain out of them have universally so odde a turne and corruption of mind that they have a way of dealeing peculiar to them selves, and not conformed to the good of Society, and that general fairness that cements man kind. Whether it be that these instruments of truth and knowledg will not bear being subjected
― 315 ―
to any thing but those noble ends, without revengeing them selves on those who medle with them to any other purpose, and prostitute them to mean and misbecomeing designes I will not enquire. The matter of fact I think you will find true, and there we will leave it, to those who sully them selves with printers ink. till they wholy expunge all the candor that nature gives, and become the worst sort of Black cattle.
As to our main Engine the Chaise, that which I enquire after is not a stuffed back for the ease of my head in leaning against it, but whether when it is set upright, the back and sides will be straind and stiff enough to bear my leaning against, and will not give way to my back, shoulders, or head when they press against it. Such a tightness, as will be a prop to me when I lean, will serve my turne without any quilting, and is all that I desire. I have instructed the bearer hereof my Cosin King, who will be able to explain to you my meaning herein if there be need. which is the reason of delaying my answer till his returne. And now I think all the questions about this Machin are setled, And I shall wish to have it done as soon as the Coachmaker can, that I may have the happyness of your and your neighbours company the sooner to whom pray give my service and thanks for his valuable present,†1 which I shall with attention apply my self to as soon as I am left to my self again.
Have you not yet received a word from James Tyrrel in answer to yours that inclosed mine to him? I should be sorry that letter came not to his hands, it being about business that should be dispatchd.†2
There be two people here that hearing there be some copyes of the Epistola de Tolerantia to be got in England desire each of them one. I must therefore beg of you to get two of them bound and bring them with you when you come. I am
Dear Sir
Your most humble and most obleiged servant
J LOCKE
My Lady and the family give you their service
Let the lineing of the chaise be a dust colour
This as you may perceive was to come by my Cosin King who intended for London this day. But good nature and ill weather have
― 316 ―
wrought on him to stay till monday. So that now I send it by the Post with thanks for yours of the 6th which I received just now
I perfectly agree with you in not making any place for a footman. Must there not be a rope and hook to cheque one†a- of the-†a wheels goeing down a steep hill
This whole family and my Cosin King give you their service.
Address: For Anthony Collins Esq at his Chamber in Serle Court in Lincolns Inne London
3557. Philippus Van Limborch to Locke, 10/21 June 1704 (3352, 3602)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 14, ff. 157-8. Copy by van Limborch in Amsterdam University Library, MS. R.K., II D 16, ff. 225-6. Printed from the copy in Some Familiar Letters, pp. 534-8. Answered by no. 3602.
(English)Vir Amplissime
Postquam afflicta tua valetudo, præsertim in extremâ senectute, nos admodum de te sollicitos habuit, tandem gratior paulo nuntius nos recreavit, calore æstatis, qui tamen nunc apud nos vernum calorem non excedit, te nonnihil respirare, et meliuscule te habere. Utinam firmam tibi valetudinem concedat benignum Numen, ut quos vitæ tuæ adjicere dignabitur dies iis quibus te consecrasti studiis impendas, et donec vitæ hujus usurâ frueris doctissimis tuis lucubrationibus orbi Christiano inservire possis! Quæ tu concordiæ Christianæ jecisti semina,†1 licet nunc ab ingratis conculcentur,
― 317 ―
gratæ posteritati fructus suos ferent.
(English)Quod licet mens certò mihi præsagiat, nihilominus quando servilia plurimorum, et pro autoritate humanâ decertantia ingenia considero, ægrè sperare licet, eos depositis præjudiciis et affectibus, animo puro ac sincero momenta rationum quibus veritas nititur ponderaturos, ac uni veritati candidè cessuros. Etiam Reformatos, qui sese opponendo Papatui nullâ se humanâ autoritate constringi velle aut posse protestati sunt, nimium humanæ autoritati tribuere, scriptaque humana majore quam par est in veneratione habere, actus singulis trienniis in patriâ nostrâ repetitus, et cujus solennem repetitionem novellæ nostrates paucas ante hebdomadas nobis retulerunt, argumentum est omni exceptione majus. Cujus quoniam nunc recens memoria est, quia illius narrationem tibi non ingratam fore confido, licet res ipsa maximè displiceat, eam distinctius et cum præcipuis circumstantiis describam. Jam anno CI&Cback;I&Cback;CXXV Ordines Generales decreverunt, ut singulis trienniis Acta authentica Synodi Dordracenæ, quæ Hagæ asservantur, a Deputatis Ordinum et Ecclesiarum inspiciantur. postquam deinde anno CI&Cback;I&Cback;CXXXVII prodiit Nova Bibliorum Versio, jussu Synodi ejusdem adornata, illius†a- etiam ultimæ correctionis, quam revisionem vocant typographi, exemplar, quod-†a
― 318 ―
Lugduni Batavorum asservatur, inspici solet.
(English)Redeunte itaque quolibet triennio Deputati Synodorum Hollandiæ Australis et Borealis cœtum convocant ex omnium Provinciarum Ecclesiis, nec non ex Ecclesiâ Walonicâ. Hi Patres Conscripti ubi convenere, Præsidi Ordinum Generalium adventum Fratrum indicant. Precibus à pastore loci, si cœtus membrum sit, habitis, et literis credentialibus lectis, præses et Scriba eliguntur. Præcedentis cœtus acta præleguntur: exinde Deputati cœtus ad Ordines Generales mittuntur, ut scriptorum Synodalium visionem petant, et ut aliquos è Collegio suo ad eam deputent, locum et tempus statuant, quin et per epistolam Consules Lugdunenses præmoneant, et Collegii Regentem, unius clavis custodem, ut adsit, quando Lugdunum convenient ad inspiciendum autographa Versionis. His peractis certâ à Deputatis Ordinum constitutâ horâ comparent in Ordinum Generalium camerâ: primò funduntur preces, Deoque†a gratiæ aguntur quod Ecclesiam Reformatam à variis erroribus purgaverit, quod Synodum ipsis concesserit, cujus Acta authentica in præcedente cœtu adhuc incorrupta conspexerunt: et quoniam nunc convenerunt ut ea denuò inspiciant, oratur Deus, ut gratiam hanc ipsis concedat,
― 319 ―
ut integra æque ac incorrupta ab ipsis conspiciantur, perinde uti ante triennium conspecta fuere.
(English)Postquam scripta inspecta sunt, gratiæ Deo pro tanto beneficio aguntur, idque depositum denuò tutelæ divinæ committitur, ut in proximo cœtu æque sincerum ac incorruptum reperiatur, prout nunc deponitur. Postridie Lugdunum proficiscuntur, et à Magistratu in Curiam adsciti <authentica>†a Versionis inspiciunt. Hæc inspectio similibus precibus inchoatur et finitur. Exin lauto excipiuntur convivio, in quo à Præside cœtus et Scribâ Deputatis Ordinum et Magistratus Lugdunensis gratiæ aguntur. Hagam reversi in Actis scribunt, scripta illa integra adhuc, et à tineâ, vermibus et muribus inviolata esse reperta: atque ita cœtui finis imponitur. Hæc est illa triennalis solemnitas visioni scriptorum Synodalium destinata; quam paulò distinctius variisque circumstantiis vestitam tibi scribere volui, ut quantâ veneratione Synodi illius famosæ reliquiæ hic asserventur cognoscas. Hæc ego excerpsi ex narratione cujusdam Ministri, qui ipse cœtus illius membrum fuit, scripta inspexit, et in amicorum quorumdam gratiam hanc historiolam scripto consignavit.†b †1 Non credo Romæ
― 320 ―
tantâ cum veneratione tantisque sumtibus Concilii Tridentini Acta inspici.
(English)Ridenda hæc forent, si quorumdam privatorum inconsiderato zelo agerentur; nunc, quia autoritate publicâ fiunt, dolenda sunt. Quid Gallica Synodus nuperrimè contra D. Clerici Versionem Gallicam Novi Testamenti ejusque notas decreverit, quam frivolæ illius sint criminationes, quam plenè†a brevi†a edito scripto eas D. Clericus refutaverit, ipse tibi aut jam scripsit, aut brevi, ut credo, scripturus est.†1 Hæc similiaque quando considero, bonæ conscientiæ studio acquiescendum, et neglectis hominum iniquorum molitionibus veritati ac paci indefesso studio unice litandum, laborumque nostrorum benedictionem à solo Deo, qui è tenebris lucem eruere potest, exspectandam esse certus sum. Illius te tutelæ commendo, illum oro ut omnia tibi largiatur fausta ac salutaria, nec non honoratissimæ in quâ vivis familiæ. Salutant te, nec non
― 321 ―
Dominum ac Dominam Masham, unà cum dignissimâ filiâ ac filio, uxor ac filia. Salutem etiam à me dices D. Coste.
Tui Amantissimus
PHILIPPUS À LIMBORCH
Amstelodami 21 Junii

Address: Amplissimo Consultissimo Doctissimo Viro D. Joanni Locke
Endorsed by Locke: P: Limborch 21 Jun. 04 Answered Aug. 4
(Latin)Excellent Sir,
After your troubled health, especially in [your] extreme old age, had made us very anxious about you, rather more welcome tidings have at length comforted us: that with the warmth of summer, which with us, however, does not at present exceed that of spring, you breathe a little and are somewhat better. May God in His goodness grant you good health, so that you can employ the days which He shall vouchsafe to add to your life in those studies to which you have devoted yourself, and so that as long as you enjoy the use of this life you can serve the Christian world with your most learned lucubrations. The seeds of Christian concord that you have sown,†1 although they are now trampled under foot by the ungrateful, will [p. 317] bear their fruit for a grateful posterity.
(Latin)Although my mind surely presages this, yet when I consider the dispositions of the greater number, slavish and contending for human authority, I can scarcely hope that they, putting aside their prejudices and passions, will ponder with a pure and sincere spirit the weight of the reasons on which truth rests and will candidly yield to truth alone. In the act that is repeated every three years in my country, and the solemn repetition of which our newspapers reported to us a few weeks ago, there is unexceptionable evidence that even the Reformed [sc. the members of the Dutch Reformed Church], who in opposing the Papacy protested that they neither would nor could be fettered by any human authority, esteem human authority too highly and hold human writings in greater veneration than is proper. Since it is now fresh in memory, because I believe that an account of it will not be unacceptable to you, although the thing itself is highly displeasing, I shall describe it rather precisely and with all the principal circumstances. Already in the year 1625 the States General decreed that the original Acta of the Synod of Dort, which are kept at The Hague, should be inspected every three years by deputies of the States and Churches. Thereafter, in the year 1637, there appeared a new translation of the Bible, prepared by order of the same Synod; a copy of this as finally [p. 318] corrected (which printers call a 'revise') that is kept at Leyden is also customarily inspected.
(Latin)And so when a period of three years returns the deputies of the Synods of South and North Holland summon an assembly [coetus] from the churches of all the provinces and from the Walloon church. When these conscript fathers have met they make the arrival of the brethren known to the president [praeses] of the States General. After prayers have been made by the pastor of the place (if he is a member of the assembly) and the letters of credence have been read, a chairman [praeses] and secretary are elected. The acta of the preceding assembly are read; then deputies of the assembly are sent to the States General to ask for a viewing of the Synodal writings and that they should depute some of their body for it, decide place and time, and give notice by letter to the burgomasters of Leyden and to the Rector of the College, the keeper of [the] one key, so that he may be present when they meet at Leyden to inspect the original manuscript of the translation. When all this has been accomplished they present themselves in the chamber of the States General at the hour appointed by the deputies of the States: first, prayers are poured forth and thanks are given to God for having purged the Reformed Church from various errors and for having granted them the Synod, whose original Acta, still uncorrupted, they saw in their preceding assembly; and since they have now met to inspect them again they pray God to grant them this grace, that they [p. 319] may be seen by them equally whole and uncorrupted, just as they were seen three years previously.
(Latin)After the writings have been inspected thanks are given to God for so great a benefit and the deposit is anew committed to His keeping, so that at the next assembly it may be found equally entire and uncorrupted, even as it is now deposited. Next day they set out for Leyden and, being admitted by the magistracy to the town-hall, they inspect the originals of the translation. This inspection is begun and ended with similar prayers. They are then entertained at a sumptuous banquet, at which thanks are given by the chairman [praeses] and secretary of the assembly to the deputies of the States and of the magistracy of Leyden. Having returned to The Hague, they write in the Acta that those writings were found still whole, and unharmed by book-worm, worms, or mice; and thus an end is put to the assembly. Such is that triennial solemnity appointed for the viewing of the Synodal writings; I wanted to describe it to you somewhat precisely and clad with its various circumstances, so that you may know with how great veneration the relics of that famous Synod are kept here. I have taken this from the narrative of a minister who was himself a member of the assembly and inspected the writings, and who set down this little history in writing to please some friends.†1 I do not think [p. 320] that the Acta of the Council of Trent are inspected at Rome with so great veneration and with so great expense.
(Latin)All this would be laughable, were it done by the inconsiderate zeal of some private persons; as it is, because it is done by public authority it is to be grieved for. Mr. Le Clerc has either written to you already, or I think will shortly do so, what the Walloon synod has recently decreed against his French translation of the New Testament and his notes, and how frivolous are its accusations and how fully he has refuted them in a short piece that he has published.†1 When I consider these and similar things I am certain that we should acquiesce in the favour of a good conscience and that, disregarding the designs of unjust men, we should with unwearied diligence pay service only to truth and peace and should await a blessing on our labours from God alone, who out of darkness can bring forth light. I commend you to His keeping; I pray Him to grant you and that most honourable family in which you live everything that is prosperous and healthful. My wife and daughter send greetings to you and [p. 321] also to Sir Francis and Lady Masham, together with their most worthy daughter and son. Please give my regards to Mr. Coste.
Your most affectionate
PHILIPPUS VAN LIMBORCH
Amsterdam 21 June
1704
3558. Anthony Collins to Locke, 13 June 1704 (3556, 3565)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 7, ff. 45-6. Answers no. 3556; answered by no. 3565.
Lincolns Inne. June. 13/04
Dear Sir
Yours of the 9th and some discourse with Mr King have perfectly resolv'd all my Questions, and I have since given full instructions to the Coachmaker with my request to him to make what dispatch he can; which would be very Quick, could he but while he is performing his business be possest with the least degree of my inclinations.
I did not as I intended inclose your letter to Mr Tyrrell in one of mine since it was directed to him as it should be. Upon some occasion or other Dr Tyndal†1 (who is now in Town) told me that Mr Tyrrel has been lately at Oxford upon occasion of a letter that he received from you.
I have put two Copies of the Epistola de Tolerantiâ to the Binder, and intend to bring 'em with me to Oates.
Dr Sherlock in a book that will be publish'd in two or three days hath printed some part of what he formerly preach'd against your
― 322 ―
Essay etc.: and I presume intends more by his calling this a first part.†1 what relates to you I have read over and since there are several new and Extraordinary Arguments and Questions put to you I shall transcribe a little whereby you may judge of the rest and if this convinces you I dare undertake you will believe what remains to be just arguing without reading it. I do not propose now to give you any of the Insolent or Haughty Expressions that concern you in it. To Prove Connate Ideas he says if wee allow that there is a God as Mr Locke does this is demonstration. 1. That Knowledg is before things that are known, and is the Maker of 'em. 2. Hence it follows that Ideal Knowledge is Essential to a mind; for what is a mind without the Ideas of things: which is a good argument that created minds as far as they partake of the Eternal mind have the Natural Ideas of things interwoven in their constitution. For a mind is mind whether created or uncreated: and if created minds are made after the pattern of the Divine mind (and there is no other pattern for minds) Natural Ideas must be essential to Created Minds. Another argument is. That wee all know that Adam was created with Natural Ideas and if Adam was to†a beget†a a son in his own likeness his Children must have Natural Ideas as well as himself.
He asks you what is your Opinion about the souls of Infants? they as you say brought no Ideas into the world with 'em, and did not live long enough in the world to get any: Do they go without Ideas into the Other world? He says they can never reason in the other world unless they carry Ideas along with 'em.
I will not tire you with any more of this, you have read the Bishop of W.†2 and his Mind is a perfect Pattern of Sherlocks. I am with the greatest respect
Dear Sir
Your most Humble and most Obedient servant
A. COLLINS
Address: For John Locke Esq [at Oates, by Joslyn, Bishops Stortford]
Postmarks: IV 13; GC
Endorsed by Locke: A: Collins 13 Jun. 04 Answered. 19

3559. Jean Le Clerc to Locke, 13/24 June 1704 (3468)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 13, ff. 150-1. Printed in Bonno, Le Clerc, pp. 129-30.
A Amsterdam le 24 de Juin 1704.
J'ai appris, Monsieur, avec chagrin, le peu de santé dont vous avez jouï l'hiver passé, et c'est ce qui m'a empêché de vous écrire, pour ne pas vous donner la peine de me répondre. J'ai eu soin cependant de m'informer de vôtre santé chez Mr. Limbourg, mon voisin, à qui son Fils ne manque pas d'en mander des nouvelles, ou chez Mr. de la Motte,†1 que je vois souvent. Si les veux et les prieres pouvoient produire les effets, que nous voudrions bien qu'ils produisissent, il y a long-tems que vous jouïriez d'une parfaite santé et pour longues années; car ni veux, ni souhaits ne vous ont pas manqué, de ma part. Je voi si peu de gens au monde qui aiment la verité et qui la cherchent, ou qui aient des idées raisonnables de la Religion et de la Vertu, ou qui osent dire ce qu'ils pensent à cet égard, que je ne puis pas m'empêcher de ressentir beaucoup de douleur, quand je pense que le petit nombre de ces gens-là est en danger de diminuer encore plus, par la perte d'un homme comme vous; et je prie Dieu qu'il vous donne de nouvelles forces, pour continuer à rendre à ceux qui aiment la verité les services, que vous leur avez rendus. Je suis bien faché d'avoir manqué à une Auction les Entretiens de Costar etc. qu'on ne peut trouver autrement ici. J'avois donné commission à un Libraire d'en donner trois fois autant, qu'on ne l'achete communément, aux Auctions, mais il se vendit encore plus cher, de sorte qu'il n'osa l'acheter, l'ayant poussé encore plus haut, que je ne lui avois dit. Tout ce qui se vendit à cette Auction, qui est celle de Mr. Huydekooper,†2 Echevin de cette ville, se vendit au delà de ce qu'il a accoûtumé. Je prendrai garde aux autres Catalogues, que je verrai, pour tâcher de l'avoir. J'envoye à Oates deux exemplaires du IV Tome de la Bibl. Choisie, l'un pour vous, et l'autre pour Madame Masham. Je me suis donné l'honneur de lui écrire, en faveur de la Cadette de mes Belles-Soeurs,†3 qui va à Londres, par une occasion favorable, à dessein d'y chercher une place, comme je vous l'avois écrit une fois. Je vous prie, Monsieur, de la favoriser de vôtre
― 324 ―
recommendation, et de croire que vous ne pourriez m'obliger plus sensiblement. Elle demeurera à Londres, chez Mrs. Guiguer et Tourton Marchands de ses parens,†1 jusqu'à ce quelle ait trouvé quelque chose. Si vous découvriez une place de Demoiselle, dans une maison de qualité, vous m'obligeriez infiniment de lui en donner avis et de l'appuyer. Mylord de Schaftsbury est toûjours à Roterdam, où il ne voit presque personne. J'ai peur que ce Seigneur n'ait eu quelque chagrin, et qu'il ne donne dans la Mélancholie. Ce seroit un très-grand dommage. Je l'ai vû ici avec beaucoup de plaisir, et si j'en avois le tems, j'irois à Roterdam pour le voir. Il me parla beaucoup de l'étude de la Langue Greque. Ce seroit en effet un agréable amusement pour lui, s'il avoit quelcun qui l'aidât en cela et qui lui applanît les difficultez, ce qu'il ne sauroit trouver à Roterdam. Je voudrois être à portée de l'aller voir souvent, je tâcherois de lui rendre ce petit service.†2 Il ne se passe rien ici de nouveau, qui mérite qu'on l'écrive, et il ne s'imprime plus rien, que ce qui étoit commencé depuis long-tems. Je travoille fortement à mes Commentaires, sur les livres Historiques de la Bible, dont je donnerai un volume, dès que la paix approchera.†3 Sans cela, nos libraires n'osent rien entreprendre. Je suis, Monsieur, Vôtre trèshumble et très obeïssant serviteur
J LE CLERC.
Address: A Monsieur Monsieur Locke A Oates.
Endorsed by Locke: J: le Clerc 24 Jun 04
3560. Peter King, later first Baron King, to Locke, 14 June 1704 (3551, 3562)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 12, ff. 254-5. Answered by no. 3562.
London June 14. 1704.
Sir
Having so good an opportunity of writing you I would not faile of letting you know, that the Gent. I mentioned to you†4 came to Town last night, and It seems, as if all matters were like to go very fair, He will not insist on having any more secur'd than what was
― 325 ―
first propos'd, viz. 4000 l. on each side—As for the setling, he seems to be willing that it should either continue at Interest or be laid out in a purchase as it shall be found to be most convenient, that the husband and wife shall have the profits for their lives and the lives of the Survivor, and after their death to go amongst the children, not tying all to the eldest son, but, I believe, he will be willing to leave that in a great measure to the discretion of the father—This I think is fair, however should be glad to know your thoughts of it— As for his 4000 l. he must take it up, unless your friend will accept of a morgage, he intimates that if he will, he will give him 6 l. per cent. which is 40 l. per annum odds, whereas if he takes it up, he shall apply himself to Mr Edwards of Bristol,†1 where he may have it at 5 l. per Cent. I believe this fact is so, mr Edwards told me last term, that he should have whatever he pleas'd from him—It was hinted to him that it may be your servant might have occasion to transfer the morgage, and that might not be acceptable to him, he said, that if he made a morgage whether for more or less It should be at the pleasure of the morgagee to transfer if he thought fit—I am at a loss what to do in this matter—Here will be a good security, such an one as would not be in the least scrupled from another, There will be a great deal of trouble and loss of Interest saved in hunting out a new security—However, as in all humane affairs, there are Inconveniencys on the other side too, An advice of an able and judicious friend is at all times seasonable, but especially in such a juncture as this, I cannot apply my self to any one better than your self, and my experience of your past kindness makes me sure that you will not deny this, as I may call it, fatherly assistance, to let me know your thoughts thereon, and if you could send over your letter to Harlow too morrow night so as I may have it by Fridays post†2 It will be the more acceptable—
I was with the inline image . of Canterbury this day and presented him your duty, he spoke very kindly of you, and with very great respect, especially for the publick good you did about the coin, he mentioned some answers that you gave to some Objections or Querys of My Lord Lonsdales in relation to the Coin,†3 I Believe if you had a copy of them, It would be an acceptable present to the inline image .—I find Some other persons besides your self have the same opinion of the
― 326 ―
management of affairs — You had a farther proof in the last flying post in the alterations in Scotland†1 — I am Sir
Your Most Affectionate Cosin
P. KING.
Endorsed by Locke: P. King 14 June 04 Answered 15

Dear Cosin
All the proposals he makes seem so well to answer the character he has of an honest fair man that considering the £40 per annum
― 327 ―
odds in the interest besides what time may be lost in seeking out a good security for such a summ that I think you ought to refer it to him to doe what he pleases i e accept his proposal. where in there will be need of noe other care but that you have lands of value enough let out to you. He appears to me to be so fair a man that I think you should doe all things with him with frankness and easyness. I am

3563. Peter King, later first Baron King, to Locke, 17 June 1704 (3562, 3564)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 12, ff. 256-7. Answers no. 3562; answered by no. 3564.
London June. 17. 1704.
Sir
I thank you very much for your speedy advice by your last letter —I was with the Gent. last night and I find that I misapprehended him in one point, viz. the giving 6 l. per Cent. he saith that what he said was, that if your friend expected 6 l. per Cent. he must then
― 328 ―
take up the mony from Mr Edwards who would procure it for him at 5 l. per Cent. I did not seem to insist any thing on my misapprehending of him, I do not know but the mistake was on my side. But in Short that which he saith is, that he ows already 2000 l. which is charged on an estate design'd by him for his younger son, which the country values at 400 l. or 500 l. per annum†1 but I believe not really worth so much, it cost him about 6000 l. and since that several estates are dropt into hand—This 4000 l. he designs to charge on the estate design'd for his eldest son which is worth about 1000 l. per annum—As for this 4000 l. he will either take it up himself on a morgage, or let your friend have it, and transfer it as he pleaseth, and pay him the interest regularly every half year. I Suppose that Next Munday night†2 we shall be near adjusting all these matters, and therefore I once more desire the favour of you to send me a line by Harlow which may come hither by Mundays post, whether it be best for Your friend to take this morgage himself—I do not know for my part where it is now possible to get a good security for such a summ—He seems to be indifferent whether he takes it from another and pays it your friend, or whether your friend takes it himself—As for the Archbishop he did desire me to get a Copy of that mentioned in my last from you, and it will be very acceptable to him, he told me, that if you had it not, he would endeavour to get it from the Executors of My Lord Lonsdale. I am
Sir
Your Most affectionate Cosin and Most Humble Servant,
P. KING.
Address: These For John Locke Esquire at Oates.
Endorsed by Locke: P: King 17 Jun. 04 Answered 18
3564. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 18 June 1704 (3563, 3571)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 388-9. Answers no. 3563.
Oates 18 Jun. 04
Dear Cosin
My freind being satisfied as he is of the honesty of the Gent, I doe not see why he should not take a good security which is offerd to
― 329 ―
him, and he will obleige the offerer by accepting; where his money will lye safe, and his interest come easy, rather than run the venture of lucrum cessans†1 if noe other, by hunting for a security in other places, since if he take care to have a sufficient security on a good title here, he may ease himself, if any inconvenience should hereafter arise here, by transfering. This is what appears to me in the case. which I tell you because you aske me though you know these matters better than I and the particulars in this case are more in your view than mine. I suppose he will not offer a scanty security, for that would creat some difficulty to manage. But the sincerity and fairness my freind depends on will I suppose make the whole affair and every part of it goe easy. I shall look for the papers and if I can find them get them immediately transcribed for his grace. I am
Dear Cosin
Your most affectionate Cosin and humble servant

3565. Locke to Anthony Collins, 19 June 1704 (3558, 3566)
B.M., Add. MS. 4290, ff. 45v-47. Copy made by or for Desmaizeaux. Printed presumably from a copy of this text containing an additional paragraph, and with wrong date 29 June 1704, in A Collection of Several Pieces, pp. 316-18; the additional paragraph is here supplied from A Collection. Locke's endorsement of no. 3558, which this letter answers, and no. 3567, Collins's answer to this letter, show that 19 June is the correct date.
Oates. 19. June. 04.
Dear Sir
If the chaise, you have had so much trouble about, give†a me as much satisfaction afterwards as it will in the first service I shall
― 330 ―
receive from it, the Conquerors of the world will not ride in their triumphant chariots with more pleasure, than I shall in my little Tumbrel. It <will>†a bring me what I prefer to glory. For methinks he understands but little of the true sweetness of life that does†b not more relish the conversation of a worthy and ingenuous freind in retirement, than the noise and rout of the croud in the streets with all their acclamations and buzza's. I long therefore that the machine should be dispatchd, and expect it as greedily as a hungry merchant does†b a ship from the East Indies which is to bring him a rich cargo. I hope the Coachmaker does†b not live far from you; for if he be a slow man of London,†1 I would have him quickned once a day, that he may make as much hast as if the satisfaction of two lovers depended on his dispatch.
<In the mean time, give me leave to desire you, to bestow some of your spare hours on the Epistles to the Corinthians, and to try whether you can find them intelligible or no. You will easily guess the reason of this; and when I have you here, I hope to convince you it will not be lost labour: only permit me to tell you, you must read them with something more than an ordinary application.>†a †2
The samples you have sent me†c I must conclude from the abilitys of the Author to be very excellent.†3 But what shall I be the better for the most exact and best proportion'd picture that ever was drawn if I have not eyes to see the correspondence of the parts? I confess the lines are too subtle for me, and my dull sight cannot perceive their connections. I am not envious and therefore shall not be troubled if others find themselves instructed with so extraordinary and sublime a way of reasoning. I am content with my own mediocrity. And though I call the thinking faculty in me mind, Yet I cannot because of that name compare or equal it in any thing to that infinite and incomprehensible being which for want of right and distinct conceptions is cald Mind also, or the Eternal Mind. I endeavour to make the best use I can of every thing, and therefore though I am in dispair to be the wiser for these learned
― 331 ―
instructions, yet I hope I shall be the merryer for them, when You and I take the air in the Calesh together. I am
Dear Sir
Your most Humble and most Obedient servant
J LOCKE
3566. Anthony Collins to Locke, 20 June 1704 (3565, 3567)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 7, ff. 47-8. Answered by no. 3570.
Lincolns Inne. June 20/04
Dear Sir
It is out of zeal to serve you that I give you the trouble of so many questions about the affair of the Chaise, as well as, that I deferd bespeaking it till munday the 12th, when I received your final directions.†1 It is now going forward with all Imaginable dispatch. In yours of 14th†2 you desire a leading rain that a servant may lead your horse when you have a mind to it. What think you of having the rains made to part at hand? will not that equally serve your purpose?
The Clergy triumph mightily upon Dr. S.'s†3 Digression concerning Connate Ideas in his late book. Those that I am acquainted with take all occasions to tell me of it: but if reducing 'em to silence be a mark of its being indefensible, I have had that advantage over 'em. Upon which I made this observation, that it is easier to defend Nonsense in print than in discourse where they have time to invent disguises and Artifices to impose on the Reader, and where they have the liberty to omitt speaking to what is material: by which they certainly gain their ends with at least all those that beforehand desire to have 'em in the right. What I said to 'em was in short this: that, besides the misrepresentations of your meaning, which I showed 'em, what he contended for, was nothing at all: viz. Ideas in the mind that were never perceiv'd, which was all one to me as to say, Ideas in the mind that were out of the mind. in the next place that since he did not agree with you in the meaning of the words Innate Ideas, all his exceptions to your arguments against a sence of Innate Ideas that he agrees with you to be false, as if they were design'd against his peculiar opinion, was founded on a most
― 332 ―
evident mistake. For would it not be impertinent to endeavour to show that Mr Lockes arguments against Mr Lownds do not conclude against Innate Ideas, and the case is just the same with respect to Innate Ideas and raising the value of money as it is between Innate Ideas and Innate Ideas us'd in different senses. but not to trouble you with any more of this stuff who by this time are sufficiently surfeited with the Doctor, I am
Dear Sir
Your most humble and most Obedient Servant
A. COLLINS
My most humble service to all the family.
Just as I was going to close up this there were two packets brought to my Chambers by your order as the Bearer said, and directed to you.
Endorsed by Locke: A: Collins 20 Jun. 04 Answered 23
3567. Anthony Collins to Locke, 22 June 1704 (3566, 3570)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 7, ff. 49-50. Answers no. 3565; answered by no. 3570.
Lincolns Inne June 22/04
Dear Sir
Tho I cannot pretend to make any returns in expressions for those Extraordinary ones of friendship and kindness in yours of the 19th yet I can love and esteem you as much as is possible for any one to do; and it is equally out of my power to abate any thing in the latter, as it is to imitate you in the former. I heartily wish for your sake that you did not overvalue me, that I might be able to serve you more and better than I do, and for my own sake that I might never have any allay to my enjoyments of you by my fears that I can never contribute enough to your Entertainment.
I return you my thanks for recommending to me the reading of St Paul's Epistles to the Corinthians. I will employ all the leisure I have till I wait upon you to peruse 'em. I think I may say that if my penetration was equal to that disposition of mind that I carry to the reading of 'em (for I have no designs to find any particular opinion there but only to endeavour to get the same Ideas that the Author had when he wrote) I should give you all the satisfaction
― 333 ―
you can desire when I discourse with you, and should even prevent your instructions. Mr Tyrrell is come to Town to publish his History, and is very busie in making his Index.†1 He therefore desires me to acquaint you that he has performd what you desir'd of him in yours,†2 and in a few days will give you a particular account of it himself in a letter. I am
Dear Sir
Your most Humble and most Obedient servant
A. COLLINS
My most humble service to all at Oates.
The Chaise goes on with all the speed that my repeated requests can make.
Address: For John Locke Esq [at Oates, by Joslyn, Bishops Stortford]
Postmark: IV 22

3570. Locke to Anthony Collins, 23 June 1704 (3567, 3573A)
The Carl H. Pforzheimer Library, New York. Transcribed from photostat. Part printed in A Collection of Several Pieces, pp. 319-20. Answers nos. 3566 and 3567.
Oates 23 Jun 04
Dear Sir
The Gent. you speak of have a great-deale of reason to be pleased with the discourse you mention,†3 there being noe thing ever writ in their strain and way more perfectly than that is, and it may stand for a pattern for those that have a mind to excell in their admirable use of language and method of talkeing if at least there be any need
― 335 ―
of a pattern to those who so naturaly and by a peculiar genius of their own fall into that which the profane illiterate vulgar poor wretches are strangers to and cannot imitate. But more of this to make us merry when the chaise brings us together. I hope the chaise, for the more ease of it, is to hang upon short traces before and behind as that which Yonge Mr Clarke†1 had here did. I know not whether I mentioned it before or said no thing of it as supposeing it so of course. That which put it now into my head is a question which was asked me last night viz Whether my chaise hung or noe? I now every moment wish it done, not out of any impatience I am in for the Machin but for the man, the man I say that†a is to come in it. A man that has not his fellow and to all that loves me. If I regret my old age it is you that make me, and call me back to the world just as I was leaveing of it and leaveing it as a place that had very little valuable in it, but who would not be glad to spend some years with you, make hast therefore and let me ingross what of you I can. I am
Dear Sir
Your most humble and most obedient servant
J LOCKE
All this family salutes you
my service to your ingenious neighbour†2
I know not but the parting of the rains at hand will serve the purpose of leading without a rain on purpose, all the objection is whether it being fastend on to the end of the cheek of the bit as it is will lead as well as a loose rain fastend just to the cheek, an other thing is whether it will not be best for you to have the usual rains with you in the chaise constantly to prevent any mischeif may hapen by the carelesness of your leader as may happen some times? Haveing suggested my thoughts. I leave it to you
Address: For Anthony Collins Esq at his Chamber in Serle Court in Lincolns Inne London
Postmark: IV 26

3573. Locke to Awnsham Churchill, 27 June 1704 (3523, 3595)
B.M., Stowe MS. 748, f. 27. Printed in Fox Bourne, ii. 546.
Oates 27 Jun. 04
Sir
This comes to meet you in town and to bid you welcome. For I hope you have been able to make good the hopes you gave us that you would be in town this week and that I may congratulate your safe returne strong and trig†1 as you were before. I shall long to receive the assurance of it from your own hand, therefore pray write me by the first post, and put into your letter when I shall see you here. I desire it may be very speedily for I hasten a-pace to my journeys end, and can count upon but a very few days in this world, and have many things to say to you, some whereof may concerne your own interest. Doe not think I aggravate my case to hasten you, when you see me you will conclude it is for the last time, and that if your lameness had kept you away a little longer you would have returnd too late to see me at all. I am
Sir your most assured freind and humble servant
J LOCKE
My service to your brother I received his letter of the last week

3573A. Locke to Anthony Collins, 30 June 1704 (3570, 3577)

Dear Sir
I conclude that by this time the Calesh is finished or will soon be. I have therefore here inclosed sent you a bill on Mr. Churchil for twenty pounds which by what you have said to me I guess will be near about the price of that machin, what it proves over or under will easily be adjusted in an other reconing wherein I stand indebted to you. ― 338 ―
will not forget to provide a whip proper for the driver that may be fit to reach and quicken Dun if he should at any time stick in the mire.†1

I hope he is not soe taken up with the names of princes and great men in his index but that he will afford a line or two to an old freind of mean rank dormant in the Country. who when he has got you here, which he hopes to doe speedily, shall not much envie those who fill the mouth of fame in the historys and Gazets of the age. I am
Dear Sir
Your most humble and most obedient servant
JOHN LOCKE

3575. James Tyrrell to Locke, 4 July [1704] (3511, 3607)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 22, ff. 169-70. Year from Locke's endorsement.
London July 4
Dear Sir
Allmost as soon as I came hither, I desired our Freind Mr Collyns (who told me he was about then to write to you) to let you know, I was in Town, and being very busy desired your pardon for not writing to you, till I had perfected the Index to my next vol: but that I had seen all punctually performd concerning your Books sent down to Oxon, which you had enjoynd me;†2 whither he writ all that I desired him I know not, since you tell me in your last he mentiond no more, but that I was in Town; and therefore it will be no vain Repetition to assure you, that upon the Receipt of your first I went to Oxon; and deliverd the Books to Dr: Hudson, and a week or 2 after, going thither again, saw them placed in the Library and your name enterd among the Benefactors with such a space left under it as you desired;†3 and now it is done, I am very glad that you persisted in your first Resolution of sending down those very books again which were at first orderd, which shews Mr: Churchill was not so much mistaken, as I at first imagind, but I suppose you have taken this oppertunity of their being sent back to look into those volumes of Travels, in which one may find divers new Things worth a mans notice.†4 I hope my next volume will please better then either of the former, since it relates matters nearer our own Times and for which wee have larger, and more certain Accounts.
― 340 ―
when the book comes out you shall have one sent you down, which may serve some body in the Family, since I cannot expect your health and time will give you leave to peruse so long, (I wish I might not say) tedious a work; however, I think that I may boldly affirm, that I have taken notice of some things in our History not observed before by our English Historians, and I hope I have in all Points preferd the Character of a true before that of a fine writer, which I do not pretend to: I have not yet Read what Dr: Sherlock has written against your thoughts of Innate Ideas, but if that book be no better then others I have seen of the same Authours, I cannot from the Character I have received of it, but think he had done much better to have confin'd himself to practical rather then polemick divinity, in which his very Freinds allow him not to excel; but few men know their own Talents; but strive to busy themselves in somwhat for which God never designd them: as may appear likewise from Mr:Tolands late letters,†1 in which are many things boldly asserted without any due proof as I could easily shew you: had I more time and paper but I reserve these subjects for some of those pleasant conversations which I flatter myself I may some time or other with you, and in the meane w<hile>†a continue
Your Faithfull†b and humble servant
J TYRRELL.
I saw Mr: Pawling to day who presents his service to you and is glad to hear you are so well; and hopes when you have your chaise down you will trie it this summer in a Journey to Town, and I wish it might be whilest I am here.
I pray give my humble service to Francis and my good and the rest of that worthy Family.
Address (written by Tyrrell's amanuensis): For John Lock Esqr [at Oates, by Joslyn, Bishops Stortford] This with care.
Postmarks: IY 4; IS (?)
Endorsed by Locke: J: Tyrrell 4 Jul 04 Answered 28

3576. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 5 July 1704 (3574, 3578)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 390-1. Answered by no. 3578.
Oates 5 July 04
Dear Cosin
After I had romaged amongst all my papers, till I was weary, in vain, in search of what my Lord ArchBishop desired of me, I recollected that my Lord Lansdales discourse was sent to me by his grace with order to peruse it, and set down my remarques as I went along in the same paper by the sides and so return it. This command of his Grace I obeyd and so returnd it with my observations wherof there never was any other copy. This you may be pleased to acquaint his Grace, presenting at the same time my most humble duty to him. If his Grace chance to light on <these>†a papers in the hands of the Executors where he intends to inquire after them. If he thinks there be any thing in them that needs a farther explication I shall be glad to be honourd with his commands thereupon. For being both indisposed in my health, and unwilling to delay what two so extraordinary persons expected, my notes as I remember were very short and cursory.
John Rogers Sir Francis's Coachman brings you this. He lyes at the plough <overagainst>†b Mr Pawlings and comes out of town again tomorrow morning. His business is to bring down an empty coach which has been some time in town a mending. Amongst my books at Mr Pawlings is Hippocrates Workes a <large>†c folio.†1 I have given it to Mr Coste, and would be glad it might be brought hither by the convenience of this empty coach. But its comeing is not of that moment as to disturb any your least business now at the end of the terme.†2 Nor is Mr Coste in hast for it, it will doe as well an other time. But pray why will not Mr Pawling write to me or send me an account. I have orderd half a dozen pounds of Chocolate to be sent to you for me, if it come to your hands before Mr Collins come hither pray send it by him in my chaise, if not bring
― 342 ―
it with you if you have a convenience, else leave it with Mr Churchil to be sent. I see you are so busy that I must expect no thing from you till I see you and therefore I aske you noe questions. I am
Dear Cosin
your most affectionate cosin and humble servant
J LOCKE
Pray send the inclosed by the Penny post
Address: For Peter King Esqr at his Chamber in Tanfeild court in the Inner Temple London

3579. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 6 July 1704 (3578, 3581)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 392-3. Answers no. 3578; answered by no. 3581.
Oates 6 July 04
Dear Cosin
I am glad I shall see you here the next weeke when you tell me you will settle Sir Francis's business. The main difficulty I take to be in that business is the ignorance you are in what land to take in Suffolk, neither you nor Sir Francis knowing what names the farmes mentiond in the settlement bare now, nor what number of akers the farmes now let to the tenants there have respectively in them, without being assured of that methinks we shall take for security a pig in a poke and I for many reasons desire not to have an uncertain or Scanty security. Whose fault it is that we have noe light in this yet, I cannot tell, but this I am sure that there has been time enough to have had the particulars very full and clear before this time if any care had been taken about it. As for the Exemplifications of the Recovery etc I know they need make noe stop. But the other I fear will. If you have any thing to say about this matter of the particulars I desire you to write directly to Sir Francis for I shall expect a full, clear security but I am resolvd to say my self nothing to Sir Francis about the particulars, for I leave that to be setled by you and him. I am
Dear Cosin
Your most affectionate Cosin and humble servant
J LOCKE

3580. Dr. William Cole to Locke, 6 July 1704 (3299)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 6, ff. 211-12.
I must, Sir, very much owne the honour you doe me in requiring my opinion in relation to your indisposition, when your owne
― 345 ―
judgment is such as to solve all the difficulties can arise eyther about it or any other. But in obedience to your command, I conceive, Sir, your symptoms rather argue a Cachexy†1 than (what I suspect you apprehend) a Dropsy, though that many times passes into this when neglected. For I am of opinion that the causa continens of the Cachexy is the morbifick matter contained within the Fibres, but that that of the Dropsy is the same (not reckoning in this account what is thrown out by the rupture of the Lymphaticks, which kind of Dropsy I am of opinion happens very rarely) extravasated out of them into the interstices of them, or into the abdomen, or thorax. However in both cases though evacuations per sedem are very necessary, yet in the dropsy I conceive they ought to be made confestim, but in the Cachexy they should be attempted lentis gradibus, least the matter included in those very small, and already too much relaxed, channells by being too much exagitated, instead of being extruded weaken the tone of those vessells the more, and so increase the distemper. Therfore (not to range too far into Theory and since you seeme to require my thoughts only as to the Cure) I should propose for a gentle pill, to be taken at short intervalls according to the effect, the Pil. stomac cum gummi, to ℈j of which might be added [Tartari vitriolati] gr.iij vel iiij pro dosi, or more or lesse as they worke. Or if these prove too languid the pil. Hydropicæ, Pharmacopœiæ Bateanæ, might be used I hope with good effect: And though some of the Ingredients of the latter pill are of the stronger sort of Hydroticks, yet the Gums I conceive allay them, and by a dissolving power, we think they have, fit them to enter into the recesses where the minera morbosa lyes, when given in small doses wherby they cannot be caryed off too soone (as perhaps that of ℈j more or lesse as they worke). And the Calomel in them I take to be a very proper Ingredient, since it both insinuates itselfe more deeply than others can, and attenuates considerably. On the days, Sir, you purge not, I conceive you may doe well to take some bitter Infusions twice a day, adding some chalybeate Wine. For instance; ℞ rad. Helenii ℥i absynt. Vulg. centaur. min. genistæ sumnit.,†a siccatorum an. Mj. bacc. Junip. contus. ℥iiij. Macerentur in Vini albi Ulisippenensis, aq. Lact. alexit an. ffij sine admoto igne
― 346 ―
per 2 vel 3 dies ante usum. coh.†a tempore usus effundendæ, sumantur, circiter ℥iiij (plus minus pro ratione εὐφορίας) addendo Vini chalybeati (Pharmacopœiæ) cochl. j.
I propose also, Sir, that you take an Electuary of honey ℥vj, flos [sulphuris] ℥i, and Helen. ℥ss mixed, severall times in the day if it will agree with your palate. I have knowne very good effects of it in asthmaticall cases as simple as it looks.
I hope this method will answer the severall intentions you propose, and shall be very glad to heare of the effect of them; and in the meane time shall esteeme my selfe very much favoured if you please to let me know if you receive this.
I am, Sir,
Your most humble and devoted servant,
W. COLE.
Great Queenstreet. Jul. 6. 704.
I propose moreover, Sir, that you promote your Insensible Perspiration every morning for about an houre; of the advantage wherof I my selfe am very sensible.
Be pleased to pardon this scrible which I have not the time to file and transcribe.
Address: For the very much honoured Mr Locke at Sir Francis Mashams at Oates, in Essex
Postmarks: IY 6; (London receiving office, indecipherable)
Endorsed by Locke: Dr Cole 6 Jul. 04

3582. Dr. Robert Pitt to Locke, 8 July 1704 (3433, 3586)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 17, ff. 191-2.
July. 8. 1704.
Sir
I have omitted answering your letter 2 or 3 days, that I might more fully consider your Case. Your breath is shorter and more difficult, because your Cough has been less, a freeer Expectaration being the most natural cleanser of the Lungs. When you spitt more, Your breathing will be more easy. The swelling of the Legs will be less, when you receiv Air more copiously by the Lungs, the blood being made more vivid, and the Circulation stronger. It is observ'd some times to be a metastasis of the Humours to our Advantage, if the mass of blood shall in the mean time rise to greater strength, and improve the Perspiration. Your observation from the soreness of your belly upon pressure does not infer an Ascitical tumor,†2 but the acrimony of the flatulent Humours. I hope an easy depletion of the Phlegm from the stomach and bowells, and the thinner humours with it may be of use to you. I have sent Pills, which I hope will answer your Expectation. They are compounded of Aloes, Salt of
― 348 ―
Tartar, Balsam of Tolu. You may take 2 or 3 of them at your going to bed twice in the week. I recommended a Medicine to St Bartholomews Hospital about 5 years since, which is cal'd Elect. Asthmaticum, in imitation of the Electuarium Divinum of our Dr Hall.†1 Very great Quantities are constantly taken by the Patients, who have distinguisht <it>†a by a name of their own. The Dispensary has very frequently recommended it. The Basis is Sp. of Sulphur, taken up in a Conserv or Pulp. But the Sp. Sulphur. dulcis, or the Elixir Proprietatis Paracelsi will have the same effect on you and be more easy to take. It assists the Stomach, prevents the growth of Phlegm, incides†2 that in the Lungs, and is one of the surest Diuretics. I am very glad, your water retains its colour. This makes me more readily recommend to you a Remedy, which may have a very great and sure effect. It is the use of the Drawing Plaister and Oyntment, which you have often mett with in the advertisements. Apply'd between your shoulders, or within each Thigh, it will give much more benefitt then any Issues, almost as much as a Seton, will give you no sort of Trouble, may be parted with and resum'd, when you please. The Lungs and mass of blood will in the mean time rise to their strength, when so much of the Pressure shall every day be withdrawn. Sir, I heartily wish, you may be one of the great numbers, who do receiv very great advantage from its use.
My humble service to my Lady and Mr Masham. I am
Your most affectionate servant
R PITT.
Address: For Mr John Locke in Oates
Endorsed by Locke: Dr Pitt 8 Jul. 04 Answered 9
3583. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 9 July 1704 (3581, 3585)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 394-5. Answers no. 3581.
Oates July. 9. 1704
Dear Cosin
Yours of yesterday by our freind Mr C†3 was very welcome to me with the hopes of seeing you on Saturday. He also deliverd yours
― 349 ―
to the Gent whom you have not heard one Syllable from since you were last here,†1 whether you will now hear any thing from him I know not, for he took your letter and put it in his pocket without opening and whether he hath read it yet no body knows or perhaps shall. He knew when you last talked with him what farther information you expected from him, and where the matter stuck, one would have thought that being his proper business in the case he should have all this while informed himself and there upon have informed you But his usual way of doeing business is to expect that other people should minde, solicit, and doe all for him and he doe no thing towards it himself
If you see my Lord Cheif Justice Trevor again pray make him my most humble acknowledgments for his favour of enquireing after my health which is not soe good as I could wish it
You will see by the inclosed what I desire of you in reference to Dr Pitt. If you can get the things of him before Wednesday you may send them by Mr Shute whose chamber is near Mr Collins's in Serle Court. I know not whether Dr Pitt will take a fee of me I being of the Faculty however I desire it should be offerd him that it may be at his choise to take or refuse it as he thinks fit. Seale my letter to him and give it him with my respects. I am Dear Cosin
Your most affectionate Cosin and servant
J LOCKE
Of the rest when I see you

3584. Nicolas Toinard to Locke, 9/20 July 1704 (3132)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 21, ff. 232 and 66. The latter is an undated scrap. By the Triumvir superstes Toinard apparently means himself; if so, the scrap probably dates from after Formentin's death in 1703, and is more likely to have been sent with f. 232 than by itself or as part of a letter now lost. Toinard might perhaps use the expression during Formentin's visit to Rome in 1700-1 (no. 2693, etc.), but that seems unlikely; there is nothing in the correspondence between Locke and Toinard in that period, or indeed at any time before 1703, to associate with the contents of the scrap.
― 350 ―
<c>e†a 20. Juillet 1704.
Je me sers, monsieur, du retablissement du commerce des letres d'Holande pour me remetre dans l'honneur de votre souvenir, et vous prier de me donner des nouveles de votre santé, que je souhaite etre semblable à la mienne. me voila resté le dernier des Triumvirs, aiant perdu le capitene de marine à son retour de Rome, où il etoit alé fort inconsiderement, vu une furieuse descente avec sarcosele et hydroscele, dont il etoit depuis long tems incommodé.†1 Je vous exhorte à ne me pas quiter. Je suis votre ancien, et j'ay plus d'âge que n'en ont eu les deux Triumvirs.
Mr l'Abé du Bos donna l'année derniere au Public un Livre Intitulé, Les Interests de l'Angleterre mal-entendus dans la guerre presente. Il s'en veut beaucoup de bien. Il est marqué comme traduit d'Anglois, et imprimé à Amstredam. cependant l'Original, nonobstant les Anglicismes, est François, né natif de Paris, et imprimé à Brusselle. Il y en a d'autres editions posterieures de Rouen, Paris et Lion, et la siziême est sous le presse. comme l'Auteur n'a pas mis son nom, certain quidam l'a ajouté apres, dans la guerre d'Angleterre, <en>†a ces termes, Par m. l'Abé du Bos <,>†a et quelques <le>cteurs†a le raport à la ligne où il y a, mal-entendus.†2 Tu quid censes?†3 Je suis—
J'ecris ce billet sans envelope, à cause du port, mais ecrivez moi en grand volume.
Address: For Mr John Locke at Mr Paulings overagainst the ploug in little Lincolns innfeilds London

(English)Reculam, quantumvis publicam, tibi tamen intra me et te dixero.

(English)Demirantur omnes quòd Auctor Αρμονικὸς Abavum†1 habens eundem cum Primario Regni Administro, adeo ut amborum Avus et Avia frater et soror fuerint, nihil inde tamen sit honoratior, etiam τῷ Αρχηγ.†2 probè ac pridem notus.†3 Enimvero illi nesciunt quid civibus ejus perpetuum sit apud Stephanum Tornacensem Episcopum, quidve ὁ Αρμον. semper expertus fuerit nempe nullo se unquam loco apud consanguineos suos fuisse habitum.†4
(English)Exemplo id sit quod ejus Avunculus aliquando à Triumviro superstite percontabatur his verbis: Mon neveu .... sait-il quelque chose.†5 Id mihi cum risu retulit Triumvir; quod dictum velim salvâ, ut par est, modestiâ.
(Latin)Here is a small matter; however much it may be public property, still I will tell you about it between ourselves.
(Latin)Everybody is surprised that the Harmonic author, though he has the same great-grandfather†1 as the First Minister of the Kingdom, so far so that their respective grandfather and grandmother were brother and sister†2 , has yet never received more honour on that account, even though he has long been well known to the Minister.†3 Indeed they do not know what lasting fame redounds to his fellow-citizens in the writings of Stephen bishop of Tournai, nor do they know what the Harmonic one has always experienced, namely that he has never been held in any regard by his kinsmen.†4
(Latin)For example: his uncle once asked the surviving Triumvir the following question: Mon neveu ... sait-il quelque chose?†5 The Triumvir reported this to me with some amusement. I hope I have told the story with due regard to modesty.
