3586. Dr. Robert Pitt to Locke, [c. 12 July 1704] (3582, 3606)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 17, ff. 193-4. Locke enclosed a letter to Pitt in his letter to King of 13 July (no. 3587). It was presumably his answer to the present letter, in which case the date attached to this letter is to be regarded as that of his receipt of it as well as of his answer.
Sir,
Altho these Pills are not great Purgers, they will, in a due Proportion, operate as much, as probably you will well bear. You may add to the Dose, and repeat them more often. It is to be fear'd, that a severe operation might abate the strength of your Spirits, and make the mass of blood more feeble. But after one or 2 more tryals of these, you shall have another sort of a stronger Kind. One Spoonfull of the Syrup of Buckthorn in a glass of White wine may answer your purpose more nimbly, then a strong Pill, and leav not so much of the Impressions on the blood. The weather takes up your Urine, but, I hope, those Diureticks will move it. When you are more easy, your sleep will be better, which I heartily desire
Your most humble servant
R PITT.
Address: For Mr John Locke
Endorsed by Locke: Dr Pitt 13 Jul 04 Answered

3590. Jean Barbeyrac to Locke, 15/26 July 1704 (3232)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 3, f. 144.
A Berlin ce 26 Juillet 1704.
Monsieur
Quoi que la méprise où le Gazetier de Hollande m'avoit jetté, ou plutôt plusieurs personnes au rapport de qui je m'êtois fié, et qui avoient pris pour vous un Anglois d'un nom fort approchant du vôtre; quoi que cette méprise, disje, m'aît causé une véritable douleur, jusques à ce que je fûs détrompé, je ne laisse pas d'être bien aise d'avoir pris d'abord l'alarme, avant que de m'être exactement informé d'une nouvelle que je croiois véritable parce que je craignois fort qu'elle ne le fût.†1 J'ai éprouvé par là un plaisir semblable à celui des personnes qui recouvrent un bien qu'elles s'imaginoient avoir perdu; et j'ai eû de plus occasion de vous témoigner les justes sentimens que j'ai pour vous, tant à cause de ce mérite distingué auquel tous les honnêtes gens ne sauroient refuser leur estime, qu'à cause des grandes honnêtetez dont je vous suis redevable, et qui, outre leur valeur propre, tirent un prix infini de la personne de qui elles partent. Vous me fournissez, Monsieur, un nouveau sujet d'obligation, en me donnant un avis également digne de vous, et convenable aux gens comme moi, qui sachant bien quàm sibi sit curta supellex,†2 ne doivent hazarder leurs pensées qu'avec la dernière circonspection, sur tout lors qu'elles tendent à contredire celles de quelque Savant, à qui ils ne voudroient en aucune maniére se comparer. Je suis doublement fâché de la perte
― 356 ―
d'une Lettre que Monsieur Coste m'écrivoit, depuis que j'ai appris que vous m'y donniez quelque autres avis sur mon travail.†1 Comme l'impression de cet Ouvrage ne se commencera pas de cette année, je suis encore à tems de les recevoir et d'en profiter, si vôtre commodité vous permet de me faire encore cette faveur. Je souhaitte, Monsieur, que vôtre santé se rétablisse entièrement, et que vous viviez encore Nestoreos annos. Je suis avec un profond respect
Monsieur
Votre très humble et très obéïssant serviteur
BARBEYRAC
Address: A Monsieur Monsieur Locke A Oates
Endorsed by Locke: Barbeyrac 26 Jul. 04
3591. Dr. Robert South to Locke, 18 July 1704 (2645)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 18, ff. 175-6. Probably forwarded by Pawling on 20 July: p. 359.
My worthy, and learned friend,
In a Book lately published by Sherlock†2 (who I believe will leave off Writeing and Breathing together,) I find, that he is so hardy as to venture to attack you. viz. in that Part of it (which he calls a Digression) consisting of about 40 pages, beginning at p 124 and ending with Page the 164. Upon my peruseing whereof, (which I have done very attentively) my poor Opinion is that This Insolent man ought to be Answered; and so answered, as to make him Understand Himself, and you too: Which, I am sure, no man liveing can more Easily and effectually doe than your Self: and if you will but humble Him at the same rate, that you have done a much Abler man,†3 you will lay both Him and his Reputation Low enough, before his Head be so; and indeed so low, that the latter must not hope for a Resurrection.
For, Sir, He has not onely wrote against your Sentiments, (as one may write against the Opinion of Another, and yet very fairly too) but He has allso very virulently reflected upon your Person.
― 357 ―
For besides the Severall flouts scattered Here and there in his Pamphlett; in the 161 page thereof he falls upon You with such Downright Scurrility as This. Thus (says He) all the objections against the old Atheistick Hypothesis are Chargeable upon Him. viz mr Lock. And again, There is not a more formidable objection against Religion, than to teach That mankind is made without any Connate, naturall Impressions, and Ideas of a god, and of good and Evill etc. And in the next page line the 15. The Generall Reason (says he) Why men are so Zealous against these Ideas being Innate is to deliver them selves from the Necessity of Beleiving any Thing of god or Religion. And I doubt not, Sir, but, (how little Heat soever you have shewn throughout your whole Essay) you stand accounted by this Reviler for one of those Zealots; And then, how foul, how Barbarous an Intimation must this needs be, should it passe for no more? But if he should have the Confidence (when called to Account) to plead, That He onely opposed your Doctrine without Strikeing at your Person; besides, that Severall of his expressions, according to the Naturall, and Common Way of Speaking are so manifestly Personall, that the Contrary cannot without a very shamelesse front be so much as pretended; It must be farther owned by all the world, that the whole Drift of his Discourse is to represent the Doctrine asserted by you, as directly Tending to, if not allso necessarily implying, or, at least, Inferring Atheisme as the Naturall Consequent of it. And This, I affirme, shewes it self so plainly in his whole Treatise, that Impudence its self can hardly Deny it. And if not, then by his favor, I doe and must tell Him. That whosoever arraignes any Doctrine under This Character, vertually arraignes the Author of it too; and that, either as an Atheist, if he understands the Consequences of his own Doctrine, and yet adheres to it; or as a fool and Ignoramus, for abetting and adhering to the One because he understands not the other: Either of which Imputations is so Extremely and withall so justly provokeing, that it cannot but be very hard for any One of sense and Spirit, to sitt Silent under it; What Considerations soever may be offered for his doing so. And for my own Part I cannot endure to see such Rawnesse, and Insolence together, so much as seem to Carry off any Thing like a Triumph, where I am sure they can never gett a Victory. I say, I cannot, without some Indignation, behold Ignorance in State; and a pert Empty fellow Strutting it at Those whose Books (or rather Shoes) he is not worthy to carry after them. For the man whom I am now Speaking of, is certainly a very
― 358 ―
Shallow Writer, and wholly destitute of that Scholastick Learning, which all men of Sense have hitherto found Necessary, to mannage disputes by.
I must Confesse, that upon my reading over this Doughty Peice, I drew up some Remarks upon it; but such as I cannot judge worth the Sending to You: who can need no Second or Defender but your self. And if you shall Vouchsafe this Fellow any Answer; it needs not in my poor judgment Come Abroad till the Beginning of Michaelmas Terme,†1 dureing which Time you may with very little Trouble to Your Self quite Annihilate This Whiffling Companion. I heartily wish Your Health and Long Life, and remain
Worthy Sir
Your faithfull friend to serve you.
ROBERT SOUTH.
Cavesham. 18 July 1704.
The Book, wherein you are Concerned is Intituled
A Discourse Conc: the happinesse of good men and the Punishment of the wicked in the Next world. in 8vo.
Address: For his worthily esteemed friend John Lock Esq at Sir Francis mashams house in Essex These.
Endorsed by Locke: Dr South 18 Jul. 04 Answered Aug. 4

3597. Benjamin Furly to Locke, 24 July/4 August 1704 (3503)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 9, ff. 200-1. Probably the letter carried by Shaftesbury, and forwarded by William Popple the younger on 29 August: pp. 386, 388.
Dear Sir
By the return of my Lord Sh— I could not omitt to let you know how much I was troubled to see your letter to my son B—†2 I looked upon my†d- self, as-†d abundantly obliged to you for your singular care of my son Arent, and therefore was in hopes that you would
― 363 ―
have accepted that small present, else I would have let you know how much it cost me, and not left you to pay it double, I have now no other Remedy left me, but to resolve never to make you any more presents, if that be your way.
I have since heard of your great declining in strength, which grievs me more, because I fear I shall never see you more, I have been bantering my wife, at dinner, about going over with my Lord, in the same yacht that brought me over last out of England.†1 But she cannot bear the thoughts of it, during the Warr; I shall however be glad to hear from you, while you can write, and should be heartily glad could I be any ways serviceable to you, who am so long as we shal both continue in this Tabernacle—
Your most humble and sincere friend and servant
BENJAMIN FURLY
Pray give my service to Sir Fr. his Lady, and their Children— Rotterdam 4 August 1704.

3598. Francis Limborch to Locke, 25 July 1704 (3561, 3604)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 13, ff. 242-3.
London 25 July 1704
Honoured Sir
This day fourtnight my little one grew a little Indisposed off a loossnesse which was accompanied with a paine in itts gumms, whereupon j sent to Mrs Wright who judged itt to be his teeth and continued upon him till last saturday now and then accompanied with a fever, for which we by her advice gave itt all whatt was necessary and what she used to give her Children in such cases, butt since saturnight itts turned into a screeming gripe or a Convulsion in the bowels, att which she as well as the physician are mighty apprehensive and have ordered him a glister off some mutton broth and the yourk off an egg with some diascordium, and a
― 364 ―
plaister of mithridate and some oyle off mace upon his stomach and some burnt hartshorne for his dranch, being the last they can doe to him, the stools off the Child are very green waterish and Curdly and he seemes to have a greatt oppression off phleagm upon his Stomach which oppresses him mightily and hath had no sleep since saturnight. this sudden alteration hath perplexed us mightily and emboldened me to trouble you with these lines most sincerily begging iff your health and Constitution permitt to assist me with your advice iff itt might please God to spare him so long; and to pardon the freedome j use, which iff there had nott been this necessity I would nott have presumed to have done; I most sincerily wish you are better att present then when I had the honour to see you, which j shall be very glad to hear, and desiring that My Lady Sir Francis and Mr Masham may finde my most sincere respects in these I subscribe these with the most sincere respects off
Honoured Sir
Your most obliged Humble servant
FRAN LIMBORCH
Address: For John Locke Esquire [at Oates, by Joslyn, Bishops Stortford]
Postmark: IY 25
Endorsed by Locke: Fran: Limborch 25 July 04 Answered 28
3599. [Jonas Proast] to Locke, 28 July 1704
B.L., MS. Locke c. 17, f. 76. The letter was written by the author of A Third Letter Concerning Toleration (1691), who is identified by Anthony Wood as Proast. He uses the signature 'Philochristus' here and as signatory of his Second Letter (1704; p. 342) probably in answer to 'Philanthropus', the signatory of Locke's second and third Letters.
Sir
Whereas you tell me, p. 1. of your Third Letter, that p. 76 of mine, I own the Question to be, Whether the Magistrate has a Right to use Force in matters of Religion? And I tell you, p. 3. of my Letter lately publish'd,†1 that that Affirmation of yours is a meer Fiction: I do now acknowledge, that (though not p. 76. yet) p. 78. I do so express myself in your terms, as to give you some pretense to say, that I own the Question to be what you say I do; and do therefore
― 365 ―
declare, that if I had taken notice of this time enough (as I did not till within these very few daies) I would not have call'd that Affirmation a meer Fiction: Though inasmuch as you will have me to mean by using Force in matters of Religion, what you know I have constantly declared myself never to mean, so far forth I do still affirm that Affirmation to be a meer Fiction. Of this I thought fit thus to advertise you. And I doubt not but you will make such use of it, as you think fitt.
Sir, I am
Your most humble Servant
PHILOCHRISTUS
July the 28th. 1704.
Address: Be pleased to send this To the Author of the Three Letters for Toleration.
Endorsed by Locke: Philochristus 28 Jul 04
3600. Anthony Collins to Locke, 29 July 1704 (3577, 3601)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 7, ff. 53-4. Answered by no. 3608.
Lincolns Inne. July 29./04
Dear Sir
The Conversation at Oates is so extreamly agreable that how much soever I am devoted to you, and dispos'd to desire your happiness, I can wish you no greater than what I lately enjoy'd, unless it was what would arise from a belief that I have in the least contributed to your entertainment: for on this I could justly value my self that amidst a number of ungratefull people I have such a concern for the happiness of one who has done more than all mankind besides for the happiness of others.
I return you my thanks for all your favours and particularly for the free communication of your thoughts to me on such Important subjects which I hope I shall be the better for as long as I live. Sure I am that I am either very unfit to hear any thing from you or am deservedly to blame if I do not improve under such advantages.
I deliver'd yours to Mr Churchil yesterday and have this day put yours to Mr Tyrrel and that to Sir F. Drake†1 in the General
― 366 ―
Post. Mr Shute is at present out of Town, wherefore I must defer the bespeaking your Hat.
I am under infinite obligations to all the family and must desire you to give 'em my most humble service and acknowledgment who am with the greatest sincerity
Dear Sir
Your most Humble and Most Obedient servant
A. COLLINS
My service to Mr Bold†1 Mrs Boulton†2 and Mrs Burdet.†3
Address: To John Locke Esq at Oates in Essex
Endorsed by Locke: A Collins 29 Jul 04 Answered Aug. 11

3601. Locke to Anthony Collins, 2 August 1704 (3600, 3603)
The Carl H. Pforzheimer Library, New York. Transcribed from photostat. Printed in A Collection of Several Pieces, pp. 320-1.
Oates 2 Aug. 04
Dear Sir
Though I cannot by writeing make you a surer title to my self than you have already: Yet I cannot forbear to acknowledg under my hand and seale the great Sense I have of the late favour you did me. Whether that or any thing else will be able to adde any duration to my mouldering Carkass I cannot say, but this I am sure your company and kindness has added to the length of my life, which in my way of measuring does not lye in counting minutes but tasting of enjoyments. I wish the continuance and increase of yours without stint and am
Dear Sir
Your most humble and most obleiged servant

3602. Locke to Philippus Van Limborch, 4 August 1704 (3557)
Amsterdam University Library, MS. R.K., Ba 258 t. Printed in Some Familiar Letters, pp. 539-40; address, in Ollion, p. 225. Answers no. 3557.
(English)
Oates 4 Aug. 04
Vir Amplissime
Pudet me sane tam diuturni silentii nec ab infirmâ et plane fractâ valetudine satis excusatum credo, etiamsi admixta etiam aliqua tui reverentia me a scribendo aliquantulum detinuit satis ex ipso morbo desidiosum. Quorsum enim attinet te eruditioribus sermonibus aptum et commerciis literarum docto liberoque animo dignis, ægrotantis quærelis laboriosisque verbis anhelum scriptorem redolentibus fatigare? Juvat tamen experiri amicitiam tuam, veterem amicum etiamsi senio et morbo inutilem ad sepulchrum usque prosequi. Nihil sane jucundius nec est quod magis animum debilem et languescentem refocillat, quam constans et vegeta amicorum benevolentia, magnum perfugium humanæ fragilitatis, in quo reperitur magna pars voluptatis, cum reliqua plane insipida sunt, et frustra solicitantur. Gratissimæ igitur mihi fuerunt Epistolæ tuæ
― 368 ―
benevolentiæ et amicitiæ plenæ, nec quantum ex illis solatii perceperim ex taciturnitate meâ sed ex voluptate quam profitior judicare debes: Ea enim infirmi corporis morbus est, hoc†a sentientis et grati animi testimonium.
(English)Etiamsi <servilium>†b ingeniorum humana venerantium exempla cumulatè satis mihi obtulit longa dies nec melior omnino mihi spes est de futuro donec placuerit Deo Optimo Maximo ex misericordiâ suâ secundo filii sui adventu restaurare ecclesiam, maxime tamen mihi placuit historia ista quam in novissimis tuis perscripsisti. Actus ille triennalis cum omni suo apparatu partim ridiculo partim superstitioso habet in se quod et stomachum et splenem moveat, certe cum omnibus suis circumstantiis ita graphice depictus conservari debet, etiam ubi commode fieri potest, typis mandari et in publicum prodire, ut quod privatim obtinet, oculis hominum obversetur et <pudefiant>†c qui sic sacris illudunt, Deique nomen sacrosanctum, placitis inventisque suis audacter præfigunt. Vitam tibi in utilitatem religionis longam validamque et in usum familiæ et amicorum tuorum animitus precor uti et omnia prospera tibi tuisque. Optimam tuam fæminam filiamque reliquosque amicos nostros meo
― 369 ―
nomine rogo officiocissime salutes. Hæc tota familia Te tuosque salutat. Vale Vir Amplissime et me ama
Tui amantissimum
J LOCKE†a
(Latin)
Oates 4 Aug. 04
Excellent Sir,
I am truly ashamed of so lengthy a silence, nor do I think it sufficiently excused by my infirm and altogether broken health, although some regard for you, mingling with it, also kept me a little from writing when I was slothful enough owing to the illness itself. For what does it avail to weary you, who are suited to more erudite discourses and to correspondence worthy of a learned and free spirit, with an invalid's complaints and with laborious words redolent of a writer gasping for breath? Still, it is a comfort to find by experience that your friendship accompanies an old friend, although useless through old age and illness, even to the grave. There is nothing more delightful, or that more refreshes a weak and languishing spirit, than the constant and lively goodwill of friends, the great refuge of human fragility, in which is found a great part of pleasure when the rest is [p. 367] wholly without savour and is sought in vain. Your letters, full of goodwill and friendship, have therefore been most welcome to me, and you ought to judge how much solace I have received from them not from my silence but from the pleasure that I avow; for that [silence] is the illness of an infirm body; this, the testimony of a feeling and grateful heart.
(Latin)Although length of time has brought me more than enough examples of slavish dispositions reverencing human things, and although I have no better hope for the future until it shall please God Almighty in His mercy to restore the Church by the second coming of His son, yet that story that you recounted in your last letter pleased me greatly. That triennial act with all its pomp, part laughable, part superstitious, has in itself something to arouse anger and indignation; it should certainly be preserved with all its circumstances thus painted to the life; it should also be printed and published where that can conveniently be done, so that what obtains in private may appear before men's eyes; and those be put to shame who mock sacred things and boldly prefix God's most holy name to their own decrees and inventions. I pray with all my heart that you may have a long life and good health for the service of religion and for the benefit of your family and friends, as also that all things may prosper with you and yours. Please give my kindest regards to your excellent wife and daughter and to the rest off[p. 368] our friends. This whole family sends greetings to you and yours. Good-bye, excellent Sir, and love me,
Your most affectionate
J LOCKE
Address: Mijn Heer Mÿn Heer Philip van Limborch t'Amsterdam
[Van Limborch's note:] Mr. John Locke died 28 October, English style, in our New Style 8 November 1704, a Saturday, at 3 o'clock in the afternoon.
3603. Anthony Collins to Locke, 7 August 1704 (3601, 3608)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 7, ff. 55-6. Answered by no. 3608.
Lincolns Inne Aug. 7th. 04.
Dear Sir
I have taken the opportunity by Mr Churchils Coach to send you your Hat, which, if you have any dislike to, you may return by him. in another packet you will find the Resurrection Sermon I promis'd you together with the Authors Recantation, and my Lord Clarendons defence of Dr Stillingfleet where in the middle of the 22th page begins the Story of its being preached.†1
I must beg your pardon if I have sollicited you too much to answer Dr Sherlocks digression etc.†2 I intended nothing by it but to give you my opinion of the necessity of having it answer'd. if you think otherwise I perfectly submit to your judgment, and hope that the meer sollicitation of friends will not put you upon the trouble of writing so much as the answering that will oblige you to do, unless you are convinc'd that a better end may be obtain'd
― 370 ―
than to gratify them in what they may be in the wrong in. as I have a true love and esteem for you, so I desire above all things that you may enjoy yourself in the best manner that can be. could you have but the health and happiness that I desire you may enjoy, it would give me as much happiness as I desire for my self who am
Dear Sir
Your most Humble and most Obedient Servant
A. COLLINS
I just now received a Letter from Dr. Tyndal at Oxford who gives you his service. Mr Proast presented him with his late pamphlet, and the Doctor thank'd him for writing so seasonably, since it show'd so plainly what is aim'd at by the Clergy
Address: For John Locke Esq at Oats in Essex
Endorsed by Locke: A Collins 7 Aug 04 Answered 11
3604. Francis Limborch to Locke, 8 August 1704 (3598, 3643)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 13, ff. 244-5.
London 8 Agust 1704
Honoured Sir
I received yesterday your most acceptable favour off the 4 the 2 inclosed for Holland j forwarded this night and the other for Dr South also;†1 I am surprised to see by this that you had written before to me, which letter never came to my hands and made afrayde your self was jndisposed; that night I wrote you the Child after 12 begon to rest and next day was a little better and so continued slowly a mending and now his teeth trouble him mightily with a violent dry Caugh, so have been last Saturday and Monday looking for some lodgeing for him in the Contry and hope I shall have some either att Wanstead or Woodford upon which I expect an answhere to morrow, he is very weake and Contains nothing off what he eats, and j fear he ll be hardly able to bear to be removed to the Country, and is mightily decayed;†2 I hope and heartily wish you are pretty well at present and hope shortly to have the honour to see you, in
― 371 ―
the mean while I desire My Lady Sir Francis Mrs Masham and Coste may finde our most sincere respects and that you'll be so kind as to accept the same from
Honoured Sir
Your Most oblidged Humble servant
FRAN LIMBORCH
Address: For John Locke Esquire [at Oates, by Joslyn, Bishops Stortford]
Postmark: AV 8 (?)
Endorsed by Locke: F: Limborch 8 Aug. 04
3605. William Grigg to Locke, 8 August [1704] (3484)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 10, ff. 190-1. Year from Locke's endorsement. The day in his endorsement is probably a slip.
Jes: Coll: Aug: 8th
Sir
Having subscrib'd for two Copy's of the Geoponic's,†1 and one of 'em being enough in all reason for my little study, I know not to whom I can offer the Other as a mark of my respect so justly as to your self. I doubt not but you percieve my design in this, is to endeavour to continue in the favourable Opinion you may have of me. Every one would be ambitious of this Advantage, but the many Obligations I have to you do not only warrant, but seem to claim an acknowledgement of them upon Every Opportunity, at least, I doubt not but you will excuse the repeated assurances, I herewith send you of my being with all due Gratitude
Your most Obedient servant
W: GRIGG
Suidas I am told will be finishd a little after Michaelmas†a next.†2
Address: To the Honoured Mr Locke at Oates near Bishop Stortford in Essex with a parcell carriage†b paid†b
Endorsed by Locke: W: Grigg 9 Aug. 04

3606. Dr. Robert Pitt to Locke, 9 August 1704 (3586)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 17, ff. 195-6. The day in Locke's endorsement is probably a slip.
Sir
I have sent you Pills, which, I hope, will answer your Design. I presume four of them will be a sufficient Dose, if you observ the Operation too feeble, you may add to the Number, tho probably four will be sufficient for you, designing to evacuate the Humours by the more frequent use of them. I am very glad you make water more freely, and of a good Colour. I wish you the best success, and am
Your most humble servant
R PITT.
Aug.9.†a 1704.
Address: For Mr John Locke

3607. James Tyrrell to Locke, 10 August [1704] (3575)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 22, ff. 171-2. Damaged. Year from contents.
Shot: Aug: 10
Dear Sir
In complieance with your desire expressed in your last, I went on saturday to the publick Library, and looked again upon the Register, and find I am not at all mistaken when I told you in my last that All the bookes you had given were there particularly set down and I shall now (since my hast or inadvertency made me omit it before) send you the words of Title to them, as follows.
Joannes Lock Generosus et hujus Academiæ olim Alumnus, præter Opera ab ipso Edita, ob ingenii Elegantiam, Doctrinæ varietatem, et Philosophicam Subtilitatem, omnibus suspicienda viz:
1 Essay of humane etc. then after the names of the first. it is thus before the second parcel.
Insuper ex suo in optimas Art<e>s†b Amore, animoque ad supellectilem litterariam augendam propenso, Bibliothecæ huic dono dedit
― 373 ―
Libros sequentes. 1 A curious Collection of Travells etc, which being a very handsom and gratefull acknowledgment of your Goodwill to promote knowledg. I thanked Dr: Hudson on your behalf, and if you shall think fit to give any more in your lifetime, it may not be amiss if you wrote him a few lines expressing your sense of his great Respect for you.
I thank you for your Kind thoughts of my Performance as well to what is publishd as what remains behind,†1 and I shall make it my endeavour to answer your Character of it; and I wish God may afford you life and health to peruse whatever I shall write on this subject, since the approbation of so critical a Judgment would not fail to be a great advantage to the Work, as well as credite to the Authour.
As to what you desire concerning Dr: Mills Greek Testament, I am sorry I cannot <at>†a present obey your Commands, He being now gone to Canterbury to take possession of the Præbend, the Queen has given him upon the promotion of Dr: Beveridge to a Bishoprick: but when He returns I will propose your desire to him concerning the Copy; and will d<oe>†a my utmost endeavour to obtain his complyance with it: tho I cannot promise you that I shall succeed therein.†2
As for news I have none, onely that there is now in Oxon a certain yong man of yellowish hair and fair complexion who calls himself Ge: Psalmaneasar; and says he is by birth of the Isle of Formosa, and as such has lately publishd an Account of that Island, which I have read and find so plain marks in it of an Impostor, that I wonder the Bishop of London and some other Divines who contribute to his maintenance as a Convert to the Christian Faith could be so impos'd upon: I wish if you have read the book you would give me your thoughts of it in your next and if you have not, it may be worth your while to look into it, all I can tell you more of him is, that he eats raw flesh: and especially snakes.†3 no more but am
your affectiona<te and>†a humble servant
J TYRRELL
― 374 ―
Looking a few days since in Ant: Wood's Athenæ Oxon: vol: I<I>†a p. 91. after an Account of the life and writings of Henry Jacob a learned man once of Merton Col: follows a story of his appearing not long after his death to Dr: Jacob of Cant: who told it to Drs Du Moulin, and Causabon; and If am not mistaken you once at Okely told me the like thing, which you had from Dr: Jacobs own mouth, when you once met him at Sir Jo: Banks's in Kent.†1 pray if it were so let me know it in your next. and pray give my humble service to Sir Fran: his good Lady and all the rest, as if nam'd.
Address: For John Locke Esqr [at Oates, by Joslyn, Bishops Stortford]
Postmark: AV 11
3608. Locke to Anthony Collins, 11 August 1704 (3603, 3613)
A. Donnadieu collection sale-catalogue: Puttick and Simpson, 29 July 1851, lot no. 557. Transcribed from lithographic facsimile in sale-catalogue. The address forms part of the lithograph, but is not a facsimile. Printed with omissions in A Collection of Several Pieces, pp. 321-2. Answers nos. 3600 and 3603.
Oates 11 Aug 04
Dear Sir
Kind and goodnaturd freinds doe like you bestow their favours and thank those that receive them. I was never more obleiged nor better enterteined than by your company here, and you heap upon me your acknowledgments as if I had made a journey to London for your sake and there done you I know not how many courtesies. This however has the effect you could wish upon me. I beleive all that you would have me. And since one naturaly loves as well those that one has done good to as those whom one has received good from, I leave it to you to manage the account as you please, soe the affection and good will between us does but increase. whose hand lays most fuel on the fire that warms us both I shall not be nicely solicitous, since I am sure you cannot impute to me more than I really wish, but at the same time know that wishing in me is all for I can doe just noe thing
I thank you for the care you took of my letters which I sent to
― 375 ―
town by you, and for the things you sent me by Mr Churchill. The Hat fits extremly well and is just such as I would have had and the books are raritys. Make noe Apologie to me I beseech you for what you said to me about the Digression. Tis noe more but what I find other people agree with you in and it would afford as much diversion as any hunting you could imagin, had I strength and breath enough to pursue the chase. But of this we may perhaps have better oportunity to talke when I see you next: For this I tell you before hand I must not have you be under any restraint to speake to me what ever you think fit for me to doe whether I am of the same mind or noe. The use of a freind is to perswade us to the right not to suppose always that we are in it: To facilitate your journey hither when ever your affairs allow you to doe me that kindness, I must tell you that I am now very well fited with a horse for my chaise which upon the least notice from you shall be sent to meet you at Epping or where you shall appoint. I am
Dear Sir
your most humble and most obedient servant
J LOCKE
Sir Francis My Lady and all this family are perfectly your servants
[Addressed to Anthony Collins Esquire, Serle Court, Lincolns Inn.]

3612. Elizabeth Clarke to Locke, 14 August 1704 (3507)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 6, ff. 173-4. Printed in Rand, pp. 601-2.
Chipley August the 14th: 1704
Dear Sir
My thankes for the Noble present you Lately made me had waited on you Sooner, had I followed my owne inclinations; but I was unhappily prevented till this time.†1 however be assured Sir I retain the sense of your favours with the utmost gratitude, and must and will for ever acknowledg my obligations to you, perticularly this Last, which I can never hope to Merit.
I am pleased to hear you Continue tolerably well and I heartily wish I could give you a good account of My Fathers health, but I am willing to hope he is Somthing better than when he came first down, and that in time his Cure will bee perfected.
I'll detain you from your better employments no Longer than whilst I assure you that My Father and Mother together with the rest of this family are your most humble Servants, and that I am with all fervency imaginable
Sir
Your most obedient humble servant
ELIZ CLARKE
all this family are servants to that at Oates.
Address: For John Locke Esqr [at Oates, by Joslyn, Bishops Stortford]
Postmark: AV 21
Endorsed by Locke: El: Clarke 14 Aug 04

3613. Locke to Anthony Collins, 16 August 1704 (3608, 3617)
Stanford University Libraries, Stanford, California: Department of Special Collections: Manuscript Division. Transcribed from Xerox print. Incomplete copy, made by or for Desmaizeaux, in B.M., Add. MS. 4290, ff. 49v-50. Part printed presumably from that text, or a copy of it, in A Collection of Several Pieces, pp. 323-4.
Oates 16 Aug. 04
Dear Sir
Which way soever I turn my self I meet on all sides your freindship in all manner of shapes and upon all sorts of occasions besetting me. Were I as averse as I am pleased with my happyness in†a your kindness I must however yeild to so powerfull and constant attaques. But it is past that time of day. I have long since surrenderd my self to you And I am as certainly in your Coach as Count Tallard in the Duke of Marlebroughs†1 to be disposed of as you please, onely with this difference, that he was a prisoner of warr against his will: I am your captive by the soft but stronger force of your irresistible obligations, and with the consent and joy of my owne mind. Judg then whether I am willing my shadow should be in the possession of one with whom my heart is and to whom all that I am, had I any thing besides my heart worth the presenting, does belong. Sir Godfrey I doubt not will make it very like.†2 If it were possible for his pencil to make it a speaking picture, it should tell you every day how much I love and esteem you. And how pleased I am to be so much as in effigie near a person with whom I should be glad to spend an Age to Come.
Sir Francis and my Lady bid me assure you that not onely Sir Godfrey Kneller upon this but any body at any time will be very welcome to Oates upon your occasion, and they shall always take it for a kindness when ever you will afford them an oportunity to express the esteem they have for you by shewing their respects to any one that any way relates to you. They hope you intend to come down with Sir Godfrey to compleat the favour. This I presume you intend and thereupon take the liberty to beg a favour of you. You know how hard it is at this distance from London for a Gentlemans
― 379 ―
table to be furnishd with any thing but what his owne yard or the next Butcher can afford. Permit me therefore to desire you to get some partredges rabbets or what other good things of that kind the polterers shops afford, as also Lobsters, Craw fish, Crabs or Prawns. These things packd up in a basket or little hamper may be fastend before or behind the coach. This I would beg may not be omitted, For I would entertain Sir Godfry here as well as I could both out of respect, and to keep him in good humor that he may draw the better. Though you come down with Sir Godfrey in his coach or in any other coach tomorrow you may goe back at your leisure when you will, for my Calesh is fitted with a most excellent horse which may carry you whether you please. Pray get Sir Godfrey to put up colours enough to draw more pictures than one, for I suppose the drawing of two whilst he is here will be little more trouble than one, and that he will consent to draw my Ladys whom I have prevailed on to sit, if he will doe me that favour. Pray give him my service. I am
Dear Sir
Your most humble and most obedient servant
JOHN LOCKE
Sir Francis and My Lady give you their service and doe again and again desire you to come down with Sir Godfrey
3614. Peter King, later first Baron King, to Locke, 16 August 1704 (3596, 3627)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 12, ff. 269-70.
Exeter August. 16. 1704.
Sir
Being last week at Buckland at Sir Francis Drakes†1 I did from thence trouble you with a letter, as also inclos'd the other that I left for you at London when I†a came out of Town—I can add no more about mr Cost than what I writ in my last, saving that if mr Coste hath any inclination to undertake any such Imploy, I know no family where he will be more at his ease†2 —I shall be next week at
― 380 ―
Wells, and Saturday sevennight at Bristol†1 and the Tuesday or Wensday after at Colonel Colchesters,†2 and as fast as I can after that for London and Oates, that I may have a little time to wait upon you—You forgot in your last letter one very principal thing, which was to let me know how you do, I hope I shall hear by your next letter that you are pretty well Consideratis Considerandis, To Expect a perfect recovery is what I cannot expect, but God Almighty may make your condition more easy and less painfull, and so you may be continued longer with us, which I earnestly desire and especially for the great advantage that the Christian Religion may receive by the perfecting of the good work you are now about†3 —
I am
Sir
Your Most Affectionate Cosin and Obliged Servant.
P. KING.
My father presents you his humble service.
Address: For John Locke Esquire at Oates.
Endorsed by Locke: P: King 16 Aug. 04
3615. Awnsham Churchill to Locke, 17 August 1704 (3595, 3637)

London Aug. 17. 1704.
Sir
This afternoon just as mr Collins was taking Coach, the Printer brought me a foul proof ill wrought of the Galations.†4 I made it up with the MMS. and sent it you by him. I had not time to consider it, but I perceived the Compositor had putt the figures of the verses <on>†a the wrong side of the page, which he shall mend—When you have considered the whole Composition, and whether the page and paper be not too large I begg youl give me your Directions—I thought the first page was begun to neer the Top.
I had a letter from mr Bold. he gives his serv to you, and the

family. he gott cold goeing home,†1 and has had a feavour, but writes that hes better

3616. Rabsy Smithsby to Locke, 17 August 1704 (3610, 3630)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 18, ff. 146-7.
17 August 04
Sir
I humbly thank you for the Favouer of yours, and am not a little pleased with the good acount you give me of your gooing abroad, and the benefitt you receive by <so>†a doeing.
I am very glad the Chocolate and Caps which I sent to you by Mr King, Came safe to you, and that the Caps fitt and please you.
I hope the Flanell Shirts and Chocolate which I sent to you by Mr Collins, are safe with you before this time. I sent them to him as you directed by John Gray,†2 who allso brought to me the next day, a quarter of an hundred of Cacoa nuts, which I gave you an account of the 20th of June, in a Letter which I find Came not to your hand. I then acquainted you there was thirty three yards of the Flanell you sent to me for shirts, and that there remained eight yards with me. As allso that the 15th of may Last, I received five pounds on a five pound Tickett of 1704. I will take Care to make up your Chocolate as soon as the weather is a little Coole for I observe it grinds best and finest then, and will marke it as you desire. If att your Leasure you spare me a word or two, you know it will oblige
Sir
your Faithfull Friend and humble servant
RABSY SMITHSBY
Address: For Mr Locke [at Oates, by Joslyn, Bishops Stortford]

3617. Anthony Collins to Locke, 19 August 1704 (3613, 3619)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 7, ff. 57-8. Answered by no. 3619.
Lincolns Inne 19th of Aug 04
Dear Sir
Upon my return to Town I found a letter from Mr Tyrrell who desires me in my next to you to inquire whether you have receiv'd his last letter in answer to yours.†1 He puts nothing more into his Question, and so you may answer this as you can. I am infinitely oblig'd to you for all your favours and particularly the opportunity you have given me of being almost always near you in Effigie, and being put in mind of a Friendship that I shall reflect on as long as I live with the greatest satisfaction. It is a happiness that I shall subsist on, and spread through the several parts of my life to ballance the Burden of it: and since happiness consists mostly in a comparison with the Condition of others I shall at least prefer my own to that of those who were never acquainted with you Who am Dear Sir
Your most Humble and most Obedient servant
A. COLLINS
my most humble service to Sir F my Lady and the Rest of the family to whom I am infinitely oblig'd
Address: For John Locke Esq [at Oates, by Joslyn, Bishops Stortford]
Postmarks: AV 19; GC
Endorsed by Locke: A Collins 19 Aug 04 Answered 21

3619. Locke to Anthony Collins, 21 August 1704 (3617, 3624)
Maggs Brothers, catalogue no. 445, item no. 2651. From Oates. Answers no. 3617.
... This affair being happily dispatched I come to something of a little more difficulty and that is whether you or I am to return thanks for what passed lately at Oates. Not that I think there is any difficulty in the case of it self, that plainly makes the obligation to be received by me. But you are a person not otherwise to be dealt
― 384 ―
with, you must be permitted to doe the kindnesse, and give the thanks to.
If the shadow you have got could convey the substance to you, you should never want near you one that highly esteems you, perfectly loves you and heartily wishes you well....
Locke to Anthony Collins, 23 August 1704
This letter was to be delivered after Locke's death and is placed therefore after 28 October: no. 3648.
3620. John Freke to Locke, 24 August 1704 (2686)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 8, ff. 239-40. No. 2686 is the last letter written by Freke and Clarke in conjunction. Freke's most recent individual letter is no. 1032. The College is mentioned last in no. 2861.
24 August 1704
Sir
Accept my thanks for your obliging Letter of the 21st and doe me the Justice to believe that if I thought I could render you any service by it I should make noe difficulty of waiting on you at Oats and therefore you may Command me to attend you there when ever you please. But I confess tis not easie to make me goe much out of my way in a meer visit
As to the Enquiry's you make 1 Whether I will accept the Trust you mention I must beg you to doe me the like Justice in believing That I will not refuse any office of friendship that you can desire of me whether it be to be performd in your life time or after your decease and that I will Execute any power you Comitt to me as near as I can according to your intention or at least what I apprehend to be your intention. (2) whether the Charity and methode you propose be not the best I answer That for the End tis certain none can be better nor doe I know any soe good unless providing an Hospitall for ancient and disabled Seamen worn out in merchants service may not be reckond so. And for the methode I have but one objection to it and that is that your Trustee may dy soon after you before the Trust is executed and therefore it might not be amiss to make more than one Trustee as is usually done in all Trust for the same reason. But Pray Sir pardon me that I suggest
― 385 ―
to you that the same end is designd by the Corporation and workehouse at Bristoll which is at presant and like to Continue in the management of persons of worth who are prudent and dilligent as to name a Few Mr Yates mr Wade mr Edwards Sir Tho Day†a etc:†1 3 In what form the clause by which you should give such a Legacy ought to be drawn I Answer In the plainest and most expressive words of your meaning for tho Forms are requisite in Deeds nothing is regarded in Wills but the Intent of the Donor And therefore a bequest in the very words of your Letter viz I give to A B the sume of to be paid him by my Executor in five equall yearly payments that is to say twenty pounds a year for five years successively next after my decease In Trust that the said A B will employ and dispose of the same in such parts and proportions and to and for the use of such person and persons as he shall think fit and which he in his Judgement shall think to be a work of charity or beneficiall to the publick or for the advancement of such good end or work as he shall think I would have contributed if I were alive
These or any other words plainly expressing your mind will be sufficient†2
I must now tell you I have not heard from mr Clarke since he left London though I writt to him before I went to Tunbridge and severall times since I returnd thence which makes me think he continues indisposed
Lord Shaftesbury Landed at Aldborough in Suffolk Tewsday last after having been long at sea and Escaped a dangerous Storm†3 he is expected in Town this night or tomorrow
We have noe News the Dutch mail not being arrived.
Tender to all at Oates the humble service of
Your obliged friend and humble servant
JOHN FREKE
Address: For John Locke Esqr [at Oates, by Joslyn, Bishops Stortford]
Postmarks: AV 24; RS
Endorsed by Locke: J. Freke 24 Aug. 04 Answered 28
― 386 ―

Sir

3622. Dr. Pieter Guenellon to Locke, 29 August/9 September 1704 (3554)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 11, ff. 122-3.
d'Amst. ce 9 Sept. 1704
Monsieur,
Le triste recit que vous nous faitte de votre état, nous a extremement affligé. il faut que je vous dise que Monsieur Huyberts,†2 que vous connoissez peutétre de reputation, etant un homme de lettre et grand Mathematicien, demeurant a Utreght, vieux garcon, environ de votre age, attaqué de la mesme maladie, et qu'on croyoit mourant ce printems, s'est remis de la maniere la plus surprenante du monde. il avoit les jambes fort enflées, son athme ne luy permettoit pas de remüer de place: il a eté fort languissant depuis longtems.
― 387 ―
il se mit dans l'esprit quil prenoit trop de nouriture a proportion de son etat, et dans cette idée, il se mit a jeuner et prendre si peu de nouriture, qu'on croyoit impossible quil put survivre long tems. cepandant il est arrivé tout le contraire. ses jambes desenflerent depuis peu a peu, et son Athme a tellement diminué, quil est sur pied et quil marche. je vous fais ce recit non pas pour vous exciter a faire de mesme: je scay que vous este fort sobre, et que vous vivez d'un grand regime; mais c'est pour vous faire voir, quil ne faut pas tout a fait desesperer de votre restablissement. vous scavez celuy de ma femme, qui a etée comme a l'agonie d'une maladie comme desesperée. Dieu suscitera quelque moyen pour votre soulagement. c'est ce que nous souhaittons ardemment. il est vray que vous avez beaucoup d'age: qu'a la fin la Nature succombe par elle mesme, que cette vie a ses bornes, au dela desquelles on ne scauroit aller. quelques annees plus ou moins ne flatte guere un esprit comme le votre, acoustumé a bien penser, et disposé de longue main, a se resigner a tout ce que Dieu voudra; mais pourtant souffré que vos amis souhaittent que vous surviviez encore a l'etat affligant ou vous este. un homme comme vous, si utile au public, orné de tant de grandes et rares vertus, et qui a penetré si profondement dans toutes les sciences humaines, devroit avoir une double mesure de vié, et cette immortalité que vótre reputation a aquise, devroit contribuer a vous faire vivre plus longtems. quelle preadvertence que vous nous vouliez donner de votre mort prochaine, pour nous armer contre un si grand malheur, la nouvelle nous en sera tousjours tres triste, et fort accablante, et nous serons assurement inconsolables de vous perdre, quand il nous restera que le souvenir de votre chere amitié. plaise a Dieu que vous viviez encore longtems! et d'une vie souffrable! Monsieur veen a beaucoup decliné depuis sis mois, il est aussi athmatique, il a peine a marcher, et pert courage. je crain fort cet hyver pour luy. le reste de la famille se porte assez bien, et vous fait ses complimens. Ma femme vous presente ses treshumbles respects. elle continue de vivre par artifice, n'osant tout a fait quitter ses remedes, sans lesquels †a elle est sans fievre, sort et agit par la maison, pourtant sa santé est imparfaitte et foible. je croy que sa grande sobrieté contribue beaucoup a la soutenir, et j'espere que la votre contribuera a vous remettre. un bon air, peu et bons alimens, et une grande tranquilité d'esprit, sont les trois grans soutiens de notre vie, et†b- c'est ce qui vous manque pas.-†b Dieu
― 388 ―
veuille benir les remedes dont vous vous servez! et donner un prompt amendement a vos maux. je suis avec le dernier respect,
Votre tres humble et tres obeissant serviteur
P. GUENELLON.
Address: For Mr John Locke at Mr Powlings over against the plough in Little Lincolns inne feilds London
Postmark: SE 1
Endorsed by Locke: Dr. Guenellon 9 Sept. 04
September
3623. Anthony Ashley Cooper, third earl of Shaftesbury, to Locke, 7 September [1704] (3139)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 7, ff. 124-5. Printed in Rand, Shaftesbury, p. 323. Year from Locke's endorsement.
Chesley†1 Sept: 7th:
If I had been in a Condition to have writt a line, I shou'd not have sent you Mr Furly's†2 without something from my self. and were I now so well as to undertake a Journey, I shoud be thinking of seeing you in the first place before any other of my Friends; especially hearing (as I do with great Trouble) that you are of late wors than you us'd to be.
My own Distemper (which was an ill Feavour gott at Sea after having been 3 weeks a ship-board) is, I hope, quite off, tho' I am yet very weak.
When perfectly recover'd (if it so please God) my first thoughts will be of a Journey to you.
Mean while pray beleive me (as I hope you ever did and will)
Your Faithfull Friend and Humble Servant
SHAFTESBURY.
Pray present my Humble service to Sir Francis and my Lady.
Being oblig'd now ere long to sett about a Work long delayd,†a a Monument for my Grandfather as enjoynd me by my Grandmother, I shou'd be extreamly glad that you wou'd so far remember
― 389 ―
my Granfather as to lett me have some lines from you for an Inscription, in Latin and English.†1
Address: For Mr Lock at Sir Francis Masham's in Oates near Bishops stafford Essex
Postmark: SE 7
Endorsed by Locke: E: Shaftesbury 7 Sept 04
3624. Locke to Anthony Collins, 11 September 1704 (3619, 3632)
The Historical Society of Pennsylvania, Philadelphia. Transcribed from photostat. Printed in part in A Collection of Several Pieces, pp. 324-6. Collins's answer is lost: see p. 399.
11 Sept 04
Dear Sir
He that has any thing to doe with you must own that freindship is the natural product of your constitution and your soule a noble soyle is inriched with the two most valuable qualitys of humane nature Truth and Freindship. What a treasure have I then in such a freind, with whom I can converse and be enlightend about the highest speculations, and yet be assisted by in the most trivial occasions. When one hears you upon the principles of knowledg or the foundations of government one would hardly imagin your thoughts ever descended to a brush or a curry comb or other such trumpery of life, and yet if one employ you to get but a pair of shoebuckles you are as ready and as dexterous at it as if the whole business of your life had been with nothing but shoe buckles
As to my Ladys picture†2 pray in the first place see it and tell me how you like it. In the next place pray get Sir Godfry to write upon it with paint on the back side Lady Masham 1704 and on the backside of mine John Locke 1704. This he did on Mr Molineux's†3 and mine the last he drew,†4 and this is necessary to be done or else the pictures of private persons are lost in two or three generations
― 390 ―
and so the picture looses of its value it being not <known>†a whom it was made to represent.
As to the frame I think Sir Godfrys people got me the fram for Mr Molineux's picture. This is certain they can give you directions about it and I would have such a handsome frame as such pictures are now put in. When it is in a frame and fit to be hung up I desire you to place it either in your own or my Cosin Kings chamber to hang there till its remove out of town.†1 which of these two chambers you will choose for its present abode is left wholy to you. When you have paid for this picture and for my Chaise I suppose you will allow me to be then in your debt. How much will remain to Balance the account when you are at leisure to let me know I shall think it time to pay it and remain
Dear Sir
Your most humble and obleiged servant
J LOCKE
Sir Francis and my Lady and the rest of this family give you their service. My Cosin King visited me Saturday in the Evening†2 and returns this morning by whom I send this
Address: For Anthony Collins Esq at his Chamber in Serle Court in Lincolns Inne London
3625. John Bonville to Locke, 14 September 1704 (3609, 3628)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 4, f. 144.
London September 14th 1704
Honoured Cousin
Sir I Am heartely Glad with This Opertunitie To Aquaint you That Sir George Rook have Obtained A victory Over the french at sea, The perticulers Is Not yet Know, but we have Now Great Rejoycing This Night, Just Now the Gunns at the Tower was discharged, It Is Now between: 9 and 10 Aclock,†3 And Now My Deare Cousin, I must Return you my Hearty Thanks for your
― 391 ―
Signall faviours Towards Me. The same Munday Night As soone as I got home†1 I carried your Things and Gave them to Mr Churchells own hand, be pleased To Give my Servis to Sir Frances and My Lady, I desier To Give them my Thanks for The Kind Entertainement, what busines I had to doe for them I disspatched The Next day, My wife son and Daughter Give you Their Humble Servis, with My Self, I shall be heartely Glad to heare of your Health, My Cousin King came to my Doore with his Cousin†2 in his coach, but In soe much hast that I could Not under by him how you were when he Left you
I shall Ever Remaine
My Deare Cousin your Faithfful Kinsman And Humble Servant

3626. Arent Furly to Locke, 15/26 September 1704 (3503)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 9, ff. 7-8.
Rotterdam 26 September 1704
Sir
I give myself the honour to acquaint you with these lines, that According to your desire I have waited on Mr Bayle to give him the two Epistolæ de Tolerantia, which you sent me, with your humble thanks and Service; I told him that since you had met with some copys of them at a Booksellers in London you desired him to return the one to his Friend and to accept of the other as an evidence that you should not have refused his and his Friends kindness if you had not been Supplyed before it reached your hands; he received it upon that condition and desired me to give you his humble thanks and to assure you of the Great esteem he has for anything that comes from you; for the former he would needs return me what I had paid him for it.†3 If I am capable of doing you any more Service
― 392 ―
either in buying of Books in Auction, which occur here dayly, or in any thing elce, I shall esteem it as a particular favour to be honoured with your commands.
Sir The respect and deference which I ow to your person, doe not permitt me to trouble you with many letters; I am glad if I can lay hold of any such opportunity as the present to present my humble duty to you and to assure you how much I am always concerned for Your health; I was very much afflicted to hear that the Swelling of your legs has been so troublesome to you of late; I am afraid it has kept you from Your Summer exercise of riding, which has always been so refreshing to you: On the other side I am not altogether without hopes, that the great successes which we have had this summer by Land and Sea, God be praised, may have revived your Spirits and proved a Cordial to your old Age, Sure I am that it has affected the publick spirited Company about you; which will at last have some influence upon you; Sir excuse my Zeal for the Publick and pardon me if I have trespassed upon your patience: I shall onely add the fervent wishes and hearty prayers for your health and preservation of him who is with all due respect and an humble reverence
Sir
Your devoted and Most Obliged humble Servant
ARENT FURLY
P:S:
My Father, Mother and brothers tender their humble Service to you and to all the Good Company At Oates; to which I add My humble Respects.
Address: For John Locke Esqr at Oates
Endorsed by Locke: Ar: Furly 2 Sept. 04
3627. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 16 September 1704 (3614, 3631)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 402-3. Part printed in Campbell, Lives of the Lord Chancellors, iv. 579-80.
Oates 16 Sept 04
Dear Cosin
I am just rose from diner where the Bride and Bridegrooms health was heartily drank again and again with wishes that this day may
― 393 ―
be the begining of a very happy life to them both.†1 We hope we have hit the time right, if not it is your fault who have misled us
I desire you to bring me down twenty guineas. The wooden Standish at Mr Pawlings and the Turkie travels of your Exeter man†2 I know you will not forget. But there are other things of more importance on this occasion which you must not forget nor omit viz†a
4 Dried neats tongues
12 Partredges that are fresh and will bear the cariage and keep a day after they are here, for tis to noe purpose to bring down stinking things of good name that will stink and must be thrown away after all the trouble one has been about them, as I was lately served. Therefor if you cannot light upon partredges that are very fresh bring but 6
4 Pheasants the same I said of the partredges I say of the pheasants the full number if fresh else onely half
4 Turkey poults ready larded if they be not out of season
4 Fresh Aburn†3 Rabets if they are to be got
Plovers or woodcocks or snipes or whatever else is good to be got at the poulterers except ordinary tame foule
12 Chichester Male Lobsters if they can be got alive if not 6 dead ones that are sweet
2 Large Crabs that are fresh
Crawfish and prawns if they are to be got A double Barrell of the best right Colchester Oysters if they are fresh come in
Not knowing whether you have any body better to get these things for you I have by this post writ to Mr Gray†4 to find you out as soon as he receives my letter and offer you his service to get such things as you shall direct him. He was bread up in my old Lord Shaftesburys Kitchin and was my Lady Dowagers Cooke and Caterer. I got him to be messenger to the Council of Trade and
― 394 ―
Plantations and have often imploid him when I have had occasion in matters of this nature wherein I have found him very diligent and usefull to me and therefor I send him to you at this pinch to be made use of in geting the things I write for if you have nobody that you can better depend upon. If you imploy John Gray you must furnish him with money to goe to market
I desire you also to lay out between twenty and thirty shillings in dried sweet meats of several kinds such as some woman skilfull in those maters shall choose as fit and fashionable (excepting Orange and Lemon pils†1 candied of which we are provided). Let them be good of the kind and doe not be spareing in the cost but rather exceed thirty shillings. These drie sweet meats must each sort be put up in a paper by it self and then all the papers put up in a box together, I mention this because for want of this care some that my Lady lately sent for were quite spoiled.
These things you must take care to bring down with you, that I may in this short warning have something to entertain your freind and may not be out of Countenance while they are here. If there be anything that you can find your wife loves be sure that there be provision made of that and plentifully whether I have mentioned it or no
Pray†a- let there be a pound of Pistachios, and some China Oranges if there be any fresh come in-†a
Mr Seys's intention of a visit to me I take as I ought for a very great honour. If he comes you may assure him he will be extreamly welcome to Sir Francis and my Lady but must be content to receive from me an Old Batchelor a plain country enterteinment. In particular you must prepare him, if he comes, for a poor lodging for you know how much this house is in want of rooms to receive and lodg persons of condition. Pray give your wife and him my most humble service
If you know noebody that you prefer I have thought of Mrs Churchil†2 to buy the sweet meats for you, for that is a woman's business. If Mr Churchil hath anything for me, pray bring it with you. I heartily wish you joy and am Dear Cosin
your most affectionate Cosin and humble servant
J LOCKE
― 395 ―
One thing more let me mind you of. All these things that I send for should I think be put in a little hamper and packd with care and the hamper put up before the coach if possible, for if it be put behind and there be no servant that rides behind or followes the coach on horse back you will be in danger to have it cutt of (as some of our neighbours have been lately served) unless it be extremly fast tied on and somebody in the coach constantly look out after it espetially rideing through the town or any of the towns by the way
Address: For Peter King Esqr member of Parliament. To be left at Mr Potmans in the Middle Temple London Frank
Postmark: SE 18
Endorsed by King: Mr Locke September 16. 1704
3628. John Bonville to Locke, 16 September 1704 (3625)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 4, f. 145.
London September 16: 1704
Honoured Cousin
Sir yours of the 13 Instant I Received, Emediatly I went to Mr Locke I gave to himself the Inclosed. he perused it Over, soe we both went Into his valt. he Tould me About 3 weeks henc, he shall have wine for your purpos, but sum wine he had of which he desired me to give my Opinnion off, whether It would pleas you, which I hope It will, of which I have This day sent by Lenham 24 Quart Bottles all sealed, I Am sur well packt, Deare Cousin I have don the best I could, I hope It will pleas you, It Is Not to strong, It Is A sort of white, but they have A cramp Name for It, The Nues of the fight at sea, This day It was publisht, we find that severall of our men of war had Noe Amonition Left at Last, how Ever the French shered off In the Night, but It was A very hot Ingagment, but yet we have Not the particulers.†1 God be thanked It Is Noe wors, My wife Gives you Her Humble Servis, The Lord be with you,
I Am My Deare Cousin
Your faithfull Kinsman And Humble Servant
JNO: BONVILLE

3629. Samuel Locke to Locke, 16 September 1704 (3476)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 14, ff. 227-8.
London 16 September 1704.
Sir
I am sorry I cannot (this weeke) sende you som of the verry best Clarret which by your acceptable letter of 13th Instant, you desyre; Burgundy wine wee have none (I dare say) in the kingdom that is good: in 14 days tyme I shall have som of the verry best old Clarret fyne enough to be botled off: of which shall Reserve for you what you please. I am prowd if any wine I have doth please you;
mr Bonville our verry good friend thinks that in the mean tyme some of the white may serve the occation. I wish it may: I can only say tis true and neat wine:
Sir my selfe and especialy my sons are extreamly obleedgd to you for the favour of your notice and care of our wellfare; I wish it were in our Power to Contribute any thing to your satisfaction. be pleas'd to accept of my Kind Respects to your selfe, Sir Francis and his family. I am
Sir
Your most humble servant and affectionate Cosin
SAM: LOCKE

3630. Rabsy Smithsby to Locke, 16 September 1704 (3616, 3634)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 18, f. 148.
16 Sept 04
Sir
Tho your Chocolate and quilted Caps, I mentioned in my Last, Came not to your hands so soone as I desiered, as I find by Mr Grays Inquirey, Mr King and his servant being out of towne when I sent the Chocolate as you directed, However I tooke Care thay might be deliverd to him as soone as he Came home, so that I hope, they may be with you, before this waits on you with my most humble servise, from
Sir
your most affect Friend
RABSY SMITHSBY.

3631. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 17 September 1704 (3627, 3638)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 404-5. Part printed in Campbell, Lives of the Lord Chancellors, iv. 581.
Oates 17 Sept 04
Dear Cosin
Though I writ to you yesterday yet understanding by yours of 16th that the business is compleated, at which I rejoyce, I cannot forbear to write to you again today to wish you and my Cosin your wife joy, to whom pray give my hearty service. I expected noe more in your letter than you writ. It was enough for a man on his wedding day. And therefore I hope, though you say nothing of it, that you have prepared my present of a toilete furniture for my Cosin your wife and will give it her from me before you come out of town else I shall complain to her of you when I see her.
Your Stay in town till Wednesday†1 giveing you the convenience of an other day, there will be†a time and oportunity to make provision of those things that are fresh and good which I have writ to
― 398 ―
you to bring down with you. For I would have plenty of things to be used not to be spoiled. If you make use of John Gray If he comes not to you Monday night himself, you may send to him at the Goat in Charles court near Hungerford market in the Strand†1 to come to you Tuesday morning and then you may take your measures together how to be furnished with what is best and Freshest in the town for our purpose. Though I have mentioned particulars in m<y>†a letter to you, yet I doe not tie you up precisely to those parti<culars>†a because the market must govern the buyer. I shall expe<ct you>†a Wednesday evening the time you set, I would have something of the Poulterers or Fishmongers shop for Second course that night. Be plentifully furnished with them for dinner and supper Thursday. And if any thing can be got that will keep till Friday it will doe well. We shall be eight at table if Mr Seys comes. if he comes not we shall be but seven.†2 By this I suppose John Gray will be able to make a bill of fare and regulate what will be fit for you to bring with you from the Polterers shops which cannot be had here and Fishmongers too
Pray forget not to informe your self of John Gray how long a good Chine of beef will require to be by a good fire to be throughly roasted
Doe not forget the Drye sweet meats. I shall be glad to bid you and my Cosin your wife joy my self and am
Dear Cosin
Your most affectionate Cosin and humble servant
JOHN LOCKE
Sir Francis and my Lady and all this family wish you Joy
Address: For Peter King Esqr member of Parliament. To be left at Mr Potmans in the Middle Temple London Frank.

3632. Anthony Collins to Locke, 26 September 1704 (3624, 3636)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 7, ff. 59-60. Answered by no. 3636.
Lincolns Inne. Sept. 26th 04
Dear Sir
How much soever I am delighted with the wellfare of my Friend Mr King, I am the more so, to think of what you enjoy on the occasion of it, for I can easily conceive you to have thrown off your Age and Weakness, and to bear a Principal part in the mirth at Oates, and that it is not in your power to attend the decays of nature while you have such agreable company to entertain yourself with. And therefore that I may not wish you any thing that you are now in actual possession of, I wish you the continuance of all that you now enjoy. You may allow me to be very sincere in it when I assure you that it is the most self-interested wish that I can make.
I have now my Ladys Picture in a frame hung up in my Chamber. when you desire to have it, it shall be sent you down with all imaginable care. To make you some amends for my severe judgment upon it in my last†1 I must acquaint you, that Several Virtuosi in Painting have seen it, and that they all agree in giving it an excellent Character.
Yesterday I received a letter from Mr Bold the first I have had from him since his return into Dorsetshire wherein he gives his service to Every body at Oates. He has been much indispos'd but is now as he says in some measure recover'd.
I am with perfect Sincerity
Dear Sir
Your most Humble and most Obedient Servant
A. COLLINS
my most humble service to Mr King and his Lady; Sir F. and my Lady, Mr Masham and Mr Coste.
Address: For John Locke Esq [at Oates, by Joslyn, Bishops Stortford]
Postmarks: SE 26; GC
Endorsed by Locke: A Collins 26 Sept 04 Answered Oct 2
― 400 ―

3633. Edward Fowler, bishop of Gloucester, to Locke, 26 September 1704 (3501, 3635)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 8, ff. 149-50.
Dear Sir
little did I think of having the Satisfaction of a letter in your own hand; and most thankfull am I for it. I give you many thanks too, for your readiness to part with your Horse to me. You say you will send him up, upon such terms as I shal not dislike, if my mind be not Changed. This it is not, I having not writ about him, till about 3 weeks or more, after mrs Bolton†1 told me you had wholly betaken yourself to a shays. As to your terms, you may be sure, Sir, I shal not dislike them, such an Esay Horse being a very great Rarity, and can hardly be purchased at too dear a rate, by one in my Condition, and that is not in Circumstances (as I thank God I am not) to think much of paying a good price, for so great a Convenience. But I have no reason to fear, that your terms will otherwise be disliking, than as too low. If I shal find, that I can not ride (having not been on an Horse back to try, since the last Spring) should you have not a farther Occasion for him, I will take Care to have him sold here, at as good a Price as can be had. I thank God, I have not had another fit since my last; but 'tis early days yet. I have ever since kept to my Bath Water, for about every each day, a bottle in a morning. With my most hearty prayers, for your gaining of more Strength, and our Humble and Affectionate Services to yourself, Sir Francis and my Lady et. I remain
Sir
Your for ever,
E G
Sept.26—704
Pray, Sir, my H Service to all your good Guests.
Address: To John Locke Esqr, at Oates To be left at the Crown in Harlow Essex Glocest. frank
Postmark: SE 26
Endorsed by Locke: Bp. Glocester 26 Sept. 04 Answered Oct. 8

3634. Rabsy Smithsby to Locke, 26 September 1704 (3630)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 18, ff. 149-50.
26 Sept 04
Sir
With my thanks for the Favouer of yours I desier to acquaint you, that the Last weeke I made up your Chocolate of those Cocao nuts I received Last, which weere very good, and I hope are made up as you desier tho before I received your direction. The same man made it which formerly you aproved of, and it did not want my Care and observation. if it prove†a- such as-†a you may be pleased with, it will be my satisfaction, and I wish it may Contribute to your health, which is most earnestly desiered by
Sir
your most affect: humble Servant
RABSY SMITHSBY

3635. Edward Fowler, bishop of Gloucester, to Locke, 28 September [1704] (3633, 3642)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 8, ff. 151-2. Year from Locke's endorsement.
Dear Sir,
Your Horse is come well; and I give you many thanks for the Care you have taken to have him Conveyed. Most tender shal I be of him, for your sake: And Oh (if it be the Will of God) that I may have the great Pleasure of returning him to you, for your own riding. Sir, What you write of the Extreme decay of your Strength, dos greatly Affect me. I was earnestly praying for you, but just before I had your letter, and for those very Blessings you mention. God Almighty give us a Happy Meeting in the Other State! But, it may be, as much haste as you are making, I may get the start of you.
― 402 ―
The Messenger waits, and I can't Enlarge as I would do; and therefore,
Dear Sir,
Adieu at Present,
E G
Sept. 28 -
Sir, My Humble Service to my Good Lady, whose exceeding kind letter I yesterday received: And will return my thanks for it.
I know you Cant forget me on your knees; and I Earnestly beg your remembrance of mine with me.
Address: To the Honoured, John Lock Esqr, at Oates
Endorsed by Locke: Bp: Glocester 28 Sept. 04 Answered Oct. 8

3636. Locke to Anthony Collins, 1 and 2 October 1704 (3632, 3640)
The Carl H. Pforzheimer Library, New York. Part of the letter was written by Peter King. Transcribed from photostat. Part printed in A Collection of Several Pieces, pp. 326-7. Answers no. 3632.
Oates Oct. 1. 1704
Dear Sir
To compleat the satisfaction I have lately had here there has been no thing wanting but your company. The comeing of his father in law joynd with the straitness of <Lodging>†a in this house hinderd me from haveing my Cosin King and you togeather and so cut off one part of the enjoyment which you know is very valuable to me. I must leave it to your kindness and charity to make up this loss to me. How far the good company I have had here has been able to raise me into a forgetfullness of the decays of age and the uneasiness of my indisposition my Cosin King is judg But this I beleive he will assure you that my infirmitys prevail so fast on me that unless you make hast hither I may loose the satisfaction of ever seeing again a man that I value in the first rank of those that I leave behind me
My Ladys picture must be cased in deale to make it fit for cariage. But I doe not think every box maker or Joyner has the skil to doe it as it should be for it must be so fastend in the case that neither the
― 403 ―
frame nor picture may be hurt with rubing or touching the boards. They who made the frame are the likelyest men to doe it well or to direct you to those who understand how to doe it, when†a it is up in a case and directed for me at Oates to be left at Mr Harrisons at the Crown in Harlow, if you please to send to my Cosin Bonvile a Pewterer in Hounsditch and put it into his hands, he will take care to see it well plac'd in the Dunmow Waggon, and will upon your minding him of it write to mr Harrison to pay the carriage and give me notice of its being there, I find you forward enough to mention and do all things of kindness for me, but there is one thing I perceive I must desire again of you to remember, and that is, an account what I am in your debt, it is fit I even it before I leave the world, and leave my thanks with you for the obligation I have to you in every part of it, you†b may conclude I am brought pretty low since I am tired with so little a motion as writeing is. My Cosin King siting by me observed it and offerd me his service. But by whose hand soever it is expressed you may be assured that my heart and whole self as 'tis is yours. I am
Dear Sir
your most humble and most faithfull servant
J LOCKE
My†c Lady presents you her service, and hopes you will remember your promise of being here within a month, which you have outrun already.
I†d am glad the Virtuosi in painting approve my Ladys picture. Pray what say they to my picture and how doe you like it? I have made two sitings to this letter and it is now the 2d of October
Address (written by Locke): For Anthony Collins Esq at his Chamber in Serle Court in Lincolns Inne London

3637. Awnsham Churchill to Locke, 4 October 1704 (3615, 3644)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 5, ff. 224-5.
London October 4. 1704
Sir
I herewith send the Two treatises of Government†1 and a pacquet, which I begg you deliver mr Coste.
I hope to send you the second sheet of the quarto on Saturday and shal observe your Directions.†2
The Collector demands this day half a years Taxes—which is now but 10l. pray must I pay it?
my humble Service to all with you. Sir
Your most faithfull servant
A CHURCHILL
