3638. Peter King, later first Baron King, to Locke, 5 October 1704 (3631, 3639)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 12, ff. 271-2. Answered by no. 3639.
London. Octob. 5. 1704.
Sir
I have been extremely oblig'd to you on several occasions, and most particularly for this last great favour received from you, and if it lay in my power in any sort to make an acknowledgment of it I should most readily do it—I have been with Mr Newton, who seems to be very busy about framing of Devises for Medals to be coin'd on the occasion of the successes this last summer, he showed me several specimens, which are all to be laid before the Councill or My Lord Treasurer†3 —But as to your busyness, I left the two bottles with him with all the directions you gave me, he saith as
― 405 ―
you do, that he knows no Chymist or refiner whom he can trust, but believes that by the latter end of next week he shall be able to gratify you himself, Seing the two bottles are to be tried apart, they must be cast into two Ingots, because till they are in Ingott the Essay†1 cannot be made—As for putting the Coronation stamp he saith, that he can do that, or stamp it with the Queens head, or any other stamp, so as it be not the stamp used on the coin—I desire therefore your farther directions thereon—My wife is yet at Lowlayton,†2 whither I am going this Evening, we came safely to Lowlayton Tuesday†3 half an hour before sunset, she was in Town Yesterday, and all day Yesterday and today hath been employed in finding an house or Lodgings, but can't yet find Convenient— Mr Collins intends to wait on you Tuesday next, He designs to come in the Bishopstartford Coach, and desires that your Chaise may meet him at Harlow—Mrs Barrington is dead†4 —My Humble service and Thanks to Sir Francis, my Lady and the whole family
I am Sir
Your Most Obliged and affectionate Cosin and Servant
P. KING.
Endorsed by Locke: P. King 5 Oct. 04 Answered 8
3639. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 8 October 1704 (3638, 3641)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 406-7. Answers no. 3638.
Oates Oct 8. 04
Dear Cosin
I am very glad to hear that you and my Cosin your wife got safe and in time to Low Layton and should be pleased to hear that you have got into a lodging or an house to your satisfaction.
I desire you to take the first oportunity to wait upon Mr Newton with my thanks and to tell him that I would by noe means have my little affair in the bottles trouble him whilst the business of the new medals are upon his hands it will be time enough when that is over
― 406 ―
and setled. you may be pleased to tell him that all the gold may be cast into one ingot because I know 'tis all of the same finess and so one essay will serve for all. When I know how much the gold precisely is, and what weight the Coronation and these other medails be I shall then beg to have my ingot coind into one or tother of them if it may be done without any great trouble to him. pray tell him I make account my gold is perfectly fine at least you may tell him of the Galatians.†1 My service to my Cosin your wife and the rest of my freinds. I am
Dear Cosin
Your most affectionate Cosin and servant
J LOCKE
Address: For Peter King Esqr member of Parliament. To be left at Mr Potmans in the Middle Temple London Frank.
Postmark: OC 9
Endorsed by King: Mr Locke Octob. 8. 1704.
3640. Locke to Anthony Collins, 8 October 1704 (3636, 3648)
The Houghton Library, Harvard. Transcribed from photostat.
Oates 8 Oct: 04
Dear Sir
It rejoyces me to hear that you will be with us on Tuesday†2 and that I have the hopes to see and imbrace you again. You and I have other satisfaction togeather than eating and drinking, and yet there is noe fault in expresseing ones wellcome to a freind in a good dish of meat. Let that be my excuse for desireing you to bring down with you half a dozen partridges, or some other such dish for second course. Let it be phesants or larks or rabets or what you like best, and a double barrell of the best Colchester Oysters if there be any fresh in town, that I may be merry with you. But this is on condition you have time, and it is easye to you. I will excuse my self to you for this the best I can when I see you, and tell you how much I am
Dear Sir
Your most humble and most obedient servant
J LOCKE
All this family long to see you
― 407 ―
I thank you for the picture of the King of Spain which you did me the favour to send me†1
Address: For Anthony Collins Esquire at his Chamber in Serle Court in Lincolns Inne London
Postmark: (G.P.O., indecipherable)
3641. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 12 October 1704 (3639, 3645)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 408-9.
Oates 12 Oct 1704
Dear Cosin
I am glad you are got into Lodgings to your likeing. This being so I wish you could steale down hither on Saturday†2 though you goe back as soon as you please Monday morning. My strength decays so fast that I doubt whether I may last an other week, This will be an excuse to your wife for comeing. Besides other business I have to talke with you, your provision for Frank is very kind and I thank you for it.†3 But there are some things to be considerd in it and fit for us to discourse, letters will scarce doe it
I desire you would write your account on Stampd paper and bring it with you. My Strong Chest is at your service. But pray let me see you if possible. If you goe on horse back to Low Layton Saturday evening and steal over hither early Sunday morning may perhaps save you some time. I only suggest this to a man that has his head full of business. But tis for you to choose your time and way. My service to my Cosin. I am
Your most affectionate Cosin and servant
J LOCKE
If you have time to get from the dispensary by Dr Pitt another box of the same pills he last sent me. Syr de Spina cervina ℥iiij i e four ounces and a pound of the best Peruvian bark not poudered
― 408 ―
bring them with you, it will be a convenience to me but not worth the constraining your self for. The Peruvian bark must not be poudered
Address: For Peter King Esqr member of Parliament. To be left at Mr Potmans in the Middle Temple London Frank.
Postmark: OC 13
Endorsed by King: Mr Locke Octob. 12. 1704.
3642. Edward Fowler, bishop of Gloucester, to Locke, 12 October 1704 (3635)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 8, ff. 153-4.
Dear Sir
I give you many thanks for the very kind offer you make me, in reference to your Horse; and the use you would make of him, to Express your Charity to such poor as you limit it to, Who are indeed the greatest objects of Compassion. But assure your self, I will not be the man that shal Estimate the Value of him, but desire your self to do it, because you are best able. If you will not, and I keep him, I will do my best to have him praised†1 to his worth; but then there must be some guess at his Age, which you can best do. I say if I keep him, because of the 5 times I have rode on him, the two last viz. on Saturday and this morning; and indeed every time but one, I made bloody water at my return, as Easy a pacer as he is. So that I much fear I shal not be for a Horse, but a little longer; and then I had much rather return him to his mercifull Owner, than sell him to one that may abuse him. This bloody water itself, I do very little matter, but I have a Melancholy Apprehension that you were too much in the right, in fearing it might proceed from some ragged Stone: Which I much doubt lyes in, or near, the neck of my Bladder. Typping promiseth great things of his water; and I have these 3 days used it, tho' with as little hope of success as can be. God give Patience and intire Submission. Sir, This I beg your Constant prayers for, as you have, and always shal have mine, who am,
Sir,
your Much Obliged Servant,
E G
Octob.12.—704
― 409 ―
Mrs Bolton shal have the great debt you owe her. H Services to Sir Francis, my good Lady, et. I am pleased, that you Complain not of this Cold East Wind, as to your breath: which I told my Lady in my last, I feared might have an ill effect upon you.
Address: To the Honoured John Locke Esqr [at Oates, by Joslyn, Bishops Stortford] E Glocest. frank
Postmark: OC 12
Endorsed by Locke: Bp Glocester 12 Oct. 04 Answered 16
3643. Francis Limborch to Locke, 12 October 1704 (3604)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 13, ff. 246-7.
London 12 October 1704
Honoured Sir
I heartily wish j could be able to finde out such boots for you heere as you desire me to look after by your most acceptable favour off the 6 Instant; butt there none to be had heere unlesse upon any Russia ships arrivall from some Captains or Merchant that deal that way; My Father has had a pair this dosen year being the outside russia leather and the lining a bear skinn, which a good friend procured him, and since they have been sold by the furriers in Holland the purchase there being either 12 or 14s English money, butt heere att present are none. Iff you please upon our Russia fleets arrivall Ill inquire off some merchants whether they have any to spare and so procure you a paire or elce write for'm to Holland, from whence I am sure I may have'm, there are two sorts with blak and red leather the outside so that you may chuze what Coullour you please; j should be very glad Sir iff there be any thing wherein j could be servicable to you; I hope now in short time to have the honour to waite on you, which hitherto by the Continuall busines has been impossible to me; My wife j thank God hath pretty near recovered her health, and j heartily wish itt may please Almighty God to preserve you yett a good while in health and
― 410 ―
prosperity, and assicure you Sir that nothing can or ever shall be more acceptable to
Honoured Sir
Your Most obliged Humble servant
FR LIMBORCH
Address: For John Locke Esquire These.
Endorsed (not in Locke's hand): Mr Francis Limborch September. 12. 1704.
3644. Awnsham Churchill to Locke, 17 October 1704 (3637)

Sir
I Received yours per mr Collins with the MMS. Coppy which does not supply what the printer wants and is that part of the Coppy, which has the Paraphrase. I would begg you to send it by first post, and shall gett it soon despatched.
I†a- have paid mr Collins your Bill of 29: 5.4 and have received this day at the Bank 57.15.0 which is 8 1/4 per Cent which is 4 1/4 profits and 4 Principall—due Michaelmas-†a
I think my Legg mends, but I am advised yet to forbear useing it. I will soon draw out your account, and will allso endeavour to make you a visitt. I am much oblidged to you and all the family with you and am Sir with h service
Your most faithfull servant

3646. Peter King, later first Baron King, to Locke, 21 October 1704 (3645, 3647)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 12, ff. 273-4.
London octob. 21. 1704.
Sir
I have been at Mr Newtons about your Gold, but it is not yet done, He saith, it will not be done till towards the latter end of next week, I will mind him of it again the beginning of the Week— Mr Freke†3 and mr Collins did me the honour to dine with me both this day, where we Remembered you, I hope Sir Francis will not faile of being here Next Munday,†4 I Long to hear by him how you do this change of Weather, Sir W.Yonge is I believe come to Town too night, Mr Clark designs to be here next Saturday—I told mr Freke about your horse and your advise to him to ride, he gave me no other answer, but that he seem'd to apprehend it as a kind way of Inviting him to Ride to Oates, Here is no news but what is
― 412 ―
in the paper—My Wife presents you her humble service, We Do Both the same to the whole family—And I am Sincerely Sir
Your Most obliged and affectionate Cosin and Servant. P. KING.†a
Endorsed by Locke: P. King 21 Oct. 04 Answered 23
3647. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 4 and 25 October 1704 (3646)
B.L., MS. Locke c. 40, ff. 412-15. Some parts of the passages relating to Locke's writings are used in the 'Advertisement to the Reader' prefixed to Locke's Posthumous Works, 1706; generally they are adapted or paraphrased, but some phrases and sentences are reproduced verbatim. Parts of the paragraph beginning 'I wish you all manner of prosperity ...' are printed in a postscript to J. Le Clerc, An Account of the Life and Writings of John Locke, 3rd ed., 1714, p. 28. Two passages (the opening paragraphs and the passages relating to Francis Cudworth Masham, with Locke's adieu) are printed in Campbell, Lives of the Lord Chancellors, iv. 582-3. According to the postscript to the Account of the Life this letter was dispatched before Locke's death.
Oates 4 Oct 1704
Dear Cosin
That you will faithfully Execute all that You finde in my Will I cannot doubt my Dear Cosin. Nor can I lesse depend upon your following my directions and complying with my desires in things not fit to be put into so solemn and publique a writeing
You will find amongst my papers several subjects proposed to my thoughts, which are very little more than extempory views, layd down in suddain and imperfect draughts, which though intended to be revised and farther looked into afterwards, yet by the intervention of business, or preferable enquiries happend to be thrust aside and so lay neglected and sometimes quite forgotten. Some of them indeed light upon me at such a time of leisure and in such a temper of mind that I laid them not wholy by upon the first interruption, but took them in hand again as occasion served, and went on in pursuance of my first designe till I had satisfied my self in the enquiry I at first proposed. of this kind is
1° My discourse of Seeing all things in god†1 which though upon examination it appears to me and I think I have shewn to be a very
― 413 ―
groundless opinion, and thereupon have been pressed by some freinds (who have seen and thought what I have writ upon it to be a sufficient confutation of it) to make it publique. yet I could never consent to print it Both because I am noe freind to controversie and also because it is an opinion that spreads not and is like to die of it self or at least to doe noe great harm. I therefore think it best that it should not be published. But†a- yet I doe not absolutely forbid it. If you and others of my judicious freinds should find occasion for it hereafter-†a
2° You are not a stranger to a little discourse I writ about miracles on occasion of Mr Fleetwoods book on that subject and an anonymous answer published to it.†1 A learned friend or two as well as your self that have seen it were mightily for publishing of it. If upon serious consideration you and some other of my judicious freinds think it may be of use to the Christian Religion, and not unseasonable at this time, and fit for the publique, For it being writ for my own satisfaction I never went beyond the first draught, you may doe with it as you think good.
3° The Conduct of the understanding†2 I have allways thought ever since it first came into my mind to be a subject very well worth consideration, though I know not how, it seems to me for any thing that I have met with to have been almost wholy neglected: what I have done in it is very far from a just treatise. All that I have done has been, as any miscariage in that point has accidentaly come into my minde, to set it downe, with those remedies for it that I could think of. This method though it makes not that hast to the end which†b one would wish, is yet perhaps the onely one can be followed in the case, it being here as in physick impossible for a physitian to describe a disease or seek remedies for it till he comes to meet with it. But those particulars that have occurd to me and I have set down being as I guess sufficient to make men see some faults in the conduct of their understandings, and suspect there may be others you may also doe with as you think fit. For they may perhaps serve
― 414 ―
to excite others to enquire farther into it, and treat of it more fully than I have done. But†a- the heads and chapters must be reduced into order.-†a
4 In some papers inscribd Physica†1 you will see the entrance upon some thoughts that deserve to be revised and farther considerd. But what is in them being onely the first draught of an hasty view which ought to be weighd again and again, is by noe means fit to be communicated to the publique. whether you will think it fitest for the fire or a corner of your study I must leave to you. I†a- am sure the present temper of this age will not bear it and whether you shall ever live to see the time fit to communicate it you must judg-†a
5° Those who have seen what I have done upon some of St Pauls Epistles are all very desirous it should be printed, perswadeing themselves it will be of great use to religion in giveing the true sense of those Epistles and therein a quite other view of the doctrine of Christianity than what is to be found in the expositions of Commentators, or the systems of divinity espoused in the several churches and sects of Christians. This enquiry into the sense of St Pauls Epistles was began by me for my own private instruction, not in favour of any opinion I already had, but in search of what St Paul taught. Wherein I used the same indifferency that I should have done in searching the meaning of any other author whose sense I had a mind to know nor have been lead into that which I have given by any prejudices of my owne but by what, upon the best judgment I could make, I took to be his. This, if ever it be published must be my excuse for whatever may appear a novelty, or dissonant from the interpretation of other Commentators. I have neither out†b- of choise-†b forsaken them any where, nor given my self up blindly to any ones authority. St Paul I have made my guide as much as possible, and concludeing him to be as I every where find him a rational pertinent arguer his sense is not so liable to be mistaken as some have imagined. That†c- I have every where
― 415 ―
endeavourd to follow impartialy every where and if it shall be judgd usefull to the Christian religion am not unwilling what I have donne on St Pauls Epistles should be published but in this method That after the Galatians the 1 to the Corinthians. The 2d to the Corinthians. To the Romans and to the Ephesians. Each of which Epistles in this order should not till after 3 months from the publication of the former†a and as much later as you shall see occasion be conveyd to Mr Churchil to be printed as that to the Galatians is. Accordingly you will find them sealed up seperately ready to be sent in their order when you find it seasonable. If Mr Churchill prints them tis well if not you may take your own measures-†c†1
I know not whether you will find any other papers of mine worth the keeping. I am sure there are none that look towards printing
You will find two or three sheets of Memoires,†2 they were writ at the request of a Person you will easilye guess to preserve the memory of some facts which he thought he might some time or other have use of. I had gon on farther if my time and health would have permitted. What there is of them pray deliver to him.
You will find a little packet sealed up and directed to Mr Newton to whom pray let them be delivered, with a brass ruler made by Butterfeild†3 in Paris which I have given to Mr Newton.
You were present when Mr Clarke begd my hone and I gave it him. Pray let it be delivered to him. These are not fit things to be put into a Will.
When you pay the legacy I have given to my Trustees for the use of Francis Cudworth Masham and his mother It is my will, and I leave it so with you, though not fit to be put into my will that I publish, that you should by noe means assigne over any debt that
― 416 ―
may be due to me at the time of my death from Sir Francis Masham upon morgage or other wise, to my said Trustees for the use of his son or Lady because it may have very ill consequences and cause differences in the family. And therefore for the same reason it is my desire to you, which I request may be as much observd as if it were in my will under the formes of hand and seale, that no part of the said legacy in the hands of my said Trustees, whereof you are one, for the use of the said Francis Cudworth Masham or his mother Shall at any time be lent to Sir Francis Masham, which I doe to prevent inconveniencies which I who am acquainted with the family forsee it would unavoidably occasion.†a
Remember it is my earnest request to you to take care of Sir Francis's yongest son Francis Cudworth Masham in all his concernes as if he were your brother. He has never failed to pay me all the respect and doe me all the good offices he was capable of performeing with all manner of Cheerfulness and delight in it so that I cannot acknowledg it too much and therefore must desire you and leave it as a charge upon you to help me to doe it when I am gon. Take care to make him a good, an honest and an usefull man. I have left my directions with him to follow your advice and rules to him and I know he will doe it for he never refused to doe what I told him was fit. If he had been my owne sone he could not have been more carefull to please and observe me
I wish you all manner of prosperity in this world and the everlasting happyness of the world to come. That I loved you I think you are convinced. God send us a happy meeting in the resurrection of the Just. Adieu
JOHN LOCKE
You will find what I have done upon St Pauls Epistles tied up in several bundles and directed to Mr Churchil in the order they should be conveyd to him. The distance of time I leave to your judgment. I have not sealed them because my seales comeing to your hands you may doe that when it is time and may perhaps have occasion to open and look into them before they goe out of your hands, which when they doe conceale your self and let it not be known they come from you. If my Paraphrase and notes on the Ephesians are not wholy transcribed before I dye (as I fear they will not. For however earnestly I have pressed it again and again I have
― 417 ―
not been able to prevaile with Will:†1 to dispatch the two first Chapters in three months) you must get it to be transcribed out of my filed papers after I am dead, that so it may be in a condition to be printed. Will after all will I think be the fitest to transcribe them because he can read my hand and knows my way of writeing with the use of the references
If by any backwardness you shall find (which I doe not suspect) in Mr Churchill, you should have any apprehension that the preface†2 might be lost in his hands, pray consider whether you will think it worth while to take a copy of it in short hand before you let it goe out of your keeping, there being noe other copy of it but that first and foul draught which is directed to Mr Churchill
My will is left sealed up in my Lady Mashams hands
In the assigneing to the Trustees the Three thousand pounds given them in my will for the use of my Lady Masham and Frank, I would have you let them have any of those securitys which my Lady Masham and the two other Trustees shall like best upon your giveing them a particular account of those securitys. I wish you happyness. Adieu
J LOCKE
25th Oct 1704
Address: For Peter King Esqr of the Inner Temple London
3648. Locke to Anthony Collins, 23 August 1704; delivered after 28 October (3640)
The Carl H. Pforzheimer Library, New York. Transcribed from photostat. Excerpt ('May you live long ... to make up the account') printed in the postscript to Le Clerc, Account of the Life of Locke, 3rd ed., 1714, p. 28. Part printed in A Collection of Several Pieces, pp. 328-9; the whole, from a copy, B.M., Add. MS. 4290, ff. 1-2, in Fox Bourne, ii. 550-1. For the posthumous delivery see the address.
Oates 23 Aug 1704
Dear Sir
By my will you will see that I had some kindness for Frank Masham And I knew no better way to take care of him than to put him and what I designed for him into your hands and management.
― 418 ―
The knowledg I have of your vertue af all kinds secures the trust which by your permission I have placed in you. And the peculiar esteem and love I have observed in the yonge man for you will dispose him to be ruled and influenced by you, so that of that I need say nothing. But there is one thing which It is necessary for me to recommend to your especial care and memory. And that is that when the Legacy, which I have given you Trustees for the use of him and his mother, comes to be put into your hands, whether you take it in money or any other securities, a morgage which I have of Sir Francis in the name of my Cosin King and Mr Churchill should be no part of it. I know the family and foresee what inconveniencies and disorders it will produce if Sir Francis should be under any such obligations to his wife or children. which I think so carefully to be avoided, that if decency had not forbidden it I should have put it into my will it self. The money I have given you in trust for my Lady and her son I would have always placed in such hands where they may at any time freely call for it without scruple or offence; and if there be need, sue for it. Fathers and husbands usualy exspect other treatment and are disobleiged when such relations demand their due. Heads of familyes must be forborne till they please, and if a wife or a child uses importunitie or the assistance of the law to get from them, what they have their hand and seal for, the father complains of disrespect and injury, a breach of affection is made where it should be studiously avoided; and the foolish world usualy joyns in with their censures to widen and keep open the breach. To prevent this I think there should be noe such transactions as borrowing or lending between such persons or securities passed from a father to a son but in cases that are absolutely necessary. In all other cases where it is at a mans choise to put out his money upon security as he thinks fit, let him take such security as he can upon any occasion make use of, and let the hand and seale he has for his money be of such a man as he can without restraint produce and urge upon him when there is need, to what purpose else is hand and seale? If I use them not I have not my owne when I need it; and if I use them I loose my quiet and reputation and perhaps my father. But I have dwelt too long upon this matter, the fatall consequences I have seen of such things in the disturbance of familyes and the ill effects it has had, has made me very carefull to prevent it In one that I wish well to. May you live long and happy in the enjoyment of health, freedom, content and all those
― 419 ―
<blesseings>†a which providence has bestowed on you and your virtue intitles you to. I know you loved me liveing, and will preserve my memory now I am dead, All the use to be made of it is that this life is a scene of vanity that soon passes away and affords no solid satisfaction but in the consciousness of doeing well and in the hopes of an other life. This is what I can say upon experience and what you will find when you come to make up the account. Adieu. I leave my best wishes with you
JOHN LOCKE
Address: For Anthony Collins Esq. To be delivered to him after my decease
Locke: Will
B.L., MS. Locke b. 5, item no. 14. Copy, probably made by an officer of the Prerogative Court of Canterbury. The whole is printed, probably not from the present text, and with some omissions, in [Le Clerc], An Account of the Life and Writings of John Locke Esq.; The Third Edition Enlarged, 1714, pp. 29-34. Part of the codicil is printed, probably from the present text, in King, pp. 266-7.
I John Locke of High Laver in the County of Essex being infirme in body but of sound mind for which I thanke Almighty God do make and declare this my last Will and Testament as to the disposeing of all my worldly goods in manner and forme following hereby revoakeing and anulling all former Wills with all bequests and things therein contained Imprimis I give to Dr. Veen of Amsterdam five pounds Item I give to Dr. Peter Guenellon of Amsterdam twenty pounds Item I give to his Wife Cornelia Maria Veen ten pounds Item I give to their Son Peter Guenellon five pounds Item I give to Mr. Benjamin Furly of Rotterdam five pounds All which said Legacies given to the persons abovesaid liveing in Holland it is my Will should be paid out of that money of mine which is in the hands of the said Dr. Guenellon Item I give to my Cosen Peter Stratton fifty pounds Item I give to Edward Clarke of Chipley Esquire two hundred pounds Item I give to his daughter Elizabeth Clarke two hundred pounds and her Mothers picture which hangs up in the parlour at Oates†1 Item I give to Mr. William Grigg of
― 420 ―
Jesus Colledge in Cambridge two hundred pounds and Grotius's annotations on the Bible in four volumes in folio which books were a legacy given me by his Father Item I give to my Cousin John Bonville of London Pewterer two hundred pounds which it is my Will should be paid him out of the mony he owes me Item I give to Sir Francis Masham of High Laver Baronett ten pounds and the hangings and other furniture belonging to me in the Chamber over my Study in his house at Oates Item I give to Dame Damaris Masham his wife my ruby ring and my diamond ring with one stone and any four folio's eight quarto's and twenty books of lesse volume which She shall choose out of the books in my Library not otherwise disposed of by name and ten pounds in mony for which her receipt shall be a discharge Item I give to Mrs. Esther Masham daughter of the said Sir Francis Masham ten pounds Item I give to Francis Cudworth Masham youngest Son of the said Sir Francis Masham ten pounds for which his receipt shall be a discharge Item I give to my good Freind Mr. Anthony Collins of the Middle Temple my Plautus in folio of Lambins edition Gesneri Bibliotheca aucta per Simlerum Kerckringii Spicilegium anatomicum, Catalogus librorum bibliothecæ Raphaelis Trichett du Fresne Bibliotheca Thuana et Bibliotheca Heinseana and Witsens Map of Tartary that hangs up in my Study Item I give to my Cosen Peter King of the Inner Temple my Harmonia Evangeliorum Toinardi Poli Synopsis Criticorum, les Memoires de Monsieur Deagent Marci Antonii de Dominis Epistola de pace Religionis ad Josephum Hallum all my Manuscripts and all my books that are interleaved and the one moiety of all the rest of my books not herein otherwise disposed of together with the boxes that they stand in the other moiety of my said books together with the boxes they stand in I give and bequeath to Francis Cudworth Masham aforesaid when he shall attain the age of one and twenty yeares†1 Item I give to the said Francis Cudworth Masham when he shall attain the age of one and twenty yeares my silver Snuffers Snuffdish and Candlestick which were a legacy given me by his Grandmother Cudworth my silver Screen to preserve the eyes in reading by Candlelight my Clocke and Mr. Molineux's picture which hangs up in the parlour at Oates All which said moyety of my books together with the peeces of plate Clocke and picture hereby given or intended
― 421 ―
to be given to the said Francis Cudworth Masham as aforesaid it is my will shall be and remain during his minority in the custody of his Mother Dame Damaris Masham or of such person or persons as She shall appoint with power to the said Dame Damaris Masham to use them whilst they are in her custody But in case her said Son Francis Cudworth Masham shall dye before he attain the age of one and twenty yeares then my Will is that the said moyety of my books so left in the custody of the said Dame Damaris Masham shall forthwith upon such death of the said Francis Cudworth Masham be delivered up to my said Cosen Peter King to whom in that case I do give them but the severall peeces of plate the Clocke and picture aforesaid left in the custody of the said Dame Damaris Masham it is my Will shall be and remain to her the said Dame Damaris Masham to whom in that case I do give them Item I give to my Cosen Mary Doleman six pounds per annum during her natural life for her sole and separate use to be paid her quarterly or otherwise as my Executor shall thinke best for her for which her receipt shall be a discharge Item I give to the poor of the Parish where I shall dye forty shillings Item I give to the poor of the parish of Publoe in the County of Somersett forty shillings Item I give to the poor of the Parish of St. Thomas in Pensford in the same County forty shillings and I do hereby order and appoint that my <Executor>†a hereinafter named shall during his natural life and no longer sett apart the summe of ten pounds per annum for the releife and assistance of such industrious poor people inhabitants of the said Parishes of Publoe and St. Thomas and of the adjoyning tithing of Beluton alias Belton and in such manner as He shall thinke fitt but more especially for the binding out Apprentices born within the said Parishes of Publoe and St. Thomas and titheing of Beluton my Will and meaning being that my said Executor shall as often as he shall thinke fitt out of such sumes of mony so to be sett aparte by him as aforesaid bind or cause to be bound out Apprentice such Child or Children as aforesaid to such honest art vocation or Trade as He in his discretion shall thinke most meet and proper it being my intention that my said Executor shall have the sole disposal of the said ten pounds per annum and not account to anyone for the same Item it is my Will that my Executor shall pay the sume of ten pounds per annum to Dame Damaris Masham
― 422 ―
aforesaid during the first ten yeares after my death if She shall so long live to be disposed of by her the said Dame Damaris Masham to such poor as She in her discretion shall thinke fitt it being my intention that She the said Dame Damaris Masham shall have the sole disposall of what She shall receive of the said ten pounds per annum and not account to anyone for the same and also that her receipt for so much thereof as She from time to time shall receive shall be a discharge Item I give to Mr. Anthony Collins aforesaid ten pounds Item I give to Mr. Awnsham Churchill of London Bookseller ten pounds Item I give and bequeath to Anthony Collins Peter King and Awnsham Churchill aforesaid the sume of three thousand pounds upon the severall trusts and for the severall uses hereinafter mentioned and declared that is to say in trust that they the said Anthony Collins Peter King and Awnsham Churchill the Survivours or Survivour <of them>†a shall from time to time by and with the approbation and consent of Dame Damaris Masham aforesaid testified by writing under her hand and until Francis Cudworth Masham Son of the said Dame Damaris Masham shall attain the age of five and twenty yeares or other times hereinafter mentioned put out at interest or otherwise improve the said three thousand pounds and the interst and other profitt ariseing thereout shall pay unto the said Dame Damaris Masham <who of>†b such interest and profitt so paid unto her shall retain one third parte for her own sole and separate use and shall lay out the other two third parts in such manner as She shall see fitt for the better Education and maintenance of the said Francis Cudworth Masham and the receipt of her the said Dame Damaris Masham <whither>†c sole or under coverture shall be a sufficient discharge of my said Trustes for such sume or sumes of mony so paid her But if the said Dame Damaris Masham shall dye liveing the said Francis Cudworth Masham and before the said Francis Cudworth Masham shall attain the age of five and twenty yeares then my said Trustees shall untill the said Francis Cudworth Masham shall attain the age of five and twenty yeares or other times herein and equity and on this further trust that if the said Francis

chargeable with the losse of the said sume of three thousand pounds or any part thereof so putt out as aforesaid at interest or in any other way of improvement and that during the time of their management of the said three thousand pounds they shall from time to time retain one twentieth parte of the interest and clear profitt thereof to their own use in

recompence of their trouble and that all their costs and charges shall be born and paid out of the interest and profitt of the said three thousand pounds And my Will likewise is that if at any time or then such person or persons so nominated and appointed by her shall be admitted thereunto and be joyned to my said Trustees to be concerned in the said trust with them as if they had been particularly named by me Item I give to Sir Francis Masham's cheif Maid Servant forty shillings Item I give to all his other menial Servants twenty shillings a peece Item I give to my Servant William Shaw five pounds and all my wearing apparell if he shall be in my service at the time of my death Item my Will is to be buried as privately as with decency may be perticularly my Will is to be buried in a plain wooden Coffin not covered with Cloth or any otherwi: adorned that cost will better be laid out in covering the poor and therefore my Will is that instead thereof four honest poor labouring men of the Neighbourhood such as I (or for want of my doing it Dame Damaris Masham aforesaid) shall name shall each of them have a coat and pair of breeches of cloth a Hatt a pair of Shooes and Stockings which will be better than the vain wast of a covering and other ornaments on my Coffin Item my Will is to be buried in the Church Yard of the Parish where I dye All the rest of my goods Chattells Mortgages and reall Securities I give and bequeath to my Cosen Peter King aforesaid whom I do hereby make and constitute full and sole Executor of this my last Will and Testament which is all writt with my own hand and hath in the third page the word (years) and in this page the word (Masham) interlined John Locke Signed Sealed and published by the Testator in the presence of us and by us Signed in his presence this eleaventh day of April in the third year of the Reign of our Soveraigne Lady Queen Ann Anno Domini 1704. Roger Hazard. Peter Coste. Thomas King.
Whereas I John Locke have in and by a certain writing bearing date the eleaventh day of April last past made my last Will and Testament now I doe hereby ratifye confirme and establish my said last Will and Testament only I desire that these additions may be made to it that is to say I give and bequeath unto my
― 425 ―
good Freind John Freke of the Middle Temple Esquire the summe of one hundred pounds of lawfull mony of England to be paid him in five severall yearly payments of twenty pounds a year the first payment to be made within a year after my decease and the four remaining payments in the four immediately succeeding years the same to be disposed of by him in such parts and portions and to such person and persons and to such good use or uses and at such time or times as He in his discretion shall thinke fitt without being accountable for such his disposal to any person whomsoever And in case the said John Freke shall dye before the whole hundred <pounds>†a be paid him then my Will is that the residue of the same which shall not have been actually paid to him shall be paid by like payments unto such person and persons as the said John Freke shall by his Will nominate and appoint to receive and dispose of the same for the purposes and in the manner aforesaid†1 Item I give to the said John Freke ten pounds to buy himselfe a Ring Item I give and bequeath to Anthony Collins in my said last Will and Testament mentiond twenty pounds a year to be paid him yearly during the first five years after my decease if He shall so long live the first payment to be made within six months after the day of my decease and so on yearly during the said terme <of five>†b years if he shall live so long the same to be disposed of by him in such parts and portions and to such person and persons and to such good use or uses and at such time or times as he in his discretion shall thinke fitt without being accountable for such his disposal to <any>†c person whomsoever Item Whereas the Reverend Dr. Hudson Library Keper of the Bodleian Library in the University of Oxford writt to me some time since desireing of me for the said Library the books whereof I was the Author I did in return to the honour done me therein present to the said Library all the books that were publishd under my name which though accepted with honourable mention of me yet were not understood fully to answer the request made me it being supposed that there were other treatises whereof I was the Author which had been publishd without my name to them In compliance therefore with what was desired in the utmost extent of it and in acknowledgement of the honour done me in thinking my writings worthy to
― 426 ―
be placed among the works of the learned in that August repository I do hereby further give to the publick Library of the University of Oxford these following books that is to say three letters concerning Toleration the first whereof I writt in Latin and was published at Tergow in Holland 1689 under this title Epistola de Tolerantia and afterwards translated into English without my privity 2°. A second letter concerning Toleration printed for Awnsham and John Churchill 1690. 3°. A third letter for Toleration to the Author of the third Letter concerning Toleration printed for Awnsham and John Churchill 1692. two Treatises of Government whereof Mr. Churchill has published severall editions but all very uncorrect The reasonableness of Christianity as delivered in the Scriptures A vindication of the reasonableness of Christianity from Mr. Edwards's reflections A second vindication of the reasonableness of Christianity xca. These are all the Books whereof I am the Author which have been publishd without my name to them Item I give to the said Bodleian Library the Argument of the Letter concerning Toleration breifly considered and answered printed in Oxford 1690 and a third letter concerning Toleration in defence of the Argument of the letter concerning Toleration breifly considered and answered printed in Oxford 1691†1 Both which Treatises it is my Will should be bound up in one volume with my three letters on the same subject that therein any one who pleaseth may have the convenience to examine what my Opponent and I have sayd in this controversy Item Whereas there is intended speedily another Edition of my Essay concerning Humane Understanding wherein there will in the 31 Chapter of the second book be some small alterations which I have made with my own hand that the University which hath been pleased to honour it with a place in its Library may have that Esay in the Estate that my last thoughts left it in, it is my Will that my Executor shall in my name present to the said Bodleian Library one coppy of the next edition of my said Essay well bound†2 Item Whereas I am informed that there is a designe of publishing two other volumes as a continuation of the collection of voyages publishd this year by Awnsham and John Churchill in four volumes in folio It is my Will that my Executor shall in my name present to the said Bodleian Library these two intended volumes also when
― 427 ―
they come out and which I do hereby give to the University of Oxford to make up the four volumes of the Collection aforesaid which I have already presented to the said university a compleat Sett†1 Item I give to Mrs. Mary Clarke wife of Edward Clarke of Chipley Esquire ten pounds Item I give to James Johnstown of Twittenham Esquire ten pounds Item I give to Mr. Robert Pawling ten pounds to be paid him when the mony his daughter Justice and he owe me is paid to my Executor Item I give to my Cosen Ann Hazel wife to my Cosen James Hazel of Bishops Sutton in the Parish of Chew in the County of Somersett five pounds Item I give to Francis Cudworth Masham in my Will mentioned his Mothers picture drawn by Sir Godfrey Kneller when he attains the age of one and twenty yeares†2 Item Whereas in my said last Will and Testament I ordered four poor men of the neighbourhood to be cloth'd those whom I name thereunto are Edward Saltmarsh Carpenter Robert Clarke Thatcher John Douset and William Pilly, day Labourers All which legacyes and bequests I order to be taken as a Codicill to be annexed to my said last Will and Testament and to be reputed as parte and parcell thereof In Witnesse whereof I have hereunto putt my hand and seale this fifteenth day of September in the third year of the Reigne of our Soveraigne Lady Queen Ann Anno Domini 1704 John Locke. In the presence of us and by us signed in the presence of the Testator Roger Hazard Thomas King†3

Addendum: Additional Letters

1675
May
298A. Locke to Richard Lilburne, 12 May 1675 (290, 300)
B.L., MS. Locke d. 9, pp. 87 and 236. A copy of part of the letter to which 300 is the reply. Written by Locke as far as the first two words of query 2, thereafter by an amanuensis. Entered under marginal key-word 'Pisces', and headed 'Some Quaeres concerning the poysonous fish about New Providence sent to Mr. Lilburne. 12 May. 75.' Continued after query 8 on p. 236 under keywords 'Pisces virulenti'.
1 Whether all sorts or species of fish about that Island have that quality to cause pains in the joints of some of those that eat them?
2 If not all sorts what and how many sorts there are that have that poysonous quallity
3 Whether the same sorts be found in other parts and whether in other parts of the world they have the same poysonous quality?
4 Whether upon nice Equiry and observation there be noe difference to be found between the wholsome and poysonous fish of the same sort or species?
5 Whether this quallity be most in the Male or female fishes?
6 Whether they be more poysonous at ane time of the yeare or moone or season of the weather than at another?
7 Whether this poyson works alike upon all ages constitutions and sexes, or whether some be more liable to it than others?
8 Whether there be noe other symptoms which are the effects of this poyson but pains in the joynts
Pisces virulenti
9 Whether that pain never lasts above 2 or 3 days and what kind of pain it is, and whether in all persons?
10 Whether those who have been once infected finde the returne of those pains upon the eateing of any sort of fish or of that species only which at first hurt them, and whether the paines soe returneing upon eateing of fish are as violent and lasting as at first?
― 429 ―
11 Whether there be noe antidote yet knowne against this poyson?
12 After what manner cats and doges die, when this poyson kills them?
13 How long after eating doe men begin to finde the effects of this poyson and in what maner does it first seise them?

1624A. James Johnstoun to Locke, [2 May 1693] (1616, 1640A)
Scottish Record Office, Johnstoun's letter-book, SP 3/1, ff. 134v-135r. Year from letter-book; day and month from Johnstoun's marginal: Edinburgh†a 2 May To Mr Lock. Transcribed from photostat.
Sir
I know to you a litle is enough. The parliament has made a hearty answer to the Kings Letter. They have continued all the present burdens and troops to mertimess 94 against which time they hope the war will be at an end if not that they shall meet before that time. They have voted fonds for two new regiments of dragoons and four of foot to pay them to the said terme. They have done this the more frankly that they hope honest officers will be named such as major generall Livingston the Comander in cheif here. They have resolved too to give a fond for repairing the Castles and providing the Magazines with armes and amunition and (which possibly is more necessary then all the rest) for paying the Army their arrears and paying the Country what the army owes them. All this will amount to much more than this Nation ever gave. The truth is, honest men here (who are by far the majority in this†b parliament) would give more than they are well able to give provided they may be Secure, but it is impossible to give them a good opinion of a great many in the Government. we shun Church matters and such like subjects till the State concernes of greater moment be over, but I despair not
― 433 ―
of doing a great dale in those matters too. much art has been used to perswade the Nation that they were in no danger, which put me upon the necessity of doing what the minuts will Shew you I have done. I was not prepared for it, but a good cause goes far. I drunk two botles of bath waters this morning and have Spoke since in parliament I beleive three hours till I carryed my point. I was dispensed with for the rules of the house since I was only ripe on the subject and consequently only fit to mannage it. The whole of the report of the SubCommitty was approved in parliament unanimously, but that which concerned the Town of Edinburgh†a was disputed. You know the Kings Councell etc. carryed it on. I at last explained the words King James Interest at bottom, that K: James interests were carryed on in a hidden manner etc. and so carryed the approve by three to one. had I had leave to go as far as the evidence I have would have carryed me I would have made another work on it. What was brought into parliament will be printed. my humble Service to the Keeper and to my Lady St Johns. farewell.
1640A. James Johnstoun to Locke, [20 June 1693] (1624A, 2099)
Scottish Record Office, Johnstoun's letter-book, SP 3/1, ff. 172v-173r. Year from letter-book, day and month from Johnstoun's marginal: 20th June To Mr Lock. Transcribed from photostat.
You'le allow me to dictat for I have toilled so all day that I cannot writ. I had yours of the 30th and the other but cannot trust true answers to writing, and if you have been in town and seen my brother you are answered already. I hope the King is Satisfyed since representations have been delayed. The Satisfaction would be more universall if ther were nothing to be represented. You may I own it think strange that after such fair beginings nothing more has been done, but if you knew the truth, you would think strange that any thing is done, not but that the parliament is honest and zealous, for it is the Convention parliament, but we the mannagers faill. The number and quality of persons concerned in Payn were such, that we could not resist their applications. In a word he owes his Safety to the dimensions of his crime. If we had tryed him we
― 434 ―
should have had a more particular discovery, But never was ther a plainer discovery made than by not trying him. I askt in open parliament how those very men, who to my knowledge had given it out that all was sham and forgery could be so destitute of Shame as to oppose the triall. I added that the reason was plain, that many were concerned, and guilt was stronger than Shame. To this no man replyed a word, Tho' I spoke visibly to members of his Majesty's Councell†a and men of the best quality, who upon other occasions would bear such Language of no man, But they care not what was said provided they carryed their point, as they did, by the assistance and authority of his Majesty's Commissioner. Thus was lost the true opportunity of putting an end to plotts in this Kingdom. As to all other matters ther are Laws made which if duely executted will prove of great advantage both to the King and Country. But without a new Spirit in the administration, as the parliament says in their Letter to the King, all will certainly prove ineffectuall; For this Nation is a two edged tool which may happen to Cutt deep one time or other either for the present Setlement or against it. I hope to see you in a litle time. farewell.

1695
March
1854A. Locke to Andrew Fletcher of Saltoun, 1 March 1695 (1851, 2381)
National Library of Scotland, MS. 17851, ff. 22-3. The end of the postscript is apparently lost. Answers 1851. MS. 17851, ff. 24-5, 132-3 and 154, containing Locke's advice concerning Margaret Fletcher's case, date from June to September of this year. Transcribed from photostat.
Oates 1° Mar 94/5
Sir
you could not be more glad of your sister in laws perfect recovery than I should be in doeing you any acceptable service in reference to it. But whatever occasion the title of my booke may have given you to complement me to her, you have either said or shee beleived too much, and you will be both mistaken if you take me for a prophet. That which makes me suspect this is That you say not a word but leave me to divine what she hath done and with what successe since you formerly sent me her case, which concludeing from what you write that I guessed pretty right at, that which
― 435 ―
must lead me to doe any thing farther is to know all the circumstances of her present state, what has been done and what alterations she has found since I writ to you concerning her. Her stomach, her sleep, her recovery of strength or flesh, and any other observable good or bad it would be requisite to know. neither is it enough to†a- be told-†a that she is now much better as to her obstructions, tis necessary to be informed whether nature be come yet to its former course as she enjoyd that benefit before her ilnesse, or at least approaches pretty near it. For upon that depends much of what is at present to be done, besides her diet and exercise is to be regulated but that depends on her strength†b- stomach and sleep-†b. I guesse she is but weak yet. But tis not to proceed upon guesses in things that may be known. When the patient has told all she can even to the least circumstance there is too much, after all the†c best information, left to guesseing. Her sweating I allow ought to be taken care of, but I know noe receit against sweating though when I know the present state she is in I may be able to guesse at some way to abate and remove it for that depends upon something else that is amisse to which the cure must be directed. I guesse the Bath waters would now be good for her, and that even her journy thither would doe her good but I know not whether she be able to bear it and I have not light enough to be determind. I am thinking also of an Electuary that I could prescribe her that might be usefull but I have one rule which I always observe in physick, that it is better to doe noe thing than to doe amisse and he practises madly who prescribes at hap hazard, which has made me always very backward to prescribe at a distance. Your Authority prevaild with me before to tell my opinion. But I must not venture in the dark to doe harme because I have a great compliance with your commands or because you think well of me. When I am better inlightend and instructed in the Ladys present condition you shall readily have my opinion. In the mean time I think neither she nor you ought to dispond of her recovery. I looke on the great difficulty of her cure as over and advise her keep on the same course which has advanced her soe far till she be advised to alter or adde any thing to it. I hope all her pains are gon and she hears noe more of her vapors but tis hard to conclude any thing positively from your silence.
― 436 ―
The Lady whom you think soe happy invites you to come and share it with her for you cannot she says have soe much of that happynesse which you count ignorance of what passes upon the stage makes, any where as here. And she perswades herself one more curious of news than she might passe many days in learning from you the wisdom and virtue of the ancients without careing what this foolish world is doeing. I made her a promise in your name that she should see you here before this and she often reproaches me for your not keeping your word. Be content to live a few days out of the Chocolate house if it be but to returne from us poore honest country folke, with the better stomach, to the Witts and the Braveries
I am very glad you are traceing what you tell me and that you can discover soe much of it soe far back as the time of the Ægyptians. I did not at all doubt but in†a that politik and religious country there had been much of it but I feard time had rubd out the footsteps of it. but their feet what ever they trample on make pretty deep impressions. you cannot doe better, pray therefor goe on. My humble service to Mr Secretary. We hoped to have seen him here before this time. I am
Sir your most humble servant
J LOCK
There was a Pamphlet printed in 83†b intituled Reasons†c- humbly offered for a Liberty of unlicensed printing-†c. I had a sight of it two or three days since. The printers or booksellers name is not to it nor is there any thing very extraordinary in it. But yet if you could light of it and would give it to my Lord Monmouth, either from your self or me it matters not, before the bill for printing comes up to the Lords house you would obleige me
Pray present my most humble service in Arlington street when you goe there.
After I had writ and was ready to seale my letter I received a litle booke thus intituled†d A looking glasse for the Black band of Doctors wherein may be seen the ignorance and malice of those physitians who have Clubd under the name of Doctor Black for suppressing by their Scriblings and other Calumnies soe great a benefit to the
― 437 ―
world as the new cure of fevers. printed at Edingburgh. 1692. With this booke came a

Depuis ma derniere du 8eme de ce mois d'Avril j'ay examinè ce que j'ay de letres dont il est question avec la liste que vous avez eu la bontè de m'envoier et ie trouve qu'il me manque six lettres de ceux que vous avez dans votre recueil.†a de ces grands hommes avec celle de Mr Dodwel qui est le 7eme qui me manque. mais au lieu de ces sept qui manquent j'ay dans mon MS trois autres sans nomme et sans date, qui sont courtes et n'entrent pas en matiere. dont voici les commencements†b
Primae postremae tuae transmissae erant R. P. Ant. Pagi. Hìc parisiis agit ——

De reliquiis bibliothaecae Corvinianae ——

gros lot de la petite vous en soulagera il n'y a rien que je souhaite davantage et pour l'amour de vous pour vous delivrer de ce fascheuse chicane dont vous etez menacè,†1 et pour l'amour de moi pour me donner le plus grand bonheur qui me peut ariver en cette vie en vous embrassant encore une fois

La version Francoise de mon essay touchant l'entendement est à ce que je crois imprimèe. On a donnè ordre au libraire d Amsterdam de vous envoier deux exemplaires dont je vous prie d'accepter l'un†c et de donner l'autre a Monsieur Deshaies Gendron si vous le croiez à son goût, ou autrement à tel de vous amis que vous trouverez à propos. Dans la même bale il y aura un troisieme exemplair†d pour Monsieur du Bos dont je baise tres humblement les mains
J'esperois la reforme de notre calandrier et j'en ay parlè a quelques uns de mes amis et ecrit à des autres même je proposois une methode fort facile et aise de reduire notre an au Gregoriene mais ie n'ay n'en avance laffair. les erreurs se confirment par l'age. Je suis
Monsieur Votre tres humble et tres obeissant serviteur
J Locke
You tell me that you have seen the Bishop of Worcester's Chronological†e account of the life of pythagoras This confirms me in the beleif of what Monsieur du Bos writ me, that you had learnt English This makes me enquire of you in that language your opinion of P: Lamys Introduction à l'Ecriture Sainte, and if it be worth the haveing which is best that in French or that in Latin?
