2839A. Locke to Peter King, later first Baron King, 6 January 1701 (2831, 2840)
Mr. Ralph Baxter, Brasted, Kent. Answered by 2852. Transcribed from photostat.
Oates 6 Jan 00/01
Dear Cosin
You will probably wonder to have this lettre follow you soe quick on the heels. you went from hence but at ten and we are not yet at dinner yet as it usualy happens before this short time after your departure I have light on what I should have shewn you whilst you were here had it come to hand, I mean Carpenters lease
The 6th of July 1650 Alexander Popham Granted to my father amongst†b- other things the tenement-†b of Buckhill for 99 years if my mother my brother and I should soe long live.
― 440 ―
The 23 of March 1696 I grant to Francis Carpenter all†a- that part of the tenement called Buckhill Now in the tenure or occupat of Joseph Barnes and Joseph Hanny and part of the Estate which the said John Locke holds by lease from Alexander Popham Esqr deceased. from the 25 of March now†b next ensueing the day of the date hereof for the terme of 59 years fully to be complete and ended if he the said John Locke shall soe long live.-†a These are the words of my grant to Carpenter. where by it is plain I have not granted him all my terme. For my Grant to him if I should out live the terme, would end 25° Mar 1749. But Al. Pophams†c grant to my father will not end till 6 Jul 1749 whereby I think I have still the power of distreining for my rent. But this I only say only to answer your question about that matter. But my question in†d- reference to Carpenter whose lettre I here with send you-†d arises from Hence. That because my father dureing his life time rented about 1/8 of an acre of Land which one Pope had lyeing mixed with his in†e- that which-†e is called the common mead, and I also when the estate came to my hand did the same thing for the convenience of my tenants as my father did, therefor Carpenter would have me still hire that slip of meadow which is Popes and pay the rent of it for him. which haveing never granted him as I think, though Barnes enjoyd it when I made the lease, I conclude he must rent it of Pope if he will have it, or let Pope enjoy it him self and sel out that part of the common meadow to him every year at†f- mowing time as was donne when my father did not rent it-†f according to the mear stones whereby it is distinguishd, and†g- which as I take it he Pope†h has a title to by lease from the same Land Lord. But-†g with it I think my self now†i to have noe thing to doe haveing long since sent Pope word that I would noe longer rent his slip of meadow. When you goe next to Sutton I would desire you to give Carpenter an answer according to what he has a right to claime from me for that I shall always doe. But I have noe reason to rent ground of an other for his use, who bought this estate over my head without my consent or ever soe much as†j acquainting me with his designe to doe it, for had he been soe just to me as to have thought fit to consult me in it tis like (If†k- I had consented to have resigned to him my tenant right)-†k
― 441 ―
It might have fallen in my way in discourse to have remembred and soe have explaind this particular to him. But now he must enjoy the bargain he has made with the Lord of the mannor and not expect from me, more then I granted, to make up what perhaps he may find proves a worse purchase then he expected. The other particulars he complains of are noe thing but what was agreed on when I granted him his lease, wherein they are covenanted, and are noe additions since, and soe afford noe reall pretence of complaint
I have here inclosed also the table or index I promised your Cosin King. pray give it him with my service and thanks for the favour of his company. I hope you got all well to town, and shall be glad to be assured of it. I thank you for your company and am
Dear Cosin
your most affectionate Cosin and humble servant
J LOCKE
Addressed: For Peter King Esqr.
April
2908*. Locke to Henry Fletcher, 23 April 1701 (2901)
National Library of Scotland, MS. 17851, ff. 35-6. The letter of which 2908 is a draft. Answers 2901. Transcribed from photostat.
Oates 23 Apr 1701
Sir
I have received yours of the 10th instant and with it the account you have sent me of your Ladys case: Any one who reads it will easily see that her distemper has mightily broken her constitution, and disorderd the whole Oeconomie of her body; which will require more to be donne to set it right again than the present circumstances of her breeding will admit. All that I think can rationaly be proposed or aimed at now is to support her till she is brought to bed and help her to goe out her time. That which seems most to threaten her with danger before that time is want of breath and want of Strength.
The first of these however painfull and terrible it be yet I imagin will not put an end to her life within that time because I am of opinion that symptom is not from any decay in her lungs but is produced by the disorder in the humors and animal spirits. But yet
― 442 ―
noe thing is to be neglected which may give her ease and breath in her asthmatick fits
As to her weakness which threatens most it is in my opinion impossible to stop the increase of it and hinder her from sinking unless her stomach be taken care of, that her appetite may be recoverd and digestion performed, and this the rather because what increase of strength can be got this way will also help to lessen her short breathing
To this purpose I propose a diet of such wholesome nourishments as she finds most agreeable and acceptable to her stomach and she can most easily digest. To know what her stomach will most easily close and deale with, her stomach must be consulted, for noe body else can tell. Only I think her diet should have†a as little mixture of medicinal drugs with it as may be for fear of offending a weak and consequently a nice stomach which must be humord as much as possible with what best pleases her palate and appetite
In the next place seeing†b she has tried Ens veneris since her being with child, and has always found benefit in takeing of it, and seems now to long after it, I am of opinion she may now take it again, proceeding to larger doses as she grows nearer her time, espetialy after soe many drops as she has taken of Elixir proprietatis
And for the better strengthening of her stomach I propose that a large plaister of Caranna with a little mixture of balsam of Peru should be applied regioni†c- ventriculi-†c: to which perhaps her physitian may think fit to adde a little Galbanum as she approaches nearer to her time. I mention her physitian for I would have all this course (if you think fit to follow it) under the direction of a physitian upon the place who upon emergencies not to be foreseen may accomodate it to the present circumstances as occasion may require. For it is impossible before hand and at a distance to tell what symptoms will or what will not need to be applied to under this view of barely supporting her till she be brought to bed, leaveing the thoughts and endeavours after a radical cure till after that time. And therefor I think several of the symptoms she now complains of may at present be neglected and none of them any farther applied to than as they directly thwart the main designe of supporting and recovering her strength and threaten her with imminent danger.
― 443 ―
For she is in my opinion too weak to bear many medicines, which if multiplied, her stomach will not have time to demand and digest food sufficient for her support till child birth
If the faintings you mention returne I think that hot cordials whose basis is the spirits of distild vinous liquors are by noe means proper. Instead of those a pretty large dose of Orange flower water a little sweetend with syrop of clove July flowers is what I would propose or if that be not found effectuall enough some good old Venice Treacle may be dissolved in the Orange flower water instead of the Syrop of clove July flowers
If sweet smels as you say comfort and releive her let her by all means use them, and those which she finds most agree with her, for noe thing but triall in her present case can discover which is best Though a tincture of Ambergris seems to me to be as likely to be good as any
By all means let her burn wood in her chamber, and though she is not able to walke abroad, yet if she could be carried out of her chamber in a chair into the open air when the weather is favourable I guess it would much releive her
I hope by such a method as this under the direction of a skilfull physitian on the place she may be supported till her lyeing in, which is all that I think can be proposed at present. I wish good successe to this or what ever else may be used for her releif and recovery. I am
Sir
Your most humble servant
JOHN LOCKE
Endorsed by Fletcher: Mr Locks letter 23 April 1701.
― 444 ―
Appendix I: Locke's Portraits
Locke's correspondence contains a number of notices relating to portraits of him; the present appendix is concerned with these notices and the identification of the portraits in them, and not with the portraits in general.
Locke's friends wanted painted portraits of him and he wanted engravings for his Essay. There arose also a larger demand. In 1697 Evelyn listed him among persons worthy of commemoration in medallions or in some other way.†1 In the eighteenth century painted portraits of him were wanted, and copies of those by Kneller are common; there were also some sculptured representations of him. Almost all the latter representations of him are irrelevant for this appendix; I have mentioned only those that bear on the notices in the correspondence.
I have drawn freely on David Piper, Catalogue of Seventeenth-Century Portraits in the National Portrait Gallery, 1963, and have also had much help from Mr. Piper and Mr. J. F. Kerslake.
JOHN GREENHILL (c. 1644-76)
Notices: nos. 748 (1683); 1020, 1028, 1030, 1038, 1045, 1046, 1047, 1050, 1060, 1077 (all 1688); 1165, 1166, 1171, 1173 (all 1689); Locke's Journal 12 July 1679; 16 Jan. 1683.
Most of these notices relate to a dispute between Locke and Thomas Stringer about the ownership of a portrait of Locke. Locke left it with Stringer when he went to France in 1675. Stringer claimed later that Locke had given it to Mrs. Stringer; Locke denied this. In 1688 he wanted the use of the portrait for an engraving to be prefixed to the first edition of the Essay; Stringer refused to let him have it. The latest mention is in 1689, when Stringer still had possession.
Locke names the painter of the portrait in dispute in no. 1165; he is (John) Greenhill. Writing about May 1688 he states that it was 'drawn' twenty years ago: no. 1046; the 'twenty' is to be taken loosely. Stringer writes that Dr. (David) Thomas has a portrait of Locke smaller in size than his own and generally considered to be better painted: no. 1028. He does not name the artist. As Mrs Thomas was a sister of Greenhill it may well have been by him. In 1689 the
― 445 ―
portrait was in the studio of an artist in London (Herman Verelst?): no. 1165. This might be for copying or repair.
Coste's translation of the Essay, published at Amsterdam in 1700, contains an engraving by Pieter van Gunst of a portrait of Locke by Greenhill. Beneath the portrait there are some verses by Le Clerc. Locke sent Le Clerc a portrait of himself in the summer of 1699: no. 2595; see also nos. 2588, 2609. It may have been an original by Greenhill or a copy of one in preparation for the engraving.
Between 1721 and 1725 George Vertue made a note:
of Mr Locke a fine half lenght picture painted by Sr P. Lilly. in poses Ld. C. Justice King. Greenhill.
What seems to be the same picture is mentioned in a succeeding note with another portrait of Locke by Greenhill:
two pictures of Mr Locke one in poses of Ld C. Just. King. and another in poses of a Relation. both done by Greenhill certainly. says Mr Geekie who offerd 50 pounds for one but coud not have it they are both fine pictures.†1
King's picture is probably the original of the engraving by W. H. Worthington prefixed to the seventh Lord King's Life of Locke (1829). Its present location is unknown.†2
In 1954 the Trustees of the National Portrait Gallery bought a portrait of Locke by Greenhill. It had belonged prior to 1954 to the Sanford family at Nynehead Court. The family was descended from Locke's friend Edward Clarke, and it was probably through him that it came into the family's possession. He is unlikely to have owned it in 1688 when Locke had his clash with Stringer.†3
So far the ground is fairly firm; the rest is guessing. Whether or not he owned it, about 1699 Locke had access to a portrait of himself by Greenhill in preparation for van Gunst's engraving; whether this was the picture whose ownership was claimed by Stringer in 1688, or that owned at that time by Dr. Thomas, or an unrecorded third picture, is not ascertainable. Locke does not mention any portrait of himself in his will; there was no occasion for him to do so as, if he owned one, it would be provided for by his general bequest to King. As there is no mention of a portrait of himself in Locke's extant correspondence with Mrs. Thomas or William Thomas after Dr.
― 446 ―
Thomas's death in 1694 it is likely that he owned a portrait of himself by then. Presumably he had already obtained the Stringer picture.†1 No precise date can be suggested for that. The use of Brounower's drawing for the frontispiece of the second edition of the Essay (1694) does not provide a terminus ante quem as for one reason or another Locke may have changed his mind since 1688 about the most appropriate representation of himself.
It is likely that the portrait by Greenhill now in the National Portrait Gallery is that owned by Dr. Thomas in 1688. It is possible that after Dr. Thomas's death one of his sons parted with it. In this way a portrait of Locke by Greenhill could have come into Clarke's possession.
HERMAN VERELST (c. 1641-90)
No. 1165 (25 July 1689). About July 1689 Locke was sitting for his portrait by an unnamed artist. This is probably the portrait signed 'H. Verelst / 1689'. Locke probably gave it to Edward Clarke, from whom it descended to the Sanford family at Nynehead Court; it was acquired thence in 1953 by the National Portrait Gallery.
SYLVESTER BROUNOWER
No. 1718 (10 March 1694). The notice relates to the engraved portrait of Locke prefixed to the second edition of the Essay, 1694. The artist is not named in the letter, but the plate names him. The engraver was P. Vanderbanck.
Tinted plumbago drawing. It belonged to Francis Cudworth Masham, who gave it to the Speaker Arthur Onslow. It was acquired by the National Portrait Gallery in 1958.
According to Professor Cranston the drawing was made in 1685. No authority is cited.†2 It seems more likely to be later and to depend to some extent on one or other of the painted portraits of Locke, or a drawing for one of them.
On 29 June 1693 Locke sent Mr. Peter Vander Bank an order for £3.†3 This was presumably in payment or part payment for the engraving.
Syl apparently alludes to this drawing or to a copy of it, as 'my copy of your Picture' in no. 2505 (1 November 1698).
THE PORTRAIT OWNED BY MR. CHURCHILL
No. 2100 (6 June 1695). Mr Churchill (presumably Awnsham) has a portrait of Locke; William Molyneux, the writer of the letter, wants
― 447 ―
a copy of it; but asks Locke to sit for another portrait if it is not good enough. Locke answers in no. 2115.
Nothing further is known of this portrait.
MICHAEL DAHL (1656-1743)
Nos. 2129, 2131, 2170, 2189, 2202, 2221, 2240, 2254, 2416. Painted in response to Molyneux's request of 6 June 1696 (no. 2100); completed by 12 September. The artist is not named.
Identifiable as the painting by Michael Dahl belonging (1963) to the Molyneux family of Dublin; it is inscribed on the back 'John Lock Esq. / M. Dahl pinx / Ao 1696'.
A version, said to have been given by Locke to Paul D'Aranda, was acquired by the National Portrait Gallery in 1860.
A portrait of Locke by Dahl is the subject of an epigram by J. Elsum; it was published in 1700.†1
SIR GODFREY KNELLER (1646-1723)
Nos. 2505, 2508, 3260, 3613, 3617, 3619, 3624. Two or more paintings from the life are concerned. On 11 September 1704 (no. 3624) Locke wrote to Anthony Collins, asking him to get Kneller to paint on the backs of recently painted portraits of Lady Masham and himself their names and the year (1704); 'this he did on Mr Molineux's and mine the last he drew'. By 'mine the last' Locke could mean either the most recent of several portraits or the portrait preceding the present one, two only being concerned. Further the phrase can be either coupled with Molyneux's portrait, indicating one painted at the same time, or taken separately, indicating one painted at some other time.
Only two images by Kneller are known; there are many copies and engravings.
i. In 1703 Dr. Alexander Geekie acquired from Kneller 'that Picture he did of' Locke: no. 3260. This is the portrait engraved by Vertue in 1713 for Locke's Works, 1714; the engraving is 'Ex Archetypo quod in Musæo Alexandri Geekie Chirurgi adservatur expressa'. According to Vertue it was painted in 1697. It belonged later to Sir Robert Walpole and is now in the Hermitage in Leningrad.
Writing to Locke on 1 November 1698 Sylvester Brounower mentioned 'your picture at Kneller's' and Molyneux's; on the 15th neither was finished. If, as seems likely, this was something other than the portrait acquired by Geekie, there is nothing to show for whom it was painted.
― 448 ―
ii. The portrait painted for Anthony Collins in 1704. Kneller was expected to visit Oates in August. It was probably on that occasion that he made the drawing now belonging to Dr. Paul Mellon, or a version of it. A mezzotint engraving was made by John Smith in 1721 when the portrait still belonged to Collins. What is believed to be the Collins version is now in the Virginia Museum of Fine Arts, Richmond, Virginia.
There was a version of this image at Oates; it was engraved by Anthony Walker about 1750.
THE PORTRAIT SENT TO LE CLERC
Nos. 2588, 2595, 2609. Apparently a copy of a version by Greenhill for the engraver's use. See above.
THE PORTRAIT BY DU MOULIN
No. 2629. Locke sat to him about October 1699 for a portrait for Mrs. Furly. Nothing is known of artist or portrait.
THE PORTRAIT MADE FOR GUENELLON
No. 3155. Life-size painting based on an engraving. Nothing is known about it apart from the notice.
― 449 ―
Appendix II: Distribution Lists for Copies of Locke's Books
Among Locke's papers in the Lovelace Collection there are thirteen lists of persons, all except one written entirely by Locke, all except three headed by designations of Locke's publications;†1 it is here assumed that they are lists of persons to whom Locke gave, or considered giving, copies of some of his publications; the following list is compiled from them.
They relate only to books whose authorship Locke acknowledged on their title-pages or during his lifetime. No lists are extant for anonymous books such as the Epistola de Tolerantia.†2
Four lists are on separate pieces of paper†3 and are to be regarded as drafts or Locke's working lists; in one at least there are later additions. The nine further lists are written in separate columns on a full sheet of paper;†4 five of them are to be regarded as file copies, some of them with later additions, of the operative lists; that is, the lists sent to the bookseller at the time of publication; of the rest three appear to be memoranda of copies given at various times and one (no. ix) is perhaps a preparatory list. One of the four and one of the five are for the same book.
The twelve books concerned are distinguished here as A, B, etc. They are arranged by date of publication so far as it is ascertainable.
A f.50. No heading. Endorsed by Locke: Copys 90.
Evidently for the first edition of the Essay. On 3 and 6 December 1689 Locke sent his binder John Graves to the printer for copies; copies were on sale in Oxford about a fortnight later.†5
The list provides for forty-eight copies. Locke began by writing a column of capital letters, either attaching some names as he went down it and writing others later, or waiting until the column was complete and then writing the names; some more names were added
― 450 ―
later. One of the earliest names to be written is Sydenham's; the name is deleted; as he died on 29 December presumably no copy was sent. Ten names are underlined with a row of dots; thirty-four are underlined in the ordinary way; four are not underlined. These markings are probably to distinguish the kinds of binding appropriate to the various recipients. On 9 January Graves presented his bill. Eight copies were bound in turkey at 6s. each (two of them were extra, one at 8s., the other reduced from 8s. to 6s.); thirty-two had gilt backs (i.e. spines) at 2s. each; one was in parchment at 1s. 2d. On 4 July Graves charged Locke for two further copies, by their price with gilt spines; in November there was a further copy in turkey.†1 As the ten persons distinguished by dotted underlines include Pembroke and Queen Mary it is reasonable to assume that they were to receive the copies bound in turkey; those whose names are underlined in the ordinary way would receive copies with gilt spines. Of the remaining four names one, Christ Church, is a late addition to the list; a second, Sibelius, may also be a late addition.†2 The other two names are Grigg and Guise, the first and last in the line for G.†3
B f.53 (iii.) Headed: Education 1693.
Some Thoughts concerning Education. It was published about May 1693.
The list contains 26 names or official designations. They are numbered. The last name is F. Limborch. Locke is unlikely to have given him the book before he anglicized his name, about 1700 or 1701.†4
C f.51. Headed: Copys 94, with the words 'of understanding' inserted.
The Essay, second edition. A bound copy was ready for dispatch on 26 May 1694.†5
The list provides for 24 copies. The names are in two columns. The first contains fifteen names numbered 2-16, the first name, Ashley, being marked for two copies; the second contains eight names; the names in each column are in rough alphabetical order. All the names are crossed through except Clarke, Clericus (i.e. Le Clerc), Furly, Glanville, Guenelon, Montague, and Slone. All are followed by crosses except Clarke, Clericus, Furly, Molesworth, Sommers,
― 451 ―
Glanville, Guenelon, Pepys, and Slone. I cannot guess the significance of these markings.
Thirty-five of the names on the list for the first edition are omitted, one, Lady Shaftesbury, perhaps being provided for by one of the two copies for Lord Ashley; ten names are new.
D f.53 (iv). Headed: Education 1695.
Some Thoughts concerning Education, third edition. It was published about June: nos. 1916, 1921.
The list contains twenty-five names or official designations. They are numbered.
E f.53 (v). Headed: Human Understanding 3d Edit
The Essay. Production was well forward on 2 July 1695: no. 1921.
The list contains three names. The first man, Pierre Coste, received a copy about August 1696: nos. 2107, 2121. The third, Boulton, is probably Richard Boulton; Locke is unlikely to have known him long before 1698: no. 2421. The second, Sir Fleetwood Sheppard, died on 25 August 1698.
F f.52. List written apparently by Lady Masham, with notes, etc. by Locke. No heading.
f.53 (i). Headed: Further considerations concerning the raiseing the value of money 1695. 1 Edit
Further Considerations. It was published shortly before 31 December 1695: no. 1981 n. It appears from their contents that both lists refer to the same book.
The Lady Masham list provides for thirty-six persons by their proper names and for six by their official designations (the three commissioners of the Treasury collectively).†1 Locke inserted some men's addresses and added in the last line after Lady Masham's 'Mr Moleneaux' '2 Sr Jos: Child'; the number is for the general count of copies, not for two copies for one or the other of the two men. Popple is to have five stitched copies besides his own (which would be bound). All the entries except the last five are marked by crosses; I cannot guess their significance. Locke presumably dictated this list to Lady Masham shortly before 20 December. On that day he left Oates, where he had stayed since 18 November; he went to London and did not return until after the book was published.
The list f. 53 (i) provides for forty-eight copies by name or official designation of the recipients. The entries are numbered. The original no. 48 was Lady Masham, but her name was replaced by that of
― 452 ―
Cornelius Lyde. Some of the official designations are collective: the Lords Commissioners of the Treasury (probably Fox, Montagu, and Smith, as Godolphin is provided for later); the Council of Trade (Bridgewater, Stamford, Blathwaite, Meadows, Pollexfen, S. Clarke); the Lords Justices (Tenison, Somers, Pembroke, Devonshire, Shrewsbury, Dorset, Godolphin); and the Lords Chief Justices (Holt, Treby). Somers and Tenison are entered again as Lord Keeper and Archbishop; Molesworth is also entered twice.
The 'Raincrowne' of the Lady Masham list is probably identifiable with 'Monsr Leencrun' in Locke's; that is C. Leijoncrona, Swedish representative in London from 1691 to 1710. Her list has four names that do not occur in Locke's: Mr. Ashley (probably Maurice Ashley Cooper), Popple, Vernon, and Yard (probably James Vernon and Robert Yard, at this time under-secretaries in the office of Shrewsbury, who was one of the two secretaries of state). Locke's list provides for seven persons not included in hers: four of the Lords Justices, Somers, Pembroke, Shrewsbury, and Godolphin; Lord Monmouth, Sir William Trumbull (the second secretary of state), and Cornelius Lyde.†1 There was no need to include Somers in the Lady Masham list as the book is dedicated to him. Pembroke's absence from it is noteworthy; Locke could not fail to ensure that he received a copy. Monmouth's absence may be casual. Popple's absence from Locke's list is perhaps due to his having seen the book through the press; he would receive a copy by gift from Awnsham Churchill.
In my composite list I have included all the persons indicated in both lists.
G f.53 (ii). Headed: Several Papers relateing to Money Interest and Trade 1696.
I have found no close date of publication. It is likely to have been not later than November 1696.
The list has three names, Lady Masham, Chadwick, and Leibniz. They are numbered. Locke sent Leibniz a copy about April 1697: no. 2228 n.
H f.53 v. (vi). Headed: 1697.
The first entry is 'Bp of Worcester'. Somers is designated Lord Keeper; as he became Lord Chancellor on 22 April 1697 the operative list is likely to have been compiled before that date. It evidently relates to A Letter to the ... Bishop of Worcester. The book was published about March 1697.
― 453 ―
The list contains forty-two names. They are numbered. No. 34, Mr. Toland, has been struck out and replaced by Syl (Brounower). No. 39, Newton, is followed by 'and ye first letter'. His and the succeeding names, Richardson and Kneller, may be additions to the operative list. The final name is Mr. Crell; he is probably Samuel Crellius, with whom Locke became acquainted about 1699. Nine names are marked with crosses, probably for specially bound copies.
J f.55. Headed: Reply Copys 97
Mr. Locke's Reply to the ... Bishop of Worcester's Answer to his Letter. Locke dates the piece 29 June 1697; it was published probably about August.†1
The list provides for thirty-eight copies; it ends with 'Money Mr Stepney', which probably refers to Several Papers (G) and is not connected with the present book. The preceding name, Mr. Newton, has a note, 'this and my first'. Stillingfleet is absent. I take it that the list is preparatory to the operative list. Seven (or eight) components, Leibniz among them, are marked by crosses, probably for gilt bindings. Twenty-two (or twenty-three) further components are marked by full-stops; I cannot suggest a meaning. Eleven, some from both these groups, are marked by 'd', probably for delivered.
K f. 53v (vii). Headed: 3 Rep to Bp Worcester 1698
Mr. Locke's Reply to the ... Bishop of Worcester's Answer to his Second Letter. It was published about 15 November 1698.†2
The list provides for forty-one copies. The entries are numbered, but not in numerical sequence. The last name is Crell, probably Samuel Crellius and an addition to the operative list.
L f. 53v (viii). Headed: Education 99.
Some Thoughts, fourth edition, 1699. It was published between 11 October 1698 and 25 January 1699.†3 It came out perhaps before the preceding item.
Three names, not numbered. The third is Crell.
M f. 54 (ix). Headed: Humane understanding 1700.
The Essay, fourth edition. It was advertised on 11 December 1699; copies are likely to have been available for private distribution by the end of November.†4
― 454 ―
Thirteen names, not numbered. Four, Pembroke, the Lord Chancellor, Col. Cothrington (Codrington), and Ld Masham (a slip for Lady Masham), are marked 'gt', that is, for gilt bindings. The list is carefully written, with no deletions; it is perhaps a preparatory list rather than a copy of the operative list.
In the composite list persons are entered generally under the designation by which they are commonly distinguished in my notes to the letters between 1689 and 1700. All official designations are replaced by proper names; peers are entered under their titles; there are crossreferences only for some changes of title and for alternative proper names.
The identity of the persons in the several lists is sometimes certain: there can be only one Lady Masham. In less specific cases I have assumed that the persons in the several lists are the persons of the same names who figure in the correspondence and Locke's Journals; in a few cases letters of thanks confirm the assumption. I have assumed further that if a name appears in several lists it applies to the same person in all of them.

Appendix III: Unprinted Items In B.L., MSS. Locke c. 3-23
Almost all the letters to Locke in the Lovelace Collection are brought together in MSS. Locke c. 3-23 in the Bodleian Library. These volumes contain also some items that do not need to be printed in the present work. First there are legal documents, accounts, prescriptions, medical reports on persons other than Locke, and so on; these are generally sufficiently represented by short descriptions in the notes and should be traceable through the index to the correspondence. Secondly there are a few scraps, too slight to be worth printing by themselves and too detached to be associated with letters printed here. Thirdly there are a few third-party letters that have little or no immediate relevance for Locke, even though some are endorsed by him. The present list records the scraps and third-party letters.
c. 4, f. 152. Scrap: cover for some of Boyle's papers sent to Locke, Nov. 1692.
c. 7, f. 147a. Coste: account for clothing, etc., for F. Cudworth-Masham, Nov. 1698.
c. 7, f. 151.—to Coste, 1698?
c. 7, ff. 207-8. R. Dimsdale to W. Crouch, 3 Sept. 1692.
c. 7, f. 227. Du Bos: scrap.
c. 8, ff. 114-15. J. Firth to H. Lukin, 11 Apr. 1692.
c. 8, ff. 243-4. W. Fuller, bishop of Lincoln, to Shaftesbury, 27 Jan. 1675.
c. 9, ff. 9-10. B. Furly: indenture, 25 Mar. 1701.
c. 9, ff. 11-12. B. Furly to Lord Bellomont, 10 Oct. 1690.
c. 9, ff. 15-21. Papers relating to book-auctions (B. Furly?), Sept. 1687.
c. 10, f. 14. Sidney Godolphin to Lucius Cary (Lord Falkland), N.D.
c. 11, ff. 186-7. Electio (J. Henshawe, 1652?).

Index of Correspondents in this Volume
This index is by the numbers of the letters, italics being used as in the text to indicate letters not by Locke. A bracketed number is either for a letter between persons other than Locke, in which case it appears under the names of both writer (or writers) and person addressed, or for an item other than a letter. If there is correspondence in earlier volumes between Locke and a person in this index it is indicated by the citation of the nearest volume in which any of it occurs.
The citation immediately following a name is that of a biographical note on the person. For persons concerned with only one or two letters the citation is omitted sometimes if the biographical note occurs in the headnote and is insignificant.

The ensuing digest is related to 'A Discourse concerning the Immateriality of the Soul', an examination of the book dated 10 September of this year and appended by Bold to his Discourse concerning the Resurrection of the Same Body (no. 3243 n.). I have not checked the digest or the 'Discourse' by Psychologia generally; some passages in them are quoted from it. The passage from the preface below is quoted correctly.
†1 The act of pushing or thrusting: O.E.D.

Locke appears to have known the book already; he mentions it in 'Some thoughts concerning reading and study for a gentleman': p. 54 below.
†1 A family of this name lived at Smedmore within 2 or 3 miles of Steeple: Hutchins, Dorset, i. 597 (pedigree pp. 570-1).
†2 The piece printed by Desmaizeaux as 'Some thoughts concerning reading and study for a gentleman': A Collection of Several Pieces, pp. 231-45. 'Mr. Locke having one day in conversation discoursed upon the Method, that a young Gentleman should take in his Reading, and Study; one of the company was so well pleas'd with it, that he desir'd him to dictate to him the substance of what he had been speaking: which Mr. Locke immediately did': Desmaizeaux, dedication. Desmaizeaux probably used a manuscript written by Bold and now B.M., Add. MS. 4290, ff. 11-14. This contains no false starts by Locke: occasionally Bold rewrites a word or two, but there are no deleted words or phrases that are not repeated immediately in the text. It is uncertain therefore whether it is what Bold wrote from Locke's dictation or a copy made by him from the dictated original.
Desmaizeaux diverged from the manuscript at two points. The text of the manuscript is printed in Locke, Educational Writings, ed. J. L. Axtell, 1968, pp. 397-404.

†4 In his note to no. 3435 Desmaizeaux identified this book as Reasons against restraining the Press, 1704, which is generally attributed to Matthew Tindal. Desmaizeaux presumably derived some information from Collins, but this identification is questionable. First, Awnsham Churchill sent Locke a copy on 22 January: p. 175; he would scarcely have done so if he had known, as is likely, that the author had sent Locke a copy through Collins a week earlier. Secondly, Locke's remarks in no. 3435 about the book sent to him seem scarcely applicable to Reasons. It is possible that the book was Newton's Opticks, which was published on 14 February: London Gazette, 14 February; Post-Man, 12 February. Newton gave Locke a copy: L.L., no. 2082, 'Ex dono Authoris, Viri Doctissimi, Intigerrimi, Amicissimi'. There is

†1 Collins in part summarizes a passage in Norris, in part gives a general representation of his views: (i) 'Our Author [Locke] makes no just Parallel ... when from the Addition of Motion, Vegetation, Life, etc. to Matter without making any essential change in it, he carries the Gradation of his Argument to Thought: For these things (Sense only excepted, if he means Perception by it, otherwise not) are contain'd within the Idea of Matter, at least by way of consequential Result. Whereas Thought, even by his own Confession, is not contain'd therein, and therefore cannot be added without a specifick Variation; for what tho' it be as he speaks, but one step higher, yet if it be such a step as passes into another kind, 'tis plain that Matter cannot have it and remain what it is ...': p. 54; Norris is answering Locke's second Reply to Stillingfleet, pp. 396-415 (Works, i. 557-63). (ii) I have not found in Norris's Essay, pt. ii, a specific statement that thought is superior to motion, etc., but it is implied in various utterances: 'Thinking is the noblest Operation that I am conscious to my self of'; 'I have in me the Idea of a Being absolutely perfect ... as such an Idea must include Thought, as being a Perfection, so for the contrary Reason it must exclude Extension'; 'the Souls of Beasts are upon this supposition more Noble and Excellent than their Bodies. 1. As Immaterial. 2. As Thinking.': pp. 5, 33, 68.
Vegetation is here the faculty of growth or development; in scholastic philosophy it is common to plants, animals, and men: O.E.D., svv. Vegetation, Vegetative, Sensitive.

Antoine Arnauld's Des Vrayes et des fausses Idées, Cologne, 1683, was regarded by its author and others as a response to the Traité as well as to La Recherche de la Verité. The reference cannot be to Reflexions ... sur le ... Système de la nature et de la grâce, published in July, 1685.
